**8.1.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then, O king, when Droṇa had been slain, the kings led by Duryodhana, their minds sorely troubled, came to Droṇa's son.

**8.1.2**  Grieving for Droṇa, their vigour struck down by despair, stricken with sorrow they gathered round the son of Śāradvatī's daughter.

**8.1.3**  Having for a while consoled one another with reasons drawn from the scriptures, when night came on those guardians of the earth withdrew to their own quarters.

**8.1.4**  Above all the son of the charioteer, and King Suyodhana, and Duḥśāsana, and Śakuni could find no sleep.

**8.1.5**  Even within their dwellings, O Kaurava, those lords of the earth found no ease; brooding on the dire ruin, they could not sleep at all.

**8.1.6**  Gathered together that night in Duryodhana's abode, out of their fierce and burning hatred for the high-souled Pāṇḍavas,

**8.1.7**  recalling how they had dragged Kṛṣṇā, tormented by the dice-game, into the assembly hall, they were filled with remorse, their minds sorely distressed.

**8.1.8**  And brooding on those afflictions of the sons of Pṛthā that the dice-game had wrought, O king, they passed the night in misery, and it seemed to them as long as a hundred years.

**8.1.9**  Then in the clear dawn, abiding under the rule of fate, they all performed their necessary rites with the observances ordained by scripture.

**8.1.10**  Having done their needful tasks and taken heart again, O Bhārata, they gave the order to array the troops and marched out for battle.

**8.1.11**  Having made Karṇa their commander, performing the auspicious rites of consecration, having the foremost of the twice-born pronounce blessings over them with bowls of curds, clarified butter, and unbroken grains,

**8.1.12**  honoured with gold coins, with cattle, with gold, and with garments of great worth, and exalted with benedictions of victory by bards, minstrels, and panegyrists,

**8.1.13**  likewise the Pāṇḍavas, O king, having completed all their daily observances, marched out from their camp, O king, resolved upon battle.

**8.1.14**  Then there broke out a tumultuous, hair-raising battle between the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas, each side seeking the slaughter of the other.

**8.1.15**  For two days the battle of those two armies, the Kuru and the Pāṇḍava hosts, raged on while Karṇa was commander, O king, a wondrous sight to behold.

**8.1.16**  Then, when the Bull among men had wrought a very great destruction of the foe in battle, he was struck down by Phalguna before the very eyes of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons.

**8.1.17**  Then Saṃjaya, going to the city named after the elephant, related the whole of it to Dhṛtarāṣṭra, all that had come to pass in the land of the Kurus.

**8.1.18**  Janamejaya said: When the aged king, the son of Ambikā, heard that the son of the river-goddess and Droṇa had been slain in battle by their foes, and was plunged into the deepest anguish—

**8.1.19**  when he heard that Karṇa too, who sought Duryodhana's welfare, was slain, how, O best of the twice-born, did he in his grief sustain his life?

**8.1.20**  That king reposed his sons' hope of victory in Karṇa; when he was slain, how did the Kaurava keep his life within him?

**8.1.21**  Hard to die indeed, I think, is it for men, even those who dwell in misery, since on hearing that Karṇa was slain the king did not yet give up his life.

**8.1.22**  So too the aged son of Śaṃtanu, O brahmin, and Bāhlika as well, and Droṇa, and Somadatta, and Bhūriśravas—

**8.1.23**  and likewise his other friends, and his sons and grandsons struck down—that on hearing of these he did not let his life go, that, O twice-born one, I deem a thing hard to do.

**8.1.24**  Tell me all this in full, O you whose wealth is austerity; for I am not sated, hearing the great deeds of the men of old.

**8.1.25**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When Karṇa was slain, O great king, the son of Gavalgaṇa, downcast, set out that night for the city of the elephant with horses swift as the wind.

**8.1.26**  Reaching Hāstinapura, his mind sorely troubled, he went to the dwelling of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, now bereft of its kinsmen.

**8.1.27**  Beholding the king, his vigour struck down by despair, with joined palms he made his obeisance, and touched the king's feet with his head.

**8.1.28**  And having honoured Dhṛtarāṣṭra, lord of the earth, as was due, and crying, "Alas, what calamity!", he then took up his speech.

**8.1.29**  "I am Saṃjaya, O lord of the earth. I trust your Majesty fares well. Having come to this disaster through your own fault, are you not today utterly bewildered?

**8.1.30**  Recalling the wholesome counsels spoken by Vidura, Droṇa, the son of Gaṅgā, and Keśava, counsels you did not heed—do you not now feel anguish?

**8.1.31**  Recalling the wholesome words spoken by Rāma, Nārada, and Kaṇva in the assembly hall, words you did not heed—do you not now feel anguish?

**8.1.32**  Recalling your friends, devoted to your good and led by Bhīṣma and Droṇa, slain in battle by the foe—do you not now feel anguish?"

**8.1.33**  To the charioteer's son who spoke thus with joined palms, the king, drawing a long, deep sigh, stricken with grief, said this:

**8.1.34**  "Now that the heroic son of Gaṅgā, who wielded divine weapons, has been slain, O Saṃjaya, and Droṇa too, that supreme bowman, my mind is sorely afflicted.

**8.1.35**  He who, born of the Vasus and full of splendour, day after day slew ten thousand mailed warriors on their chariots—

**8.1.36**  that he was slain by Yajñasena's son Śikhaṇḍin, shielded by the Pāṇḍavas—at this my mind is sorely afflicted.

**8.1.37**  He to whom, the high-souled one, the descendant of Bhṛgu gave the supreme weapon, he who in boyhood was instructed in the science of the bow by Rāma himself—

**8.1.38**  by whose grace the sons of Kuntī, those mighty princes, and other lords of the earth as well, attained the rank of great chariot-warriors—

**8.1.39**  hearing that this Droṇa, true to his word, that great bowman, was slain in battle by Dhṛṣṭadyumna, my mind is sorely afflicted.

**8.1.40**  He whose equal in the science of arms was not to be found among men in all the three worlds—when they heard that this Droṇa was slain, what did my men do?

**8.1.41**  When the host of the saṃśaptakas was sent, by the valour of the high-souled Pāṇḍava Dhanaṃjaya, to the abode of Yama,

**8.1.42**  and when the Nārāyaṇa weapon of the wise son of Droṇa had been undone, and the surviving ranks of the army stood, what did my men do?

**8.1.43**  I think them put to flight, sunk in an ocean of grief when Droṇa was slain, drifting like men whose ship has foundered upon the sea.

**8.1.44**  Of Duryodhana, of Karṇa, of the Bhoja Kṛtavarman, of Śalya the king of the Madras, and of Droṇa's son, and of Kṛpa—

**8.1.45**  and of my surviving sons, and likewise of the others, O Saṃjaya—when their ranks were scattered, what hue was upon their faces?

**8.1.46**  Tell me, O son of Gavalgaṇa, all this just as it came to pass in battle, truly and in full, of both the Pāṇḍavas and my own men."

**8.1.47**  Saṃjaya said: What the Pāṇḍavas did to the Kauravas, O honoured one—hearing it, do not give way to anguish; over what is destined the mind is not afflicted.

**8.1.48**  Since whatever outcome is to befall a man, whether it shall come to pass or not, no wise man is troubled by its coming or its failing to come.

**8.1.49**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: No anguish whatever is in me as I listen, Saṃjaya; this, I think, was ordained long ago. Tell it as you will.

**8.2.1**  Saṃjaya said: When Droṇa the great bowman was slain, your sons, those mighty chariot-warriors, grew downcast of face, dejected, and bereft of spirit.

**8.2.2**  With faces bowed down, those bearers of weapons, every one of them, O lord of peoples, not even looking at one another, stricken with grief, spoke not among themselves.

**8.2.3**  Seeing them in their stricken aspect, your troops, O Bhārata, gazed only upward, oftentimes panicked with grief.

**8.2.4**  And their weapons, O lord of kings, all of them stained with blood, slipped from their fingertips at the sight of Droṇa struck down.

**8.2.5**  Those weapons, still bound to them yet unwelcome, hanging loose, O Bhārata, appeared, O great king, like the stars in the sky.

**8.2.6**  Seeing his own army thus stricken and motionless, standing as though its very life had gone out of it, O great king, King Duryodhana spoke:

**8.2.7**  "Relying upon the strength of your arms, I challenged the Pāṇḍavas to battle, and this war was set in motion.

**8.2.8**  Now that Droṇa is slain, this host seems sunk in dejection; yet warriors who fight in battle are struck down on every side.

**8.2.9**  Whether victory or death befall the man who fights in battle—what marvel is there in that? Fight on, then, facing every quarter!

**8.2.10**  And behold the high-souled Karṇa, son of the Sun, that great bowman of mighty strength, ranging through the battle with his divine weapons!

**8.2.11**  He, from dread of whom in battle Kuntī's son Dhanaṃjaya ever turns back in vexation, as a small beast from a lion—

**8.2.12**  he by whom Bhīmasena, mighty in strength, with the vigour of ten thousand elephants, was brought to such a plight by mere human combat—

**8.2.13**  he by whom that hero Ghaṭotkaca, knower of divine weapons and master of illusions, was slain in battle with the unfailing spear, even as he roared a fearsome roar—

**8.2.14**  the inexhaustible might of the arms of that wise hero, true to his word and unconquerable in valour, you shall behold this day in battle.

**8.2.15**  The prowess of both these high-souled ones, of Droṇa's son and of the son of Rādhā, you shall also behold amid the hosts of the Pāṇḍavas and the Pāñcālas.

**8.2.16**  And you yourselves, all of you—heroes, wise, nobly born, of good conduct, and skilled in arms—shall this day behold one another's deeds."

**8.2.17**  When this had been said, O great king, Karṇa, son of the Sun, the mighty prince, raising aloud a lion's roar, gave battle with all his great strength.

**8.2.18**  Before the eyes of all the Sṛñjayas, the Pāñcālas, the Kekayas, and the Videhas, he wrought a great slaughter.

**8.2.19**  From his bow there poured forth showers of arrows by the hundred, joined point to feathered shaft like ranks of bees.

**8.2.20**  Having harried the Pāñcālas and the swift Pāṇḍavas, and having slain warriors by the thousand, he was at last struck down by Arjuna.

**8.3.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When he had heard this, O great king, Dhṛtarāṣṭra the son of Ambikā, seeing no end to his grief and believing Suyodhana slain, fell distraught upon the ground like an elephant bereft of consciousness.

**8.3.2**  When that best of kings, distraught, had fallen upon the ground, there arose a great cry of anguish from the women, O best of the Bhāratas.

**8.3.3**  That sound filled the whole earth on every side; the women of the Bharatas were plunged in a vast and terrible ocean of sorrow.

**8.3.4**  And drawing near to the king, O bull of the Bhāratas, Gāndhārī fell senseless to the ground, and so did all the women of the inner apartments.

**8.3.5**  Then Saṃjaya, O king, comforted those stricken women, who were swooning again and again and shedding the water born of their eyes.

**8.3.6**  Though comforted, those women trembled again and again, shaken on every side like plantain trees buffeted by the wind.

**8.3.7**  And Vidura too then comforted the king, that lord whose only sight was wisdom, sprinkling the Kaurava with water.

**8.3.8**  Slowly regaining his senses, and beholding those women, O king, the king stood silent like one out of his mind, O lord of peoples.

**8.3.9**  Then, having brooded a long while and sighing again and again, he reproached his own sons and held the Pāṇḍavas in high regard.

**8.3.10**  Having condemned his own judgement and that of Śakuni the son of Subala, and having pondered for a very long while, trembling again and again,

**8.3.11**  and having steadied his mind once more, the king, now possessed of fortitude, questioned again the charioteer Saṃjaya, the son of Gavalgaṇa.

**8.3.12**  The words you have spoken, Saṃjaya, I have heard; but tell me, charioteer—surely Duryodhana has not gone to the abode of Yama? Speak truly, Saṃjaya, and tell over again this same account.

**8.3.13**  Thus addressed, Janamejaya, the charioteer answered the king: Slain is Vaikartana, O king, together with his sons, the mighty warriors, and with his brothers, the great archers, the sons of charioteers who gave up their bodies in battle.

**8.3.14**  And Duḥśāsana was slain by the glorious son of Pāṇḍu, and in his wrath Bhīmasena drank his blood upon the field of battle.

**8.4.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hearing this, O great king, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the son of Ambikā, his mind distracted with grief, spoke to Saṃjaya the charioteer.

**8.4.2**  My son, my soul is overwhelmed by my own ill counsel; hearing that Vaikartana is slain, grief cuts me to the quick.

**8.4.3**  Among the Kurus and the Sṛñjayas, supreme masters of weapons longing to cross to the further shore of sorrow—who now live, and who are dead?

**8.4.4**  Saṃjaya said: Slain, O king, is the son of Śaṃtanu, the unassailable and majestic one, after he had slain a host of the Pāṇḍava warriors in the space of ten days.

**8.4.5**  Then Droṇa, the great archer, hard to overcome in battle, having slain the chariot-ranks of the Pāñcālas, was afterward struck down—he of the golden car.

**8.4.6**  And Karṇa, son of Vikartana, having slain half of the army that remained after the slaughter wrought by Bhīṣma and the great-souled Droṇa, was himself slain.

**8.4.7**  Viviṃśati, O great king, the mighty prince, having slain the Ānarta warriors by hundreds, was himself slain in battle.

**8.4.8**  Then your son Vikarṇa, mindful of the kṣatriya's vow, the hero whose mount and weapons were spent, stood facing the foe.

**8.4.9**  He was struck down by Bhīmasena, who remembered the many dreadful torments wrought by Duryodhana, and his own oath as well.

**8.4.10**  Vinda and Anuvinda, the two princes of Avanti, mighty in strength, having performed a deed no easy thing to do, went to the abode of the son of Vivasvat.

**8.4.11**  That hero under whose sway ten realms, the kingdom of Sindhu chief among them, abode, and who stood firm in your command—

**8.4.12**  that Jayadratha of great valor, having vanquished eleven divisions of troops with his sharpened arrows, was slain by Arjuna, O king.

**8.4.13**  So too the swift son of Duryodhana, frenzied for battle, abiding by his father's command, was struck down by the son of Subhadrā.

**8.4.14**  So too the heroic son of Duḥśāsana, strong of arm and fierce in battle, was sent valorously to the seat of Yama by the son of Draupadī.

**8.4.15**  The lord of the Kirātas who dwell along the sea-marshes, righteous of soul, the dear and highly esteemed friend of the king of the gods—

**8.4.16**  that king Bhagadatta, ever devoted to the kṣatriya's law, was sent valorously to the seat of Yama by Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.4.17**  So too the heir of the Kurus, the son of Somadatta, of great renown, the hero Bhūriśravas, was slain in battle by Sātyaki, O king.

**8.4.18**  And Śrutāyu of the Ambaṣṭhas, a bowman among kṣatriyas, ranging fearlessly through the fray, was slain by Savyasācin.

**8.4.19**  Your son Duḥśāsana, ever skilled in arms in battle and frenzied for the fight, was struck down by Bhīmasena, O great king.

**8.4.20**  That Sudakṣiṇa, O king, whose elephant host was many thousands strong and wondrous, was slain in the encounter by Savyasācin.

**8.4.21**  The lord of the Kosalas, having slain his foes by the hundreds, was sent valorously to the seat of Yama by the son of Subhadrā.

**8.4.22**  Your son Citrasena, a great chariot-warrior, having fought many a bout with Bhīmasena, was struck down by Bhīmasena himself.

**8.4.23**  The heroic son of the king of the Madras, who heightened the terror of his foes, the resplendent bearer of sword and shield, was struck down by the son of Subhadrā.

**8.4.24**  Vṛṣasena, of great splendor, swift with his weapons and firm of resolve, the equal of Karṇa in battle, before the very eyes of Karṇa—

**8.4.25**  recalling the slaying of Abhimanyu and his own vow as well, Dhanaṃjaya sent him valorously to the seat of Yama.

**8.4.26**  That lord of earth Śrutāyu, ever bound in unceasing enmity with the Pāṇḍavas, having proclaimed his feud, was struck down by Pārtha.

**8.4.27**  And the valiant son of Śalya, Rukmaratha, your nephew on the mother's side, O honored one, was slain in battle by Sahadeva, O king.

**8.4.28**  The aged king Bhagīratha and Bṛhatkṣatra of the Kekayas, two valiant ones of surpassing might, striving in their prowess, were slain.

**8.4.29**  The son of Bhagadatta, O king, sagacious and mighty in strength, was struck down by Nakula, who ranged through the battle like a hawk.

**8.4.30**  Your grandsire too, Bāhlika, together with the Bāhlīkas, was sent valorously to the seat of Yama by Bhīmasena.

**8.4.31**  So too Jayatsena, son of Jarāsaṃdha, of great strength, the Māgadha, was slain in battle by the great-souled son of Subhadrā.

**8.4.32**  Your sons Durmukha and Duḥsaha, the great chariot-warrior, O king, who held themselves heroes, were both slain by Bhīmasena with his mace.

**8.4.33**  Durmarṣaṇa, Durviṣaha, and Durjaya the great chariot-warrior, having performed a deed no easy thing to do, went to the abode of the son of Vivasvat.

**8.4.34**  Your counselor Vṛṣavarman, the charioteer of supreme valor, was sent valorously to the seat of Yama by Bhīmasena.

**8.4.35**  The king Nāgāyutabala, mighty with the strength of ten thousand elephants, together with his troop, was slain by the son of Pāṇḍu, Savyasācin.

**8.4.36**  The Vasātis, O great king, two thousand fighting men, and the valiant Śūrasenas, were all struck down in battle.

**8.4.37**  The mailed Abhīṣāhas, smiting and frenzied with battle-fury, and the noble car-warriors among the Śibis, together with the Kaliṅgas, were slain.

**8.4.38**  The bands reared ever among the cow-herds, supremely skilled in war, the troops many thousands strong, and the hosts of the saṃśaptakas—all these, having come against Pārtha, went to the abode of the son of Vivasvat.

**8.4.39**  Your two brothers-in-law, O great king, the kings Vṛṣaka and Acala, who strove valiantly for your sake, were slain by Savyasācin.

**8.4.40**  The king of the Śālvas, a great archer fierce in deed both in name and in act, O great king, was struck down by Bhīmasena.

**8.4.41**  Oghavat and Bṛhanta together, O great king, striving in battle for their ally's sake, went to the abode of the son of Vivasvat.

**8.4.42**  So too the foremost of car-warriors, Kṣemadhūrti, O lord of peoples, was slain with the mace by Bhīmasena in the fray, O king.

**8.4.43**  So too the king Jalasaṃdha, a great archer of mighty strength, having wrought a vast slaughter, was slain by Sātyaki in battle.

**8.4.44**  Alāyudha, lord of the rākṣasas, who rode a car drawn by asses, was sent valorously to the seat of Yama by Ghaṭotkaca.

**8.4.45**  The sons of Rādhā and the charioteers' sons, and his brothers the great car-warriors, and the Kekayas as well, were all slain by Savyasācin.

**8.4.46**  The Mālavas and the Madrakas, the Draviḍas of fierce prowess, the Yaudheyas and the Lalitthas, the Kṣudrakas and the Uśīnaras—

**8.4.47**  the Māvellakas, the Tuṇḍikeras, the Sāvitrīputras and the Kāñcalas, the men of the East and the North, of the West and of the South, O honored one—

**8.4.48**  the throngs of footmen were slain, and tens of thousands of horses, and the ranks of chariots were destroyed, and the noble elephants slain.

**8.4.49**  Heroes with their banners, their weapons, their armor, raiment, and ornaments, prepared over a long span of time and reared each in his own line—

**8.4.50**  these were slain in battle, O king, by Pārtha of untiring deeds, and others of measureless strength who sought one another's destruction.

**8.4.51**  These and many other kings with their hosts were slain by the thousands in battle, O king, as you ask of me; thus came about this destruction at the meeting of Karṇa and Arjuna.

**8.4.52**  As Vṛtra was slain by great Indra, as Rāvaṇa by Rāma, as Muru was cast down and slain in battle by Kṛṣṇa, and as Kārtavīrya was slain by Rāma of the Bhṛgu line—

**8.4.53**  so the hero with his kinsmen and friends, frenzied for the fight in battle, having waged a great and terrible war renowned through the three worlds—

**8.4.54**  even so Karṇa, the best of smiters, frenzied for the fight, was slain in single chariot-combat by Arjuna, together with his counselors and kinsmen, O king.

**8.4.55**  The hope of victory of the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and the very source of the feud, that have the Pāṇḍavas now passed beyond, O king—that which long ago you would not perceive.

**8.4.56**  Though told of it, O great king, by kinsmen who wished you well, this calamity of great ruin has now overtaken you.

**8.4.57**  Wishing well to your sons who craved a kingdom, O king, you wrought what was as if to their harm; the fruit of those deeds has now come upon you.

**8.4.58**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: You have told me, my son, of my own men slain in battle by the Pāṇḍavas; now tell me, Saṃjaya, of the Pāṇḍavas' men slain by mine.

**8.4.59**  Saṃjaya said: The Kuntīs, valiant in battle, of great courage and great strength, with their followers and counselors, were struck down in battle by Bhīṣma.

**8.4.60**  Satyajit, the equal in battle of the diademed one in valor and in strength, was slain in the fray by the true-vowed Droṇa.

**8.4.61**  So too the aged kings Virāṭa and Drupada, together with their sons, striving for their ally's sake, were slain in battle by Droṇa.

**8.4.62**  He who, though but a boy in battle, was reckoned the equal of Savyasācin, of Keśava, and of Baladeva, unassailable and overpowering—

**8.4.63**  that very Abhimanyu, skilled in war, having wrought a great slaughter, was surrounded by six foremost mighty chariot-warriors who could not withstand Bībhatsu, and was struck down.

**8.4.64**  That hero, made carless yet steadfast in the kṣatriya's law, the son of Subhadrā, was slain in battle by the son of Duḥśāsana, O great king.

**8.4.65**  Bṛhanta, the great archer skilled in arms and frenzied for the fight, was sent valorously to the seat of Yama by Duḥśāsana.

**8.4.66**  Maṇimat and Daṇḍadhāra, two kings frenzied for the fight, striving for their ally's sake, were struck down by Droṇa.

**8.4.67**  Aṃśumat, the Bhoja king, a great chariot-warrior, together with his army, was sent valorously to the seat of Yama by the son of Bharadvāja.

**8.4.68**  Citrāyudha and Citrayodhin, having both wrought a great slaughter, were slain in battle by Karṇa, who struck them valorously by a wondrous course of arms.

**8.4.69**  The Kekaya prince, the equal of Vṛkodara in battle and steadfast in the fray, was struck down valorously by his own brother, a Kekaya—brother by brother.

**8.4.70**  Janamejaya, the mace-fighter of the mountain-folk, full of majesty, was struck down by your son Durmukha, O great king.

**8.4.71**  The two Rocamānas, tigers among men, shining like two planets, were sent at once to heaven by Droṇa's arrows, O king.

**8.4.72**  And kings who fought back valiantly, O lord of peoples, having performed a deed no easy thing to do, went to the abode of the son of Vivasvat.

**8.4.73**  Purujit Kuntibhoja, the maternal uncle of Savyasācin, was sent by Droṇa's shafts to the worlds won by battle.

**8.4.74**  Abhibhū, the king of Kāśi, surrounded by many men of Kāśi, was made to lay down his body in the fight by the son of Vasudāna.

**8.4.75**  Yudhāmanyu of measureless might and the valiant Uttamaujas, having slain heroes by the hundreds, were slain by the foe in battle.

**8.4.76**  Kṣatradharman the Pāñcāla and Kṣatravarman, O honored one, those two supreme archers, were sent to the seat of Yama by Droṇa.

**8.4.77**  The son of Śikhaṇḍin, Kṣatradeva, a lord of warriors, was slain in battle by Lakṣmaṇa, your grandson, O king, O descendant of Bharata.

**8.4.78**  Sucitra and Citradharman, father and son, both mighty chariot-warriors of great valor, while they ranged across the field were slain in battle by Droṇa.

**8.4.79**  Vārdhakṣemi, O great king, after wreaking havoc in the fray, was struck down, O great king, by the Kaurava Bāhlika.

**8.4.80**  Dhṛṣṭaketu, O great king, foremost chariot-warrior of the Cedis, having done a deed hard to accomplish, went to the abode of Vaivasvata.

**8.4.81**  So too Satyadhṛti, my son, having wrought slaughter in the fray and shown his prowess for the Pāṇḍavas' cause, was sent to the dwelling of Yama.

**8.4.82**  And the son of Śiśupāla, Suketu, O lord of the earth, having slain his foes in the clash, was himself slain in battle by Droṇa.

**8.4.83**  Likewise the hero Satyadhṛti, the valiant Madirāśva, and the bold Sūryadatta were laid low by the arrows of Droṇa.

**8.4.84**  And Śreṇimat, O great king, valorous as he fought, having performed a deed hard to do, went to the abode of Vaivasvata.

**8.4.85**  In the same way the Māgadha, bold in battle, slayer of enemy heroes, valorous as he fought, was slain, O king, by Bhīṣma.

**8.4.86**  And Vasudāna, wreaking great carnage in the conflict, was sent by the prowess of the son of Bharadvāja to the dwelling of Yama.

**8.4.87**  These and many other great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas were slain by Droṇa through his valor — this is what you ask of me.

**8.4.88**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Now that the foremost heroes of this my army are slain, O best of speakers, tell me, O Sūta, of those unslain — whoever among them still lives.

**8.4.89**  Now that those you have named are slain, even those whom you reckon unslain — they too, I deem, are as good as won over by heaven.

**8.4.90**  Saṃjaya said: He in whom the great weapons were vested — wondrous, gleaming, of four kinds, and divine, O king — bestowed upon him by Droṇa, best of heroic brahmins;

**8.4.91**  that great chariot-warrior, skilled and swift of hand, firm in his weapons, firm in grip, firm in his arrows — the valiant, impetuous son of Droṇa stands ready, eager to fight for your sake.

**8.4.92**  That dweller of Ānarta, the son of Hṛdika, a great chariot-warrior, foremost among the Sātvatas — Kṛtavarman the Bhoja himself, master of weapons — stands ready, eager to fight for your sake.

**8.4.93**  The son of Śaradvat, the Gautama too, O king, mighty in strength, a fighter with many varied weapons, taking up his wondrous bow, great and able to bear the strain, stands ready and willing to fight.

**8.4.94**  The son of Ṛtāyana, unshakable in battle, leader of the host, first among your men — having spared those his nephews, the sons of Pāṇḍu, the impetuous one, wishing to make true his pledged word.

**8.4.95**  Having vowed before Ajātaśatru in the clash to quell the splendor of the Sūta's son, the unassailable Śalya, of valor equal to Śakra's, stands ready, eager to fight for your sake.

**8.4.96**  With horses of high breed, those of Sindhu and the mountains, and the river-born ones of Kāmboja, Vanāyu, and Bāhlika, the king of Gāndhāra, joined with his own host, stands ready, eager to fight for your sake.

**8.4.97**  And your son too, O foremost of the Kurus, mounting a chariot blazing like fire and sun, Kurumitra stands ready, O lord of men, like the sun shining in a cloudless sky.

**8.4.98**  Duryodhana, amid his host of elephants, of mighty valor, together with the foremost warriors of the army, on his chariot adorned with gold, stands ready in the battle, eager to fight.

**8.4.99**  That foremost of men, amid the kings, shone forth in his bright golden mail, lotus-hued, like a fire with little smoke, radiant as the sun gleaming between the clouds.

**8.4.100**  So too Suṣeṇa, sword and shield in hand, your son, and the hero Satyasena, both stand together with Citrasena, glad of heart, eager to fight in the battle.

**8.4.101**  The Bharata princes who shun all that is shameful — Citrāyudha, Śrutakarman, and Jaya, and Śala, and Satyavrata and Duḥśalā — stand ready, mighty and eager to fight.

**8.4.102**  The lord of the Kaitavyas, proud in his valor, a slayer of foes in battle after battle, that prince, with foot and horse, marching with his elephants and chariots, stands ready, eager to fight for your sake.

**8.4.103**  The hero Śrutāyus and Śrutāyudha, Citrāṅgada and that hero Citravarman — those foremost of men in the army stand ready, smiters all, proud and true to their pledges.

**8.4.104**  The son of Karṇa, the high-souled Satyasena, stands ready in battle, eager to fight; and two other worthy sons of Karṇa, deft of hand, O lord of men, stand ready, leaning upon a mighty host hard for the faint-hearted to break, willing to fight for your sake.

**8.4.105**  With these and other chief warriors, foremost in arms, of boundless might, the king of the Kurus stands amid his host of elephants, like great Indra, bent on victory.

**8.4.106**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: You have told me truly of those who yet live and of the others as well; thus I clearly perceive how things will come to pass, by reckoning the course of events.

**8.4.107**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Even as he spoke thus, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the son of Ambikā, hearing that his own army was shattered, its heroes slain, with but a remnant left, fell into a swoon, his senses overwhelmed by grief.

**8.4.108**  Swooning, he said: Hold a moment, Saṃjaya; my mind is distraught, my son, hearing of so great a sorrow. And then the lord of the earth lost his senses.

**8.5.1**  Janamejaya said: Hearing that Karṇa was slain in the battle and that his sons did not flee, what did the king, somewhat recovered, then say, O best of the twice-born?

**8.5.2**  He who had won the utmost grief, the great sorrow born of his sons' ruin — what he said at that hour, tell me that, for I ask of you.

**8.5.3**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hearing of Karṇa's death — incredible, marvelous, bewildering to all beings, and terrible as the overturning of Meru;

**8.5.4**  like an unaccountable bewilderment of mind in the wise descendant of Bhṛgu, like a defeat of Indra of fearsome deeds at the hands of his foes;

**8.5.5**  like the fall of the radiant sun from heaven to the earth, like the unthinkable drying up of the ocean of inexhaustible waters;

**8.5.6**  like the marvelous total destruction of earth, sky, and the guardians of the quarters, like the fruitlessness of both deeds, the meritorious and the sinful alike.

**8.5.7**  Pondering it shrewdly in his mind, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, lord of the people, reflecting upon Karṇa's death, thought: "This cannot be."

**8.5.8**  "Yet, since this is the very nature of living things, such destruction may indeed be" — and his heart was consumed by the fire of grief, as though fanned into flame.

**8.5.9**  His spirit broken, sighing, wretched, crying "Alas, alas!" in deep anguish, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the son of Ambikā, lamented, O great king.

**8.5.10**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Saṃjaya, the heroic son of Adhiratha, of the prowess of a lion and an elephant, the bull among men of matchless shoulders, with the eyes, the gait, and the voice of a bull;

**8.5.11**  a bull who, like a bull, would not turn back in battle even from great Indra his foe — young, his frame hard as the thunderbolt;

**8.5.12**  he at the twang of whose bowstring and palm-guard and the roar of whose shower of arrows, chariots, horses, men, and elephants could not stand their ground in the fray;

**8.5.13**  relying upon whom — the mighty-armed, unfaltering destroyer of enemy hosts — Duryodhana waged his feud against the sons of Pāṇḍu of great strength:

**8.5.14**  how was that best of chariot-warriors, Karṇa, the tiger among men of irresistible prowess, slain by force in battle by Pārtha?

**8.5.15**  He who, ever relying on the might of his own arm, esteemed neither the unfaltering Kṛṣṇa nor Dhanaṃjaya, nor those other Vṛṣṇis;

**8.5.16**  who said, "The wielders of the Śārṅga and the Gāṇḍīva, united and unvanquished — I alone shall bring them down from their divine chariot in battle";

**8.5.17**  thus spoke he ever to that dullard, Duryodhana, deluded by greed, base, craving the kingdom, and distressed.

**8.5.18**  He who conquered foes most mighty and hard to subdue — the Gāndhāras, the Madrakas, the Matsyas, the Trigartas, the Taṅgaṇas, the Śakas;

**8.5.19**  the Pāñcālas and the Videhas, the Kuṇindas, the Kāśis and Kosalas, the Suhmas and the Aṅgas, the Puṇḍras, the Niṣādas, the Vaṅgas, and the Kīcakas;

**8.5.20**  the Vatsas, the Kaliṅgas, the Taralas, the Aśmakas, and the Ṛṣikas as well — that hero, having conquered them in battle, made them tribute-bearers of old.

**8.5.21**  As Uccaiḥśravas is the best of horses, as Vaiśravaṇa is the best of kings, as great Indra is the best of the gods, so Karṇa was the best of smiters.

**8.5.22**  He whom the king of Magadha, having won, by conciliation, honor, gifts, and esteem, hemmed in all the warrior princes of the earth, save the Kauravas and the Yādavas.

**8.5.23**  Hearing that Karṇa was slain in single combat by Savyasācin, I am plunged in an ocean of grief, like one without a raft upon the sea.

**8.5.24**  If I am not destroyed by such sorrows, Saṃjaya, I deem my heart harder than the thunderbolt and impossible to break.

**8.5.25**  Hearing of this defeat of kinsmen, relations, and friends, what other man in the world but me, O Sūta, would not give up his life?

**8.5.26**  I would choose poison, or fire, or a plunge from a mountain's peak; for I shall not be able to bear these grievous sorrows, Saṃjaya.

**8.5.27**  Saṃjaya said: For your splendor, your lineage, your fame, your austerity, and your learning, the good this day reckon you the equal of Yayāti, son of Nahuṣa.

**8.5.28**  In learning you are the peer of a great sage; you have fulfilled your purpose, O king. Compose yourself; do not set your mind upon despair.

**8.5.29**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: I deem fate supreme; a curse upon manly effort, which is in vain — for there Karṇa, the equal of Rāma, was slain in battle.

**8.5.30**  That great chariot-warrior, having slain Yudhiṣṭhira's host and the chariot-squadrons of the Pāñcālas, scorching all the quarters with his rain of arrows —

**8.5.31**  having bewildered the Pārthas in battle as the thunderbolt-handed one bewilders the Asuras — how is it that he, slain, lies low like a tree shattered by the wind?

**8.5.32**  I see no end to my grief, as a drowning man sees none to the ocean. My sharp anguish grows, and a longing to die is born in me.

**8.5.33**  Hearing of Karṇa's death and of Phalguna's victory, I hold the slaying of Karṇa beyond belief, Saṃjaya.

**8.5.34**  Surely my heart is made of the essence of adamant, and most firm, since, hearing that Karṇa, the tiger among men, is slain, it does not burst asunder.

**8.5.35**  Surely a very long life was ordained for me by the gods of old, since, hearing that Karṇa is slain, I yet live on here in deep sorrow.

**8.5.36**  A curse upon this life of mine today, bereft of friends, Saṃjaya. This day, Saṃjaya, fallen into this contemptible state, I shall pass my days in misery, pitiable to all, dull of wit.

**8.5.37**  I who once was honored by all the world — how, O Sūta, despised, shall I be able to live on again? From sorrow to deeper sorrow I have come, into calamity, Saṃjaya.

**8.5.38**  Therefore, after the slaying of Bhīṣma and of the high-souled Droṇa, now that the Sūta's son too is slain in battle, I see no remnant left.

**8.5.39**  For he was the great further shore for my sons, Saṃjaya — that hero, slain in battle while loosing many arrows.

**8.5.40**  What use is life to me without that bull among men? That surpassing chariot-warrior must have fallen from his car, tormented by arrows,

**8.5.41**  like a mountain's peak riven by the fall of the thunderbolt; surely he lies upon the earth, adorning it, drenched in blood, like an elephant struck down by a rutting elephant.

**8.5.42**  He who was the strength of the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra and from whom came the Pāṇḍavas' fear — that Karṇa, the standard by which bowmen are measured, was slain by Arjuna.

**8.5.43**  For that hero, the great archer, who gave my sons their freedom from fear — that hero now lies slain, as Bala was slain by Śakra.

**8.5.44**  Duryodhana's design has proved like the journeying of a cripple, like the desire of a pauper, like the water-droplets craved by one athirst.

**8.5.45**  The deed is purposed one way, yet it comes to pass in another. Ah, how mighty is fate, and how impossible to overstep is time!

**8.5.46**  Fleeing piteously, his spirit broken, his manhood failing — has my son Duḥśāsana not been slain, O Sūta?

**8.5.47**  Did he not act basely in the fray, my son? Was that hero not slain — slain not as warriors are slain in honor?

**8.5.48**  Yudhiṣṭhira's word, ever "Let there be no war" — that the deluded Duryodhana would not accept, as a fool will not take wholesome medicine.

**8.5.49**  When Bhīṣma, that most high-souled one, lying upon his bed of arrows, asked for water, Pārtha pierced the surface of the earth.

**8.5.50**  Seeing that stream of water sent forth by the Pāṇḍava, the mighty-armed one said, my child: "Make peace with the Pāṇḍavas."

**8.5.51**  "For from reconciliation peace would come; let this raging war be ended. In brotherhood enjoy the earth together with the sons of Pāṇḍu."

**8.5.52**  Surely my son now grieves, having not heeded his words; for now there has come to pass this very utterance of that far-sighted one.

**8.5.53**  But I, my counsellors slain and my sons slain, O Saṃjaya, have through the dice-game fallen into misery, like a bird with its wings clipped.

**8.5.54**  For just as little boys, O Saṃjaya, seizing a bird and clipping its two wings, set it loose in their delight, sporting at play—

**8.5.55**  and with its wings cut off no flight is possible for it—so too am I now reduced, like a bird with its wings clipped.

**8.5.56**  Wasted away, stripped of every aim, without kinsmen, bereft of my relations—to what quarter shall I turn, wretched and fallen into the power of my foes?

**8.5.57**  He, the lord who for Duryodhana's increase had conquered the earth, has been vanquished by the Pāṇḍavas—heroes, mighty, abounding in valour.

**8.5.58**  When that great archer Karṇa was slain in battle by the Diademed one, which heroes still stood their ground? Tell me that, O Saṃjaya.

**8.5.59**  Was he not abandoned and alone when the Pāṇḍavas struck him down in battle? You told me before, brave one, how the heroes were laid low.

**8.5.60**  Bhīṣma, who would not fight back, Śikhaṇḍin felled in battle with his finest shafts—him, the best of all who bear weapons.

**8.5.61**  So too Droṇa—that great archer who had laid down all his weapons in the fight and was rapt in yoga, pierced with many arrows—was slain by Draupadī's son, by Dhṛṣṭadyumna with upraised sword, O Saṃjaya.

**8.5.62**  By stealth were these two slain, and above all by trickery—so I have heard, that Bhīṣma and Droṇa were brought down.

**8.5.63**  For Bhīṣma and Droṇa, were they fighting by fair means, not even the Wielder of the Thunderbolt himself could slay them in battle—this truth I declare to you.

**8.5.64**  But Karṇa, hurling his many celestial weapons—how did death touch in battle that hero who was the equal of Indra?

**8.5.65**  He to whom, in exchange for his earrings, Purandara gave that celestial spear, gleaming like lightning and adorned with gold, the slayer of foes—

**8.5.66**  he whose celestial serpent-mouthed shaft, gold-adorned and feathered, the destroyer of foes, lay resting amid the sandalwood—

**8.5.67**  he who, scorning such heroes and great chariot-warriors as Bhīṣma and Droṇa, learned from the son of Jamadagni the most terrible Brāhma weapon—

**8.5.68**  he, the mighty-armed, who when he saw Droṇa and the rest turned away and tormented by arrows, shattered with his shafts the bow of Subhadrā's son—

**8.5.69**  he who, having made my brother's foe Bhīmasena chariotless—him whose strength was that of ten thousand elephants, swift as the wind and unfaltering—laughed at him in mockery—

**8.5.70**  he who, having vanquished Sahadeva with his straight-jointed arrows and left him chariotless, out of compassion and his knowledge of the right did not slay him—

**8.5.71**  he who, having scattered a thousand illusions, struck down with Śakra's spear that lord of the Rākṣasas, the battle-frenzied Ghaṭotkaca—

**8.5.72**  all these days Dhanaṃjaya, afraid of him in battle, that hero, never came to single combat—how then was he slain in the fight?

**8.5.73**  If his chariot was not stuck fast, if his bow did not shatter, if his weapons did not fail him—how then was he slain by the foe?

**8.5.74**  Who indeed was able in battle to conquer Karṇa, that tiger among men—shaking his great bow, loosing his dreadful shafts and celestial weapons in the fray, swift as a tiger in its rush?

**8.5.75**  Surely his bow was cut through, or his chariot sank into the earth, or his weapons failed—since you tell me he is slain; for I can see no other cause of his destruction.

**8.5.76**  "I will not wash my feet until I have slain Arjuna"—such was the most terrible vow of that great-souled one.

**8.5.77**  He of whom Yudhiṣṭhira, the King of Righteousness, that bull among men, was ever afraid in the forest, and for thirteen years could find no sleep—

**8.5.78**  he upon whose valour—the valour of that mighty, great-souled one—my son relied when he dragged by force into the assembly the wife of the Pāṇḍavas—

**8.5.79**  and there too, in the midst of the assembly, before the very eyes of the Pāṇḍavas, called Pāñcālī "a slave's wife" in the gathering of the Kurus—

**8.5.80**  he who, reckoning nothing of the fierce touch of shafts loosed from the Gāṇḍīva, said "Kṛṣṇā, you have now no husband," and gazed upon the sons of Pṛthā—

**8.5.81**  he who, relying on the strength of his own arms, had no fear of the sons of Pṛthā, even for a moment, O Saṃjaya—not though their sons and Janārdana stood with them—

**8.5.82**  his slaying I cannot believe possible even by the gods with Vāsava himself rushing against him—how much less, my child, by the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.5.83**  For when the son of Adhiratha touched his bowstring and took up his leather guard, no man living was fit to stand before him.

**8.5.84**  Sooner might the earth be bereft of the radiant beams of moon and sun than that lord of men, who never fled the field, should be slain in battle.

**8.5.85**  If that dull-witted fool, with his brother Duḥśāsana for ally, took pleasure in rejecting the counsel of Vāsudeva—

**8.5.86**  surely now, beholding bull-shouldered Karṇa struck down and Duḥśāsana slain, my poor son must grieve—so I think.

**8.5.87**  Hearing that Vaikartana was slain in single combat by the Ambidextrous one, and seeing the Pāṇḍavas victorious, what then did Duryodhana say?

**8.5.88**  Hearing that Durmarṣaṇa was slain, and Vṛṣasena too in the fight, and seeing his host broken and cut down by the great chariot-warriors—

**8.5.89**  and seeing his kings turned away, bent only on flight, his chariot-warriors scattered in rout—my poor son, I think, must grieve.

**8.5.90**  Unteachable through pride, childish of mind, unforgiving—seeing his host's spirit broken, what then did Duryodhana say?

**8.5.91**  Seeing his brother slain in the fight by Bhīmasena, who drank his blood—what then did Duryodhana say?

**8.5.92**  "Karṇa shall slay Arjuna in battle"—so he declared in the assembly together with the king of Gāndhāra; now that Karṇa is slain, what did he say?

**8.5.93**  Śakuni, the son of Subala, who once played the dice in glee and cheated the Pāṇḍavas—what, my child, did he say when Karṇa was slain?

**8.5.94**  Kṛtavarman, that great archer, the mighty chariot-warrior of the Sātvatas, the son of Hṛdika—seeing Karṇa utterly slain, what did he say?

**8.5.95**  He at whose instruction Brāhmaṇas, Kṣatriyas, and Vaiśyas wait, longing to master the science of the bow—the wise son of Droṇa—

**8.5.96**  that youthful one, endowed with beauty, fair to look upon, of great renown—Aśvatthāman, when Karṇa was slain, what did he say, O Saṃjaya?

**8.5.97**  Kṛpa, the son of Śaradvat, who had attained mastery in the science of the bow and knew the highest truths—what, my child, did he say when Karṇa was slain?

**8.5.98**  Śalya, the king of the Madras, that great archer, the ornament of assemblies—surely all that was ordained by him, the manner in which Karṇa was brought down.

**8.5.99**  And whatever kings of the earth had come to do battle—seeing Vaikartana slain, what did they say, O Saṃjaya?

**8.5.100**  When the hero Karṇa was slain—that tiger among chariot-warriors, that bull among men—what, O Saṃjaya, became of the front ranks of your several armies?

**8.5.101**  How came Śalya, the king of the Madras, the best of chariot-warriors, to be appointed to the charioteering of Vaikartana? Tell me that, O Saṃjaya.

**8.5.102**  Who guarded the right wheel of the Sūta's son in the fight, who the left wheel, and who guarded the hero from behind?

**8.5.103**  Which heroes stood by Karṇa, and which base ones fled in fear? And how, while you were all assembled together, was the great chariot-warrior Karṇa slain?

**8.5.104**  And how did the heroic Pāṇḍavas come on against that great chariot-warrior as he poured forth showers of arrows, like a cloud loosing streams of rain?

**8.5.105**  And how did that celestial serpent-mouthed shaft, the choicest of great arrows, then come to naught? Tell me that, O Saṃjaya.

**8.5.106**  Of this my host, its eminence now plucked away, O Saṃjaya, I can see no remnant left, its very crest crushed.

**8.5.107**  Those two heroes, those great archers, those best of the Kurus—Bhīṣma and Droṇa—slain in my cause: hearing it, what worth has life for me?

**8.5.108**  And I cannot bear that Rādheya is slain, the ornament of battle, the strength of whose two arms was equal to a hundred hundred elephants.

**8.5.109**  And what befell the Kauravas, the heroes among men, in their battle with the foe after Droṇa was slain—tell me that, O Saṃjaya.

**8.5.110**  And how Karṇa joined battle with the sons of Kuntī, and how that slayer of foes was stilled in the fight—let that be told.

**8.6.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the great archer Droṇa had been slain on that day, O descendant of Bharata, and when the great chariot-warrior, Droṇa's son, had been made vain in his resolve—

**8.6.2**  as the host of the Kauravas streamed away in flight, O great king, the son of Pṛthā drew up his own army in array and stood firm together with his brothers.

**8.6.3**  Knowing him to stand his ground, O bull of the Bhāratas, and seeing his own force in flight, your son by his manly might held it back.

**8.6.4**  Having steadied his own ranks and taken his stand upon the strength of his arms, he fought for a long while with the Pāṇḍavas, O descendant of Bharata—

**8.6.5**  with foes who had found their mark, exultant, long striving against him; and then, as the hour of twilight came on, he ordered the withdrawal.

**8.6.6**  Having drawn back their forces and entered their own camp, the Kurus then took counsel together for their own welfare.

**8.6.7**  Seated upon priceless couches spread with coverings beyond compare, upon excellent seats, they were like the immortals upon their pleasant beds.

**8.6.8**  Then King Duryodhana, addressing those great archers with words most winning and gentle, spoke what the moment called for:

**8.6.9**  "You best of the wise, declare your counsel, all of you, without delay—what task, things being thus, would best serve our purpose, O kings."

**8.6.10**  When the lord of men had thus spoken, those lions among men, eager for the fight, then made gestures of many kinds, seated upon their lion-thrones.

**8.6.11**  Marking the bearing of those who longed to offer up their lives in battle, and gazing upon the face of the king, whose splendour was like the rising sun, the Preceptor's son, wise and skilled in speech, took up the word:

**8.6.12**  "Zeal, application, skill, and good policy—these means of achieving an end have been named by the learned; yet all of them rest upon fate."

**8.6.13**  "Those foremost heroes of the world among us, the godlike great chariot-warriors, prudent, disciplined, skilled, and devoted—they have been slain."

**8.6.14**  "Yet we must not despair of victory; by sound policy in all our undertakings even fate may be turned to favour."

**8.6.15**  "So let us make Karṇa, the foremost of men, endowed with every host of virtues, our commander, and crush the foe."

**8.6.16**  Then Duryodhana, delighted to hear that pleasing speech—charged with the grace of affection, true, salutary, and auspicious to himself—

**8.6.17**  having steadied his mind and leaning upon the strength of his arms, Duryodhana, O great king, said this to Rādheya:

**8.6.18**  "Karṇa, I know your valour and your supreme love for me; yet even so, mighty-armed one, I shall speak to you a word for your good."

**8.6.19**  "Having heard it, do then as you will, brave one, whatever pleases you; for you are ever the wisest of all, and my supreme refuge."

**8.6.20**  Bhīṣma and Droṇa, those peerless warriors, my two commanders, are slain. Let your good self be my commander, richer in might than both of them.

**8.6.21**  Those two mighty archers were aged, and both bore tenderness toward Dhanaṃjaya; yet, O Rādheya, those heroes were honored by me at your word.

**8.6.22**  Mindful of his grandsire's bond, dear one, Bhīṣma spared the sons of Pāṇḍu in the great battle for ten whole days.

**8.6.23**  And when his weapons were laid down, the grandsire Bhīṣma was slain in the great battle by Phalguna, who put Śikhaṇḍin to the fore.

**8.6.24**  When that illustrious one fell and lay upon a bed of arrows, then, O tiger among men, at your word Droṇa became the foremost.

**8.6.25**  He too spared the sons of Pṛthā in this combat, for they had been his pupils; and that aged one was swiftly slain by Dhṛṣṭadyumna.

**8.6.26**  Now that those two chief warriors are slain, O man of measureless valor, however I ponder it, I see no other fighter in battle who is your equal.

**8.6.27**  You alone are able to win us the victory, of this there is no doubt; before, in the midst, and after, all of this has been known to you indeed.

**8.6.28**  You, then, like a yoke-bearing steed, are worthy to bear the burden in battle; consecrate yourself by your own hand to the office of commander.

**8.6.29**  As Skanda is the imperishable lord and commander of the gods, so let your good self sustain this army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son for me; strike down all the hosts of foes, as great Indra struck the Dānavas.

**8.6.30**  When they learn that you, the great car-warrior, stand firm in battle, the Pāṇḍavas with the Pāñcālas will flee as the Dānavas fled at the sight of Viṣṇu; therefore, O tiger among men, lead forth the mighty host.

**8.6.31**  When you stand fast, the Pāṇḍavas, together with their counselors and with the Pāñcālas and the Sṛñjayas, will be robbed of their senses.

**8.6.32**  For as the risen Sun, blazing with his own splendor, drives away the thick darkness, so do you drive away our foes.

**8.6.33**  Karṇa said: This was spoken by me before, O son of Gāndhārī, in your presence: I shall conquer the Pāṇḍavas, O king, with their sons and with Janārdana.

**8.6.34**  I shall be your commander, herein there is no doubt; be steadfast, O great king, and know the Pāṇḍavas already vanquished.

**8.6.35**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed, the mighty-spirited king Duryodhana then rose up together with the kings, like Indra of the hundred sacrifices amid the gods, to honor Karṇa with the command, as the immortals honored Skanda.

**8.6.36**  Then swiftly, with rite ordained by scripture, the kings led by Duryodhana, O king, desiring victory, consecrated him with golden jars and with earthen ones over which the sacred mantras had been pronounced,

**8.6.37**  and with water-filled horns of leopards and rhinoceroses and great bulls, and with others made of gems and pearls, and with fragrant herbs of holy scent.

**8.6.38**  He was seated upon a throne of fig-wood draped with linen, consecrated by the rite seen in the scriptures and with the requisites well assembled.

**8.6.39**  "Conquer the sons of Pṛthā, with Govinda and their followers, in the great battle!" Thus, O bull of the Bhāratas, the bards and the twice-born addressed him.

**8.6.40**  "Strike down the sons of Pṛthā with the Pāñcālas, O Rādheya, for our victory, as the ever-rising Sun strikes the darkness with his fierce rays!"

**8.6.41**  "For they, with Keśava, are no more a match for the arrows loosed by you than the ungrateful are for the gaze of the blazing rays of the Sun."

**8.6.42**  "For the sons of Pṛthā with the Pāñcālas can no more stand before you, weapon in hand in battle, than the Dānavas before great Indra."

**8.6.43**  Consecrated, Rādheya of boundless radiance overflowed in his splendor of form, like a second maker of the day.

**8.6.44**  Having consecrated Rādheya to the command, your son, driven on by fate, then deemed his purpose accomplished.

**8.6.45**  And Karṇa too, O king, that subduer of foes, having gained the command, ordered the army to make ready against the rising of the Sun.

**8.6.46**  Surrounded by your sons, Karṇa shone there, O Bhārata, as Skanda shone amid the gods in the war against Tāraka.

**8.7.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When Karṇa Vaikartana had gained the command and was thus addressed by the king himself with tender words, as to a brother,

**8.7.2**  having ordered the army to make ready when the Sun had risen, what did that man of great wisdom do? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**8.7.3**  Saṃjaya said: Learning Karṇa's purpose, O bull of the Bhāratas, your son ordered the array to be made ready, heralded by auspicious music.

**8.7.4**  In the great hours before dawn, O honored one, there arose all at once in your son's host a mighty din: "To arms! To arms!"

**8.7.5**  As the elephants were being harnessed and the guarded chariots made ready, as the foot-soldiers and the horses were being armed, O lord of peoples,

**8.7.6**  and as the warriors hurried, shouting to one another, there rose then a tumultuous uproar, vast and reaching the sky.

**8.7.7**  Then with a white banner, with horses of the hue of the morning sun, with a gold-backed bow, with the elephant-girth ensign,

**8.7.8**  with a quiver full of arrows, with an arm-band, guarded all round, bearing the hundred-slayer engines, bells, javelins, lances, and pikes,

**8.7.9**  furnished with a bow, with the radiance of the spotless sun, upon his tall-bannered chariot, the son of the charioteer appeared.

**8.7.10**  He was blowing his conch, dear one, adorned with a net of gold, and brandishing his great bow decked with gold.

**8.7.11**  Seeing Karṇa, the great archer, the foremost of car-warriors, standing on his chariot like the rising Sun dispelling the darkness a thousandfold—

**8.7.12**  none of the Kauravas there, O honored one, then took to heart the calamity of Bhīṣma, nor of Droṇa, nor of the others, O tiger among men.

**8.7.13**  Then, urging on the warriors with the blast of his conch, O honored one, Karṇa led forth the host of the Kauravas.

**8.7.14**  Having arrayed his troops in the crocodile-formation, the great archer Karṇa, scorcher of foes, then advanced against the Pāṇḍavas, longing for victory.

**8.7.15**  At the snout of the crocodile Karṇa took his stand, O king; at its two eyes were the brave Śakuni and Ulūka, the great car-warrior.

**8.7.16**  At the head was the son of Droṇa; at the neck were all your full brothers; in the center stood King Duryodhana, encircled by a mighty force.

**8.7.17**  At the left foot, O lord of kings, stood Kṛtavarman, joined with the Nārāyaṇa hosts, the cowherds, hard to vanquish in war.

**8.7.18**  At the right foot, O king, was Gautama of true valor, surrounded by the Trigartas, great archers, and by the men of the south.

**8.7.19**  At the left hindfoot stood Śalya, together with a great army raised from the land of the Madras.

**8.7.20**  At the right, O great king, was Suṣeṇa, true to his pledge, encircled by thousands of chariots and by hundreds of tusked elephants.

**8.7.21**  At the tail stood the two great heroes, the royal brothers, Citrasena and Citra, encircled by a mighty army.

**8.7.22**  Then, when Karṇa, the best of warriors, had set forth, O lord of kings, the King of Righteousness looked upon Dhanaṃjaya and said this:

**8.7.23**  "Behold, O Pārtha, the great army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son in the battle, arrayed by Karṇa, O hero, and guarded by heroic car-warriors."

**8.7.24**  "Robbed of its bravest, this great host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, with but its dregs remaining, O mighty-armed one, I deem no better than straw."

**8.7.25**  "One great archer alone stands firm here, the son of the charioteer, the best of car-warriors, who is unconquerable by the three worlds, moving and unmoving, with their gods, demons, gandharvas, kiṃnaras, and great serpents."

**8.7.26**  "Slaying him today, O mighty-armed Phalguna, victory shall be yours, and the thorn of twelve years' standing shall be plucked from me; knowing this, O mighty-armed one, array the troops as you wish."

**8.7.27**  Hearing his brother's words, the white-horsed son of Pāṇḍu countered that host with a half-moon formation.

**8.7.28**  On the left flank, O king, Bhīmasena took his stand, and on the right the great archer Dhṛṣṭadyumna of mighty strength.

**8.7.29**  In the very center of the array stood the son of Pāṇḍu with Kṛṣṇa for his charioteer; behind him were Nakula and Sahadeva and the King of Righteousness.

**8.7.30**  The two Pāñcālas, Yudhāmanyu and Uttamaujas, his wheel-guards, did not forsake Arjuna in battle, themselves protected by the Diademed One.

**8.7.31**  The remaining heroic kings, mailed, took their places in the array, each according to his rank, his ardor, and his strength, O Bhārata.

**8.7.32**  Having thus arrayed that great formation, O Bhārata, the Pāṇḍavas and your own great archers set their minds upon battle alone.

**8.7.33**  Seeing your host arrayed for battle by the son of the charioteer, your son, with his kinsmen, deemed the Pāṇḍavas already slain.

**8.7.34**  And likewise, seeing the Pāṇḍava army arrayed, Yudhiṣṭhira deemed the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, with Karṇa, already slain, O lord of men.

**8.7.35**  Then the conches and kettledrums, the tabors, drums, and cow-horns were all at once sounded together, resounding on every side.

**8.7.36**  In both armies, O king, the great-voiced instruments rang out, and the lion-roar of the heroes greedy for victory arose.

**8.7.37**  The neighing of horses, the trumpeting of elephants, and the fierce rumble of chariot-wheels arose, O lord of men.

**8.7.38**  No one took to heart the calamity of Droṇa, O bull of the Bhāratas, on seeing the great archer Karṇa, mailed, at the front of the array.

**8.7.39**  Both armies, of great might, with men and elephants exultant, eager to fight, stood ready, O king, to slay one another at once.

**8.7.40**  There, intent and inflamed with wrath, having beheld each other take their stand in the midst of the hosts, Karṇa and the son of Pāṇḍu shone forth, O lord of kings.

**8.7.41**  As the two armies seemed to dance, they came together against each other; and from their wings and from the wings beyond the wings the eager combatants sallied forth.

**8.7.42**  Then began the battle of men, elephants, and horses, and of car-warriors, O great king, smiting one another fiercely.

**8.8.1**  Saṃjaya said: Those two armies, vast, with horses, men, and elephants exultant, closing upon each other, fell to blows, like the hosts of gods and demons.

**8.8.2**  Then elephants, chariots, horses, and foot-soldiers in the great battle dealt one another a supreme onslaught that made an end of body and of sin.

**8.8.3**  With the severed heads of lions among men—heads matching in loveliness, luster, and fragrance the full moon, the sun, and the lotus—the lions among men strewed the earth.

**8.8.4**  With crescent-headed shafts, with broad arrows, with razor-tipped darts, with swords and spears, and with battle-axes, they cut off the heads of those who fought.

**8.8.5**  By the long, outstretched arms of warriors with long, outstretched arms, the long arms of others were made to fall, their fists, weapons, and arm-bands severed.

**8.8.6**  The earth then gleamed with those quivering arms, their fingers and palms blood-red, like fierce five-hooded serpents struck down by Garuḍa.

**8.8.7**  From horses, chariots, and elephants the heroes, slain by their foes, fell as the dwellers in heaven fall from their celestial cars when their merit is spent.

**8.8.8**  Crushed by heavy maces, by iron bludgeons and by pestles, the heroes fell by the hundreds, struck down by braver heroes in the battle.

**8.8.9**  Chariots were destroyed by chariots, frenzied elephants by frenzied elephants, and horsemen by horsemen, in that utmost tumult.

**8.8.10**  Chariots slain by noble chariots and by elephants, and horsemen by foot-soldiers, and foot-soldiers by horsemen, lay outstretched, slain in the fight.

**8.8.11**  Chariots, horses, and foot-soldiers were slain by elephants, and elephants and foot-soldiers by chariots; chariots, foot-soldiers, and elephants by horses, and horses, chariots, and elephants by men.

**8.8.12**  A great slaughter of chariots, horses, elephants, and men was wrought by men, horses, elephants, and chariots—by hands and feet, by weapons, and by chariots alike.

**8.8.13**  As that host was being cut down and slain by the brave, the sons of Pṛthā, with Vṛkodara at their head, advanced upon us.

**8.8.14**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, the sons of Draupadī, the Prabhadrakas, Sātyaki and Cekitāna, together with the Draviḍa soldiers—

**8.8.15**  and the Pāṇḍyas, the Auḍras, and the Keralas, paid with great wealth—broad-chested, long-armed, tall, and fair to look upon—

**8.8.16**  crowned with garlands, their teeth reddened, valorous as rutting elephants, clad in robes of many dyes and powdered with fragrant scents,

**8.8.17**  with swords girded on and nooses in hand, able to ward off charging elephants, sworn to a common death, O king, standing in the ranks shoulder to shoulder—

**8.8.18**  bearing quivers and bows in hand, long-haired and loving battle—the Āndhra footmen of Sātyaki, dreadful in form and prowess.

**8.8.19**  Then yet other warriors—the Cedis, the Pāñcālas, the Kekayas, the Karūṣas, the Kosalas, the Kāśis, and the Māgadhas—rushed forth.

**8.8.20**  Their chariots and elephants and choicest footmen, exultant with cries of every kind, danced and laughed for joy.

**8.8.21**  Surrounded by the foremost of mighty elephant-drivers, Vṛkodara, mounted at the head of his elephant, drove into the midst of that great host of thine.

**8.8.22**  That foremost of elephants, most terrible and duly caparisoned, shone like the lofty mansion of the eastern mount when the risen sun stands upon it.

**8.8.23**  Its splendid iron armor, adorned with the choicest gems, gleamed with a radiance equal to the autumn sky aglow with stars.

**8.8.24**  With lance and javelin in hand, fair-crowned and well-adorned, ranging like the noonday sun, he scorched his foes with his splendor.

**8.8.25**  Beholding that elephant from afar, Kṣemadhūrti, himself mounted on an elephant, charged exultantly upon the still more exultant one, calling him to combat.

**8.8.26**  A battle arose between those two elephants of dreadful aspect, like that between two great mountains, tree-clad, met by chance.

**8.8.27**  Their elephants locked together, those two heroes, piercing each other again and again with lances bright as the rays of the sun, roared mightily.

**8.8.28**  Drawing their elephants apart, they wheeled in circling courses, and taking up their bows, they smote each other.

**8.8.29**  With shouts and the slapping of arms and the twang of arrows on every side, gladdening the warriors, the two raised lion-roars.

**8.8.30**  Both skilled and mighty, they fought upon their two elephants—their trunks upraised, their banners tossed by the wind.

**8.8.31**  Having cut each other's bows, the two roared, raining spears and lances upon one another as the rain-clouds pour down water.

**8.8.32**  Then Kṣemadhūrti, roaring, pierced Bhīma forcefully between the breasts with a lance, and with six others besides.

**8.8.33**  Bhīmasena, his body blazing with wrath, the lances clinging to his limbs, shone like the sun, the seven-horsed, amid the clouds.

**8.8.34**  Then Bhīma, with effort, hurled at his foe a lance of straight flight, wrought wholly of iron and bright as the sun.

**8.8.35**  Then the lord of the Kulūtas, drawing his bow, cut down that lance with ten arrows and pierced the Pāṇḍava with a spear.

**8.8.36**  Then, taking up a bow that thundered like a great storm-cloud, the furious Pāṇḍava tormented the foe's elephant with his arrows.

**8.8.37**  That elephant, afflicted by the flood of arrows from Bhīmasena in the fight and held in check, could not stand its ground, like a cloud scattered by the wind.

**8.8.38**  The royal elephant rushed upon the elephant of Bhīmasena, as one wind-driven cloud rushes upon another stirred by a mighty gale.

**8.8.39**  Wheeling back his own elephant with effort, Kṣemadhūrti pierced with arrows the charging Bhīmasena together with his elephant.

**8.8.40**  Then that bull among men, with a well-aimed razor-headed shaft, cut his foe's bow and tormented the enemy's elephant.

**8.8.41**  Then Kṣemadhūrti struck Bhīma hard with a barbed shaft and smote his elephant with iron arrows in all its vital parts.

**8.8.42**  Before his elephant could fall, Bhīmasena leaped down and, standing upon the ground, crushed the foe's elephant with his mace.

**8.8.43**  As Kṣemadhūrti leaped down from that shattered elephant and came on with uplifted sword, Vṛkodara struck him with his mace.

**8.8.44**  Slain, his sword still in hand and his life gone, he fell beside his own elephant, like a lion struck by the thunderbolt beside a mountain riven by the same.

**8.8.45**  Beholding slain the glorious king of the Kulūtas, thy army, O bull of the Bhāratas, fled away in distress.

**8.9.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then Karṇa, the great archer, that hero, smote the host of the Pāṇḍavas in battle with his straight-jointed shafts.

**8.9.2**  Even so, O king, the Pāṇḍavas, those great car-warriors, enraged, slaughtered the army of thy son before the very face of Karṇa.

**8.9.3**  Karṇa, O king, the mighty-armed, slew the Pāṇḍava host with iron shafts bright as the sun's rays, polished smooth by the smith.

**8.9.4**  There, O Bhārata, the elephants struck by Karṇa's iron shafts trumpeted and sank down and grew faint and reeled through the ten directions.

**8.9.5**  As that host was being slain by the son of the charioteer, O venerable one, Nakula swiftly charged upon the Sūtaputra in the great battle.

**8.9.6**  Bhīmasena withstood the son of Droṇa, who was performing deeds hard to achieve, and Sātyaki withstood the Kaikeya brothers Vinda and Anuvinda.

**8.9.7**  King Citrasena met the advancing Śrutakarman, and Citra, with his bow of brilliant device and standard, met Prativindhya.

**8.9.8**  Duryodhana met King Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Dharma, while Dhanaṃjaya, enraged, rushed upon the bands of the saṃśaptakas.

**8.9.9**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna met Kṛpa, and in that destruction of the foremost of heroes Śikhaṇḍin closed with the steadfast Kṛtavarman.

**8.9.10**  Śrutakīrti met Śalya, and the valiant Sahadeva, son of Mādrī, met thy son Duḥśāsana, O great king.

**8.9.11**  The two Kekaya brothers covered Sātyaki in battle with a gleaming shower of arrows, and Sātyaki in turn covered the two Kekayas, O Bhārata.

**8.9.12**  Those two brothers struck that hero hard in the breast, as two elephants strike a rival elephant with their tusks in a great battle.

**8.9.13**  Their armor riven by arrows, those two brothers in the fight pierced Sātyaki, true in deed, with their shafts, O king.

**8.9.14**  But Sātyaki, O great king, laughing, covered those two on every side with a shower of arrows and held them in check, O Bhārata.

**8.9.15**  Held back then by the showers of arrows from the grandson of Śini, the two swiftly covered the chariot of the Śaineya with their shafts.

**8.9.16**  But the grandson of Śūra, having cut their two bright bows in that great battle, then covered them both with sharp and unbearable arrows.

**8.9.17**  Then, taking up two other polished bows and great arrows, the two ranged lightly and skillfully, raining their shafts upon Sātyaki.

**8.9.18**  The great arrows loosed by the two, feathered with heron and peacock plumes and adorned with gold, flew on, lighting up all the quarters.

**8.9.19**  A darkness of arrows arose between them in that great battle, O king, and those great car-warriors cut one another's bows.

**8.9.20**  Then the Sātvata, O great king, enraged and frenzied for battle, took up another bow and strung it, and in the fight, with a keen razor-headed shaft, struck off the head of Anuvinda.

**8.9.21**  That great head, weighed down by its ear-rings, fell to the earth like the head of Śambara slain in a great battle, and, withering all the Kekayas, it swiftly passed to the ground.

**8.9.22**  Beholding that hero slain, his brother, the great car-warrior, strung another bow and withstood the grandson of Śini.

**8.9.23**  Piercing Sātyaki with a spear and with gold-feathered, stone-whetted shafts, he roared a mighty roar and cried, "Stand, stand!"

**8.9.24**  That great car-warrior of the Kekayas, enraged again, tormented Sātyaki in the arms and breast with arrows shaped like tongues of fire.

**8.9.25**  His every limb wounded by the arrows, the Sātvata, skilled in courage, shone in the battle, O king, like a kiṃśuka tree in full leaf.

**8.9.26**  Sātyaki, pierced in battle by the high-souled Kekaya, pierced the Kekaya in turn with twenty-five shafts, as though laughing.

**8.9.27**  Taking up their shields decked with a hundred moons, those two strong-armed warriors, bearing excellent swords, shone in the great arena like the mighty Jambha and Śakra in the war of gods and demons.

**8.9.28**  Then those two, wheeling in circling courses in the great battle, swiftly smote each other with their swords in the fray.

**8.9.29**  Then the Sātvata cleft the Kekaya's shield in two, and the prince likewise cleft the shield of Sātyaki.

**8.9.30**  Having cleft the shield set about with hundreds of stars, the Kaikeya ranged in circling courses, advancing and retreating.

**8.9.31**  As he ranged in the great arena bearing his excellent sword, the grandson of Śini, in haste, cut him down with a back-handed stroke.

**8.9.32**  The Kekaya, with his armor, O king, cleft in two in that great battle—that mighty archer fell like a mountain smitten by the thunderbolt.

**8.9.33**  Having slain him in battle, the heroic Śaineya, that best of car-warriors, that scorcher of foes, swiftly mounted the chariot of Yudhāmanyu.

**8.9.34**  Then, mounting another chariot duly equipped, Sātyaki once more scattered the great host of the Kekayas with his arrows.

**8.9.35**  That great host of the Kekayas, being slain in the battle, abandoned that hostile chariot and fled through the ten directions.

**8.10.1**  Saṃjaya said: Śrutakarman, O great king, enraged, struck King Citrasena in battle with fifty arrows.

**8.10.2**  But King Abhisāra, striking Śrutakarman with nine sharp arrows, pierced his charioteer with five.

**8.10.3**  Then Śrutakarman, enraged, tormented Citrasena at the head of the army, striking him in a vital spot with a keen iron shaft.

**8.10.4**  Meanwhile the illustrious Śrutakīrti covered that guardian of the earth with ninety feathered shafts.

**8.10.5**  Then, regaining his senses, Citrasena, the great car-warrior, cut his bow with a broad-headed shaft and pierced him with seven arrows.

**8.10.6**  Taking up another bow, swift and gold-adorned, he made Citrasena yet more many-hued with his waves of arrows.

**8.10.7**  Decked with arrows, O king, that youth, wearing a bright garland, shone like a youth well-adorned in the midst of an assembly.

**8.10.8**  Then, enraged, he pierced Śrutakarman between the breasts with an iron shaft in the battle and cried, "Stand, stand!"

**8.10.9**  Śrutakarman too, tormented in the battle by the iron shaft, poured forth abundant blood like a mountain streaming with red ochre.

**8.10.10**  Then, his limbs smeared with blood and his hue made ruddy by it, he shone in the battle, O king, like a kiṃśuka tree in full flower.

**8.10.11**  Then Śrutakarman, O king, charged upon by his foes, warded off the enemy and cut his bow in two.

**8.10.12**  Then, O best of the Bhāratas, when his bow had been cut away, the illustrious Śrutakarman pierced him with three hundred iron-shafted arrows.

**8.10.13**  Then with another broad-headed shaft, exceedingly sharp, the swift warrior swept off the head of that great-souled one, helmet and all.

**8.10.14**  That mighty head of Citravarman fell upon the earth, as the moon, fallen by some chance from heaven, might drop to the ground.

**8.10.15**  Seeing their king slain and Abhisāra fallen too, O honored one, the soldiers of Citrasena rushed forward with fury.

**8.10.16**  Then that wrathful mighty bowman scattered the host with his arrows; as the lord of the dead in his fury drives all creatures before him at the hour of doom, so Śrutakarman, swiftly routing them with his shafts, shone resplendent.

**8.10.17**  Then Prativindhya, having pierced Citra with five swift arrows, struck his charioteer with three, and his banner thereafter with a single shaft.

**8.10.18**  Citra in turn tormented him in the arms and breast with nine broad-headed shafts, gold-feathered, whetted on stone, and winged with the plumes of heron and peacock.

**8.10.19**  Prativindhya, O Bhārata, cut his bow asunder with arrows, and then smote him fiercely with five sharpened shafts.

**8.10.20**  Then, O great king, he hurled at your son a lance with a golden shaft, hard to withstand, terrible as a tongue of flame.

**8.10.21**  As that lance came rushing down like a meteor from the sky, Prativindhya, as if smiling, cut it in twain upon the field.

**8.10.22**  Cleft by Prativindhya's keen arrows, it fell to earth, like a thunderbolt at the end of an age that strikes terror into all beings.

**8.10.23**  Seeing his lance struck down, Citra seized a great mace, adorned with a network of gold, and flung it at Prativindhya.

**8.10.24**  In that great battle it slew his horses and his charioteer, and crushing the chariot with its force, sank into the earth.

**8.10.25**  At that very moment, O Bhārata, leaping down from his chariot, he hurled at Citra a lance bedecked with bells of gold.

**8.10.26**  As it flew toward him, the high-souled Citra caught it, O king, and hurled that same lance back at Prativindhya, O Bhārata.

**8.10.27**  Reaching the hero Prativindhya in battle, that lance of great brilliance pierced his right arm and fell to the ground; and where it fell it lit up that place like a thunderbolt.

**8.10.28**  Then, O king, Prativindhya in his wrath sent forth a gold-decked iron dart, desiring the death of Citra.

**8.10.29**  Piercing the bright armor that guarded him, and his very heart as well, it passed swiftly into the earth, like a great serpent gliding into its hole.

**8.10.30**  Smitten by that dart, he fell then, O king, stretching out his two broad arms, thick and round as iron bludgeons.

**8.10.31**  Seeing Citra slain, your warriors, the ornaments of battle, rushed with fury upon Prativindhya from every side.

**8.10.32**  Loosing arrows of many kinds and bell-hung śataghnīs, they covered him over as banks of cloud cover the sun.

**8.10.33**  But the mighty-armed one swept them aside with his web of arrows in the fray, and routed your host as the wielder of the thunderbolt routs the army of the Asuras.

**8.10.34**  Slaughtered in the battle by the Pāṇḍavas, O king, your men were scattered all at once like clouds driven before the wind.

**8.10.35**  When that army had fled and was being slain on every side, the son of Droṇa, alone, swiftly bore down upon the mighty Bhīmasena.

**8.10.36**  Then there arose of a sudden a fearful encounter between the two, such as was the clash of Vṛtra and Vāsava in the war of the gods and the Asuras.

**8.11.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, O king, the son of Droṇa pierced Bhīmasena with a winged shaft, moving with utmost speed and displaying the lightness of his hand with weapons.

**8.11.2**  Then again he struck him with ninety sharpened arrows, marking all his vital points—knowing the vitals well, and nimble of hand.

**8.11.3**  Bhīmasena, thickly studded by the son of Droṇa with keen arrows, shone in the battle, O king, like the radiant sun crowned with its beams.

**8.11.4**  Then with a thousand well-aimed arrows the son of Pāṇḍu veiled the son of Droṇa, and let loose a lion's roar.

**8.11.5**  Then the son of Droṇa, warding off arrows with arrows in the fight, struck the son of Pāṇḍu upon the brow with an iron shaft, O king, as if in mockery.

**8.11.6**  The son of Pāṇḍu bore that arrow lodged in his forehead, O king, as a proud rhinoceros of the forest bears its horn.

**8.11.7**  Then the valiant Bhīma, as the son of Droṇa pressed on in battle, pierced him in the brow with three iron shafts, as if to amaze him.

**8.11.8**  With those arrows fixed upon his forehead the Brāhmaṇa shone forth, like some lofty three-peaked mountain drenched in the rains.

**8.11.9**  Then the son of Pāṇḍu tormented the son of Droṇa with hundreds of arrows, yet shook him not, as the wind cannot shake a mountain.

**8.11.10**  In like manner the son of Droṇa, exultant, could not shake the son of Pāṇḍu in the fight with hundreds of keen arrows, as a flood cannot shake a mountain.

**8.11.11**  Those two great car-warriors, veiling each other with terrible arrows, both heroes skilled in the maneuvers of the chariot, shone resplendent, fierce in the joy of battle.

**8.11.12**  Both blazing like two suns that work the world's destruction, they scorched each other with their finest shafts as suns scorch with their own rays.

**8.11.13**  In that great battle they strove, each to answer the other's stroke with a stroke; fearlessly the two contended, blow for matching blow.

**8.11.14**  Like two tigers the two mighty car-warriors ranged the field, their arrows for fangs, unassailable, their bows agape like jaws, terrible to behold.

**8.11.15**  The two became invisible, hidden on every side by their webs of arrows, like the moon and the sun veiled by masses of cloud in the sky.

**8.11.16**  And in a moment those subduers of foes shone forth again upon that field, like the moon and the sun in the heavens freed from the masses of cloud.

**8.11.17**  Then the son of Droṇa wheeled to the left around Vṛkodara there, drenching him with hundreds of fierce arrows as rain-streams drench a mountain.

**8.11.18**  But Bhīma could not brook that mark of his foe's mastery, and the son of Pāṇḍu, O king, wheeled in answer about him likewise to the left.

**8.11.19**  Through the divisions of their circling courses, in their advances and returns, a tumultuous combat arose between the two upon that great field.
