**8.11.20**  Coursing through their various movements—the circle and the steady stand—they smote each other with arrows loosed from bows drawn to the full.

**8.11.21**  The two great car-warriors each strove to slay the other, and each sought in the encounter to strip the other of his chariot.

**8.11.22**  Then the son of Droṇa, that mighty car-warrior, brought forth his great celestial weapons; and in the battle the son of Pāṇḍu beat them back with weapons of his own.

**8.11.23**  Then, O great king, a fearful battle of celestial weapons raged on, terrible as the war of the planets at the destruction of all created beings.

**8.11.24**  Those arrows, hurled by the two, clashed together, O Bhārata, lighting up all the quarters and that army round about.

**8.11.25**  The sky grew terrible, overspread by the throngs of arrows, as though strewn with falling meteors at the destruction of created beings, O king.

**8.11.26**  From the clash of arrows a fire was born there, O Bhārata, scattering sparks and blazing with flame, and it scorched both the contending hosts.

**8.11.27**  Then the Siddhas, O great king, hovering above, spoke these words: "This battle surpasses all battles whatsoever; greater is it than all the rest.

**8.11.28**  All other battles are not worth a sixteenth part of this; no such combat has ever been, nor shall ever be again.

**8.11.29**  Ah, how endowed with knowledge are these two, and how fierce in valor! Ah, the dreadful strength of Bhīma, and the weapon-skill of both!

**8.11.30**  Ah, the very pith of prowess, ah, the perfect mastery of these two! There they stand in the battle, like Yama the Ender at the close of time.

**8.11.31**  Like two Rudras come to being, like two suns, or like the twin Aśvins—so are these tigers among men, terrible of form upon the field."

**8.11.32**  Such words of the Siddhas were heard then again and again, and a lion's roar arose from the assembled dwellers of heaven, beholding the wondrous and unimaginable deeds of the two in that fray.

**8.11.33**  Those two heroes in the battle, O king, each having wronged the other, glared upon each other, rolling their eyes in wrath.

**8.11.34**  Their eyes red with anger, their lips quivering in anger, in their anger grinding their teeth and biting their lips,

**8.11.35**  the two great car-warriors veiled each other with a downpour of arrows, raining floods of shafts upon the field, gleaming with the lightning of their weapons.

**8.11.36**  Those two great car-warriors, having pierced each other's banner and charioteer, and having pierced each other's horses, wounded one another.

**8.11.37**  Then in their wrath, O great king, in that great battle, both seized arrows and swiftly hurled them, each seeking the other's death.

**8.11.38**  Those two arrows, O great king, gleaming at the head of the host, swift as the thunderbolt and hard to bear, met and struck home.

**8.11.39**  By the force of their rushing and grievously smitten by those two shafts, the two great heroes then sank down, each upon the floor of his own chariot.

**8.11.40**  Then the charioteer, knowing the son of Droṇa to be senseless, bore him away from the battle, O king, while all the warriors looked on.

**8.11.41**  In like manner, O king, the charioteer bore away from the fight upon his car that scorcher of foes, the son of Pāṇḍu, who reeled again and again.

**8.12.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Tell me how the battle of Arjuna went against the Saṃśaptakas, and how it fared for the others of my side against the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.12.2**  Saṃjaya said: Hear, O king, as I relate the battle as it befell—the combat of our heroes against their foes, a combat that purges away the body's sins.

**8.12.3**  The son of Pṛthā, that slayer of foes, plunging into the host of the Saṃśaptakas which was like an ocean, churned it up as a great wind churns the sea.

**8.12.4**  Striking off with keen broad-headed shafts the heads of the heroes—faces bright as the full moon, with fair eyes, brows, and teeth—Dhanaṃjaya swiftly strewed the earth with them, as with lotuses bereft of their stalks.

**8.12.5**  The arms of his foes—shapely, long, and full, anointed with sandal and aloe, bearing weapons, clad in armor, like five-hooded serpents—Arjuna severed with his razor-edged shafts in the battle.

**8.12.6**  Yoke-horses and the foremost team-horses, charioteers, banners, bows, arrows, hands and forearms—again and again the son of Pāṇḍu cut them away with his broad-headed shafts.

**8.12.7**  Elephants, horses, and chariots with their riders, Arjuna in the battle, O king, sent with arrows by the many thousands to the abode of Yama.

**8.12.8**  Advancing upon that great hero, bellowing like bulls enraged for the sake of a cow, they rushed at him with roars; and as he smote them, they struck back at him with their arrows, as bulls gore with their horns.

**8.12.9**  That battle of his with them made the hair stand on end, like the war of the Daityas with the wielder of the thunderbolt for the conquest of the three worlds.

**8.12.10**  Warding off the weapons of his foes on every side with weapons of his own, Arjuna swiftly pierced their very lives with a multitude of shafts, and roared aloud.

**8.12.11**  With their triple bamboo poles, wheels, and axles severed, their warriors, horses, and charioteers slain, their weapons and quivers shattered, their banners struck down,

**8.12.12**  their traces and reins cut through, robbed of their poles and shafts, their seats and yokes broken apart, the array of their weapons hewn down—those chariots he shattered into fragments, as the wind tears great clouds asunder.

**8.12.13**  Filling all with wonder, a sight worth beholding and a terror to his foes, Arjuna wrought a deed equal to that of a thousand great car-warriors.

**8.12.14**  The hosts of Siddhas, divine sages, and Cāraṇas sang his praises; the kettledrums of the gods resounded, showers of flowers fell upon the heads of Keśava and Arjuna, and a bodiless voice spoke.

**8.12.15**  These two heroes, Keśava and Arjuna, who forever bear the loveliness of the moon, the blaze of the sun, the force of the wind, and the splendour of fire.

**8.12.16**  Unconquerable as Brahmā and Īśāna, the two heroes stood upon a single chariot — the foremost of all beings, the two heroes who are Nara and Nārāyaṇa.

**8.12.17**  Beholding and hearing of this great wonder, O Bhārata, Aśvatthāman, fully girded, rushed upon the two Kṛṣṇas in battle.

**8.12.18**  Then, as the son of Pāṇḍu was loosing arrows like Yama, Time, and the Ender, the son of Droṇa hailed him with arrow-laden hand, and laughing spoke.

**8.12.19**  "If, O hero, you reckon me come to you as a guest deserving of welcome, then with all your soul grant me this day the hospitality of battle."

**8.12.20**  Thus challenged by the teacher's son with desire for combat, Arjuna held himself in high honour, and said this to Janārdana:

**8.12.21**  "The Saṃśaptakas too are mine to slay, and the son of Droṇa now summons me. O mighty-armed one, instruct me which of these is to be met first."

**8.12.22**  So addressed, Kṛṣṇa bore Pārtha into the presence of Droṇa's son — as the Wind bears Indra summoned by victorious rite into the sacrifice.

**8.12.23**  Addressing him with single mind, Keśava said to Droṇa's son: "Aśvatthāman, stand firm, strike swiftly, and endure!"

**8.12.24**  "This is the hour for dependants to repay the bread of their lord. Subtle is the dispute of brāhmaṇas; gross are the victory and defeat of the kṣatriya."

**8.12.25**  "Since out of folly you cannot brook the divine welcome Pārtha would do you, then, desiring to win it, stand firm this day and fight the son of Pāṇḍu."

**8.12.26**  So addressed by Vāsudeva, the best of the twice-born said "So be it," and pierced Keśava with sixty iron shafts and Arjuna with three.

**8.12.27**  At this Arjuna, sorely enraged, severed his bow with three broad-headed arrows; whereupon Droṇa's son took up another, still more terrible, bow.

**8.12.28**  Stringing it in the blink of an eye, he pierced Arjuna and Keśava — Vāsudeva with three arrows and the son of Pāṇḍu with a thousand.

**8.12.29**  Then, straining himself, Droṇa's son loosed arrows by the thousand, by the million, by the hundred million, and in the battle held Arjuna in check.

**8.12.30**  From his quiver, his bow, his bowstring, his fingers, O honoured one, from his arms, his two hands, his breast, his face, his nostrils, his eyes,

**8.12.31**  from his two ears, his head, his limbs, even from the very pores of his skin, and from his chariot and banner, the arrows of that reciter of the Veda streamed forth.

**8.12.32**  Having pierced Keśava and the son of Pāṇḍu with a vast net of arrows, Droṇa's son roared in delight with a voice like a mass of great thunderclouds.

**8.12.33**  Hearing him roar, the son of Pāṇḍu said to Acyuta: "Behold, Mādhava, the malice of Droṇa's son toward me."

**8.12.34**  "Having shut us within a house of arrows, he thinks us slain; this resolve of his I shall undo by skill and by strength."

**8.12.35**  Cutting each of the arrows loosed by Aśvatthāman into three and three, the best of the Bhāratas scattered them as the wind scatters mist.

**8.12.36**  Then the son of Pāṇḍu once more pierced the Saṃśaptakas — with their horses, drivers, chariots, and elephants, their banners and bands of foot — with fierce arrows.

**8.12.37**  Whoever there beheld any form, in whatever wise, each thought that form, and his own self, all covered over with arrows.

**8.12.38**  Sped from the Gāṇḍīva, those winged shafts of many shapes struck down elephants and men standing a full krośa away in the battle.

**8.12.39**  Severed by broad-headed arrows, the trunks of the rut-streaming elephants fell, like full-grown autumn trees felled by axes.

**8.12.40**  And thereafter those elephants fell like mountains, together with their riders, even as ranges of hills shattered by the thunderbolt of the Thunderer.

**8.12.41**  The chariots, fashioned by rule and like to the cities of the Gandharvas, drawn by horses trained to swiftness and well-guided, mounted by warriors mad for battle —

**8.12.42**  these Dhanaṃjaya rained his foes with arrows, hewing them to splinters, and slew the bedecked horsemen and the foot-soldiers.

**8.12.43**  Dhanaṃjaya, the sun of the world's ending, dried up the great, unfordable ocean of the Saṃśaptakas with the fierce rays of his arrows.

**8.12.44**  Then again, with swift, sun-bright iron shafts of mighty force, he hastened to cleave the great mountain that was Droṇa's son, as the Thunderer cleaves a hill.

**8.12.45**  The teacher's son, enraged and eager to fight, sought with swift arrows to strike him together with his horses and driver, but could not, for Pārtha cut the shafts down in mid-air.

**8.12.46**  Then Aśvatthāman, exceedingly enraged, loosed whole sheaves of arrows upon the worthy Arjuna — as one offers a house to a guest.

**8.12.47**  Then, leaving the Saṃśaptakas, the son of Pāṇḍu fell upon Droṇa's son — as a giver leaves an unworthy man to attend a worthy suppliant.

**8.12.48**  Then there arose a combat between those two, splendid as Śukra and Aṅgiras — like Venus and the planet of Aṅgiras around a star in the sky.

**8.12.49**  Scorching each other with the blazing rays of their arrows, they stood, striking terror into the worlds, like two planets straying from their courses.

**8.12.50**  Then Arjuna pierced him sorely between the brows with an iron shaft; and Droṇa's son shone thereby like the sun with rays flung upward.

**8.12.51**  Then the two Kṛṣṇas, sorely afflicted by Aśvatthāman with hundreds of arrows, stood with their masses of beams entangled, like two suns at the world's ending.

**8.12.52**  Then Arjuna, guarded by Vāsudeva, loosed the weapon that strikes on every side, and smote the son of Droṇa with shafts like the thunderbolt, like fire, like the rod of Yama.

**8.12.53**  He of surpassing fire and most fearsome deeds pierced Keśava and Arjuna in their vital parts with well-loosed arrows of utmost speed, by which even Death, if struck, would tremble.

**8.12.54**  Warding off the arrows of the straining Droṇa's son with twice as many fair-feathered shafts, Arjuna enveloped that lone hero with his horses, driver, and banner, and then turned upon the Saṃśaptaka host.

**8.12.55**  Their bows, their arrows, their quivers, their bowstrings, their hands, their arms, the weapons held in their hands, their parasols, their banners, their horses, their garments, their garlands, and their ornaments —

**8.12.56**  their shields, their armour, their cherished hopes, all that was dear to them, and their very heads — Pārtha clove asunder with the arrows he loosed at his foes, who stood without turning their faces away.

**8.12.57**  The well-equipped chariots, horses, and elephants, mounted by manful heroes who strove with effort, struck down by the hosts of arrows Pārtha sped, fell together with those best of men.

**8.12.58**  Faces fair as lotuses, as the sun, as the full moon, splendid with diadems, garlands, and crowns — smitten by broad-heads, crescent-arrows, and razor-shafts, the heads of men fell ceaselessly to the earth.

**8.12.59**  Then with elephants like the elephant of the lord of the gods, swelling with the pride and wrath of the foes of the gods, the heroes of the Kaliṅgas, Vaṅgas, Aṅgas, and Niṣādas, seeking to slay him, charged upon the son of Pāṇḍu.

**8.12.60**  The armour, the vital parts, the trunks, and the drivers of those elephants Pārtha rent apart; and their banners and pennons then fell down like the peaks of a mountain struck by the thunderbolt.

**8.12.61**  When these were broken, the Diademed one covered the teacher's son with arrows the hue of the new-risen sun — as the wind veils the toiling sun with great nets of cloud.

**8.12.62**  Then, beating off Arjuna's arrows with his own, Droṇa's son covered Arjuna and Vāsudeva with shafts as the moon and sun are veiled in the sky, and roared like a raincloud at the end of the hot season.

**8.12.63**  Sorely pressed by him and by your warriors, whose weapons were yet unbroken, Arjuna made at once a darkness of arrows and pierced them all with fair-feathered shafts.

**8.12.64**  Neither taking up, nor nocking, nor loosing his arrows could Savyasācin be seen in the battle; men saw only the slain elephants, horses, foot-soldiers, and chariots, their bodies sewn through with shafts.

**8.12.65**  Nocking ten choice iron shafts in haste, Droṇa's son loosed them as though they were but one; and of these, five well-sped shafts struck Arjuna and five pierced Acyuta.

**8.12.66**  Struck by these, those two foremost of all men, streaming blood, like Kubera and Indra, seemed overcome by one whose lore was complete; and others wondered, "Are these two slain?"

**8.12.67**  Then the lord of the Dāśārhas said to Arjuna: "Why are you heedless? Slay this warrior! Neglected, he will work mischief, and grow grievous as a malady left untreated."

**8.12.68**  Saying "So be it" to Acyuta, and now alert, he hewed at Droṇa's son with arrows in earnest; and cutting the reins, he struck the horses, which bore him far away from the battle.

**8.12.69**  Wheeling about, that wise one, best of the line of Aṅgiras, desired no further combat with Pārtha; for, reflecting, he knew that victory was assured to the Vṛṣṇi hero and to Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.12.70**  When Aśvatthāman of hostile frame had been borne off from the battle by his horses — as a disease is driven from the body by spells, herbs, treatment, and gifts —

**8.12.71**  Keśava and Arjuna advanced together to face the Saṃśaptakas, upon their chariot, its banner tossed by the wind, thundering like a flood.

**8.13.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then to the north, in the army of the Pāṇḍavas, an uproar arose from chariots, elephants, horses, and foot-soldiers being slaughtered by Daṇḍadhāra.

**8.13.2**  Turning the chariot back, Keśava, even as he urged on the horses swift as Garuḍa and the wind, said to Arjuna:

**8.13.3**  "The lord of Magadha too has pressed forward upon his crushing elephant, in no way inferior to Bhagadatta in skill and in strength."

**8.13.4**  "When you have slain him, you shall slay the Saṃśaptakas again." And at the end of his words he brought Pārtha into the presence of Daṇḍadhāra.

**8.13.5**  That chief of the Māgadhas, his goad in hand, irresistible among adversaries as a comet among the planets, that dreadful one churned the enemy's host as a flaming comet would scourge the whole earth.

**8.13.6**  Mounted upon his well-caparisoned elephant — like a Dānava-elephant, roaring like a great cloud, a crusher of foes — he slew chariots, horses, and herds of elephants by the thousand, and elephants too, with his arrows.

**8.13.7**  Bearing down upon the chariots with their horses and drivers, in haste he ground the chariots beneath the elephant's feet; and mounted on the elephant he slew other elephants with its legs, its feet, and its trunk, like the wheel of Time.

**8.13.8**  And men decked in armour of black iron, with their horses, together with the foot-soldiers, he cast down and crushed with his furious, noble tusker, as one tramples thick reeds with a crackling sound.

**8.13.9**  Then Arjuna, amid the twang of bowstring, the slap of the leather guard, and the rim's roar, amid the din of drums, kettledrums, and many conches, and the clamour of men, horses, and elephants by the thousand, swept down upon that noble elephant on his peerless chariot.

**8.13.10**  Then Daṇḍadhāra smote Arjuna with twelve choice arrows, Janārdana with sixteen, and the horses with three apiece; and thereupon he roared and laughed again and again.

**8.13.11**  Then Pārtha with his broad-headed arrows cut away his bowstring, his arrows, and his bow, and his bedecked banner too, and again the drivers along with the foot-guards; and at this the lord of Girivraja flew into a rage.

**8.13.12**  Then, seeking to throw Arjuna into utter confusion with his elephant, its temples burst with rut, dense as a thundercloud and swift as the wind, he smote Janārdana and Dhanaṃjaya with lances.

**8.13.13**  Then the son of Pāṇḍu, with three razor-shafts, severed at one stroke his two arms, like elephant trunks, and his head, with its face like the full moon; and thereafter he smote the elephant with hundreds of arrows.

**8.13.14**  That elephant, clad in golden mail, shone with Pārtha's arrows tipped with refined gold, and so blazed as at night a mountain blazes, its herbs and trees set alight by a forest fire.

**8.13.15**  Tormented with pain, trumpeting with a voice like a thundercloud, staggering, reeling, stumbling, stricken, in flight, broken, that elephant fell together with its driver, as a mountain falls crushed by the descent of the thunderbolt.

**8.13.16**  When his brother had been slain in battle, Daṇḍa came on upon an elephant white as snow, garlanded with gold, and like the peak of the snowy mountain, seeking to slay Dhanaṃjaya, the younger brother of Indra.

**8.13.17**  Striking Janārdana with three lances bright as the sun's rays and Arjuna with five, he roared as he tormented them; then the son of Pāṇḍu hewed off his two arms.

**8.13.18**  Cleanly cut off by razor-arrows, the two arms, still grasping their lances, their armlets fallen, smeared with sandal-paste, falling together from the elephant, gleamed like two great serpents tumbling from a mountain crag.

**8.13.19**  Then the head of Daṇḍa, struck off by the Diademed one with a crescent-arrow, fell from the elephant to the earth; and that blood-red head, as it fell, gleamed like the sun sinking from his setting-place toward the western quarter.

**8.13.20**  Then Pārtha pierced the elephant, like the peak of a white mountain, with choice arrows bright as the sun's rays; and it fell trumpeting, as a peak of the snowy mountain falls when struck by the thunderbolt.

**8.13.21**  Then other noble elephants like to that one, seeking in the fray to conquer Savyasācin, were made by him even as those two elephants had been; and thereupon the foe's vast host was shattered.

**8.13.22**  Elephants, chariots, horses, and men in throngs, slaying one another, fell in the battle, stumbling against one another and struck down; and calling out their several clan-names, they were sorely slain.

**8.13.23**  Then his own soldiers, gathering about Arjuna as the hosts of the gods about Purandara, said: "He of whom we went in dread, as creatures dread death — that foe, O hero, by good fortune you have slain!"

**8.13.24**  Had you not rescued these folk from their terror, hard-pressed as they were by such mighty foes, the joy of our enemies would have been as great as our own joy now is, when you have slain these foes of ours here.

**8.13.25**  Hearing such words and yet more spoken by his comrades, Arjuna, glad at heart, honored those people as was fitting, and then, the slayer of the host of the Saṃśaptakas, set forth once more.

**8.14.1**  Saṃjaya said: Returning once again, the victorious one slew many of the Saṃśaptakas, moving with crooked turns and counter-turns like the planet Aṅgāraka.

**8.14.2**  Struck by Pārtha's arrows, O king, men, horses, chariots, and elephants reeled, wheeled about, cried out, fell, and wilted, good sir.

**8.14.3**  The Pāṇḍava cut down draft-beasts, sturdier draft-beasts, drivers, and chariots, and likewise hands, the weapons in those hands, arms too, and heads,

**8.14.4**  with broad-headed shafts, razor-arrows, crescent-points, and calf-tooth barbs—the heads of the hostile warriors who fought against him in battle.

**8.14.5**  As bulls eager to fight over a cow rush upon a rival bull, so the heroes fell upon Arjuna by hundreds and by thousands.

**8.14.6**  The battle between them and him was such as to make the hair stand on end, like that of the Daityas with the wielder of the thunderbolt in the conquest of the three worlds.

**8.14.7**  A warrior of fierce weapons pierced him with three arrows, like venomous biting serpents; thereupon Arjuna struck that man's head from his body.

**8.14.8**  Enraged, they rained down on Arjuna from every side with weapons of every kind, as clouds driven by the Maruts pour upon the Himavat in the hot season.

**8.14.9**  Warding off the enemies' weapons on all sides with his own weapons, Arjuna, with well-aimed shafts, slew all those many foes together.

**8.14.10**  With their triple bamboo poles, shafts, and supports severed, their flank-guards and drivers slain, their reins, traces, and axles cut, their draw-bars and yokes broken, and all their gear shattered—such did Arjuna swiftly make the chariots with his arrows.

**8.14.11**  Those priceless chariots, shattered there in their numbers, gleamed like the mansions of the wealthy ruined by fire, wind, and flood.

**8.14.12**  Elephants, their vital parts pierced through by arrows like thunderbolt and lightning, fell as mountain-top dwellings fall before bolt, gale, and fire.

**8.14.13**  Many horses with their riders fell, struck down by Arjuna, their tongues and entrails hanging out, spent upon the earth, drenched in blood, ghastly to behold.

**8.14.14**  Men, horses, and elephants, sewn through by Savyasācin's iron shafts, wheeled about, stumbled, fell, cried out, and wilted, good sir.

**8.14.15**  With fine arrows whetted on stone, like thunderbolt, lightning, and venom, Pārtha struck them down as great Indra struck the Dānavas.

**8.14.16**  Heroes in costly armor and ornaments, with garments and weapons of every kind, with their chariots and banners, lie slain by Pārtha.

**8.14.17**  Vanquished, men of pious deeds, of distinguished birth and learning, they have gone with their bodies to the earth, and by their mighty deeds to heaven.

**8.14.18**  Then your heroes rushed upon Arjuna's chariot—lords of various realms, with their troops, their wrath kindled.

**8.14.19**  Borne by chariot-horses, and the foot-soldiers too, bent on killing, they ran at him, swiftly hurling weapons of every kind.

**8.14.20**  That great downpour of weapons, hurled by those great storm-cloud warriors, the wind that was Arjuna swiftly scattered with his sharp arrows.

**8.14.21**  That shoreless flood of mighty weapons—with its horses, foot, elephants, and chariots—Pārtha sought all at once to cross by the causeway of his missiles and shafts.

**8.14.22**  Then Vāsudeva said to Pārtha: "Why do you toy with them, O sinless one? Crush these Saṃśaptakas and then make haste to the slaying of Karṇa."

**8.14.23**  Saying "So be it," Arjuna at once assailed the Saṃśaptakas who yet remained, and with his weapon slew them by force, as Indra slew the Daityas.

**8.14.24**  Some saw Arjuna in the fray drawing his arrows or fitting them to the string; by others he was seen only as he loosed the shafts, so swift was he.

**8.14.25**  Crying "A marvel!" Govinda urged on the horses; white as a goose's wing, they entered the enemy host as geese enter a lake.

**8.14.26**  Then Govinda, gazing over that field of battle while the slaughter of men went on, spoke to Savyasācin:

**8.14.27**  "O Pārtha, here rages the dread destruction of the Bharatas, the great ruin of the kings of the earth, brought about for Duryodhana's sake."

**8.14.28**  "Behold, O Bhārata, the gold-backed bows of the great archers cast aside, and the bundles of arrows and the quivers as well."

**8.14.29**  "And the arrows with golden nocks and bent joints, and the oil-washed iron shafts, like serpents that have shed their skins."

**8.14.30**  "Swords with ivory hilts adorned with gold, lances scattered about, and bows bright and decked with gold."

**8.14.31**  "Coats of mail flung off, gold-backed, O Bhārata, and lances wrought with gold, and javelins adorned with gold."

**8.14.32**  "And great maces bound with bands of pure gold, and pikes of gold, and spiked clubs adorned with gold."

**8.14.33**  "Battle-axes with shafts bright with gold flung about, and iron-pointed goads fallen, and heavy pestles."

**8.14.34**  "Behold the gaily-wrought hundred-slayers, and the huge iron bludgeons, and the discuses cast away, and the many hammers on the field."

**8.14.35**  "Grasping weapons of every kind, those swift warriors, greedy for victory, though their life is fled, seem still as though alive."

**8.14.36**  "Behold the warriors by thousands—their limbs crushed by maces, their heads broken by pestles, trampled by elephants, horses, and chariots."

**8.14.37**  "By arrows, spears, swords, and lances, by sabres, pikes, javelins, claw-weapons, and clubs, men, elephants, and horses—"

**8.14.38**  "—with bodies hewn in many ways, awash in floods of blood, their breath gone, the battlefields are covered, O slayer of foes."

**8.14.39**  "With arms smeared with sandal paste, bearing armlets and fair ornaments, with guards on them and bracelets, the earth shines, O Bhārata."

**8.14.40**  "With finger-guarded hands and forearms flung about, all bedecked, and with the severed thighs of those swift warriors, like elephants' trunks."

**8.14.41**  "With the severed heads of those bull-eyed men, bound with choice crest-jewels and wearing earrings, the earth is resplendent."

**8.14.42**  "With headless trunks smeared with blood, their limbs and necks lopped off, the earth shines, O best of the Bhāratas, as if with fires whose flames have died."

**8.14.43**  "Behold the chariots of many sorts, broken, fair with their golden bells, and the horses too, in many places drenched with blood."

**8.14.44**  "And the great conch-shells of the warriors, and the white whisks scattered about, and the elephants with their tongues lolling out, lying like mountains."

**8.14.45**  "And the slain elephant-fighters bright with their banners, and the housings of the elephants, with their well-fitted cloths and woolen rugs."

**8.14.46**  "And the gay rugs of varied figures torn apart, and the bells broken in many ways, ground to bits by the falling elephants."

**8.14.47**  "And the goads with handles of beryl gems fallen to the ground, and the gold-wrought whips bound to the banner-tops of the riders."

**8.14.48**  "And the horse-cloths and coverings, varied and gem-bright, wrought of gold and made of antelope-wool, fallen upon the ground."

**8.14.49**  "The crest-jewels of the kings, the bright golden garlands, the parasols flung aside, and the yak-tail whisks and fans."

**8.14.50**  "Behold the earth, miry with blood, strewn with the faces of heroes—faces bright as moon and stars, with lovely earrings and trimmed beards, exceedingly adorned."

**8.14.51**  "And behold the men still living, moaning on every side, tended by many who have laid down their weapons, O lord of peoples."

**8.14.52**  "There, with kinsmen gathered round, weeping again and again, having covered over other warriors who have departed, the swift ones, greedy for victory and full of wrath, go forth again to battle."

**8.14.53**  "Others, proud men, run hither and thither there, begged for water by their fallen kinsmen, the heroes."

**8.14.54**  "And some who went for water, O Arjuna, lie breathless, many of them; and those heroes who came back, at the very sight of them, lost their senses."

**8.14.55**  "At the sight of water they rush forward, crying out to one another; behold, O Bhārata, some dead from drinking the water, and others still drinking."

**8.14.56**  "Others, O lover of kinsmen, forsaking their dear ones and their kin, were seen passing away here and there in that great battle."

**8.14.57**  "Behold yet others, O best of men, biting their lips, gazing all about with faces knit in scowling brows."

**8.14.58**  "This deed in the great battle befits you alone, O Arjuna, or the king of the gods in heaven—what you have wrought in this fight."

**8.14.59**  Thus Kṛṣṇa, showing that field of battle to the Diademed One as he drove on, heard a great clamor from Duryodhana's army.

**8.14.60**  The blare of conches and kettledrums mingled with tabors and drums, the din of chariots, horses, and elephants, and the dreadful clash of weapons.

**8.14.61**  Entering that host with his wind-swift horses, Kṛṣṇa halted, beholding your army hard pressed by Pāṇḍya.

**8.14.62**  For he, the foremost of bowmen, with arrows of every kind slew the throngs of his enemies in battle, robbing them of life like Death himself.

**8.14.63**  That best of strikers, piercing with sharp arrows the bodies of elephants, horses, and men, made them depart from their flesh, bereft of life.

**8.14.64**  Cutting apart with his shafts the various weapons hurled by the foremost of his foes, Pāṇḍya slew those enemies as Śakra slew the Asuras.

**8.15.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: This great hero, renowned in the world, you named before, but his deeds in battle you have not recounted, O Saṃjaya.

**8.15.2**  Tell me now in full the valor of that great hero—his skill, his might, his prowess, his measure, and his pride.

**8.15.3**  Saṃjaya said: Droṇa, Bhīṣma, Kṛpa, the son of Droṇa, Karṇa, Arjuna, and Janārdana—those masters of their craft whom you deem foremost with the bow in battle—

**8.15.4**  —by him a parity with Karṇa and Bhīṣma was shown in himself, nor would he own himself inferior to Vāsudeva and Arjuna.

**8.15.5**  That Pāṇḍya, best of kings, foremost of all bearers of arms, slew Karṇa's host like Death scorned and roused to fury.

**8.15.6**  That force, with its surging chariots and horses, its choice foot-soldiers and elephants, beset by Pāṇḍya, whirled about like a potter's wheel.

**8.15.7**  His foes, their horses, drivers, banners, and chariots gone, their weapons flung from their hands, Pāṇḍya scattered with well-aimed shafts as the wind scatters clouds.

**8.15.8**  Elephants with their trunks struck off, their pennons, banners, and weapons gone, together with their foot-guards, that slayer of foes slew as the foe-slayer slew his enemies with the thunderbolt.

**8.15.9**  Horsemen with their lances, javelins, and quivers, and their horses too, the Pulindas, Khaśas, Bāhlīkas, the Niṣādas, and the Taṅgaṇas,

**8.15.10**  the southerners and the Bhojas, cruel and hardened in war—stripping them of weapons and armor with his arrows, Pāṇḍya robbed them of life.

**8.15.11**  Seeing Pāṇḍya, undaunted, smiting the fourfold host with his arrows in the fray, the son of Droṇa, more undaunted still, advanced upon him.

**8.15.12**  Addressing him sweetly, dancing forward as if without fear, that best of strikers spoke, smiling first, as he summoned him:

**8.15.13**  "O king, lotus-petal-eyed, master of the finest weapons and mounts, you whose frame seems forged of the thunderbolt, foremost in strength and manhood,"

**8.15.14**  "twanging your great bow with both long arms, your weapon clasped fast in your fists, you shine exceedingly like a great rain-cloud."

**8.15.15**  As you rain upon your foes torrents of arrows of tremendous force, I see no champion in this battle fit to stand against you save myself.

**8.15.16**  Single-handed you crush chariots, elephants, foot-soldiers, and horsemen in their multitudes, as a fearless lion of terrible strength crushes herds of deer in the forest.

**8.15.17**  With the mighty roar of your chariot you make heaven and earth resound, as the sun at the rains' end, drinking up the grain-fields, floods all things with his rays.

**8.15.18**  Drawing keen shafts from your quiver, like venomous serpents, do battle with me alone, as the Andhaka demon fought with the three-eyed Rudra.

**8.15.19**  Thus addressed, the king of the Malaya banner answered, "So be it," and crying "Strike!" he pierced the son of Droṇa with a barbed arrow.

**8.15.20**  Then Droṇa's son, foremost of the teacher's line, smiling, struck the Pāṇḍya king with vital-piercing, terrible arrows that resembled tongues of flame.

**8.15.21**  Then Aśvatthāman loosed nine more sharp iron shafts, fledged with heron feathers and endowed with the tenfold speed of flight.

**8.15.22**  Of these the Pāṇḍya cut five apart with five whetted arrows; the remaining four struck his horses, and they were swiftly bereft of life.

**8.15.23**  Then, having cut those arrows of Droṇa's son with whetted shafts, the Pāṇḍya, splendid as the sun, severed the drawn bowstring.

**8.15.24**  Restringing his unstrung bow, Droṇa's son, the slayer of foes, sent forth thousands of arrows at the Pāṇḍya, choking the sky and all the quarters with shafts.

**8.15.25**  Then the Pāṇḍya, that bull among men, though he knew the arrows of the great-souled Droṇi to be inexhaustible, cut down all those that were hurled at him.

**8.15.26**  Having with effort cut those arrows that Droṇi sped at him, the foe then drove off his two wheel-guards with whetted shafts.

**8.15.27**  Then, seeing the deftness of his foe, Droṇa's son bent his bow into a circle and let fly his arrows in a downpour, as the younger brother of Pūṣan pours down rain.

**8.15.28**  That store of weapons which eight wagons, each drawn by eight oxen, bore away — all that, O honoured one, Droṇi hurled in an eighth part of a day.

**8.15.29**  Whoever looked upon him there — wrathful as Death itself, like the destroyer at the end of time — most of them fell senseless.

**8.15.30**  As the rain-cloud at summer's end drenches with its showers the earth with its mountains and trees, so the teacher's son drenched that army with a downpour of arrows.

**8.15.31**  That well-nigh unbearable rain of arrows loosed by the cloud that was Droṇi, the Pāṇḍya, a very wind, swiftly checked with the weapon of Vāyu, and roared aloud.

**8.15.32**  As he roared, Droṇi cut down his standard, adorned with sandal and aloe and rising like the Malaya mountain, and slew his four horses.

**8.15.33**  Slaying his charioteer with a single arrow, with a crescent-headed shaft he cut his bow, loud as a great thundercloud, and shattered his chariot to fragments fine as sesame.

**8.15.34**  Having warded off weapon with weapon and cut all the foe's arms apart, Droṇi, though his enemy was now in his power, did not slay him, in his longing for the fight.

**8.15.35**  A noble, well-caparisoned elephant, its driver slain, was sent in haste against him; armoured and mighty, the great lord of Malaya mounted it, as a lion roaring climbs a mountain peak.

**8.15.36**  Goading the elephant, the lord of the mountain-king, roaring, swiftly hurled at the teacher's son a lance bright as the sun's rays, with all the force, fury, and supreme effort of his weapon's release.

**8.15.37**  That lance, adorned with strings of gems, fine diamonds and gold, with silk, garlands and pearls, he drove home, crying again and again in his joy, "You are slain!" — and it pierced the splendid ornament on Droṇi's body.

**8.15.38**  That ornament, gleaming like the sun, moon, planets and fire, fell shattered to powder by the violent blow, as a great forest on a mountain peak, smitten by mighty Indra's bolt, falls to the ground.

**8.15.39**  Then he blazed up with surpassing fury, like a serpent-king trodden underfoot, and fitted to his bow fourteen arrows like the rod of Death, makers of an end to foes.

**8.15.40**  With five he struck off the elephant's feet and trunk, with three the king's arms and head, and with six he slew the six great car-warriors who followed the Pāṇḍya king, splendid as the six seasons.

**8.15.41**  The king's two arms, long and rounded, anointed with choice sandal, decked with gold, pearls, gems and diamonds, fell to the earth and writhed there like serpents struck down by Garuḍa.

**8.15.42**  And his head, its face bright as the full moon, with long eyes coppery in wrath and a high-bridged nose, falling earthward with its earrings, shone like the moon set between the two Viśākhā stars.

**8.15.43**  Then your son the king, approaching the teacher's son — perfect in his learning, his deed accomplished — surrounded by his friends, honoured him exceedingly with joy, as the lord of the immortals honoured Viṣṇu when Bali was conquered.

**8.16.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When the Pāṇḍya was slain, Saṃjaya, what did Arjuna do in the battle, and when the foe had been driven off by Karṇa, that lone champion?

**8.16.2**  Perfect in learning, mighty, disciplined, a hero is that son of Pāṇḍu, granted favour over all beings by the great-souled Śaṃkara.

**8.16.3**  Grievous and keen is the fear that comes from Dhanaṃjaya, slayer of foes. Tell me, Saṃjaya, what Pārtha did there.

**8.16.4**  Saṃjaya said: When the Pāṇḍya was slain, Kṛṣṇa hastily spoke salutary words to Arjuna: "Behold the overproud king, and the Pāṇḍavas in retreat.

**8.16.5**  By Aśvatthāman's resolve and by Karṇa the Sṛñjayas have been slain, and great is the slaughter wrought among horses, men, and elephants" — thus the unassailable Vāsudeva spoke to the diademed one.

**8.16.6**  Having heard this and seen the terrible, great peril to his brothers, the son of Pāṇḍu said, "Hṛṣīkeśa, drive the horses on, swiftly!"

**8.16.7**  Then Hṛṣīkeśa set forth with that chariot none could withstand, and once more a fearful encounter arose there.

**8.16.8**  Then again, O best of kings, a battle broke out between Karṇa and the Pāṇḍavas, swelling the realm of Yama.

**8.16.9**  Bows, arrows, iron clubs, swords, lances, spears, maces, slings, javelins, daggers, and battle-axes —

**8.16.10**  maces, darts, swords, spears, slings, and great goads — seizing these, the warriors swiftly fell upon one another, each longing to overcome the rest.

**8.16.11**  Making heaven, the quarters, the intermediate points, and the sky resound with the noise of arrows, bowstrings and palm-guards, and the earth with the rumble of chariot-wheels, they rushed upon their foes.

**8.16.12**  Thrilled by that mighty din, the heroes joined battle with heroes, fiercely terrible, each eager to fight the quarrel through to its end.

**8.16.13**  The sound of bowstrings, palm-guards and bows, the trumpeting of elephants, and the cries of the stricken as they fell — a tremendous uproar arose.

**8.16.14**  Hearing the manifold whistling of arrows and the roaring of the brave, O Bhārata, men trembled exceedingly, struck others down, and themselves grew faint.

**8.16.15**  As they roared and loosed a rain of weapons, Karṇa, the son of Adhiratha, crushed many of them with his battle-shafts.

**8.16.16**  Fifteen chariots of the Pāñcāla heroes, with their horses, drivers, and standards, Karṇa with his arrows sent to the abode of Yama.

**8.16.17**  The foremost warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, of great valour and swift weapons, veiling the sky, surrounded Karṇa in the battle on every side.

**8.16.18**  Then Karṇa, churning the enemy host with showers of arrows, plunged into it as a lord of the herd plunges into a lotus-pond filled with birds.

**8.16.19**  Springing into the midst of the foe, the son of Rādhā, shaking his peerless bow, with sharpened arrows struck off heads and made them fall.

**8.16.20**  Cutting through shields and armour, as if extinguishing the lives of embodied beings, they could not endure the touch of a second of his winged shafts.

**8.16.21**  With arrows sped from his bow that shattered armour, body, and life, and with the bowstring and palm-guards, he slew them as a charioteer drives his horses with the whip.

**8.16.22**  He brought the Pāṇḍus, Sṛñjayas, and Pāñcālas within range of his arrows, and Karṇa crushed them with vehemence, as a lion crushes herds of deer.

**8.16.23**  Then, O honoured one, the sons of the Pāñcālas and the sons of Draupadī, the twins, and Yuyudhāna together rushed upon Karṇa.

**8.16.24**  Striving with all their might, the Kuru, Pāṇḍava, and Sṛñjaya warriors, casting away their dear lives in battle, closed with one another.

**8.16.25**  Well-girt, mailed, adorned with helmets, the great car-warriors — some with maces, some with clubs, and others with iron bars —

**8.16.26**  rushed fiercely upon each other, like gods with raised rods, roaring, challenging, and bounding about, O honoured one.

**8.16.27**  Then they slew one another and fell, smitten in the fray, vomiting blood from their limbs, their brains and eyes dashed out in battle.

**8.16.28**  With faces full of teeth, blood-smeared and red as pomegranates, propped upright by the weapons within them, these slain men yet stood as though alive.

**8.16.29**  And still others fell upon one another with spears, swords, lances, slings, claw-weapons, darts, and javelins,

**8.16.30**  and others, in their wrath, hacked, cut, pierced, hurled, mangled, slew, and tore one another asunder.

**8.16.31**  Slain by one another, lifeless and drenched in gore, they fell, oozing their own bright blood like sandal trees that bleed their sap.

**8.16.32**  Chariots were destroyed by chariots, elephants by elephants, men by foremost of men, and horses by horses fell in their thousands.

**8.16.33**  Standards, heads, parasols, elephants' trunks, and the arms of men, severed by razor-shafts, broad-heads, and crescent-blades, let fall their weapons.

**8.16.34**  Men, elephants, chariots, and horses were crushed in the battle; the brave were slain by horsemen, and the tuskers had their trunks cut off.

**8.16.35**  Standards with their banners fell like crumbling mountains; and foot-soldiers, swarming up, brought down both elephants and chariots.

**8.16.36**  Smitten, being struck down, and fallen on every side, horsemen swiftly closed and were slain by foot-soldiers, and bands of foot-soldiers, killed by horsemen, lay slain upon the field.

**8.16.37**  Like crushed lotuses and withered garlands, O most wise one, were the faces and the limbs of the slain.

**8.16.38**  The once exceedingly beautiful forms of elephants, horses, and men, O king, like soaked garments, became most grievous to look upon.

**8.17.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then the elephant-riders, urged on by your son, wrathful and seeking to slay Dhṛṣṭadyumna, rushed with their elephants upon the son of Pṛṣata.

**8.17.2**  The eastern and southern champions skilled in elephant-warfare — the Aṅgas, Vaṅgas, Puṇḍras, Māgadhas, and Tāmraliptakas,

**8.17.3**  the Mekalas, Kośalas, Madras, Daśārṇas, and Niṣadhas, skilled in elephant-battle, together with the Kaliṅgas, O Bhārata —

**8.17.4**  all of these, like rain-laden clouds, drenched in the battle the mountain that was the Pāñcāla prince with arrows, lances, and iron shafts.

**8.17.5**  Eager to crush those elephants, sorely goaded by heels, toes, and hooks, the son of Pṛṣata drenched them with arrows and iron shafts.

**8.17.6**  Each one of those mountain-like elephants he pierced with ten, with six, or with eight arrows hurled at them, O Bhārata, even as he was being veiled by the elephants like the sun by clouds.

**8.17.7**  The Pāṇḍus and Pāñcālas, roaring, their weapons whetted, surrounded those elephants, raining upon them with the twang of bowstrings and the cry of arrows.

**8.17.8**  Nakula and Sahadeva, the sons of Draupadī, the Prabhadrakas, Sātyaki, Śikhaṇḍin, and the valiant Cekitāna —

**8.17.9**  those elephants, sped on by the barbarians, seizing men, horses, and chariots with their trunks, in their great fury crushed them underfoot.

**8.17.10**  They gored with the points of their tusks, snatched up and flung men about, and others, terrible to behold, rushed on with bodies impaled upon their tusks.

**8.17.11**  But the elephant of the Vaṅga that stood at the forefront, Sātyaki, piercing it in a vital spot with an iron shaft of terrible speed, brought down.

**8.17.12**  As that warrior, his elephant felled, was springing down from it, Sātyaki pierced his breast with an iron shaft, and he fell to the earth.

**8.17.13**  As the Puṇḍra's elephant, like a moving mountain, came rushing on, Sahadeva with effort struck it down with three iron shafts.

**8.17.14**  Having stripped that elephant of its banner, its driver, its armour, its standard, and its very life, Sahadeva once more bore down upon the king of Aṅga.

**8.17.15**  But Nakula, holding Sahadeva back, himself afflicted the Aṅga, striking the king with three iron shafts like the rods of Yama, and the elephant with a hundred.

**8.17.16**  The Aṅga hurled at Nakula eight hundred javelins bright as the rays of the sun, but he cut each one into three pieces.

**8.17.17**  Then with a crescent-headed arrow the Pāṇḍava severed his head, and the slain barbarian fell to the ground together with his tusked beast.

**8.17.18**  When the preceptor's son, skilled in the lore of elephants, had been slain, the enraged elephant-drivers of the Aṅgas rushed upon Nakula with their beasts.

**8.17.19**  With fluttering banners on their foreheads, with golden girths and body-coverings, they came on swiftly, eager to crush him, like blazing mountains in motion.

**8.17.20**  The Mekalas, Utkalas, and Kaliṅgas, the Niṣādas and the men of Tāmraliptaka, bent on slaughter, loosed showers of arrows and javelins.

**8.17.21**  Seeing Nakula overspread by them as the sun is veiled by clouds, the Pāṇḍavas, Pāñcālas, and Somakas, much enraged, rushed forward all around him.

**8.17.22**  Then there arose a battle between the car-warriors and the elephants, both pouring forth showers of arrows and javelins by the thousand.

**8.17.23**  The frontal globes of the elephants burst asunder, and their various vital parts, and the tusks of the deeply pierced beasts, and their ornaments, were shattered by the iron shafts.

**8.17.24**  With sixty-four keenly sharpened arrows Sahadeva quickly slew eight of those great elephants, and they fell together with their riders.

**8.17.25**  Drawing his excellent bow with effort, Nakula, O delight of the Kurus, smote the elephants with straight-flying iron shafts.

**8.17.26**  Then the descendant of Śini and the Pāñcāla prince, the sons of Draupadī, the Prabhadrakas, and Śikhaṇḍin drenched the great elephants with showers of arrows.

**8.17.27**  Those mountainlike elephants of the foe, struck by the arrow-showers of the Pāṇḍava warriors who were like rain-clouds, fell as mountains fall beneath downpours of thunderbolts.

**8.17.28**  Having thus slain your elephants, those bulls among men of the Pāṇḍava side looked upon your army, swiftly scattering like a river whose banks have been breached.

**8.17.29**  Beholding that host, the warriors of the son of Pāṇḍu threw it into confusion, and then once again rushed straight at Karṇa.

**8.17.30**  Then, O great king, Duḥśāsana the brother advanced against his brother Sahadeva, who in his fury was consuming your host.

**8.17.31**  Seeing those two meet in mighty combat, the lords of men there raised lionlike roars and waved their garments aloft.

**8.17.32**  Then, O Bhārata, the mighty son of Pāṇḍu was struck upon the chest with three arrows by your wrathful bowman son.

**8.17.33**  Thereupon, O king, Sahadeva pierced your son with an iron shaft, then struck him with seventy more and his charioteer with three apiece.

**8.17.34**  Then, O king, Duḥśāsana, having cut his bow asunder in that great fray, afflicted Sahadeva with seventy-three shafts upon his arms and chest.

**8.17.35**  Then the wrathful Sahadeva, best of warriors and full of splendour, seized a sword in that great battle and whirled it at your son.

**8.17.36**  That great blade, having cut through his bow together with its fitted arrows, then fell to the earth like a serpent dropping from the sky.

**8.17.37**  Then the valiant Sahadeva took up another bow and let fly at Duḥśāsana a death-dealing arrow.

**8.17.38**  As that shaft came flying on, gleaming like the rod of Yama, the Kaurava cut it in two with his keen-edged sword.

**8.17.39**  As that sword came suddenly hurtling on, Sahadeva, as if smiling, struck it down in the battle with his sharp arrows.

**8.17.40**  Then, O Bhārata, your son in that great battle quickly let fall sixty-four arrows upon Sahadeva's car.

**8.17.41**  Those many arrows speeding upon him in the fight, O king, Sahadeva cut to pieces, each one with five shafts.

**8.17.42**  Having warded off the great arrows sent by your son, the son of Mādrī then gathered very many shafts and aimed them at him.

**8.17.43**  Then in his fury, O great king, the valiant Sahadeva fitted a dreadful shaft like Death, Time, and the Ender, and drawing his bow mightily, loosed it at your son.

**8.17.44**  Piercing him with speed and rending his great armour, the shaft entered the earth, O king, as a serpent enters an anthill; and thereupon, O king, your son, that mighty car-warrior, swooned away.

**8.17.45**  Seeing him senseless, his charioteer, sorely afraid, swiftly bore the car away as it was being raked by sharp arrows.

**8.17.46**  Having vanquished him in battle, the son of Pāṇḍu, O descendant of Pāṇḍu's line, joyfully crushed Duryodhana's force on every side.

**8.17.47**  As a man in anger tramples an anthill of ants, O king, so was that Kaurava army crushed by him, O Bhārata.

**8.17.48**  Then, O king, Karṇa the son of the sun checked Nakula, who in his fury was rending the host asunder in the battle.

**8.17.49**  And Nakula, smiling, then said this to Karṇa: "After long, alas, the gods have looked upon me with kindly eyes,

**8.17.50**  since you, O wretch, have come within the range of my sight in battle. For you are the root of all our calamities, of the enmity and the strife.

**8.17.51**  Through your fault the Kurus have been wasted, falling upon one another. Slaying you this day in battle, I shall have achieved my purpose and be freed of fever."

**8.17.52**  Thus addressed, the son of the charioteer answered Nakula in words befitting a prince and, above all, a bowman:

**8.17.53**  "Strike in battle, O boy, that we may see your manhood. Having done some deed in the fight, O hero, then are you fit to boast.

**8.17.54**  Without speaking, my son, do the brave fight to the utmost of their power. So fight with me with all your might; this day I shall humble your pride."

**8.17.55**  Having said this, the charioteer's son swiftly smote the son of Pāṇḍu, and pierced him in the battle with seventy-three arrows.

**8.17.56**  Then Nakula, thus pierced by the charioteer's son, O Bhārata, struck the charioteer's son in turn with eighty shafts like venomous serpents.

**8.17.57**  Karṇa, that foremost of archers, cut his bow asunder and afflicted the Pāṇḍava with thirty stone-whetted, golden-feathered arrows.

**8.17.58**  Piercing his armour, they drank his blood in the battle, as venomous serpents, having broken through the earth, drink water.

**8.17.59**  Then, taking up another bow, gold-backed and hard to withstand, he pierced Karṇa with twenty arrows and his charioteer with three.

**8.17.60**  Then in his wrath, O great king, Nakula, slayer of hostile heroes, cut off Karṇa's bow with an exceedingly sharp razor-headed shaft.

**8.17.61**  Then, while his bow was severed, the hero, laughing, smote that mighty car-warrior renowned in all the worlds with three hundred shafts.

**8.17.62**  Seeing Karṇa thus sorely pressed by the son of Pāṇḍu, O sire, the car-warriors, together with the gods, were filled with the utmost amazement.

**8.17.63**  Then Karṇa, the son of the sun, taking up another bow, afflicted Nakula with five arrows in the region of the collarbone.

**8.17.64**  With those arrows fixed upon his breast, the son of Mādrī shone resplendent, like the sun shedding its light upon the world with its own rays.

**8.17.65**  Then Nakula, having pierced Karṇa with seven iron shafts, once more cut off the tip of his bow, O sire.

**8.17.66**  Taking up another bow of swifter force in the battle, Karṇa then with his arrows blocked the quarters on every side around Nakula.

**8.17.67**  Being suddenly overspread by the arrows loosed from Karṇa's bow, that great car-warrior swiftly cut those arrows asunder with arrows of his own.

**8.17.68**  Then a net wrought of arrows was seen stretched across the sky, as the heavens appear when filled with swarms of darting fireflies.

**8.17.69**  By the hundreds of arrows loosed by the two, the sky was then covered over, and it was as thronged as when swarms of locusts fill it.

**8.17.70**  Those arrows wrought of gold, falling again and again in ordered rows, gleamed like ranks of wild geese flying in lines.

**8.17.71**  When the sky was veiled in the net of arrows and the sun was hidden, only with difficulty could any creature move about, O lord of peoples.

**8.17.72**  With the path obstructed all around by masses of arrows, the two illustrious warriors shone like two newly risen morning suns.

**8.17.73**  The Somakas, being slain by the arrows loosed from Karṇa's bow, shrank away, O king of kings, tormented by pain and afflicted by the shafts.

**8.17.74**  Likewise your host, being slain by Nakula's arrows, scattered to the quarters, O king, as clouds are driven apart by the wind.

**8.17.75**  Both armies, being smitten by those two with great celestial arrows, withdrew beyond the reach of the shafts and then stood by as mere onlookers.

**8.17.76**  When the host had been driven back by the arrows of Karṇa and the Pāṇḍava, those two high-souled warriors pierced each other with showers of shafts.

**8.17.77**  Displaying their celestial weapons at the head of the battle and suddenly veiling each other, each sought the other's death.

**8.17.78**  The arrows loosed by Nakula, feathered with heron and peacock plumes, having shrouded Karṇa, then took their stand as though foes encircling him.

**8.17.79**  Entered as they were into a dwelling of arrows, neither could be seen by anyone, O king, like the moon and sun veiled at the coming of the rains.

**8.17.80**  Then Karṇa, wrathful in the fray, assuming a still more terrible aspect, covered the Pāṇḍava on every side with showers of arrows.

**8.17.81**  Though overspread in the battle by the charioteer's son, the Pāṇḍava felt no distress, O king, like the sun amid the rain-clouds.

**8.17.82**  Then the son of Adhiratha, laughing, O sire, sent forth nets of arrows in the battle by the hundred and by the thousand.

**8.17.83**  By the arrows of that great-souled one all became one unbroken shadow; with the excellent shafts falling thick, it grew like the shade of clouds.

**8.17.84**  Then Karṇa, O great king, having cut the bow of that high-souled one, struck down his charioteer from the floor of the car, as if in sport.

**8.17.85**  And with four sharp arrows he swiftly dispatched his four horses to the abode of Yama, O Bhārata.

**8.17.86**  Then he swiftly shattered his car to fragments small as sesame seeds with his arrows, and his banner, his wheel-guards, his standard, his sword, O sire, his hundred-mooned shield, and all his equipment.

**8.17.87**  His horses slain, his car destroyed, his armour gone, O lord of peoples, he quickly stepped down from the car and stood his ground, seizing an iron club.

**8.17.88**  That uplifted, most terrible iron club of his the charioteer's son shattered with his shafts, O king, by the hundred and by the thousand.

**8.17.89**  Seeing him weaponless, Karṇa afflicted him many times over with smooth-jointed arrows, yet did not press him hard.

**8.17.90**  Being smitten in the battle by that mightier master of arms, Nakula, his senses confounded, suddenly fled, O king.

**8.17.91**  Rushing after him, the son of Rādhā, laughing again and again, cast his strung bow about Nakula's neck, O Bhārata.

**8.17.92**  Then he shone resplendent, O king, with the great bow hung about his neck, as the moon in the sky encircled by a halo, or as a white cloud adorned by the bow of Indra.

**8.17.93**  Then Karṇa said to him: "In vain have you spoken. Speak now again, full of joy, that I am one to be slain by you, again and again."

**8.17.94**  Do not fight against your elders and against the mighty, O Pāṇḍava; fight rather against your equals, my child. Be not ashamed, O Pāṇḍava. Go home, O son of Mādrī, or go to where Kṛṣṇa and Phalguna are.

**8.17.95**  Having spoken thus, O great king, he then let him go. Though that man had come within reach of death, O king, the son of the charioteer did not slay him; remembering the words of Kuntī, O king, he set him free.

**8.17.96**  Released by the bowman son of the charioteer, the Pāṇḍava, O king, went off as if abashed toward the chariot of Yudhiṣṭhira.

**8.17.97**  Scorched by the son of the charioteer, he mounted his chariot, sighing and burning with grief, like a serpent flung into a jar.

**8.17.98**  Having let him go in the battle, Karṇa swiftly advanced upon the Pāñcālas with his chariot of towering banners and moon-white horses.

**8.17.99**  At that, O lord of peoples, there arose a great wail among the Pāṇḍavas as they beheld the general advancing upon the ranks of Pāñcāla chariots.

**8.17.100**  There, O great king, the son of the charioteer wrought havoc, that lord ranging like a wheel as the sun reached the midday sky.

**8.17.101**  With wheels shattered, with standards and banners cut down, with their charioteers slain or yet alive, with axles broken, O honored one, we saw the ranks of Pāñcāla chariots being borne away.

**8.17.102**  Here and there, bewildered, the maddened elephants roamed about, their limbs as though encircled by a forest fire in a great wood.

**8.17.103**  With their temple-bosses split, streaming blood, their trunks lopped off, their finest limbs gashed and their tails sheared away, O honored one, the elephants, slaughtered by that great-souled one, fell like sundered clouds.

**8.17.104**  Other elephants, terrified by hundreds of iron shafts and lances, rushed straight toward him like moths into a flame.

**8.17.105**  Other great elephants were seen trumpeting in agony, pouring blood from their bodies like mountains streaming with cascades of water.

**8.17.106**  And the horses, stripped of their breast-plates and tail-trappings, and of their ornaments of silver, of bronze, and of gold,

**8.17.107**  bereft of their saddle-cloths and rid of their bits, deprived of their chowries and housings, with their quivers fallen away,

**8.17.108**  and their riders slain — heroes who had been the splendor of the fight — we saw those splendid horses wandering aimlessly there in the battle.

**8.17.109**  Pierced through by darts, by swords, and by spears, O lord of men, we saw the cavalrymen, clad in their corselets and turbans.

**8.17.110**  We saw chariots adorned with gold and well-yoked with swift horses careering wildly about, their warriors slain.

**8.17.111**  Some with their axles and shafts broken, some with wheels cut off, O honored one, and others stripped of pennon and standard, with pole, yoke, and seat hewn apart.

**8.17.112**  There we saw the warriors who had lost their cars running about in every direction, terror-stricken by the arrows of the Sun's son, O lord of peoples.

**8.17.113**  And we saw others, both weaponless and still bearing arms, slain in great numbers — your own men, sheathed in mail and adorned with breast-bells.

**8.17.114**  We beheld the foot-soldiers, decked with banners gay in many varied hues, running about on every side.

**8.17.115**  By the arrows loosed from Karṇa's bow we saw still others in the battle, their heads, their arms, and their thighs lopped off, made limbless.

**8.17.116**  A great and terrible carnage of warriors was seen — of men struck down by Karṇa's missiles, slain by his whetted shafts.

**8.17.117**  The Sṛñjayas, slaughtered in the battle by the son of the charioteer, rushed straight toward him like insects into a flame.

**8.17.118**  That mighty warrior, burning down the armies here and there, the kṣatriyas shunned as one shuns the raging fire of the world's end.

**8.17.119**  And those great car-warriors of the Pāñcālas who survived the slaughter — broken and in flight, their armor and standards shattered — Karṇa, blazing with valor, pursued from behind, scattering arrows upon them.

**8.17.120**  The mighty car-warrior, the son of the charioteer, scorched them with his arrows, as the dispeller of darkness, having reached the noontide, scorches all beings.

**8.18.1**  Saṃjaya said: As your son Yuyutsu was rushing against the great host, Ulūka swiftly fell upon him, crying, "Stand! Stand!"

**8.18.2**  Then Yuyutsu, O king, with a sharp-edged feathered shaft struck Ulūka as Indra strikes a mountain with the thunderbolt.

**8.18.3**  Thereupon Ulūka, enraged, in the encounter cut your son's bow with a razor-edged arrow and struck him with a barbed shaft.

**8.18.4**  Casting aside that severed bow, Yuyutsu, his eyes red with rage, took up another very great and more powerful bow.

**8.18.5**  And then, O bull of the Bhāratas, he pierced the son of Śakuni with sixty arrows, struck his charioteer with three, and wounded him yet again.

**8.18.6**  But Ulūka, having pierced him with twenty gold-decked shafts, then in his wrath cut down his golden standard in the battle.

**8.18.7**  That great standard, its staff severed and crumbling, blazing with gold, fell before Yuyutsu's eyes, O king.

**8.18.8**  Seeing his standard struck down, Yuyutsu, swooning with rage, smote Ulūka in the middle of the breast with five arrows.

**8.18.9**  With a broad-headed shaft burnished in oil, O honored one, Ulūka instantly struck off the head of his charioteer, O best of the Bhāratas.

**8.18.10**  He slew the four horses too and pierced him with five arrows; sorely wounded by the mighty one, Yuyutsu withdrew to another chariot.

**8.18.11**  Having vanquished him in battle, O king, Ulūka swiftly went on, smiting the Pāñcālas and the Sṛñjayas with his whetted shafts.

**8.18.12**  Your son Śrutakarman, O great king, in half the twinkling of an eye and without flurry, robbed Śatānīka of horses, charioteer, and car.

**8.18.13**  Standing in his car whose horses were slain, the mighty Śatānīka, in fury, hurled his mace at your son, O honored one.

**8.18.14**  That mace, reducing the chariot to ashes along with the horses and the charioteer, swiftly fell to earth as if cleaving it open, O Bhārata.

**8.18.15**  Both those heroes, deprived of their chariots, augmenters of the Kurus' glory, withdrew, eager still to fight, gazing upon each other.

**8.18.16**  Your son, agitated, mounted the chariot of Vivitsu, while Śatānīka in haste went over to the car of Prativindhya.

**8.18.17**  Sutasoma, full of fury, pierced Śakuni with his whetted shafts, yet could not shake him, even as a flood of water cannot shake a mountain.

**8.18.18**  Seeing in him his father's bitterest foe, Sutasoma covered him with many thousands of arrows, O Bhārata.

**8.18.19**  Śakuni swiftly cut down those arrows with other winged shafts — light-handed with his weapons, a brilliant fighter, and ever wearing the look of victory in the fray.

**8.18.20**  Having warded off those arrows in the battle with his whetted shafts, he, exceedingly wroth, struck Sutasoma with three arrows.

**8.18.21**  With his arrows your brother-in-law of mighty valor cut that man's horses, banner, and charioteer to pieces; thereupon the people cried out.

**8.18.22**  His horses slain, his car destroyed, his bow cut, O honored one, the bowman took up another excellent bow and, descending from the chariot, stood upon the ground, and loosed shafts gold-feathered and whetted on stone.

**8.18.23**  Then those flights of arrows covered the car of your brother-in-law, that great warrior, as swarms of insects might cover it.

**8.18.24**  Though he saw them descending upon the floor of his car, the son of Subala was not dismayed in the least, but, illustrious one, crushed those arrows with his own showers of shafts.

**8.18.25**  At this the warriors there, and the perfected beings stationed in the sky, were filled with delight, beholding that incredible and wondrous feat of Sutasoma — that, himself on foot, he gave battle to that prince who stood upon his car.

**8.18.26**  With keen, swift, smooth-jointed broad-headed shafts the prince utterly shattered his bow and his quiver as well.

**8.18.27**  His bow cut, he in the battle, roaring aloud, raised on high a sword the hue of lapis and the blue lotus, its hilt fashioned of ivory.

**8.18.28**  As wise Sutasoma whirled that blade, gleaming like the cloudless sky, it seemed then to all like Death himself.

**8.18.29**  Sword in hand, endowed with training and strength, he suddenly moved through circling figures by the thousand — four-and-twenty of them, O great king.

**8.18.30**  Then the valiant son of Subala hurled arrows at him, and those, even as they fell upon him, he swiftly cut apart with his excellent sword.

**8.18.31**  Then, enraged, O great king, the son of Subala, slayer of hostile heroes, sped at Sutasoma arrows like venomous serpents.

**8.18.32**  These too he cut with his sword, by skill and by strength, displaying his nimbleness in the fight, radiant with valor like Garuḍa.

**8.18.33**  As he ranged about, O king, weaving his circling movements, the son of Subala then cut his gleaming sword with a keen razor-edged shaft.

**8.18.34**  That great sword, severed, fell at once to the ground; only the hilt with a fragment of blade remained, perforce, in his hand.

**8.18.35**  Knowing his blade was cut, Sutasoma, that great car-warrior, sprang back six paces and then hurled the remnant at his foe.

**8.18.36**  That fragment, decked with gold and diamond, cut the bowstring and bow of the great-souled foe in the battle and swiftly fell to the earth; thereupon Sutasoma went over to the great chariot of Śrutakīrti.

**8.18.37**  The son of Subala too, taking up another terrible and well-nigh unbearable bow, advanced upon the Pāṇḍava host, slaying many bands of foes.

**8.18.38**  At that there arose a great outcry among the Pāṇḍavas, O lord of peoples, as they beheld the son of Subala ranging fearlessly through the battle.

**8.18.39**  Those proud and mighty armies, well-armed though they were, were seen put to flight by the great-souled son of Subala.

**8.18.40**  As the king of the gods once crushed the host of the Daityas, O king, even so did the son of Subala destroy the army of the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.18.41**  Kṛpa, O king, checked Dhṛṣṭadyumna in the encounter, as a śarabha in the wild would check a proud elephant in combat.

**8.18.42**  Held in check by that mightier descendant of Gautama, the son of Pṛṣata could not move there even a single step, O Bhārata.

**8.18.43**  Beholding the form of the descendant of Gautama bearing down upon Dhṛṣṭadyumna's chariot, all beings trembled and deemed that destruction had come.

**8.18.44**  At this the car-warriors and horsemen, downcast in spirit, said: "Surely the best of men is enraged at the slaying of Droṇa."

**8.18.45**  "The son of Śaradvat is of great splendor, knower of celestial weapons, lofty of mind. Will it be well today for Dhṛṣṭadyumna at the hands of this descendant of Gautama?

**8.18.46**  Will this whole army be delivered from its great peril? Will this brāhmaṇa not slay us all, assembled here together?"

**8.18.47**  "Such is the form he wears, terribly like unto Death, that today in the battle he will follow the path of the son of Bharadvāja."

**8.18.48**  "The preceptor is swift of hand and ever victorious in fight, master of weapons, endowed with valor, and now possessed by wrath."

**8.18.49**  "And the son of Pṛṣata is even now seen sorely averse to the fight." Such were the manifold sayings of your men, mingled with those of the foe.

**8.18.50**  Then, hissing in his wrath, Kṛpa the son of Śaradvat, O king, covered the son of Pṛṣata, now grown motionless, in all his vital parts with arrows.

**8.18.51**  Assailed in the battle by the great-souled descendant of Gautama, he, bewildered in that supreme combat, knew not what was to be done.

**8.18.52**  Then his charioteer said to him: "Is all well with you, O son of Pṛṣata? Never before in battle has such a calamity been seen in you."

**8.18.53**  "It is by the working of fate that those vital-piercing arrows, loosed by the foremost of the twice-born and aimed at all your vital points, have not made an end of you."

**8.18.54**  "I am turning the chariot back from there, as one draws it back from a river's torrent rushing toward the sea; for I deem that brāhmaṇa unslayable, by whom your valor has been undone."

**8.18.55**  Then, O king, Dhṛṣṭadyumna slowly spoke these words: "My mind grows faint, dear charioteer, and sweat breaks out upon my limbs;

**8.18.56**  behold too the trembling in my body and the bristling of my hair. Avoiding that brāhmaṇa in the fight, drive slowly to where the unfaltering one stands."

**8.18.57**  "Reaching Arjuna or Bhīmasena in the battle, O charioteer, safety would be ours this day, driver—such is my settled conviction."

**8.18.58**  Then, O great king, the charioteer set forth, urging on the horses toward where Bhīma, the mighty archer, was fighting with your soldiers.

**8.18.59**  But seeing Dhṛṣṭadyumna's chariot rushing away, O honored one, the descendant of Gautama then pursued him, scattering hundreds of arrows.

**8.18.60**  And again and again the tamer of foes blew his conch, and gave chase to the fleeing son of Pṛṣata, as great Indra pursued Śambara.

**8.18.61**  But Śikhaṇḍin, the unassailable death of Bhīṣma, the son of Hṛdika warded off in the battle, smiling as it were, again and again.

**8.18.62**  And Śikhaṇḍin, closing with that great warrior of the Hṛdikas, struck him in the region of the collarbone with five sharp broad-headed shafts.

**8.18.63**  But Kṛtavarman, enraged, having pierced him with sixty swift arrows, with a single one cut through his bow, laughing, O king—that great car-warrior.

**8.18.64**  Then the mighty son of Drupada, taking up another bow, enraged, cried out to the son of Hṛdika, "Stand! Stand!"

**8.18.65**  Then he sent forth at him ninety arrows, gold-feathered and keen-edged, O lord of kings; but they glanced off from his armor.

**8.18.66**  Marking those arrows fallen short and dropped upon the ground, the mighty one cut through his bow with a sharp razor-headed shaft.

**8.18.67**  Then, his bow severed, like a bull with a broken horn, he afflicted him in the arms and chest with eighty shafts in his wrath.

**8.18.68**  But Kṛtavarman, enraged and wounded by those arrows, taking up another bow together with a store of shafts, O lord, struck Śikhaṇḍin upon the shoulder with choice arrows.

**8.18.69**  And with the arrows lodged in his shoulder Śikhaṇḍin shone resplendent, like a great tree with its spreading, glistening branches.

**8.18.70**  Those two, having pierced each other sorely and drenched with blood, shone like two bulls that have gored one another with their horns.

**8.18.71**  Striving each to slay the other, those two great car-warriors wheeled there in their chariots through circling courses by the thousand.

**8.18.72**  Kṛtavarman, O great king, pierced the son of Pṛṣata in the battle with seventy keen, stone-whetted arrows feathered with gold.

**8.18.73**  Then in the fray the Bhoja, foremost of strikers, loosed at him in haste a dreadful arrow that brings life to its end.

**8.18.74**  Struck by it, O king, he swiftly swooned into a faint, and suddenly, overcome with stupor, leaned against his banner-staff.

**8.18.75**  And his charioteer bore that best of car-warriors away from the fight, tormented by the son of Hṛdika's shaft and sighing again and again.

**8.18.76**  When that hero, the son of Drupada, was thus overcome, O lord, the Pāṇḍava host, slaughtered on every side, broke into flight.

**8.19.1**  Saṃjaya said: He of the white horses too, O great king, scattered your force, as the wind, falling upon a heap of cotton, drives it on every side.

**8.19.2**  Against him advanced the Trigartas, the Śibis together with the Kauravas, the Śālvas, and the Saṃśaptakas, and that host of the Nārāyaṇas.

**8.19.3**  Satyasena, Satyakīrti, Mitradeva, Śrutaṃjaya, Sauśruti, Citrasena, and Mitravarman, O Bhārata—

**8.19.4**  the king of the Trigartas in the battle, encircled by his brothers and by his sons, mighty archers, bearers of manifold weapons in the fight,

**8.19.5**  they, loosing volleys of arrows and showering Arjuna in the encounter, rushed upon him in the fray like floods of water toward the sea.

**8.19.6**  But those warriors, by hundreds and by thousands, closing with Arjuna, all came to destruction, as serpents do at the sight of Tārkṣya.

**8.19.7**  Though slaughtered in the battle, they did not then forsake the Pāṇḍava, even as moths forsake not the fire that consumes them, O king.

**8.19.8**  Satyasena pierced the Pāṇḍava in the fight with three arrows, Mitradeva with sixty-three, and Candradeva with seven.

**8.19.9**  Mitravarman with seventy-three, and Sauśruti with five, Śatruṃjaya with twenty, and Suśarman with nine arrows.

**8.19.10**  And having slain there King Śatruṃjaya with stone-whetted shafts, he struck from Sauśruti's body his head together with its helmet, and swiftly with arrows sent Candradeva to the abode of Yama.

**8.19.11**  Then the others, O great king, those great car-warriors striving in the fight, he held off, each one of them, with five arrows apiece.

**8.19.12**  But Satyasena, enraged, hurled a great iron lance, aiming it at Kṛṣṇa in the fray, and roared a lion's roar.

**8.19.13**  That fierce lance of iron, having pierced the left arm of the high-souled Mādhava, then fell to the earth.

**8.19.14**  And as Mādhava was wounded by the lance in that great battle, the goad fell from his hand, and the reins as well, O lord of peoples.

**8.19.15**  That one of great renown, taking up again the goad and the reins, drove those horses on toward Satyasena's chariot.

**8.19.16**  But seeing Viṣvaksena wounded, Pārtha Dhanaṃjaya, of mighty strength, having rent Satyasena with sharp arrows—

**8.19.17**  then with well-sharpened shafts he severed from the body, in the midst of the host, that king's great head, adorned with earrings.

**8.19.18**  Having slain him with whetted arrows, he assailed Mitravarman, and with a sharp calf-tooth shaft struck down his charioteer as well, O honored one.

**8.19.19**  Then once more with hundreds of arrows the self-controlled one, enraged, brought down the bands of the Saṃśaptakas by hundreds and by thousands.

**8.19.20**  Then with a silver-feathered razor-headed shaft that one of great renown severed the head of the high-souled King Mitradeva, and, enraged, struck Suśarman in the region of the collarbone.

**8.19.21**  Then all the Saṃśaptakas, surrounding Dhanaṃjaya, crushed him with floods of weapons in their wrath, making the ten directions resound.

**8.19.22**  But Jiṣṇu, sorely pressed by them, equal to Śakra in prowess, the immeasurable-souled great car-warrior, brought forth the Aindra weapon; and then arrows by the thousand sprang into being, O lord of peoples.

**8.19.23**  Of banners being cut down, and of bows, in the encounter, of chariots with their pennons, of quivers along with their arrows,

**8.19.24**  of axles and of harness-straps, of wheels together with their reins, of chariot-poles, of fenders, and of darts in the fray,

**8.19.25**  of stones falling, of lances together with double-edged spears, of maces and of iron bludgeons, of pikes along with iron lances,

**8.19.26**  of hundred-slayers with their wheels, of arms together with thighs, of neck-cords and armlets, and of bracelets, O honored one,

**8.19.27**  of necklaces and gold ornaments, and of corselets, O Bhārata, of parasols and fans, and of heads together with their diadems—a great din was heard here and there, O lord of peoples.

**8.19.28**  Heads with their earrings, fair-eyed, shining like the full moon, were seen upon the earth, like the host of stars in the sky.

**8.19.29**  The bodies of the slain, well-garlanded, well-clad, and anointed with sandal-paste, were seen upon the ground; and that dreadful battlefield then took on the semblance of a city of the gandharvas.

**8.19.30**  With slain princes and mighty kṣatriyas, and with fallen elephants and horses, the earth in that battle became impassable, as though strewn with shattered mountains.

**8.19.31**  There was no path for the wheels of the high-souled Pāṇḍava, as he slew his foes with broad-headed shafts and an immense host of elephants and horses.

**8.19.32**  The chariot-wheels sank down to the very nave, O honored one, as he ranged through the battle in that mire of blood.

**8.19.33**  The sinking wheels the horses dragged on with great effort, joined to mighty toil, though swift as the mind and the wind.

**8.19.34**  That army, slaughtered by the bow-bearing son of Pāṇḍu, turned its face away for the most part, and stood not its ground in the encounter.

**8.19.35**  Having conquered in battle those many bands of Saṃśaptakas, Jiṣṇu shone forth, O great king, like a smokeless, blazing fire.

**8.19.36**  Yudhiṣṭhira, O great king, loosing many arrows, King Duryodhana himself received fearlessly in combat.

**8.19.37**  That mighty son of yours, as he rushed suddenly on, the King of Righteousness swiftly pierced, and cried, "Stand! Stand!"

**8.19.38**  And he in turn pierced him with nine sharp arrows, and, sorely enraged, struck his charioteer with a broad-headed shaft.

**8.19.39**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira hurled at Duryodhana thirteen gold-feathered, stone-whetted arrows.

**8.19.40**  With four he slew his four steeds, that great car-warrior, and with the fifth he struck the head of his charioteer from the body.

**8.19.41**  With the sixth the king's banner, with the seventh his bow, and with the eighth his sword he cast down upon the ground; and with five more the King of Righteousness sorely afflicted the king himself.
