**8.19.42**  Leaping down from that chariot whose horses were slain, your son, fallen into utmost distress, stood there upon the ground.

**8.19.43**  But seeing him fallen into peril, Karṇa, the son of Droṇa, Kṛpa, and the rest advanced together, seeking to rescue the lord of men.

**8.19.44**  Then all the sons of Pāṇḍu, encircling Yudhiṣṭhira, advanced in the battle, O king; and thereupon the combat raged on.

**8.19.45**  Then thousands of trumpets sounded in that great battle; war-cries and tumultuous shouts arose, O lord of the earth, as the Pāñcālas closed with the Kauravas in the fray.

**8.19.46**  Men met with men, elephants with noble elephants, chariots together with car-warriors, and horses with horsemen.

**8.19.47**  There were single combats, O great king, worth the beholding in that encounter, wondrous, beyond thought, full of weapons, and surpassing.

**8.19.48**  They fought with great impetuosity, each seeking the other's death; they slew one another in the battle, holding fast to the warrior's vow; for in no wise did they give battle from behind.

**8.19.49**  For but a moment that battle was fair to look upon; then, O king, it raged on frenzied, as though deranged, and past all bounds.

**8.19.50**  A car-warrior, closing with an elephant as he ranged at the head of the battle, sent it on to Death with smooth-jointed arrows.

**8.19.51**  And elephants, closing with horses, flinging many about, exceedingly fierce, drove them off here and there, time and again.

**8.19.52**  And having driven off many horses, the elephants, O king, swollen with might, struck some with their tusks and crushed others sorely.

**8.19.53**  And in the battle they gored with their tusks the horses together with their riders, and others, seizing them with force, those exceedingly strong beasts hurled aside.

**8.19.54**  The elephants, struck by the foot-soldiers in their vulnerable parts on every side, gave forth a dreadful cry of anguish and fled in the ten directions.

**8.19.55**  And as the foot-soldiers fled suddenly in that great battle, casting off their ornaments and leaping swiftly down upon the field of combat—

**8.19.56**  the great elephants, taking them for a mark, wheeled about and seized and rent the bright ornaments as well.

**8.19.57**  And other elephants were sorely gripped upon their chest-plates, their temporal bosses, and their tusk-sockets by pikes, iron lances, and spears.

**8.19.58**  Some, gripping their maces, were broken apart there by the cruel warriors who stood at their flanks—charioteers, horsemen, and the rest—and fell to the ground.

**8.19.59**  Other great elephants there crushed to the earth with their rush a rider together with his chariot, armored and bearing his banner.

**8.19.60**  The elephants, falling upon a chariot and seizing it by the yoke-pole, O sir, suddenly flung it about there in that ghastly, mighty battle.

**8.19.61**  And a great elephant, struck down by iron shafts, fell to the ground like the peak of a mountain shattered by the thunderbolt.

**8.19.62**  Warriors, closing with warriors, struck one another with their fists in the fight, and seizing each other by the hair, they cut and pierced one another together.

**8.19.63**  And another, raising his arms and casting his foe upon the ground, set his foot upon the breast of the still-quivering man and struck off his head.

**8.19.64**  Another, O great king, trampled a dead man with his feet, while yet another plunged his weapon into the body of one still living.

**8.19.65**  A great combat of fists arose among the warriors there, O Bhārata, and fierce seizing of hair, and bare wrestling with the arms alone.

**8.19.66**  While one was locked in struggle with another, a third, unnoticed, took his life in the battle with manifold weapons in many ways.

**8.19.67**  As the warriors clashed and the tumult raged on, headless trunks rose up by the hundreds and by the thousands.

**8.19.68**  Drenched in blood, the weapons and the coats of mail gleamed like garments dyed in a deep crimson hue.

**8.19.69**  Thus that great battle, dreadful and utterly confused, like a frenzied dancing-stage, filled the whole world with its uproar.

**8.19.70**  Neither friend nor foe could be told apart, O king, by the men tormented with arrows; the kings, greedy for victory, fought only because there was fighting to be done.

**8.19.71**  Their own men slew their own, O great king, and the foes they met as well; through the heroes of both armies all became confusion.

**8.19.72**  With chariots broken, O great king, and elephants brought down, with horses fallen there and men struck to the ground,

**8.19.73**  the earth became impassable, a mire of flesh and blood, and in a moment, O great king, it flowed with streams of gore.

**8.19.74**  Karṇa slew the Pāñcālas, and Dhanaṃjaya the Trigartas; Bhīmasena, O king, slew the Kurus and their whole host of elephants.

**8.19.75**  Thus this destruction came to pass between the armies of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas in the afternoon, O great king, as both longed for an ample victory.

**8.20.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Most piercing sorrows, hard to bear and many, have I heard from you, Saṃjaya—the destruction of my sons.

**8.20.2**  From the way you recount to me how the battle proceeds, my settled conviction, O bard, is this: the Kauravas are no more.

**8.20.3**  When Duryodhana was made chariotless there in that great battle, what did the son of Dharma do, and how did the king fare in that contest?

**8.20.4**  How did the hair-raising battle unfold in the afternoon? Tell me that truly, Saṃjaya, for you are skilled in the telling.

**8.20.5**  Saṃjaya said: As the armies clashed and fought, division against division, your son, O lord of peoples, mounted another chariot.

**8.20.6**  Seized with great wrath, like a venomous serpent, Duryodhana, beholding the Dharma-king Yudhiṣṭhira, said to his driver: "Go, O charioteer, go swiftly!" O Bhārata.

**8.20.7**  "Bring me quickly, O charioteer, to where the Pāṇḍava is—where the king, armored, shines beneath his held-aloft parasol."

**8.20.8**  That charioteer, urged on by the king, sent the king's splendid chariot straight toward Yudhiṣṭhira in the fray.

**8.20.9**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, enraged like a noble bull run wild, urged on his charioteer: "Go to where Suyodhana is!"

**8.20.10**  The two heroes met, those brothers, the foremost of charioteers; and meeting, the two of mighty valor, mailed and battle-frenzied, those great archers, hewed at one another with their arrows in the combat.

**8.20.11**  Then king Duryodhana, O sir, with a stone-whetted broad-headed shaft severed the bow of the righteous-natured one in the fight; but Yudhiṣṭhira, enraged, would not brook that bold stroke.

**8.20.12**  Casting aside the severed bow, his eyes red with rage, the son of Dharma took up another bow at the head of the host,

**8.20.13**  and cut off Duryodhana's banner and his bow as well; then Duryodhana, taking up another bow, pierced the Pāṇḍava in return.

**8.20.14**  The two, deeply roused against each other, loosed showers of arrows, like two lions wholly enraged, each longing to conquer the other.

**8.20.15**  They smote one another, bellowing like bulls, and those two great car-warriors ranged about, eyeing each other.

**8.20.16**  Then, well wounded by shafts loosed from bows drawn to their full length, the two shone, O great king, like a pair of kiṃśuka trees in blossom.

**8.20.17**  Then, O king, in that great battle, the terrifying lion-roars again and again, and the clapping of palms, and the twang of bows,

**8.20.18**  and the blaring of conches—all these those two foremost car-warriors made, and they pressed one another hard, O great king.

**8.20.19**  Then king Yudhiṣṭhira, enraged, with the speed of the thunderbolt and hard to withstand, struck your son in the breast with three arrows.

**8.20.20**  Your son swiftly pierced that lord of earth in return with five sharp, gold-feathered, stone-whetted arrows.

**8.20.21**  Then king Duryodhana hurled a lance, O Bhārata—all of steel, keen, and like a great blazing meteor.

**8.20.22**  As it sped down upon him, the Dharma-king at once cut it through with three stone-whetted shafts, and pierced him with seven.

**8.20.23**  Then that lance with its golden shaft, massive and heavy, fell down, and as it fell it shone like a great meteor, resembling fire itself.

**8.20.24**  Seeing his lance struck down, your son, O lord of peoples, smote Yudhiṣṭhira with nine sharp broad-headed shafts.

**8.20.25**  Sorely wounded, that foremost of the mighty, the scorcher of foes, quickly took up an arrow, aiming at Duryodhana,

**8.20.26**  and that mighty one fitted the fierce arrow to his bow; then the king, valorous and enraged, loosed it at the king.

**8.20.27**  That arrow, reaching your son, the great car-warrior, stunned the king into a swoon, and then passed on into the earth.

**8.20.28**  Then Duryodhana, enraged, raising his mace and rushing on with speed, eager to make an end of the quarrel, charged at the Pāṇḍava.

**8.20.29**  Marking him with mace upraised, like Death with rod in hand, the Dharma-king hurled at your son a great lance, blazing, of mighty speed, like a flaming meteor.

**8.20.30**  Standing in his chariot, pierced by it as it broke through his armor in that great battle, his heart utterly shaken, he both fell and swooned.

**8.20.31**  Then Kṛtavarman, coming swiftly, came to the aid of your son, the king, who was sunk indeed in an ocean of distress.

**8.20.32**  Bhīma too, seizing his great gold-adorned mace, rushed with speed upon Kṛtavarman in the battle. Thus did that fight of your men with the foe come to pass.

**8.21.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, with Karṇa at their head, your men, frenzied for battle, turned back once more and waged a combat like that of gods and demons.

**8.21.2**  Thrilled by the sounds of elephants, chariots, men, horses, and conches, and by the manifold falling of weapons, the throngs of elephants, chariots, and foot-soldiers, rushing face to face, fell upon one another and struck.

**8.21.3**  By arrows, axes, fine swords, and pikes, and by shafts of many kinds, the elephants, chariots, and horses were laid low in that great battle by the best of men, and men were laid low by the war-beasts.

**8.21.4**  With the heads of men—like lotuses, like the sun, like the moon, with white teeth and fair faces, eyes, and noses, and with gleaming diadems and earrings—the earth lay strewn and shone.

**8.21.5**  Smitten by iron clubs, pestles, lances, and javelins, by claw-knives, slings, and hundreds of maces, the elephants, men, and horses by the thousands then became the streaming currents of rivers of blood.

**8.21.6**  With its men, chariots, horses, and elephants struck down, dreadful to behold and terrible, that host, slain by the foe, looked then like the realm of the Lord of the ancestors at the destruction of all creatures.

**8.21.7**  Then your soldiers, O lord of men, and your sons, like the sons of the gods, with hosts of measureless might before them, those bulls of the Kurus, advanced in battle against the son of Śini.

**8.21.8**  Most splendid and terrible it appeared, that host thronged with the best of men, with horses, chariots, and elephants, roaring like the upheaved waters of the salt sea, resembling the armies of gods and demons.

**8.21.9**  Then the son of the Sun, in valor the equal of the lord of the gods, struck in the fight the hero of the Śinis—who was like the younger brother of the chief of the immortals—with shafts bright as the rays of the day-maker.

**8.21.10**  Him too—that best of men, together with his chariot, horses, and charioteer—the bull of the Śinis, hastening, then covered over in the fight with arrows of various kinds, gleaming like the venom of serpents.

**8.21.11**  Vasuṣeṇa, hard pressed by the arrows of the bull of the Śinis—to him, that bull among car-warriors, your friends, mighty car-warriors, swiftly hastened with elephants, chariots, horses, and foot-soldiers.

**8.21.12**  Upon that host, vast as the ocean, assailed by the foes that rushed on still more swiftly, the mighty comrade of Drupada's son charged, and wrought then a great destruction of men, chariots, horses, and elephants.

**8.21.13**  Then those two best of men, their daily rites performed, having duly worshipped Lord Bhava, resolved upon the slaying of the foe, Arjuna and Keśava swiftly bore down upon your host.

**8.21.14**  That chariot, rumbling like a rain-cloud, its banner and standard shaken by the wind, drawn by white horses, approaching near—the foes beheld it then, their senses stolen away.

**8.21.15**  Then twanging the Gāṇḍīva, dancing as it were upon the field, Arjuna filled the sky, the quarters, and the intermediate quarters with a dense press of arrows.

**8.21.16**  Chariots like celestial cars, equipped with their gear, weapons, and banners, together with their charioteers, he then struck down with his arrows as the wind scatters the clouds.

**8.21.17**  Elephants and their drivers, with their pennons, weapons, and banners, and horsemen and horses and foot-soldiers, he sent with his arrows to the realm of Yama.

**8.21.18**  Against him, like enraged Death himself, irresistible, that great car-warrior, Duryodhana advanced alone, smiting with arrows of various kinds.

**8.21.19**  Arjuna, slaying his bow, his charioteer, his standard, and his horses—each with seven shafts—cut through his parasol with a feathered arrow.

**8.21.20**  And taking up a ninth, a piercing one, he loosed that excellent shaft at Duryodhana; but the son of Droṇa cut it into seven pieces.

**8.21.21**  Then, cutting the bow of Droṇa's son and slaying his fine horses with arrows, the Pāṇḍava likewise severed the most terrible bow of Kṛpa as well.

**8.21.22**  Cutting the bow of Hārdikya, he slew likewise his banner and his horse; and severing the excellent shaft of Duḥśāsana, he advanced upon Rādheya.

**8.21.23**  Then, leaving off Sātyaki, Karṇa, hastening, pierced Arjuna with three arrows, struck Kṛṣṇa with twenty, and the son of Pṛthā again with three.

**8.21.24**  Then Sātyaki, coming up, pierced Karṇa with sharp arrows—with ninety-nine fierce ones—and tormented him again with a hundred.

**8.21.25**  Then all the foremost heroes of the Pāṇḍavas pressed Karṇa hard—Yudhāmanyu and Śikhaṇḍin and the sons of Draupadī and the Prabhadrakas.

**8.21.26**  Uttamaujas and Yuyutsu, the twins, and the grandson of Pṛṣata, and the host of the Cedis, Karūṣas, and Matsyas, and of the Kekayas, and the mighty Cekitāna, and the Dharma-king of noble vows—

**8.21.27**  These, with chariots, horses, and elephants and with foot-soldiers of fierce valor, surrounded Karṇa in the battle and showered him with manifold weapons, all uttering harsh words, all bent upon the slaying of Karṇa.

**8.21.28**  That rain of weapons Karṇa cut apart in many ways with his sharp arrows, and swept them all away, as the wind sweeps away trees after breaking them.

**8.21.29**  Car-warriors, elephants with their mahouts, and horses with their riders, and the swarms of arrows—all these Karṇa, enraged, was seen smiting down.

**8.21.30**  That host of the Pāṇḍavas, being slain by the blazing might of Karṇa's weapons, its bodies stripped of arms and wounded, well-nigh turned its face away in flight.

**8.21.31**  Then Arjuna, having himself beaten back Karṇa's missile with a counter-missile, blanketed the quarters, the sky, and the earth with showers of arrows.

**8.21.32**  Some of his arrows fell like maces, others like iron bludgeons, others again like śataghnīs, and still others like fierce thunderbolts.

**8.21.33**  Slaughtered by those shafts, that host with its foot-soldiers, horses, chariots, and elephants reeled about in every direction, its eyes shut tight in utter distress.

**8.21.34**  Horses, men, and elephants then met an utterly merciless battle; and others, being struck down by the arrows, fled in terror.

**8.21.35**  While they were thus locked together in battle, longing for victory, the sun reached the western mountain and went to its setting.

**8.21.36**  Then, great king, by reason of the darkness and especially of the dust, we could make out nothing at all, whether fair or foul.

**8.21.37**  Those mighty bowmen, O Bhārata, dreading a battle by night, then drew off in a body together with all their horses.

**8.21.38**  And when the Kauravas had withdrawn at the day's close, O king, the sons of Pṛthā, joyful at heart at having won the victory, went off to their own camp.

**8.21.39**  They went with the sounds of manifold instruments and with lion-roars and dancing, mocking their foes and extolling Acyuta and Arjuna.

**8.21.40**  When the withdrawal had been made by those heroes, all the soldiers, those lords of men, pronounced their blessings upon the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.21.41**  Then, the withdrawal accomplished, the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas, those lords of men, well pleased, went to their encampments in the night and took their rest.

**8.21.42**  And Yakṣas, Rākṣasas, and Piśācas, and beasts of prey in throngs, repaired to that dreadful battlefield, which resembled the dancing-ground of Rudra.

**8.22.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: It is plain that Arjuna did not slay us all only by his own choosing; for in battle not even Death himself, were he the aggressor, could escape him.

**8.22.2**  For Pṛthā's son alone carried off Bhadrā, alone he sated Agni, and alone, having conquered this earth, he made its kings bearers of tribute.

**8.22.3**  Alone, wielding his divine bow, he slew the Nivātakavacas; alone he fought against Śarva, who stood there in the guise of a Kirāta.

**8.22.4**  Alone he protected the Bharatas, alone he pleased Bhava. By him alone were all my men of fierce energy vanquished. They are not to be blamed but rather praised. Tell me what it was they did.

**8.22.5**  Saṃjaya said: Slain, smitten, and shattered, stripped of their armor, weapons, and mounts, their voices pitiful and their hearts seared, those proud warriors had been overcome by their foes.

**8.22.6**  Within their camp the Kauravas once more took counsel together, like serpents whose fangs are broken, whose venom is gone, and who lie trodden underfoot.

**8.22.7**  Then Karṇa, hissing like an enraged serpent, wringing one hand with the other and gazing at your son, spoke to them.

**8.22.8**  Arjuna is ever vigilant, steadfast, skilled, and resolute; and Adhokṣaja besides keeps rousing him whenever the moment has come.

**8.22.9**  By his sudden release of missiles we have today been outwitted; but tomorrow, O lord of earth, I shall undo his every resolve.

**8.22.10**  Addressed thus, the king said "So be it," and gave the foremost of princes leave to depart. Having passed the night in comfort, they set forth gladly for battle.

**8.22.11**  They beheld the unassailable array drawn up with care by the Dharma King, that chief of the Kurus, after the doctrine of Bṛhaspati and Uśanas.

**8.22.12**  Then Duryodhana called to mind Karṇa, the bull-shouldered, the constant adversary of those who had mastered themselves.

**8.22.13**  The king's mind turned to Karṇa, the equal of Purandara in battle, of the host of the Maruts in strength, and of Kārtavīrya in valor — the charioteer's son, that mighty bowman, like a kinsman in times of peril.

**8.22.14**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Since your minds then turned to Karṇa Vaikartana, O dull-witted ones, did you look to him as men tormented by cold look to the sun?

**8.22.15**  When the withdrawal of the armies was over and battle was joined once more, how did Karṇa Vaikartana fight there, Saṃjaya, and how did all the Pāṇḍavas fight against the charioteer's son?

**8.22.16**  For the mighty-armed Karṇa alone could slay the sons of Pṛthā together with the Somakas. The strength of Karṇa's arms is held equal to that of Śakra and Viṣṇu, and so too are the most terrible weapons and the prowess of that great-souled one.

**8.22.17**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing then Duryodhana sorely afflicted by the Pāṇḍava, and seeing too the sons of Pāṇḍu prevailing in that great battle —

**8.22.18**  — relying upon Karṇa in the fray, pride rose again in Duryodhana, and he dared to think he could conquer the sons of Pṛthā along with their sons and with Keśava.

**8.22.19**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Alas, what great sorrow, that the impetuous Karṇa could not overcome the sons of Pāṇḍu in battle! Surely fate is the supreme refuge. Alas, this dreadful consummation of the dicing now comes to pass.

**8.22.20**  Alas, the sharp griefs wrought by Duryodhana — most terrible, like barbs in the flesh — these, Saṃjaya, I must endure.

**8.22.21**  And the son of Subala too, my son, he reckons a man of sound policy.

**8.22.22**  While battles fit for the gods were going forward, Saṃjaya, I have heard ever and again of my sons being slain and overcome.

**8.22.23**  In battle there is none to hold the Pāṇḍavas back; they plunge into our ranks as into a throng of women, for fate is the stronger force.

**8.22.24**  Saṃjaya said: He who broods afterward upon a deed already past — that deed of his is not undone thereby, and he himself is ruined by his grieving.

**8.22.25**  This matter of yours has gone far indeed, for though you knew, you did not do beforehand what should have been done in weighing the possible against the impossible.

**8.22.26**  Many times, O king, you were told by the Pāṇḍavas, "Do not make war"; yet out of folly toward the Pāṇḍavas, O lord of peoples, you would not heed it.

**8.22.27**  Dreadful wrongs were committed by you against the sons of Pāṇḍu; and because of you this fearful destruction of the kings' folk comes to pass.

**8.22.28**  But now let that be past; grieve not, O bull of the Bhāratas. Hear all the dreadful carnage just as it befell, O steadfast one.

**8.22.29**  When night had brightened into dawn, Karṇa went to the king; and the mighty-armed one, drawing near, addressed Duryodhana.

**8.22.30**  Today, O king, I shall meet the glorious Pāṇḍava, and I shall slay that hero — or he shall slay me.

**8.22.31**  Owing to the multitude of my tasks and of Pṛthā's son's likewise, O king, no encounter has yet come about between me and Arjuna.

**8.22.32**  But hear this word of mine, spoken to the best of my judgment, O lord of peoples: I shall not return from the field, O Bhārata, without having slain Pṛthā's son in battle.

**8.22.33**  When the chief warriors of this host have fallen, and I alone stand fast in the fight, Pṛthā's son will come against me, who shall then be deprived of Śakra's dart.

**8.22.34**  Now learn, O ruler of men, what is most to your advantage, and the power of Arjuna's weapons and his equipment.

**8.22.35**  In cleaving a mighty frame, in nimbleness, in striking at a distance, in deftness, and in the wielding of missiles, Savyasācin is not my equal.

**8.22.36**  There is that bow named Vijaya, surpassing all weapons, fashioned by Viśvakarman with great care for Indra's sake.

**8.22.37**  With it, O king, the Lord of a Hundred Sacrifices conquered the hosts of the Daityas; at its twang the ten quarters grew confused before the Daityas. That most prized bow Śakra bestowed upon the Bhārgava.

**8.22.38**  That celestial, peerless bow the Bhārgava gave to me — with it I shall fight the mighty-armed Arjuna, foremost of the victorious, as Indra in battle fought all the assembled sons of Diti.

**8.22.39**  This dreadful bow given by Rāma surpasses the Gāṇḍīva; thrice seven times was the earth conquered with that bow.

**8.22.40**  The bow whose celestial deeds the Bhārgava told of — that bow Rāma indeed gave to me, and with it I shall fight the Pāṇḍava.

**8.22.41**  This day, Duryodhana, I shall gladden you and your kinsmen by slaying in battle the hero Arjuna, foremost of the victorious.

**8.22.42**  With its mountains, forests, and islands, with its seas, the earth, its foes slain, shall this day become an abode for your sons and grandsons, O king.

**8.22.43**  Nothing is unattainable for me this day, above all for your dear sake — even as success belongs to the self-possessed man who cleaves rightly to dharma.

**8.22.44**  For he can no more withstand me in battle than a tree can withstand fire. Yet I must needs declare wherein I fall short of Phalguna.

**8.22.45**  Divine is the string of his bow, and inexhaustible his two great quivers; his celestial bow, the matchless Gāṇḍīva, is undecaying in battle.

**8.22.46**  Yet I too have the great and celestial Vijaya, a most excellent bow; therein, by that bow, I surpass Pṛthā's son, O king.

**8.22.47**  But learn from me wherein the Pāṇḍava hero is greater than I: the holder of his reins is the Dāśārha, honored by all the worlds.

**8.22.48**  And his is the celestial chariot given by Agni, adorned with gold, indestructible on every side, O hero, and his steeds are swift as thought; and his banner is divine and shining — the wondrous ape.

**8.22.49**  And Kṛṣṇa, the Maker of the world, guards that chariot. Wanting in these things, I yet wish to fight the Pāṇḍava.

**8.22.50**  But here is a hero his match, Śalya, the ornament of the assembly: if he would act as my charioteer, your victory would be assured.

**8.22.51**  Let Śalya, hard for foes to overcome, become my charioteer; and let wagons carry my iron shafts fletched with vulture feathers.

**8.22.52**  And let chief chariots, O lord of kings, yoked with the finest horses, follow ever close behind me, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**8.22.53**  Thus shall I surpass Pṛthā's son in advantages; for Śalya excels Kṛṣṇa, even as I excel Arjuna.

**8.22.54**  As the Dāśārha, slayer of hostile heroes, knows the heart of horses, so too does the great chariot-warrior Śalya understand them.

**8.22.55**  In strength of arm none is the equal of the king of the Madras; and in weapons no bowman is the equal of me.

**8.22.56**  And in the driving of horses none here is the equal of Śalya. Thus this chariot of mine shall be superior to Pṛthā's son's.

**8.22.57**  This I wish done by you, O great king, scorcher of foes; when it is so done, my every desire will be fulfilled.

**8.22.58**  Then you shall see in battle what I will do, O Bhārata: by every means I shall this day conquer all the Pāṇḍavas assembled against me.

**8.22.59**  Duryodhana said: All this I shall do as you deem fit, Karṇa. Chariots with their quivers and horses shall follow after you, O son of the charioteer.

**8.22.60**  And let wagons carry your iron shafts fletched with vulture feathers; and we and all the kings, Karṇa, will follow after you.

**8.22.61**  Saṃjaya said: Having spoken thus, O great king, your majestic son the king drew near to the king of the Madras and addressed to him these words.

**8.23.1**  Saṃjaya said: Your son, O great king, approaching the king of the Madras with humility, spoke to him this word out of affection.

**8.23.2**  O you whose vow is truth, O greatly fortunate one, increaser of the woes of your foes, O lord of the Madras, hero in battle, terror of the enemy host —

**8.23.3**  — you have heard Karṇa, O best of speakers, as he spoke, how in the midst of these lions among kings he chooses you.

**8.23.4**  Therefore, for the destruction of Pṛthā's son and indeed for my own good, O best of chariot-warriors, you should graciously consent to act as his charioteer.

**8.23.5**  In all the world there is no holder of his reins to equal you. Guard Karṇa, then, on every side, as Brahmā guards Śaṃkara.

**8.23.6**  As Kṛṣṇa, the excellent holder of the reins, is the companion of Pṛthā's son, so do you too guard Rādheya on every side.

**8.23.7**  Bhīṣma, Droṇa, Kṛpa, Karṇa, yourself, the valiant Bhoja, Śakuni the son of Subala, the son of Droṇa, and I myself — these are our strength; and among these alone, O lord of the host, was the army apportioned in nine parts.

**8.23.8**  No share of this remained for Bhīṣma or for great-souled Droṇa; surpassing the share allotted to each, those two slew my enemies.

**8.23.9**  For those two tigers among men were aged, and they were brought down by guile; yet having wrought deeds hard to perform, they passed from here to heaven, O sinless one.

**8.23.10**  So too other tigers among men were struck down in battle by the foe, and many of our own went forth to heaven in the fight, yielding up their lives and performing, to the utmost of their strength, deeds rich and abundant.

**8.23.11**  Karṇa alone, the mighty-armed, is devoted to our welfare and delight, and you too, O tiger among men, are a great chariot-warrior of all the worlds; in him my hope of victory is vast, O lord of the Madra folk.

**8.23.12**  As Kṛṣṇa is the supreme holder of the reins for Pārtha in battle, joined with whom and guarded by whom in the fight, O king, Pārtha performs the deeds that lie plain before your very eyes—

**8.23.13**  never before in battle did Arjuna slay enemies thus; yet day after day, O lord of the Madras, he is seen routing them in the fray.

**8.23.14**  There remains a share for Karṇa and for you, O greatly radiant one; that share, together with Karṇa, destroy at one stroke in the battle.

**8.23.15**  As at the sight of the sun and his red dawn the darkness perishes, O honored one, so let the sons of Kuntī perish with the Pāñcālas and the Sṛñjayas.

**8.23.16**  Karṇa is the foremost of chariot-warriors, and you are the foremost of charioteers; in all the world there is no union of two equal to yours.

**8.23.17**  As in every circumstance the Vārṣṇeya guards the Pāṇḍava, so let you wholly protect Karṇa Vaikartana in the battle.

**8.23.18**  With you as his charioteer he will become unassailable in war even by the gods led by Śakra, O lord of earth—how much more so by the sons of Pāṇḍu! Do not doubt my word overmuch.

**8.23.19**  Hearing Duryodhana's words, Śalya, filled with wrath, drew his brow into a triple-furrowed frown, shaking his hands again and again.

**8.23.20**  Rolling his great eyes red with anger, the mighty-armed Śalya, proud in the strength of lineage, sovereignty, and sacred learning, spoke these words:

**8.23.21**  You hold me in contempt, son of Gāndhārī, and surely you mistrust me, in that you tell me so freely, 'Let the charioteering be done.'

**8.23.22**  Deeming Karṇa greater than myself, you sing his praises; but I do not reckon Rādheya in battle as the equal of myself.

**8.23.23**  Let a greater share be assigned to me, O lord of earth; having slain that share in battle, I shall depart as I came.

**8.23.24**  Or else I shall fight, I alone, O delight of the Kurus; behold today my valor in battle as I consume the foe.

**8.23.25**  A man of my kind, O Kaurava, would not act after harboring such designs in his heart; do not, do not doubt me overmuch.

**8.23.26**  And in battle no slight whatsoever should be offered me; behold these two arms of mine, firm-knit as the thunderbolt itself.

**8.23.27**  Behold my wondrous bow, and my arrows like venomous serpents; behold my chariot well-arrayed with noble steeds swift as the wind, and behold, son of Gāndhārī, my mace adorned with bands of gold.

**8.23.28**  In wrath I could cleave the earth, scatter the mountains, and dry up the oceans by my own fiery might, O king.

**8.23.29**  Knowing me, then, to be of such a kind and able to subdue the foe, why do you yoke me to the charioteering of one lesser, the son of a charioteer, O king?

**8.23.30**  Truly you ought not, O lord of kings, to harness me thus in service; for being the better, I cannot bear to do the bidding of a worse man.

**8.23.31**  He who places under the sway of a lesser man one who is greater, who out of affection has come and submitted to his power—that is a sin that turns the higher to the lower.

**8.23.32**  The brāhmaṇas were created by Brahmā from his mouth, the kṣatra from his breast; from his thighs he brought forth the vaiśyas, and the śūdras from his feet—so says the śruti; and from these arise the distinct mixed castes, born against and along the grain of the orders.

**8.23.33**  And from the union of these four orders one with another, O Bhārata, the kṣatriyas are declared to be protectors, gatherers, and givers.

**8.23.34**  The brāhmaṇas, by officiating at sacrifices, by teaching, and by accepting pure gifts, were established by Brahmā upon the earth for the welfare of the world.

**8.23.35**  Tillage, the herding of cattle, and giving in every form are the duties of the vaiśyas; and the śūdras are ordained as the servants of brāhmaṇa, kṣatriya, and vaiśya.

**8.23.36**  The sūtas are ordained as servants of the brāhmaṇa and the kṣatriya, and not of the vaiśya or the śūdra; in this very matter hear my word, O sinless one.

**8.23.37**  I, being a consecrated king, sprung from a line of royal sages, am proclaimed a great chariot-warrior, one to be served and to be hymned by the bards.

**8.23.38**  Being such a one, O crusher of hostile clans, I cannot bring myself to do charioteering for the son of a charioteer in battle.

**8.23.39**  Having met with this insult, I will not fight at all; taking my leave of you this day, son of Gāndhārī, I shall go as I came.

**8.23.40**  Having spoken thus, Śalya, that tiger among men, the ornament of assemblies, rose and swiftly departed from the midst of the kings, full of indignation.

**8.23.41**  But your son, restraining him out of love and high regard, spoke sweet and conciliatory words that accomplish every purpose:

**8.23.42**  As you have said this, O Śalya, so it is without doubt; yet I have a certain intention—mark it, O lord of men.

**8.23.43**  Karṇa is not greater than you; I have no doubt of it in the least, nor would the king of the Madras do anything that would prove untrue.

**8.23.44**  For your forefathers, those best of men, bore aloft the truth alone; and therefore you are called Ārtāyani—such is my conviction.

**8.23.45**  And because on earth you are as a spear-point to your enemies, O bestower of honor, therefore your name is told as Śalya, O lord of earth.

**8.23.46**  What you have already declared, O giver of abundant largesse—do that very thing, O knower of dharma, for my sake, whatever you are asked.

**8.23.47**  Neither Rādheya nor I, valiant though we be, is greater than you; we choose you as the driver of his noble steeds in the encounter.

**8.23.48**  For just as this world holds Karṇa to surpass Dhanaṃjaya in qualities, so too it holds you, dear sir, to surpass even Vāsudeva.

**8.23.49**  Karṇa indeed surpasses Pārtha in weapons, O bull among men, and you too surpass Kṛṣṇa in the driving of horses and in strength alike.

**8.23.50**  As the great-minded Vāsudeva knows the very heart of horses, so do you know it twofold, O king of the Madras; there is no doubt of this.

**8.23.51**  Śalya said: In that you call me, O son of Gāndhārī, in the midst of the host, O Kaurava, superior to the son of Devakī, I am well pleased with you.

**8.23.52**  So be it: I will undertake the charioteering of the renowned Rādheya as he fights with the foremost of the Pāṇḍavas, even as you wish, O hero.

**8.23.53**  Yet I have one condition, O hero, with regard to Vaikartana: that I may utter freely in his presence whatever words I please.

**8.23.54**  Saṃjaya said: 'So be it,' O king, said your son together with Karṇa, O Bhārata, to the son of the Madra king, O best of the Bhāratas.

**8.24.1**  Duryodhana said: Yet further, O lord of the Madras, hear what I shall tell you—how this came to pass of old in the war between the gods and the asuras, O mighty one.

**8.24.2**  What Mārkaṇḍeya, the great sage, told to my father and to me—as I relate it in full, O best of royal sages, do you mark it, and let there be no questioning of it in your mind.

**8.24.3**  There was a great clash of the gods and the asuras, O king; the first battle, the one centered on Tāraka, took place, and then the daityas were vanquished by the gods—so we have heard.

**8.24.4**  And when the daityas had been vanquished, there were three sons of Tāraka—Tārākṣa, Kamalākṣa, and Vidyunmālin, O king.

**8.24.5**  Undertaking fierce austerity and abiding in the highest self-restraint, they wasted their own bodies through penance, O tormentor of foes.

**8.24.6**  By their subjugation of the senses, their austerity, and their self-restraint, O king, the Grandsire, well pleased, the bestower of boons, granted them boons.

**8.24.7**  And together they begged of the Grandsire of all the worlds, O king, this boon: invulnerability among all beings for all time.

**8.24.8**  Then the god, the lord and master of the worlds, said to them: 'There is no such thing as complete immortality; turn back from this, O asuras, and choose some other boon, whatever pleases you well.'

**8.24.9**  Then, O king, the three together, having deliberated again and again at length, bowed before the lord of all the worlds and spoke this word:

**8.24.10**  Grant us this boon, O god, O Grandsire: that we, dwelling in three cities, may roam over this earth and through this world, set forward by your grace.

**8.24.11**  Then, after a thousand years, we shall come together one with another, and these cities shall pass into a single union, O sinless one.

**8.24.12**  And whoever then, O blessed one, should destroy these cities once they have come together, with a single arrow—that best of gods shall be our death.' 'So be it,' the god replied to them, and entered heaven.

**8.24.13**  Having gained their boon and rejoicing, they took counsel together and, for the fashioning of the three cities, chose Maya, the great asura, the all-maker, the ageless one, honored by daityas and dānavas.

**8.24.14**  Then the wise Maya, by his own austerity, made three cities: one of gold, one of silver, and one of black iron.

**8.24.15**  The golden one stood there in heaven, the silver in mid-air, and the iron one upon the earth, set as upon a wheel, O lord of earth.

**8.24.16**  Each one measured a hundred yojanas in breadth and in length, furnished with houses and towers and lofty watch-platforms, and great ramparts and gateways.

**8.24.17**  Thronged with the increase of every excellence, yet uncrowded and free of all ill, each was adorned with palaces of many kinds and with portals as well.

**8.24.18**  And in the cities there were kings, O king, each apart: the wondrous golden one belonged to great-souled Tārākṣa, the silver to Kamalākṣa, and the iron to Vidyunmālin.

**8.24.19**  Those three daitya-kings, overrunning the three worlds swiftly by their fiery might, stood firm for whole hosts of years, as a Prajāpati.

**8.24.20**  Of those foremost dānavas there gathered from here and there millions and hundreds of millions, yea crores of warriors without rival, who, longing for great dominion, took refuge in the unassailable Tripura.

**8.24.21**  And Maya, the provider of every need, was the support of them all; relying upon him, they all dwelt without fear from any quarter.

**8.24.22**  Whatever desire any dweller of Tripura conceived in his mind, that very desire Maya then provided for him by his magic art.

**8.24.23**  And there was a son of Tārākṣa, Hari by name, of great strength, who performed the highest austerity, by which the Grandsire was well pleased.

**8.24.24**  He begged of the well-pleased god: 'Let there be in our city a well into which the slain, struck down by weapons, may be cast and rise up mightier than before.'

**8.24.25**  And having gained the boon, the hero Hari, son of Tārākṣa, fashioned there that well, the giver of life to the dead, O lord.

**8.24.26**  In whatever form and in whatever guise a daitya had died, cast into that well he was reborn in that very same form.

**8.24.27**  Having won that well, the dwellers of Tripura oppressed all the worlds; perfected by great austerity and multipliers of the gods' terror, they suffered no loss whatever in battle, O king.

**8.24.28**  Then, overcome by greed and delusion and bereft of sense, all of them, grown shameless, transgressed the established order that had been ordained.

**8.24.29**  Routing the gods together with their hosts here and there, time and again, they roamed at their own pleasure, made arrogant by the granting of the boon.

**8.24.30**  All the sacred groves of the gods and the haunts dear to the dwellers of heaven, the holy hermitages of the sages, the sacrificial posts, and the settled lands—these the dānavas, evil in their conduct, destroyed, transgressing every bound.

**8.24.31**  All those gods together, O subduer of foes, went up to the Grandsire to tell of the outrage wrought by the foes of the gods.

**8.24.32**  Having related the whole truth and bowed their heads in reverence, they asked the blessed Grandsire for a means to slay them.

**8.24.33**  Hearing that, the blessed god spoke this to the gods: 'Those evil-souled asuras, those haters of the wise, who continually offend against you and afflict you—'

**8.24.34**  "I am indeed impartial to all beings—of this there is no doubt; yet the unrighteous must be slain: thus do I declare to you.

**8.24.35**  Choose, then, O Ādityas, as your warrior Sthāṇu, the Lord Īśāna, the ever-victorious, who acts without weariness; he shall slay those foes of the gods."

**8.24.36**  Hearing this his word, the gods, with Śakra at their head, placed Brahmā before them and went for refuge to him whose emblem is the bull.

**8.24.37**  They undertook the highest austerity, chanting the eternal Brahman; and those knowers of dharma, together with the sages, drew near to Bhava with their whole being.

**8.24.38**  With meaningful words they praised him who, in terrors, grants safety best of all—the All-Self, the great-souled one, by whom the whole was pervaded through his own being,

**8.24.39**  who by many special austerities knows the yoga of the self, who knows the Sāṃkhya of the self, and whose self is ever under his own command.

**8.24.40**  They beheld that Īśāna, a mass of splendor, the lord of Umā, who in the world is like none other, steadfast in vows, untouched by stain.

**8.24.41**  And they perceived the one Blessed Lord as manifold in form; in that great-souled one they saw forms that were reflections of their own selves, and they beheld one another there, all of them utterly amazed.

**8.24.42**  And the gods and the brahmin-sages bowed their heads to the earth before that Lord who is made of all beings, the unborn, the master of the world.

**8.24.43**  Honoring them with a word of blessing and raising them up, Śaṃkara the Blessed One, smiling, addressed them: "Speak, speak."

**8.24.44**  Granted leave by the Three-Eyed One, then, though their minds were still unsettled, they said to Bhava: "Homage, homage be to you, O all-pervading Lord!"

**8.24.45**  "Homage to the God beyond the gods, the bearer of the bow, the surpassingly wrathful; you who destroyed the sacrifice of Prajāpati, you are extolled by the Prajāpatis.

**8.24.46**  Homage to him who is praised, who is worthy of praise, who is being praised, to Death itself; to the deep-red Rudra, the blue-throated, the wielder of the trident.

**8.24.47**  To the unerring, the deer-eyed, the fighter with the choicest weapons; to the irresistible, the radiant, to Brahman, to the keeper of chastity.

**8.24.48**  To Īśāna, the immeasurable, the restrainer, clad in a hide; to him constant in austerity, the tawny-hued, the keeper of vows, robed in a skin.

**8.24.49**  To the father of Kumāra, the three-eyed, the bearer of the choicest weapons; to the destroyer of the suffering of those who take refuge, the slayer of the hosts of Brahman's haters.

**8.24.50**  Homage to the lord of the forest-trees, to the lord of men; and ever homage to the lord of cattle, to the lord of sacrifices.

**8.24.51**  Homage be to you with your hosts, to the Three-Eyed One of fierce splendor; O God, accept us who have taken refuge in you by thought, word, and deed."

**8.24.52**  Then the Blessed One, well pleased, greeted them with a welcome and said: "Let your fear depart; tell me, what shall I do for you?"

**8.24.53**  When the great-souled one had granted his boon to the hosts of fathers, gods, and sages, Brahmā, doing honor to Śaṃkara, spoke a word for the world's welfare:

**8.24.54**  "By your bestowal, O Lord of the gods, I hold this office of Prajāpati; and while presiding over it I have granted a great boon to the Dānavas.

**8.24.55**  None but you, O Lord of what is and what shall be, is able to destroy them, who have overstepped all bounds; for you alone are their match in slaying them.

**8.24.56**  Therefore, O God, to these dwellers in heaven who have taken refuge and now entreat you, show your grace, O Lord of the gods, and slay the Dānavas, O bearer of the trident."

**8.24.57**  The Blessed Lord said: "That all your foes must be slain—such is my own resolve; yet I alone am not equal to the slaying of those haters of the gods.

**8.24.58**  Do you, then, all united, by the power of my weapon conquer those enemies in battle; for union is a mighty strength."

**8.24.59**  The gods said: "Whatever is the measure of our splendor and strength, theirs is twice as much—so we judge, for we have seen their splendor and strength."

**8.24.60**  The Blessed One said: "Those evil ones who have wronged you must by all means be slain; with half my splendor and strength, smite all those foes."

**8.24.61**  The gods said: "We shall not be able to bear half of your splendor, O Maheśvara; rather, with half the strength of us all, do you yourself slay the foes."

**8.24.62**  Duryodhana said: Then the Lord of the gods, thus addressed by them, said "So be it," O best of kings; and taking half from them all, he grew yet more abundant in splendor.

**8.24.63**  And that god became in strength mightier than them all; from that time onward Śaṃkara has been renowned as Mahādeva, the Great God.

**8.24.64**  Then Mahādeva said: "Bearing bow and arrow, I shall slay in battle those foes from a chariot, O dwellers in heaven.

**8.24.65**  Do you, then, look to my chariot and likewise to my bow and arrow, while this day I lay them low upon the earth."

**8.24.66**  The gods said: "Taking the whole substance of the three worlds from every side, we shall fashion for you, O Lord of gods, a chariot of mighty might."

**8.24.67**  Thus, devised by their intelligence, wrought by Viśvakarman and auspicious, those tigers among the gods then fashioned that chariot.

**8.24.68**  For its frame they made the goddess Earth, garlanded with broad cities, bearing her mountains, forests, and islands, the upholder of beings.

**8.24.69**  Mount Mandara they made its axle, the great rivers its struts, and the quarters and intermediate quarters the encircling rim of the chariot.

**8.24.70**  The blazing planets they made its underbeams, and the stars its guard-rail; the threefold pole, joined to dharma, artha, and kāma, they made its binding, and the manifold herbs, with their varied burst of flowers and fruits, were set therein.

**8.24.71**  Making the sun and moon the two wheels of that best of chariots, they set there as its front and rear flanks the auspicious night and day.

**8.24.72**  The ten lords of serpents, headed by Dhṛtarāṣṭra, they made its sturdy shaft; the sky its yoke, and the world-dissolving clouds the yoke-straps.

**8.24.73**  Firmness, wisdom, steadiness, and humility they made its yoke-pins; and the vault of heaven, with its planets, lunar mansions, and stars, became the dappled covering.

**8.24.74**  The lords of the worlds—the masters of the gods, the waters, the spirits, and wealth—they made its horses; and Sinīvālī, Anumati, Kuhū, and the devout Rākā they made the harness-ropes of the steeds, and the cross-bars too, and the throat-strap.

**8.24.75**  Action, truth, austerity, and gain were set there as the reins; the mind became the standing-place, and Sarasvatī the surrounding road.

**8.24.76**  Banners of many hues and varied design, stirred by the wind, and the lightning bound to it as Indra's bow, made the blazing chariot blaze the more.

**8.24.77**  When that best of chariots had thus been fashioned, O great king, by the gods, O tiger among men, for the crushing of the foes,

**8.24.78**  Śaṃkara laid his own chief weapons upon the chariot, and set up the bull as its banner-staff, drawn out across the sky.

**8.24.79**  The rod of Brahman, the rod of Time, the rod of Rudra, and Fever stood ready as the outriders of that chariot, raised on every side.

**8.24.80**  The Atharvan and the Aṅgiras hymns became the wheel-guards of the great-souled one; the Ṛgveda, the Sāmaveda, and the Purāṇa went before him.

**8.24.81**  The lore of old and the Yajurveda became his rear-guards; and the divine speeches and the sacred sciences were made to attend at his flanks.

**8.24.82**  The goad and the rest, O lord of kings, and the cry of Vaṣaṭ likewise, and at its front the syllable Oṃ, O king, became a maker of surpassing splendor.

**8.24.83**  Making the year, dappled with its six seasons, into the bow, and the Night of Time, the doom of men, into the bow's unaging string—

**8.24.84**  And the arrow became Viṣṇu, blazing fire and Soma alike; for Agni and Soma are the whole world, and the world is called Viṣṇu's own.

**8.24.85**  And Viṣṇu is the very self of the Blessed Bhava of measureless splendor; therefore those foes could not endure the touch of Hara's bowstring.

**8.24.86**  Into that arrow the Lord, his wrath made keen, loosed an unbearable fire of anger, born of the fury of Bhṛgu and Aṅgiras, exceedingly hard to withstand.

**8.24.87**  Dark-blue and red, smoke-hued, clad in a hide, terrible to behold, like ten thousand suns, wrapped in flames of splendor, he blazed.

**8.24.88**  Hard to shake, the overthrower, the conqueror, the slayer of Brahman's haters is Hara; ever the protector and the destroyer of folk according as they cleave to dharma or to its opposite.

**8.24.89**  Surrounded by his tormenting hosts, dread of form, fierce and towering, the Blessed Sthāṇu shone forth, encompassed by those very qualities of his own self.

**8.24.90**  Resting upon his limbs stood this whole universe, moving and unmoving, O king, and it shone, a wondrous sight to see.

**8.24.91**  Beholding that divine chariot, mailed and bow in hand, he took up that divine arrow sprung from Soma, Viṣṇu, and Agni.

**8.24.92**  Then the gods fashioned for the all-pervading Lord the feathers of that arrow, O king, and the bearer of holy fragrance, the wind, O best of kings, to speed it.

**8.24.93**  Mounting it, Mahādeva, terrifying even the gods, then ascended, intent, as though making heaven and earth to tremble.

**8.24.94**  Resplendent, the boon-giver, sword in hand, with arrow and bow, the God said, as if smiling: "Who shall be my charioteer?"

**8.24.95**  The hosts of gods said to him: "Whomever you shall appoint, O Lord of gods, he shall be your charioteer—of this there is no doubt."

**8.24.96**  The God said to them again: "Whoever is greater than I—make him my charioteer; consider this yourselves, and do not delay."

**8.24.97**  Hearing this word spoken by the great-souled one, the gods then went to the God, the Grandsire, and propitiating him, spoke thus:

**8.24.98**  "O God, by you was this destruction of the gods' foes ordained, and so we have done it; and he whose banner is the bull is well pleased.

**8.24.99**  And the chariot, decked with wondrous weapons, has been arrayed by us; but we know not who should be the charioteer upon that best of chariots.

**8.24.100**  Therefore let some charioteer be appointed, O best of gods; you ought, O Lord, to make that word of ours bear fruit.

**8.24.101**  For of old, O Blessed One, you said to us, 'I shall do you good'; that you ought now to do.

**8.24.102**  That best of chariots of ours is yoked, O God, hard to oppose, the router of the foes; and there as warrior is stationed the bearer of the Pināka, who, rousing himself, strikes terror into the Dānavas.

**8.24.103**  Even so are the four Vedas his foremost steeds, and the earth with her mountains the chariot, O great-souled one; the train of the lunar mansions follows in the guard-rail—the warrior in which must be protected by a charioteer.

**8.24.104**  For that a charioteer must be sought, surpassing all these; upon him rest the chariot, O God, the horses and the warrior alike, the coats of mail, the weapons, and the bow, O Grandsire.

**8.24.105**  Save you, we see no other for the charioteer there; for you are endowed with all virtues and surpass the gods, O Lord. Mount swiftly to the charioteer's seat and curb these matchless steeds."

**8.24.106**  Thus, bowing their heads to the Grandsire, the lord of the three worlds, the gods entreated him for the office of charioteer—so we have heard.

**8.24.107**  Brahmā said: "In nothing of this is the word you have spoken false, O dwellers in heaven; lo, I shall curb the horses of the matted-haired one as he fights."

**8.24.108**  Then that Blessed God, the Grandsire, the maker of the worlds, was appointed by the gods as charioteer of the great-souled Īśāna.

**8.24.109**  As that one honored by the worlds swiftly mounted the car, those horses, swift as the wind, at once bowed down their heads.

**8.24.110**  And as Maheśvara ascended, they sank to the earth upon their knees.

**8.24.111**  Then the great-grandsire, lord of the three worlds, taking up the reins, urged on those horses, swift as mind and as the wind.

**8.24.112**  Then, when the boon-giver had mounted and set forth against the asuras, the Lord of all, smiling, said: "Well done, well done!"

**8.24.113**  "Go, O God, where the Daityas are; urge on the horses, unwearied. Behold this day the strength of my arms as I strike down the foes in battle."

**8.24.114**  Then, O king, he urged on those steeds, swift as the rushing wind, toward that Tripura which was guarded by the Daityas and Dānavas.

**8.24.115**  Then Śarva, having strung his bow and fitted that arrow to it, joined it with the Pāśupata weapon and fixed his thought upon Tripura.

**8.24.116**  While he stood thus, O king, wrathful and with bow upraised, those three cities at that very moment, through the working of Time, became one.

**8.24.117**  And when the three cities had thus merged into one and come together, a tumultuous joy arose among the high-souled gods.

**8.24.118**  Then all the hosts of gods, the Siddhas, and the supreme sages, filled with gladness and singing his praises, sent up the cry of "Victory!"

**8.24.119**  Then before that god — of form too terrible to describe and of unbearable splendor — who was about to slay the Asuras, Tripura came into view.

**8.24.120**  Drawing that divine bow, the blessed Lord of the worlds loosed against Tripura that arrow which held the essence of the three worlds; and having burned that city together with its hosts of Asuras, he hurled it into the western ocean.

**8.24.121**  Thus was Tripura burned, and the Dānavas too without remainder, by the wrathful Maheśvara, who sought the welfare of the three worlds.

**8.24.122**  And that fire, born of his own wrath, crying "Alas! Alas!", was checked; for the three-eyed god said to it, "Do not reduce the worlds to ashes."

**8.24.123**  Then the gods, the worlds, and the sages, restored to their natural state, praised Sthāṇu of incomparable might with words rich in meaning.

**8.24.124**  Dismissed by the blessed Lord, all the gods, their desires fulfilled, departed as they had come, with Prajāpati at their head and gladdened in heart.

**8.24.125**  Even as the blessed Brahmā, the Grandsire and sustainer of the worlds, once held the reins, so do you govern the steeds of this high-souled son of Rādhā.

**8.24.126**  For you, O tiger among kings, are superior to Kṛṣṇa, to Karṇa, and above all to Phalguna; in this there is no doubt.

**8.24.127**  For in battle this one is the equal of Rudra, and you, O sinless one, are the equal of Brahmā; therefore the two of you are able to vanquish my foes, as those two vanquished the Asuras.

**8.24.128**  O Śalya, contrive it swiftly today that this Karṇa may crush and slay the son of Kuntī, him of the white steeds and Kṛṣṇa as charioteer; for upon you rest Karṇa, the kingdom, and we ourselves.

**8.24.129**  And hear from me yet this other ancient tale besides, which a brāhmaṇa learned in dharma told in the presence of my father.

**8.24.130**  And having heard this wondrous speech, fraught with cause, effect, and meaning, do you act, O Śalya, with firm resolve; let there be no hesitation in this.

**8.24.131**  Born in the lineage of the Bhārgavas was Jamadagni, of great austerities; his son, famed as Rāma, was endowed with splendor and excellence.

**8.24.132**  Undertaking fierce austerities, serene of soul, self-disciplined, and with senses restrained, he won the favor of Bhava for the sake of celestial weapons.

**8.24.133**  Pleased with him for his devotion and his tranquility, and knowing what lay in his heart, Mahādeva — Śaṃkara — showed himself to him.

**8.24.134**  The Lord said: Rāma, I am pleased with you; blessings upon you. Known to me is your desire. Make yourself pure, and all this you shall obtain.

**8.24.135**  I shall give you those weapons when you have become pure; for the weapons consume, O Bhārgava, one who is unworthy and incapable.

**8.24.136**  Thus addressed by the trident-bearing God of gods, the son of Jamadagni, bowing his head, replied to the high-souled Lord.

**8.24.137**  When you know me, O Lord of the gods, to be worthy of bearing the weapons, then upon me, who serve you faithfully, it will be fitting that you bestow them.

**8.24.138**  Duryodhana said: Then, through austerity, self-mastery, and observance, with worship, offerings, and oblations attended by sacrificial fires and sacred chants,

**8.24.139**  he propitiated Śarva for many long years; and Mahādeva became gracious toward the high-souled Bhārgava.

**8.24.140**  Again and again he spoke of his virtues in the presence of the Goddess: "This Rāma, firm in his vows, is ever devoted to me."

**8.24.141**  Thus did the gracious Lord, the slayer of foes, recount his virtues many times in the presence of the gods and the ancestral fathers.

**8.24.142**  Now at that very time there were Daityas of mighty strength, and by them, blinded with pride and delusion, the dwellers of heaven were oppressed.

**8.24.143**  Then the gods, banded together and resolved to slay them, made every effort to destroy the foe, yet were unable to conquer them.

**8.24.144**  Then the gods, approaching Maheśvara and propitiating him with devotion, said to him, "Slay the hosts of our enemies."

**8.24.145**  Then the god, having promised the gods the destruction of their foes, summoned Rāma the Bhārgava, and Śaṃkara spoke to him.

**8.24.146**  O Bhārgava, slay all the gathered enemies of the gods, for the welfare of the worlds, and likewise for my own pleasure.

**8.24.147**  Rāma said: O Lord of the gods, O Maheśvara, what power have I, untrained in arms, to slay all these Dānavas, skilled in arms and frenzied in battle?

**8.24.148**  The Lord said: Go; by meditating upon me you shall slay the Dānavas, and having conquered all your foes you shall attain abundant excellences.

**8.24.149**  Duryodhana said: And having heard this speech and wholly accepted it, Rāma, when the rites of benediction had been performed, set forth against the Dānavas.

**8.24.150**  And the Bhārgava slew those enemies of the gods, filled with frenzy, pride, and strength, with blows whose touch was like the thunderbolt and the lightning.

**8.24.151**  Though his body was wounded by the Dānavas, that foremost of the twice-born, the son of Jamadagni, when touched by Sthāṇu became at once free of wounds.

**8.24.152**  And the blessed god, pleased with that deed of his, granted boons to the high-souled Bhārgava, knower of the Veda.

**8.24.153**  And by the trident-bearing God of gods, filled with affection, he was told: "The pain that arose in your body from the fall of the foes' weapons —

**8.24.154**  by that pain your human deed has been wiped away, O delight of the Bhṛgus. Receive now from me the celestial weapons, as you desire."

**8.24.155**  Then, having received all the weapons and the manifold boons his mind had longed for, Rāma bowed his head to Śiva.

**8.24.156**  And having obtained leave of the Lord of the gods, that great ascetic departed. Thus did the sage recount this event of olden time.

**8.24.157**  And the Bhārgava too, O tiger among men, with inmost soul well pleased, imparted the whole science of archery to the high-souled Karṇa.

**8.24.158**  For had there been any taint in Karṇa, O king, the delight of the Bhṛgus would never have given him the celestial weapons.

**8.24.159**  Nor do I in any wise believe Karṇa to be born in the lineage of a charioteer; I deem him the son of a god, sprung from the race of the Kṣatriyas.

**8.24.160**  How should a doe bring forth a tiger — this long-armed great warrior, like the sun, with his earrings and his armor?

**8.24.161**  Behold his arms, brawny and like the trunks of the king of elephants; behold his broad chest, the crusher of all foes.

**8.25.1**  Duryodhana said: Thus did that blessed god, the Grandsire of all the worlds, perform the office of charioteer there where Rudra rode upon the car.

**8.25.2**  A chariot's charioteer should be a hero greater than the warrior himself; therefore, O tiger among men, do you govern the steeds in battle.

**8.25.3**  Saṃjaya said: Then Śalya, lord of the Madras, slayer of foes, embracing your son Duryodhana and well pleased, spoke these words.

**8.25.4**  If, O king, you so judge it, O son of Gāndhārī, fair to behold, then whatever is dear to you, all that I shall do.

**8.25.5**  O best of the Bhāratas, in whatsoever task I am at any time fit, there, devoting myself wholly to it, I shall take up the burden of your work.

**8.25.6**  But whatever I, seeking his good, may say to Karṇa, be it pleasant or unpleasant — let your honor and Karṇa pardon me all of it utterly.

**8.25.7**  Karṇa said: As Brahmā is ever devoted to the good of Īśāna, and Keśava to the good of Pārtha, so, O king of the Madras, be devoted always to ours.

**8.25.8**  Śalya said: Self-reproach and self-praise, the censure of others and the flattery of others — this fourfold conduct is not practiced by the noble.

**8.25.9**  Yet what I shall now declare, O learned one, for the sake of your assurance — though it be joined with praise of myself — hear it truly as it is.

**8.25.10**  I am fit to drive the chariot of Śakra, even as Mātali, O lord, through unfailing skill and through my knowledge, learning, and remedies.

**8.25.11**  Therefore, O sinless one, while you fight with Pārtha in the battle, I shall drive your steeds; be free of fever, O son of the charioteer.

**8.26.1**  Duryodhana said: This king of the Madras, O Karṇa, will be your charioteer, a driver greater than Kṛṣṇa, even as Mātali is to the lord of the gods.

**8.26.2**  As Mātali guides the chariot yoked with tawny steeds, so today shall this Śalya be the controller of your chariot-horses.

**8.26.3**  With you the warrior upon the car and the king of the Madras as charioteer, surely in battle Pārtha, foremost of car-warriors, shall not prevail.

**8.26.4**  Saṃjaya said: Then, O king, Duryodhana spoke once more to the impetuous king of the Madras, who was to govern the noble steeds in battle.

**8.26.5**  "Guarded by you, the son of Rādhā shall conquer Dhanaṃjaya." Thus addressed, O Bhārata, Śalya mounted the chariot and said, "So be it."

**8.26.6**  When Śalya had consented, Karṇa, glad of heart, said to his charioteer again and again, hastening him: "Charioteer, make ready my car for me."

**8.26.7**  Then, having duly made ready that victorious and excellent chariot, like a city of the Gandharvas, he presented it to his master, saying, "Victory!"

**8.26.8**  That chariot, which the household priest, knower of the Veda, had earlier consecrated, Karṇa, foremost of car-warriors, worshipped according to rule.

**8.26.9**  Reverently circling it and worshipping the Sun, he had the king of the Madras, who stood beside him, mount up in front.

**8.26.10**  Then the mighty-splendored Śalya ascended Karṇa's great and unassailable chariot, foremost of cars, as a lion ascends a mountain.

**8.26.11**  Then, O king, Karṇa mounted his own excellent chariot, on which Śalya was already stationed, as the sun mounts a lightning-bearing cloud.

**8.26.12**  Mounted upon one chariot, those two, in splendor like the sun and fire, shone forth as the sun and fire together blaze upon a cloud in the sky.

**8.26.13**  Those two heroes, hymned in praise, shone then with surpassing radiance, like Indra and Agni invoked by priest and assembled celebrants at a sacrifice.

**8.26.14**  There stood Karṇa upon the chariot whose steeds Śalya held, twanging his dreadful bow, like the sun girt round with a halo.

**8.26.15**  Stationed upon that foremost of chariots, Karṇa, that tiger among men, his arrows for his rays, shone like the radiant sun resting upon Mount Mandara.

**8.26.16**  To that great hero of measureless splendor, the son of Rādhā, as he stood upon the car and went forth, Duryodhana spoke these words.

**8.26.17**  That hard deed in battle which Droṇa and Bhīṣma left undone, accomplish it, O hero, son of Adhiratha, before the eyes of all the bowmen.

**8.26.18**  For it was my settled hope that the two great car-warriors Bhīṣma and Droṇa would surely slay Arjuna and Bhīmasena.

**8.26.19**  That heroic deed which the two of them left undone in the great battle, do you accomplish it, O son of Rādhā, like a second Wielder of the Thunderbolt.

**8.26.20**  Either take captive the King of Dharma, or slay Dhanaṃjaya, and Bhīmasena too, O son of Rādhā, and the twin sons of Mādrī.

**8.26.21**  Victory be yours, and blessings upon you. Go forth, O bull among men, and reduce all the armies of the son of Pāṇḍu to ashes.

**8.26.22**  Then thousands of trumpets and tens of thousands of kettledrums, being sounded, rang out splendidly, like the voices of the storm-clouds in the sky.

**8.26.23**  Receiving that speech, Rādheya, best of chariot-warriors, standing upon his car, addressed Śalya, who was skilled in war:

**8.26.24**  "Urge on the horses, O mighty-armed one, that I may slay Dhanaṃjaya, Bhīmasena, both twins, and King Yudhiṣṭhira."

**8.26.25**  "Today, O Śalya, let Dhanaṃjaya behold the strength of my arms, as I loose thousands and hundreds of heron-feathered shafts."

**8.26.26**  "Today, O Śalya, I will hurl arrows of surpassing keenness, for the destruction of the Pāṇḍavas and for Duryodhana's victory."

**8.26.27**  Śalya said: O son of a charioteer, how is it that you make light of the Pāṇḍavas, who are masters of every weapon, mighty archers, and all of them great chariot-warriors?

**8.26.28**  Men who never turn back, exalted, unconquerable, true in valor — who could strike fear into the very Lord of a Hundred Sacrifices himself.

**8.26.29**  When you hear the roar of the Gāṇḍīva in battle, O Rādheya, like the crashing of the thunderbolt, then you will speak no more like this.

**8.26.30**  Saṃjaya said: But disregarding that speech uttered by the king of the Madras, Karṇa said to Śalya, "You shall see today," O lord of men.

**8.26.31**  Seeing Karṇa, the mighty archer, stationed and eager for battle, all the Kurus, their faces glad, raised a shout, O scorcher of foes.

**8.26.32**  Then, amid the thunder of drums and the din of kettledrums, the varied twang of arrows, and the roars of the swift warriors, your men went forth to battle, having made death their only turning-back.

**8.26.33**  As Karṇa set forth and the warriors rejoiced, the earth trembled, O king, and gave forth a loud, shrill cry.

**8.26.34**  Seven great planets were seen issuing forth from the sun; a falling of meteors occurred, and a burning of the quarters of the sky; thunderbolts crashed down, and dreadful winds blew.

**8.26.35**  And flocks of beasts and birds, many in number, then circled your army from the left, foretelling a great terror.

**8.26.36**  As Karṇa set out, his horses stumbled to the ground, and a dreadful rain of bones fell from the sky.

**8.26.37**  The weapons blazed, the banners shook, and the draught-animals shed tears, O lord of the people.

**8.26.38**  These and many other most dreadful portents arose there, O honored one, for the destruction of the Kauravas.

**8.26.39**  Yet they reckoned them as nothing, all of them deluded by fate; and as the son of the charioteer set out, the men cried, "Victory!" upon the earth, and your Kauravas deemed the Pāṇḍavas already vanquished.

**8.26.40**  Then that slayer of hostile heroes, standing upon his car, gazing upon Bhīṣma and Droṇa whose valor had been spent, blazed up, O Bhārata, radiant as fire — that Vaikartana, the bull among chariot-warriors, the elephant of the car.

**8.26.41**  Addressing Śalya, he spoke these words, having beheld the matchless deeds of Pārtha; burning with pride and arrogance, blazing as if with wrath, he sighed and said:

**8.26.42**  "Bow in hand, standing on my car, I do not fear even great Indra, the wielder of the thunderbolt, in his wrath; yet, beholding Bhīṣma and the foremost ones laid low, my steadfastness still does not forsake me."

**8.26.43**  "When those two, blameless and like unto great Indra and Viṣṇu, crushers of the best of chariots, horses, and elephants, all but invincible, were slain by the foe — even now, for that, there is a trembling in me in battle."

**8.26.44**  "Looking upon the mighty ranks in battle — men, horses, elephants, and chariots slain by the kings' shafts — how is it that the preceptor, that bull among Brāhmaṇas, knower of the great weapons, did not slay all his enemies in the fight?"

**8.26.45**  "Recalling the slaying of Droṇa in the great combat, I speak the truth — mark it, O Kurus: none other than I among you could withstand Arjuna in battle, when he comes on like death itself in fearful form."

**8.26.46**  "Training, graciousness, strength, and firmness, the great weapons and humility were in Droṇa; if that great soul has gone into the power of death, then I deem all the rest stricken helpless today."

**8.26.47**  "Nothing here, they know, is to be reckoned as certain in this world, because of the impermanence of action's fruit. At sunrise, who, freed of doubt, would take pride today, when the preceptor has been struck down?"

**8.26.48**  "Surely neither weapons, nor strength, nor prowess, nor skill, nor sound policy, nor the supreme arms suffice to keep a man in happiness — for so the preceptor was slain in battle by the foe."

**8.26.49**  "Him whose splendor equaled fire and the sun, who in prowess was like Viṣṇu and Purandara, ever in counsel the peer of Bṛhaspati and Uśanas — even him that hard-to-bear weaponry could not save."

**8.26.50**  "With outcry raised, women and children weeping, the manhood of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's host brought low — I know what must be done by me, O Śalya: therefore advance against the army of our enemies."

**8.26.51**  "There, where the Pāṇḍava king, true to his word, stands firm, with Bhīmasena and Arjuna, Vāsudeva, the Sṛñjayas, Sātyaki, and the twins — who but I could endure those two?"

**8.26.52**  "Therefore advance swiftly, O lord of the Madras, against the Pāñcālas, the Pāṇḍavas, and the Sṛñjayas in battle. Either I shall slay them, joining combat, or I think I shall go the way of Droṇa."

**8.26.53**  "But know me thus, O Śalya: it is not that I will not go into the midst of those heroes. Treachery toward a friend is not to be borne by me; giving up my life, I will follow after Droṇa."

**8.26.54**  "For wise man and fool alike, at life's end, the release of breath comes to him who has reached the jaws of the Ender. Therefore, O learned one, I will go forth against Pārtha; what is ordained cannot be overstepped."

**8.26.55**  "For ever of gracious conduct toward me, O king, has the son of Vicitravīrya been; for the achievement of his purpose I give up dear pleasures and life that is so hard to surrender."

**8.26.56**  "Decked with tiger-skin, its axle never creaking, with a triple golden scabbard and a silver triple yoke-pole — this best of chariots, yoked with the finest steeds, Rāma gave to me."

**8.26.57**  "Behold, O Śalya, the splendid bows, the banner, the mace, the arrows of fearful form, the blazing sword, the supreme weapon, and the bright conch, loud-resounding and terrible."

**8.26.58**  "Mounting this bull among chariots — pennoned, roaring like the falling thunderbolt, yoked with white horses, adorned with fair quivers — I shall slay Arjuna in battle by main force."

**8.26.59**  "If death, the taker of all, ever-vigilant, guards that son of Pāṇḍu in battle, then either I shall slay him, joining combat, or I shall go to Yama by the road that Bhīṣma went."

**8.26.60**  "Even if Yama, Varuṇa, Kubera, and Vāsava, with their hosts, were to come all at once into the great battle to guard the Pāṇḍava — what need of many words? — I would conquer him together with them."

**8.26.61**  Hearing the words of one thus boasting in the frenzy of battle, that valiant king of the Madras laughed in scorn, rebuked him, and made reply:

**8.26.62**  "Cease, cease, O Karṇa, from your boasting; you are too rash, and your speech is unfitting. Where is Dhanaṃjaya, that best of men, and where, then, are you? Have done, O foolish one!"

**8.26.63**  "The dwelling of the Yadus, guarded by Upendra like heaven warded by the king of the gods — who, beholding it, could carry off by force the younger sister of that best of men, save Arjuna?"

**8.26.64**  "Who among men here could challenge to combat Bhava, the maker of the three worlds, the Lord of lords, in a quarrel over the slaying of a deer, save Arjuna, whose might equaled the valor of the king of the gods?"

**8.26.65**  "Asuras, gods, great serpents, and men, Garuḍas, piśācas, with yakṣas and rākṣasas, he conquered with his arrows out of reverence for Agni, and the victorious one gave him the oblation he desired."

**8.26.66**  "Surely you remember, O son of the sun, when that son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra was carried off by his foes and then set free by those best of men, who in the wilds slew many of those enemies?"

**8.26.67**  "When you had fled at the very first, the strife-loving sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra — surely you remember when they were rescued by the Pāṇḍavas, who overcame the hosts of the sky-rangers?"

**8.26.68**  "Again, at the seizing of the cattle, you all — with your full strength and steeds, together with the preceptor and his son and Bhīṣma — were vanquished by that best of men. Was not Arjuna unconquered by you then as well?"

**8.26.69**  "Now this further fine battle has come upon you again, this very day, for your destruction. If you do not flee out of fear of the foe, then, gone to war this day, you are as good as slain, O son of a charioteer."

**8.26.70**  Saṃjaya said: As the lord of the Madras, with cheerful mind, was thus speaking many harsh words in praise of the foe, that mighty bull, the commander of the Kuru host, exceedingly enraged, said to the king of the Madras:

**8.26.71**  "So be it, so be it — why do you boast? Surely the battle between me and him is at hand. If he conquers me in the great combat, then let this fair boasting of yours stand proven."

**8.26.72**  "So be it," said the lord of the Madras, and made no further reply. "Drive on, O lord of the Madras," Karṇa then said to him, eager for battle.

**8.26.73**  That chariot, with white horses and Śalya for its driver, advanced upon the enemies, smiting foes in battle as the sun dispels the darkness.

**8.26.74**  Then Karṇa, well pleased, advanced upon his car decked with tiger-skin and yoked with white steeds; and looking upon the bannered host of the Pāṇḍavas, he hastily asked after Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.27.1**  Saṃjaya said: Even as he advanced, Karṇa, gladdening your army, kept asking after the Pāṇḍava, scanning each warrior in the battle one by one.

**8.27.2**  "Whoever today shows me that great soul, the one with white steeds, to him I would grant whatever desired boon his heart wishes."

**8.27.3**  "And if he should think that not enough, then to him who tells me of Dhanaṃjaya I would give besides a cartload filled with jewels."

**8.27.4**  "And if that man who has pointed out Arjuna should think that not enough, then I would give him yet another gift — a golden elephant and six oxen."

**8.27.5**  "And to him I would give, besides, a hundred adorned women, dark and lovely, golden chains about their throats, skilled in song and music."

**8.27.6**  "And if that man who has shown me Arjuna should think that not enough, then I would give him another gift — five hundred white horses."

**8.27.7**  "And I would give, moreover, eight hundred other steeds, decked with golden trappings and bright jeweled ear-ornaments, and well broken in."

**8.27.8**  "And to him who tells me of Dhanaṃjaya I would give a bright golden chariot, richly adorned, yoked with the finest Kāmboja steeds."

**8.27.9**  "To him I would give another gift — six hundred elephants, covered with various golden trappings and wearing golden garlands, bred in the western lands and trained by elephant-tamers."

**8.27.10**  "And if that man who has shown me Arjuna should think that not enough, then I would give him another gift — whatever he himself might desire."

**8.27.11**  "My sons and wives, my pleasure-houses, and whatever other wealth is mine — that too I would give him, whatever his heart should wish."

**8.27.12**  "And having slain the two Kṛṣṇas together, I would give all their wealth entirely to him who tells me of Keśava and Arjuna."

**8.27.13**  Uttering these words again and again in the battle, Karṇa blew his excellent, sweet-sounding conch, born of the sea.

**8.27.14**  Hearing those well-spoken words of the son of the charioteer, Duryodhana, O great king, was delighted, together with his followers.

**8.27.15**  Then the thunder of drums and tabors on every side, the lion-roar mingled with instruments, and the trumpeting of elephants —

**8.27.16**  — arose then, O king, in your army, O bull of the Bhāratas; and such was the clamor of the warriors in their high joy.

**8.27.17**  And while the army thus rejoiced, the king of the Madras, with a laugh, addressed these words in reply to Rādheya, that tormentor of foes, who, sweeping along on his great chariot, was boasting in the midst of battle:

**8.27.18**  "Do not, O son of a charioteer, out of pride bestow the golden elephant and the six oxen upon any man today: you shall see Dhanaṃjaya for yourself."

**8.27.19**  "As if from childishness you fling away your wealth like Vaiśravaṇa; without any effort at all, O Rādheya, you shall behold Dhanaṃjaya today."

**8.27.20**  "Why do you squander all this in vain, and why, like a fool, do you talk so much? The faults that lie in giving to the unworthy — those, in your delusion, you do not perceive."

**8.27.21**  "With the wealth you proclaim, abundant indeed, you could perform many kinds of sacrifices; offer them up in worship, O charioteer's son."

**8.27.22**  "And that you seek to slay the two Kṛṣṇas — that, from delusion, is mere idle fancy; for we have never heard of two lions struck down in the fray by a jackal."

**8.27.23**  "You crave what is not to be craved; you have no true friends, since they do not at once restrain you as you fall headlong into the fire."
