**8.27.24**  "You do not know what the hour requires; you are ripened by Time, beyond doubt. Who that wished to live would utter such rambling, unfitting nonsense?"

**8.27.25**  "Like crossing the ocean with your two arms after binding a stone about your neck, or like leaping down from a mountain-peak — such is the thing you mean to do."

**8.27.26**  "Joined with all your warriors, well guarded by arrayed troops, do battle with Dhanaṃjaya, if you wish to attain what is best."

**8.27.27**  "For the good of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son I speak to you, not out of malice. Believe this that I have told you, if there is in you any wish to live."

**8.27.28**  Karṇa said: Trusting in my own valor I challenge Arjuna in battle. But you, an enemy wearing a friend's face, wish only to frighten me.

**8.27.29**  No one today shall turn me back from this resolve—not even Indra, raising his thunderbolt. What then can a mere mortal do?

**8.27.30**  Saṃjaya said: When Karṇa's speech was ended, Śalya, the lord of the Madras, spoke his reply again, eager to provoke Karṇa to the utmost.

**8.27.31**  When the heron-feathered arrows, sped by Phalguna's force, loosed from the bowstring by his skilled hand, their points whetted sharp, come pursuing you—then you shall burn with grief at Arjuna's onslaught.

**8.27.32**  When Pārtha, the ambidextrous archer, taking up his divine bow and lighting up the whole army, crushes you with his keen shafts—then, son of a charioteer, you shall repent too late.

**8.27.33**  As a child lying in its mother's lap reaches out to seize the moon, so you, standing on your chariot and striving in your folly, long today to conquer Arjuna.

**8.27.34**  Embracing a trident of razor-keen edge, you rub all your limbs against it—for, Karṇa, in seeking to fight Arjuna today you face a deed sharp as that very edge.

**8.27.35**  As a young, foolish, headstrong little beast might challenge a great, full-grown, maned lion—such, son of a charioteer, is your challenge to Arjuna today.

**8.27.36**  Do not, O son of a charioteer, challenge that prince of mighty valor, as a jackal in the forest, gorged on flesh, might challenge a lion—do not perish by approaching Pārtha.

**8.27.37**  You, a hare, summon to battle a great elephant whose tusks are like chariot-poles and whose temples and mouth run with rut—you summon Pārtha Dhanaṃjaya, O Karṇa.

**8.27.38**  In your childishness you poke with a stick at a black serpent lying in its hole—a creature of deadly venom, its poison-sac brimful—when you wish to fight Pārtha.

**8.27.39**  In your folly you cross the path of a wrathful maned lion and roar at him like a jackal—at Pāṇḍava, that lion among men, O Karṇa.

**8.27.40**  Like a small bird you summon to the swoop the fair-winged son of Vinatā, swiftest and best of all that fly—you summon Pārtha Dhanaṃjaya, O Karṇa.

**8.27.41**  Without a boat you would cross the dread abode of all waters, surging with waves and teeming with great fish, swelling at the rising of the moon.

**8.27.42**  Like a calf you summon to combat a bull with a neck deep as a drum, sharp-horned and ready to strike—you summon Pārtha Dhanaṃjaya, O Karṇa.

**8.27.43**  Like a frog you croak back at a great thundering cloud—at Arjuna, that rain-god among men, who pours upon the world the waters of every wish.

**8.27.44**  As a dog kept in its own house might bark at a tiger ranging in the forest, so do you bark, O Karṇa, at Dhanaṃjaya, that tiger among men.

**8.27.45**  A jackal too, O Karṇa, dwelling in the forest surrounded by hares, thinks himself a lion—so long as he has not yet seen a lion.

**8.27.46**  Just so, son of Rādhā, you imagine yourself a lion—having not yet beheld Dhanaṃjaya, that subduer of foes, that tiger among men.

**8.27.47**  You think yourself a tiger only so long as you have not seen the two Kṛṣṇas standing together upon one chariot, like the sun and the moon.

**8.27.48**  Only so long as you have not heard the roar of Gāṇḍīva in the great battle, O Karṇa, can you speak as you please.

**8.27.49**  When you behold him making the ten directions ring with the din of his chariot and bow, roaring like a tiger, you will turn into a jackal.

**8.27.50**  You are ever a jackal, and Dhanaṃjaya is ever a lion. Fool, through your hatred of the brave you appear forever as a jackal.

**8.27.51**  As the mouse to the cat, the dog to the tiger, in strength and weakness; as the jackal to the lion, as the hare to the elephant—

**8.27.52**  as falsehood to truth, as poison to the nectar of immortality—so are you and Pārtha, each made known by your own deeds.

**8.27.53**  Saṃjaya said: But the son of Rādhā, thus reviled by Śalya of boundless splendor, in towering rage answered Śalya, fixing in his mind that spear of words.

**8.27.54**  The virtues of a man of virtue, O Śalya, are known by one who has virtue, not by one without it. But you, ever destitute of merit, how shall you, the meritless, know merit?

**8.27.55**  Arjuna's mighty weapons, his wrath, his valor, his bow and arrows—these I know well, O Śalya; you do not know them so.

**8.27.56**  Knowing thus both my own valor and the valor that is in the Pāṇḍava, I challenge him to battle, O Śalya—not like a moth rushing into the fire.

**8.27.57**  And here, O Śalya, is this arrow, finely nocked, drinker of blood, lying apart in its own quiver, well-feathered, burnished bright and richly adorned.

**8.27.58**  It has rested, honored, for many long years in a casket filled with sandal-paste—venomous as a serpent, deadly, the slayer of hosts of men, horses, and elephants.

**8.27.59**  Peerless among shafts, of terrible might, it cleaves armor and bone alike; with it, in my fury, I could rive even Meru, the great mountain.

**8.27.60**  This shaft I would never loose at any other than Phalguna, or Kṛṣṇa, the son of Devakī—hear from me the truth of this.

**8.27.61**  With that arrow, O Śalya, in utmost wrath I shall fight Vāsudeva and Dhanaṃjaya—such a deed is worthy of me.

**8.27.62**  In Kṛṣṇa rests the glory of all the Vāsudevas, and in Pārtha rests the victory of all the sons of Pāṇḍu. Who, meeting both together, could hope to overcome them?

**8.27.63**  When these two tigers among men, standing together upon one chariot, advance against me alone—then, O Śalya, behold the noble deed that is mine.

**8.27.64**  Those two cousins, sons of a father's sister and a mother's brother, unconquered kinsmen—you shall see them slain by me, strung like two gems upon a single thread.

**8.27.65**  In Arjuna is the Gāṇḍīva, in Kṛṣṇa the discus; the one bears Garuḍa, the other the monkey on his banner. These two, terror to the timid, bring me joy, O Śalya.

**8.27.66**  But you, of base nature, a fool unskilled in great battles, sunk in fear, out of sheer terror babble much that is incoherent.

**8.27.67**  For some reason you sing the praises of those two, O man of a vile land. When I have slain them in battle, I shall surely slay you too, along with your kinsmen.

**8.27.68**  Born of a sinful land, dull-witted, base, you disgrace of warriors—having been a friend, why now, turned enemy, do you seek to frighten me with the two Kṛṣṇas?

**8.27.69**  Either those two shall slay me today, or I shall slay them as they stand in battle. I do not fear the two Kṛṣṇas, for I know full well my own strength.

**8.27.70**  Were there a thousand Vāsudevas and hundreds of Phalgunas, I alone would slay them all. Hold your peace, O man of a wretched land.

**8.27.71**  Hear from me, O Śalya, those verses about the vile Madrakas which women, children, the old, and folk gathered at play sing aloud, as though reciting a lesson.

**8.27.72**  Recited of old by brāhmaṇas, duly, in the presence of a king—listen to them with attention, O fool, and either endure them or make your reply.

**8.27.73**  The Madraka is ever a betrayer of friends; whoever hates us, he is a Madraka. There is no friendship to be had with a Madraka, that basest of men, foul of speech.

**8.27.74**  The Madraka is forever vile of soul, forever false and crooked. To the very end there is wickedness among the Madrakas—so have we heard.

**8.27.75**  Father and mother and son, mother-in-law, father-in-law, and maternal uncles, son-in-law, daughter, brother, grandson, and all their kindred—

**8.27.76**  and friends and chance-come guests, men-servants and maid-servants assembled together—and women mingling with men, known and unknown alike, at their own pleasure.

**8.27.77**  In whose houses the so-called respectable, ever feeding on parched barley-meal, having drunk strong liquor together with beef, roar aloud and laugh—

**8.27.78**  and where unbridled things go on at every whim, where they prattle wantonly to one another—among such people how can there be any righteousness?

**8.27.79**  With the Madrakas, a lawless folk notorious for foul deeds, one should practice neither enmity nor friendship.

**8.27.80**  There is no fellowship with a Madraka, for the Madraka is fickle; among the Madrakas, and among the Gāndhārakas, purity is a thing hard to come by.

**8.27.81**  By an offering performed for a priest who sacrifices for kings, the oblation given is utterly lost.

**8.27.82**  As a brāhmaṇa who performs the sacraments for a śūdra comes to ruin, so do the haters of the brahman ever come to ruin in this world.

**8.27.83**  There is no fellowship with a Madraka. The venom of a scorpion's sting is undone, and all is set at peace, by a spell of the Atharvan.

**8.27.84**  For one stung by a scorpion, or stricken by venoms of every kind, the wise prepare a remedy, and its truth is seen indeed. So, knowing this, hold your peace—and yet hear my further word.

**8.27.85**  Their women, drunk with liquor, cast off their garments and dance, unrestrained in coupling and roaming at their own desire. How can a Madraka, son of such women, presume to speak of righteousness?

**8.27.86**  They who make water standing, like she-camels at Daśeraka—you who are the son of such women, fallen from shame and shameless every way, do you wish here to speak of righteousness?

**8.27.87**  When a Madraka woman is asked for sūvīraka, she scratches her buttocks; and unwilling to give it, she utters this harsh word:

**8.27.88**  "Let no one, no one ask me for sūvīraka, which is dear to me. I would sooner give my son for the asking than give up my sūvīraka."

**8.27.89**  The Madraka women are tall and brazen, wrapped in coarse blankets, gluttonous and devoid of all cleanliness—so, for the most part, have we heard.

**8.27.90**  Of such things, and of others, much could be told by me or by another—of those followers of evil ways, who are blameworthy from the tips of their hair to the tips of their nails.

**8.27.91**  How then should the Madrakas and the Sindhu-Sauvīras know righteousness here—barbarians sprung from a sinful land, ignorant of all the laws?

**8.27.92**  This, we have heard, is the foremost duty of a warrior: that he lie slain in battle, honored by the good.

**8.27.93**  That I should come free from the clash of arms in the encounter—that is no first choice of mine, who in death desire heaven.

**8.27.94**  I am the dear friend of the wise son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra; for his sake are my very life-breaths, and whatever wealth is mine.

**8.27.95**  It is plain that you too, O man of a sinful land, have been suborned by the Pāṇḍavas, for you behave toward us in all things like an enemy.

**8.27.96**  Yet surely I cannot be turned away from battle, even by hundreds of your kind—any more than a man who knows the law can be turned by unbelievers.

**8.27.97**  Like a cātaka bird tormented by the heat, lament and wither away as you please; I, who stand firm in the warrior's calling, cannot be frightened.

**8.27.98**  Remember the goal which my teacher Rāma once declared to me—the goal of those lions among men who give up their bodies and never turn back in battle.

**8.27.99**  Know that I have taken up that noblest course, the way of Purūravas—bent on the protection of my own and on the slaughter of my foes.

**8.27.100**  I see no being in the three worlds, O Madraka, who could turn me back from this resolve—such is my conviction.

**8.27.101**  Knowing this, hold your peace. Why, out of fear, do you talk so much? Take care that I do not slay you and cast you to the flesh-eating beasts, O basest of Madrakas.

**8.27.102**  It is only by these three things, O Śalya—out of regard for friendship, for the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and through forbearance toward your abuse—that you yet live.

**8.27.103**  If once more, O king of the Madras, you speak such a word, I shall strike off your head with my mace, that is like the thunderbolt.

**8.27.104**  This very day, O man of a wretched land, men shall hear and behold whether the two Kṛṣṇas have slain Karṇa, or Karṇa has slain them.

**8.27.105**  Having spoken thus, O lord of peoples, the son of Rādhā once more said to the king of the Madras, calmly: "Drive on, drive on!"

**8.28.1**  Saṃjaya said: My lord, having heard the words of the son of Adhiratha, who gloried in battle, Śalya spoke again to Karṇa, putting forth a parable.

**8.28.2**  Even as a man is drunk with liquor, so are you—and yet perhaps not quite so; and seeing you thus heedless, I try, out of friendship, to set you right.

**8.28.3**  Listen to this comparison with a crow that I now set forth, Karṇa; having heard it, do as you please, O base man, defiler of your line.

**8.28.4**  I can recall no fault in myself at all, Karṇa, for which you, O mighty-armed one, should wish to destroy me, who am innocent.

**8.28.5**  Yet I must surely speak, if you would understand your own good—and all the more, since I stand upon your chariot and wish the king well.

**8.28.6**  Smooth ground and rough, the strength and weakness of the warrior, the unceasing toil and weariness of the horses together with the chariot-fighter—

**8.28.7**  the knowledge of weapons, the cries of beasts and birds, the load and the overload, and the drawing-out of barbs—

**8.28.8**  the deployment of missiles, the fighting itself, and the omens too—all this must be known to me, who am as the householder of this chariot. Therefore, Karṇa, I tell you once more this instructive tale.

**8.28.9**  On the shore of the sea, they say, there dwelt a Vaiśya rich in wealth and grain, a performer of sacrifices, a lord of generosity, patient, steadfast in his own duty, and pure.

**8.28.10**  With many sons, fond of his children, compassionate to all beings, he lived without fear in the realm of a king whose chief care was righteousness.

**8.28.11**  Of his many young sons, those glorious boys, a crow became the pet, fed on the leavings of their meals.

**8.28.12**  To it the Vaiśya's young sons would always give meat and rice, curds and milk, sweet milk-pudding, honey and clarified butter.

**8.28.13**  And that crow, nourished on scraps by the Vaiśya's young sons, grew arrogant and held in contempt the birds that were his equals—and his betters too.

**8.28.14**  Now one day there came flying down to the seashore some swans, swift as Garuḍa in flight, ruddy-limbed and glad of heart.

**8.28.15**  Then the boys, seeing the swans, said to the crow: "Surely you, O bird, are the best of all winged creatures!"

**8.28.16**  And the egg-born one, beguiled by those witless children, took their words for truth, out of folly and out of pride.

**8.28.17**  Puffed up by his diet of scraps, the crow flew at those many far-flying birds, eager to learn which among them was the foremost.

**8.28.18**  Whichever of those far-flying swans he judged the best, that bird the dull-witted crow challenged, saying, "Let us fly!"

**8.28.19**  Hearing this, the swans gathered there laughed aloud as the crow babbled on; and those mighty, best of fliers, the ruddy-limbed birds, spoke these words to the crow.

**8.28.20**  "We are swans, dwellers on Lake Mānasa, who roam this whole earth, and we are ever honored among birds for our far-ranging flight.

**8.28.21**  How is it, fool, that being but a crow, you challenge to a flight the strong, adamant-limbed, far-flying swan? How, crow, do you speak of flying together with us?"

**8.28.22**  Then the foolish, boastful crow, scorning the swan's words again and again, gave his answer out of native frivolity:

**8.28.23**  "A hundred and one styles of flight will I fly, no doubt of it—each one stretching a hundred leagues, varied and of many kinds:

**8.28.24**  the up-flight and the down-flight, the forth-flight and the plain flight, the low-flight and the close-flight, the sidelong and the soaring courses;

**8.28.25**  the spread-flight and the round-flight, the away-flight and the deft-flight, the high-flight and the great-flight, the swoop and the wheeling;

**8.28.26**  the goings and comings and returnings, and many a downward dart—all these I shall perform before your very eyes this day, and then you shall behold my power."

**8.28.27**  When the crow had spoken thus, one of the birds, a swan, laughed and answered the crow—hear that speech from me:

**8.28.28**  "A hundred and one styles of flight you, crow, will surely fly; but all birds know only one single style of flight,

**8.28.29**  and that one alone shall I fly, crow; no other do I know. As for you, red-eyed one, fly by whatever style you fancy."

**8.28.30**  Then the crows gathered there burst out laughing: "How shall a swan with a single style of flight outdo a hundred styles?

**8.28.31**  With one flight against a hundred, the strong, swift-striding crow will overmatch the swan's single flight!"

**8.28.32**  So then the swan and the crow flew off in contest—the ruddy-limbed swan with his one flight, and the crow with his hundred.

**8.28.33**  The ruddy-limbed one flew, and the crow flew too, eager to amaze with his flights, proclaiming his own performance.

**8.28.34**  Then, seeing the crow's flights, dazzling and diverse, the crows rejoiced and cried out with their cawings,

**8.28.35**  jeering at the swans and uttering insults; flying up again and again, they called out, "In a moment! And like this! And like that!"

**8.28.36**  From treetops and from level ground they swooped down and soared up, raising their varied clamor, confident then of victory.

**8.28.37**  But the swan set out to fly with a gentle, easy stroke, and for a moment, sir, he fell behind the crow.

**8.28.38**  Despising the swans' pace, the crow spoke these words: "That swan who flew up yonder—why, the very same now lags behind!"

**8.28.39**  Then the swan, hearing this, set off toward the western quarter, speeding higher and higher over the ocean, the abode of Varuṇa.

**8.28.40**  Then fear entered the crow, bewildered out there, seeing no island, no tree, sinking down worn out: "Where, weary as I am, can I come to rest upon this watery deep?"

**8.28.41**  For the sea is not to be withstood, the home of many a host of creatures, gleaming with hundreds of great beings—it surpasses even the sky.

**8.28.42**  By reason of the sea's depth, O lowest of your line, there is no telling its waters from the wide horizon's robe; for they who dwell upon the sea, Karṇa, are hard to conquer—how much more so a crow far out over the deep, Karṇa?

**8.28.43**  Then the swan, having outstripped him in a moment, looked back upon the crow, who—try as he might—could not get away; and the ruddy-limbed one, having passed him, gazed upon that crow.

**8.28.44**  And seeing him thus falling behind, the swan, mindful of the vow of the virtuous and wishing to lift up the sinking bird, spoke these words:

**8.28.45**  "Boasting of your many flights again and again, you now fly without naming them; this secret art you do not declare to us at all.

**8.28.46**  What style of flight, O crow, is this that you now fly, touching the water with both wings and with your beak again and again?"

**8.28.47**  Touching the water of the sea with his wings and his beak, that crow, in distress and utterly spent, suddenly dropped down.

**8.28.48**  The swan said: "Those hundred and one styles of flight, crow, of which you boasted just now, in all their variety—here, today, they are proved a lie in you."

**8.28.49**  The crow said: "Made proud by my diet of scraps, O swan, I deemed myself the equal of Garuḍa, and looked down on many crows and on other birds besides. For my very life, swan, I throw myself upon you—carry me to the shore of an island!"

**8.28.50**  "If, swan, I should come safe again to my own country, never again would I despise anyone. Lift me out of this calamity!"

**8.28.51**  As he spoke thus, wretched, wailing, witless, croaking "Caw, caw!" and sinking in the great deep,

**8.28.52**  the swan came up to the crow—drenched and pitiable to behold—and lifting the trembling creature with his feet, he set him gently upon his back.

**8.28.53**  Having set the dazed crow upon his back, the swan, Karṇa, came back to the island from which the two had set out in their contest.

**8.28.54**  And having set that bird down again and comforted him, the swan, swift as thought, departed to the region he desired.

**8.28.55**  As that crow in the Vaiśya's house grew proud from feeding on scraps, even so are you, Karṇa, nourished on scraps by Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons—no doubt of it—and you despise all your equals and your betters alike.

**8.28.56**  Guarded by Droṇa, Aśvatthāman, and Kṛpa, by Bhīṣma and the other Kauravas—why then did you not slay the lone Pārtha in Virāṭa's city?

**8.28.57**  There, scattered and gathered alike, you were all vanquished by the Diademed One, like jackals by a lion. Where then was that valor of yours?

**8.28.58**  And seeing your brother slain, defeated by the Left-handed Archer, before the very eyes of the Kuru heroes you were the first to flee.

**8.28.59**  So too in the Dvaita forest, Karṇa, when the Gandharvas fell upon you, abandoning all the Kurus you were the first to flee.

**8.28.60**  Smiting and conquering the Gandharvas led by Citrasena in battle, Karṇa, it was Pārtha who set Duryodhana free, together with his wives.

**8.28.61**  And once again, Karṇa, the ancient might of Pārtha and of Keśava was recounted by Rāma in the hall, in the assembly of kings.

**8.28.62**  And ever did you hear that word of Droṇa and Bhīṣma, declaring before the kings that the two Kṛṣṇas could not be slain.

**8.28.63**  How much shall I tell of all the ways in which Dhanaṃjaya surpasses you, surpasses all beings, as the brahmin surpasses every other?

**8.28.64**  This very moment you shall behold them, stationed on their chariot in the forefront of battle—the son of Vāsudeva and the Pāṇḍava Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.28.65**  Those two bulls among men, renowned among gods, demons, and mortals, blaze forth in their splendor; but you are like a firefly among men.

**8.28.66**  Knowing this, O son of a charioteer, do not slight Acyuta and Arjuna. They are lions among men, and you a mere cur; so hold your peace, boaster.

**8.29.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the son of Adhiratha had thus heard the unwelcome speech of the lord of the Madras, unabashed he answered Śalya: "This is well known to me—what manner of men are Arjuna and Vāsudeva.

**8.29.2**  The strength of the Pāṇḍava Arjuna, and his great weapons, while Śauri drives his chariot—all this I rightly know this day, Śalya, though to you it lies beyond your sight.

**8.29.3**  Unassailable though they are, the best of all who bear arms, those two Kṛṣṇas I shall fight without fear; yet what torments me far more this day is the curse from Rāma and from that best of brahmins.

**8.29.4**  Long ago I dwelt with Rāma in the guise of a brahmin, wishing to master the celestial weapon; and there too, Śalya, the king of the gods cast a hindrance in my path, seeking only Phalguna's good.

**8.29.5**  Entering my thigh in the misshapen body of a worm, he bored it through; yet out of fear of my teacher I did not stir, and the brahmin, awakening, beheld it.

**8.29.6**  Questioned, I told that great sage, "I am a charioteer's son." Then he cursed me: "This weapon, won by you through a charioteer's deceit, shall not come to your mind at the hour of need."

**8.29.7**  "Save only at the hour of your death—for the sacred power cannot abide unfailing in one who is no brahmin." Yet even so, this weapon will more than suffice this day, in this tumultuous and terrible battle, my friend.

**8.29.8**  As the swift and measureless lord of waters, the sea—who would drown whole hosts of creatures and swells into a vast mountain—is yet held back by the shore alone, immeasurable though he be,

**8.29.9**  so shall I give battle to the son of Kuntī, slayer of heroes, as he looses his unerring, feathered shafts that cleave the vital points—he who is the foremost bow-drawer in the world this day.

**8.29.10**  With my own strength I shall withstand that mighty one, surpassing in might, wielder of the great weapon, like unto the sea, terrible and most hard to face, who in floods of arrows drowns the kings—Pārtha I shall meet with my shafts, as the shore meets the ocean.

**8.29.11**  In battle this day, against him whose equal among men, when he takes up the bow, I deem there is none—who in war could vanquish gods and demons alike—against him behold this day my most dreadful combat.

**8.29.12**  The over-proud Pāṇḍava, eager for the fight, will come against me with his superhuman great weapons; striking down his missiles with my own in the fray, with my choicest shafts I shall lay Pārtha low.

**8.29.13**  Him who blazes hot as the very sun, his rays at their full, flaming with glory, dispeller of darkness—Dhanaṃjaya I shall veil with my arrows, as a boundless cloud veils the sun.

**8.29.14**  As one might become a cloud and with showers of rain quench the fire—Vaiśvānara, his crest of smoke ablaze, radiant, consuming this whole world—so with showers of arrows shall I quell Pārtha in battle.

**8.29.15**  The crushing, mighty, smiting one—the rending wind, fierce Mātariśvan—even him in battle I shall withstand, as the unmoving Himālaya withstands the gale: Dhanaṃjaya enraged and unforgiving.

**8.29.16**  Skilled and unhampered in the ways of chariot-warfare, ever the foremost champion in battles, the best in the world of all who bear the bow—Dhanaṃjaya I shall withstand in the fray.

**8.29.17**  This very day in battle I shall fight, and fully knowing it, against that man who, when he takes up his bow among mortals, has no equal — one who could withstand this whole earth.

**8.29.18**  He, Savyasācin, who upon the plain of Khāṇḍava conquered all beings, the gods among them — what man other than me, caring for his own life, would dare to do battle with him?

**8.29.19**  I myself, gladly, in the assembly of the kṣatriyas, would proclaim the valor of that son of Pāṇḍu. Why then, you fool, dull-witted and prating, do you recite to me the prowess of Arjuna?

**8.29.20**  A spiteful man, harsh and cruel, mean, abusive, intolerant of those who endure — of such men I could slay hundreds. Yet you I forbear, out of patience, because the hour demands it.

**8.29.21**  You have spoken hateful words for the Pāṇḍavas' sake, assailing me like an idiot, you doer of evil. Crooked in your ways toward me who am upright, you are as good as slain, a betrayer of friendship — for friendship is sealed in seven steps.

**8.29.22**  But this is the hour, fraught with death and most terrible, in which Duryodhana has come to war. Longing for the fulfillment of his cause, I shall go to him there, where no withdrawal is possible.

**8.29.23**  A friend, O giver of honor, derives from "to love," "to delight," "to cherish," "to protect," or "to gladden," and so the learned declare from the scriptures of old — and all of that is mine in Duryodhana.

**8.29.24**  A foe derives from "to fell," "to chastise," "to lay low," "to wound," or "to swell," and, with a prefix, in many ways from "to destroy" — and nearly all of that is in you, both for you toward me and for me toward you.

**8.29.25**  For Duryodhana's sake, and to spite you, for glory's sake, for my own sake, and for the lord's sake — therefore I shall fight, with all my strength, against the Pāṇḍava and Vāsudeva. Behold that deed of mine this day.

**8.29.26**  Behold this day my surpassing weapons — sprung from Brahmā, divine, and human alike. I shall close with that man of fierce might as a maddened elephant closes with the lordliest of bulls in rut.

**8.29.27**  The weapon sprung from Brahmā, unconquerable, peerless, I shall hurl by force of mind against Pārtha for victory; and from it too he shall not escape in battle — unless this day my chariot wheel should sink into broken ground.

**8.29.28**  Not from the son of Vivasvat with his rod in hand, nor from Varuṇa with his noose, nor from the lord of wealth with his mace, nor even from Vāsava with his thunderbolt —

**8.29.29**  nor from any other assailant whatsoever do I feel fear. Know this, O Śalya, that I, being fearless, feel no dread.

**8.29.30**  Therefore I have no fear of Pārtha, nor of Janārdana. This day, in the decisive clash, my battle with the two of them shall come to pass.

**8.29.31**  "Let your wheel sink into a hole" — so a brāhmaṇa once cursed me: that while I fight in battle, brought to a single, narrow pass, peril should fall upon me.

**8.29.32**  For that reason I dread, and dread greatly, the utterance of the brāhmaṇa — for these moon-pale kings are masters of pleasure and of pain alike.

**8.29.33**  For heedlessly, O Śalya, I slew with an arrow the calf of his sacred milk-cow as it grazed in a lonely place — the calf of a brāhmaṇa who was a treasury of penance.

**8.29.34**  Seven hundred elephants with tusks like chariot-poles, and hundreds of bondwomen and bondmen I offered to that foremost of the twice-born, yet he would show me no favor.

**8.29.35**  Fourteen thousand black cows with white calves I brought as gifts, yet from that best of the twice-born I won no grace.

**8.29.36**  My house, abounding in all desirable things, and whatever wealth was mine — all of that, with honor, I offered to give him, yet he would not have it.

**8.29.37**  Then he said to me, who, though at fault, entreated him with all my effort: "What I have spoken, O sūta, stands thus; it cannot be otherwise."

**8.29.38**  "A falsehood spoken would destroy my offspring, and from it I should incur sin. Therefore, to safeguard righteousness, I cannot bring myself to utter an untruth."

**8.29.39**  "Do not seek to thwart the brāhmaṇa's decree; the atonement you have offered has been made. Let no one in the world render my word false — undergo it to the end."

**8.29.40**  This much I have told you, though I was provoked, yet out of friendship. I know you for one who reviles me — be silent now, and hear my answer.

**8.30.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then once more, O great king, the son of Rādhā addressed the king of the Madras, that subduer of foes, checking his reply.

**8.30.2**  "Whatever, by way of example, you have prated at me, O Śalya — I cannot be frightened by you with mere words in battle."

**8.30.3**  "Even if all the gods, with Vāsava at their head, should give me battle, even then I would feel no fear — how much less from Pārtha, though Keśava be at his side."

**8.30.4**  "I cannot in any way be frightened by mere words. Find some other man whom you are able to frighten in battle."

**8.30.5**  "This much only is a low man's strength — the abuse you fling at me. Unable to attain to my qualities, you bluster much, O evil-minded one."

**8.30.6**  "For Karṇa was not born into this world for fear's sake, good sir. I was born for valor's sake, and likewise for glory's sake."

**8.30.7**  "But hear this from me with full attention, O lord of the Madra people — what I heard told in the presence of Dhṛtarāṣṭra."

**8.30.8**  "Brāhmaṇas, recounting many varied and curious lands and the kings of olden times, used to wait upon Dhṛtarāṣṭra."

**8.30.9**  "There an aged and foremost of the twice-born, telling certain tales of bygone days, spoke these words in censure of the Bāhlika land and of the Madras."

**8.30.10**  "Those who are cast out by the Himavat, and scorned by the Gaṅgā, by the Sarasvatī, the Yamunā, and by Kurukṣetra as well —"

**8.30.11**  "those who dwell between the five rivers, with the Sindhu for the sixth — those Bāhlīkas, outside the law and impure, one should shun."

**8.30.12**  "There is a banyan tree called Govardhana, and a market-square called Subhāṇḍa; this is the gate of the royal house — these I remember from my boyhood."

**8.30.13**  "On a matter most pressing I once dwelt among the Bāhlīkas; thus, from living among them, their conduct became known to me."

**8.30.14**  "There is a city named Śākala, a river named Āpagā, and the Bāhlīkas called Jartikā — most contemptible is their way of life."

**8.30.15**  "Having drunk liquor of fried grain and molasses, they eat beef together with garlic; gorging on cakes, meat, and parched grain, they are void of good conduct."

**8.30.16**  "They laugh, sing, and dance with their women, drunken and stripped of clothing, upon the walls and houses of the city, garlanded and anointed without."

**8.30.17**  "With sundry drunken ditties, like the braying of asses and camels, they call out to one another, hurling abuse, frenzied with their intoxication."

**8.30.18**  "'Alas, slain! Alas, slain! Alas, my lord and husband, slain!' — so wailing, the wretched women dance about, unrestrained on the festal days."

**8.30.19**  "It is said that a certain one among the Bāhlika chieftains, those defiled folk, while dwelling in the Kurujāṅgala, sang thus, his heart far from glad:"

**8.30.20**  "'Surely that tall, fair woman, clad in fine wool, lies remembering me, a Bāhlika now dwelling among the Kurus.'"

**8.30.21**  "'When I have crossed the Śatadru river and the lovely Irāvatī, and gone to my own land, I shall behold the fair women with their heavy conch-bangles.'"

**8.30.22**  "'Their eye-corners bright with red arsenic, fair-skinned, anointed with collyrium of Trikakud, wrapped in nothing but deerskin, leaping about, lovely to behold —'"

**8.30.23**  "'to the sounds of drums and kettledrums, of conches and tabors, mounted on asses, camels, and mules, drunken, we shall journey at our ease.'"

**8.30.24**  "'In the woods of śamī, pīlu, and karīra, along the pleasant roads, eating cakes and balls of barley-meal, washed down with buttermilk —'"

**8.30.25**  "'grown strong upon the highways, on the unfrequented road we shall fall to robbery and beat down many a traveler.'"

**8.30.26**  "Among such base, outcaste, evil-souled Bāhlīkas, what man of any sense would dwell even for a moment?"

**8.30.27**  "Such did the brāhmaṇa declare the Bāhlīkas to be, men of worthless conduct — and you are taker of a sixth part of both their merit and their sin."

**8.30.28**  "Having spoken thus, the worthy brāhmaṇa spoke yet again. Mark now what he told of the ill-bred Bāhlīkas."

**8.30.29**  "There, in the thriving city of Śākala, a rākṣasī sings ever on the dark fourteenth night, beating her drum in the darkness:"

**8.30.30**  "'When shall the songstresses again sing their ballads in Śākala, sated with beef, having drunk the great liquor of molasses,'"

**8.30.31**  "'together with fair, tall, well-adorned women, eating their fill of mutton washed down with mouthfuls of onion?'"

**8.30.32**  "'They who do not eat the flesh of swine, of fowl, of cattle, of asses, of camels, and of sheep — their birth is in vain.'"

**8.30.33**  "So sing the dwellers of Śākala, drunk on rum, leaping about with their children and their elders — what good conduct can there be among such folk?"

**8.30.34**  "Know this, O Śalya — and come, I shall tell you more besides — what yet another brāhmaṇa told us in the Kuru assembly."

**8.30.35**  "Where these five rivers flow, where too the pīlu-forests stand — the Śatadru, the Vipāśā, the Irāvatī third, the Candrabhāgā and the Vitastā, with the Sindhu sixth, flowing forth beyond —"

**8.30.36**  "those lands are called Āraṭṭa, lost to righteousness; let none go to them — to the outcastes, the slave-born, the Videhas who keep no sacrifice."

**8.30.37**  "Neither the gods, nor the fathers, nor the brāhmaṇas accept the offerings of those Bāhlīkas whose law has utterly perished — so runs the sacred tradition."

**8.30.38**  "Thus too declared a learned brāhmaṇa in the assembly of the good: the Bāhlīkas eat, shameless, from wooden and earthen bowls smeared with barley-gruel and licked by dogs and the like."

**8.30.39**  "The milk of ewes, of camels, and of asses, and whatever is made therefrom, the Bāhlīkas both eat and drink."

**8.30.40**  "Mixing their offspring, base wretches, devouring every kind of food and milk — the Bāhlīkas called Āraṭṭa are to be shunned by the wise."

**8.30.41**  "Mark too, O Śalya — and come, I shall tell you yet more — what still another honorable brāhmaṇa declared in the Kuru assembly."

**8.30.42**  "Having drunk milk in Yugaṃdhara, and lodged in Acyutasthala, and bathed likewise in Bhūtilaya — how shall a man go to heaven?"

**8.30.43**  "Where these five rivers flow, having issued from the mountains — there are the Bāhlīkas called Āraṭṭa; among them no Ārya should dwell two days together."

**8.30.44**  "Two piśācas there were, named Bahi and Hlīka, dwelling by the Vipāśā; the Bāhlīkas are their offspring — they are no creation of Prajāpati."

**8.30.45**  "The Kāraskaras, the Māhiṣakas, the Kaliṅgas, the forest Kīkaṭas, the Karkoṭakas and Vīrakas, and the men of evil law — these one should avoid."

**8.30.46**  "So a certain rākṣasī, dwelling a single night in a house of śamī wood, girt with a great mortar, spoke to a wanderer among the holy fords."

**8.30.47**  "Those lands are called Āraṭṭa, those people are called Bāhlika; the Vasātis, the Sindhus, and the Sauvīras — these for the most part stand condemned."

**8.30.48**  "Mark too, O Śalya — and come, I shall tell you more — hear with single-minded attention what I now duly relate."

**8.30.49**  "A brāhmaṇa once came as a guest to the house of an artisan; observing the customs there, well pleased, he said to the craftsman:"

**8.30.50**  "'Long have I dwelt alone upon a peak of the Himavat, and I have seen many lands, thronged with diverse customs.'"

**8.30.51**  "'And nowhere do these peoples set themselves against righteousness; all of them spoke the law as it is declared by those who have mastered the Vedas —'"

**8.30.52**  "'but ever wandering among lands thronged with diverse customs, O great king, when I came among the Bāhlīkas, this is what I observed:'"

**8.30.53**  "'There a man, having first been a brāhmaṇa, becomes thereafter a kṣatriya, then a vaiśya, then a śūdra; and the Bāhlika becomes thereafter a barber —'"

**8.30.54**  "'and, having become a barber, he becomes a brāhmaṇa once more; and there a man, having been twice-born, is born again even as a slave.'"

**8.30.55**  "'One man in a family becomes a brāhmaṇa, while the rest do as they please. The Gāndhāras, the Madrakas, and the Bāhlīkas are men devoid of all sense.'"

**8.30.56**  "'This confounding of all law I heard told there; and having wandered the whole earth over, it is among the Bāhlīkas that I found this perversion.'"

**8.30.57**  Know this too, O Śalya — come, I will tell you yet more — the words another man spoke in condemnation of the Bāhlīkas.

**8.30.58**  Long ago a certain chaste woman of Āraṭṭa was carried off by robbers, they say, and wrongfully ravished; thereupon she laid this curse upon them:

**8.30.59**  "Because you have approached me unlawfully — a young girl with kinsmen still living — the women of your houses shall become harlots, and never shall you be freed from this dreadful sin, O basest of men."

**8.30.60**  The Kurus together with the Pāñcālas, the Śālvas, the Matsyas and the Naimiṣas, the Kosalas, the Kāśis, the Aṅgas, the Kaliṅgas, and the Māgadhas as well —

**8.30.61**  and the illustrious Cedis — these know the eternal law; and good men in many lands, for the most part, keep to it, save those who have lapsed.

**8.30.62**  From the lands of the Kurus and Pāñcālas down to the Matsyas, and from the Naimiṣa region the excellent Cedis — these good folk live by the ancient law, all save the Madras and the crooked people of the Five Rivers.

**8.30.63**  Knowing such discourses on the law, O king, you ought to stay silent like a dolt, Śalya; for you are the protector and ruler of that people, and you take a sixth part of their merit and their sin alike.

**8.30.64**  Or rather, you take only a share of their evildoing, since you fail to protect them; the king who guards his subjects has a share in their merit, but you have a share only in their demerit.

**8.30.65**  In olden times, when the eternal law was honored in every land, the Grandsire, beholding the ways of the Five Rivers, cried out, "Shame!"

**8.30.66**  On account of those outcaste, low-bred men of unclean deeds, the Grandsire scorned the practice of the Five Rivers; even when the four orders abide each in its own duty, he would not honor that custom.

**8.30.67**  Know this too, O Śalya — come, I will tell you yet more — what the Rākṣasa Kalmāṣapāda said as he was sinking in a lake:

**8.30.68**  "Begging is the defilement of a Kṣatriya, falsehood the defilement of a Brāhmaṇa; the Bāhlīkas are the defilement of the earth, and the Madra women the defilement of all womankind."

**8.30.69**  A certain king, having pulled the night-prowler up as he was sinking, questioned him; and what that demon then declared, hear it now.

**8.30.70**  "The Mlecchas are the defilement of mankind, the Mauṣṭikas the defilement of the Mlecchas; the Śaṇḍas are the defilement of the Mauṣṭikas, and the priests of kings the defilement of the Śaṇḍas."

**8.30.71**  "And the defilement of those Madrakas who employ kings' priests as their sacrificers — that very defilement shall be yours, if you do not let me go."

**8.30.72**  Thus, for those afflicted by a Rākṣasa or struck down by the power of poison, this demon's utterance was declared a proven remedy, a charm against such ills.

**8.30.73**  The Pāñcālas keep the Vedic law, the Kauravas their own duty; the Matsyas embody truth, the Śūrasenas sacrifice; the Easterners are slaves, the Southerners low men, the Bāhlīkas are thieves, and the Surāṣṭras born of mixed castes.

**8.30.74**  Ingratitude, the seizing of another's wealth, the drinking of liquor, and the violating of a teacher's wife — for those whose law is this there is no such thing as sin; shame upon the Āraṭṭas, the people of the Five Rivers!

**8.30.75**  From the Kurus to the Pāñcālas, the Naimiṣas, and the Matsyas too — these know the law; and the Kaliṅgas, the Aṅgas, the Māgadhas, and the elders live by the ordinances of the wise.

**8.30.76**  The eastern quarter is held by the gods, with Agni the knower-of-beings at their head; the southern, guarded by Yama of holy deeds, belongs to the ancestral fathers.

**8.30.77**  The western quarter the mighty Varuṇa guards, watching over the Asuras; the northern is the lord Soma's, friend of the Veda, dwelling there together with the Brāhmaṇas.

**8.30.78**  Himavat presides over the Rākṣasas and Piśācas, Gandhamādana over the Guhyakas; Dhruva watches over all beings, and Viṣṇu Janārdana over the worlds.

**8.30.79**  The Māgadhas understand a hint, the Kosalas a mere glance; the Kurus and Pāñcālas grasp a half-spoken word, the Śālvas need the whole command; but the hillmen are as rugged as the very mountains.

**8.30.80**  The Yavanas, O king, are all-knowing and brave above all; the Mlecchas follow only their own usages; but other folk understand nothing unless it is plainly told.

**8.30.81**  The Bāhlīkas stand condemned, and no Madrakas escape it either; and you, Śalya, being of such a stock, have no right to make reply.

**8.30.82**  Knowing this, hold your peace and do not work against me; else I will slay you first, and then slay Keśava and Arjuna both.

**8.30.83**  Śalya said: The abandoning of the sick and the selling of one's own wives and sons go on among the Aṅgas, Karṇa — those people whose overlord you are.

**8.30.84**  What Bhīṣma once said of you in the reckoning of chariot-warriors and master-warriors — knowing these faults of your own, lay aside your wrath, and be not angry.

**8.30.85**  Everywhere there are Brāhmaṇas, everywhere Kṣatriyas, Vaiśyas, and Śūdras too, O Karṇa, and virtuous women faithful to their vows.

**8.30.86**  And men everywhere take their pleasure in jesting, one with another, cutting at each other in raillery; in every land there are those given to such sport.

**8.30.87**  Everyone is forever skilled at finding fault with others, but knows nothing of his own faults — or, knowing them, is blind.

**8.30.88**  Saṃjaya said: Karṇa made no answer, and Śalya turned to face the foe; then Rādheya, laughing once more, urged him on again, saying, "Drive on!"

**8.31.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then Karṇa, surveying the matchless battle-array of the Pārthas, that breaker of hostile lines guarded by Dhṛṣṭadyumna,

**8.31.2**  advanced with the thunder of his chariot, with lion-roars, and with the blare of instruments, as though making the earth itself tremble.

**8.31.3**  Quivering, as it were, with rage, that battle-mad scorcher of foes, the mighty-splendored one, drew up his ranks in due order, O bull of the Bhāratas,

**8.31.4**  and scattered the Pāṇḍava host as Maghavat scatters the army of the Asuras; bearing down upon Yudhiṣṭhira, he turned him to his left.

**8.31.5**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How, Saṃjaya, did Rādheya array himself against the Pāṇḍavas — those heroes led by Dhṛṣṭadyumna and guarded by Bhīmasena?

**8.31.6**  And who, Saṃjaya, were the flanks and the wings of my army, and how were they posted, drawn up each in its proper place?

**8.31.7**  And how too did the sons of Pāṇḍu array themselves against my men, and how did that great and most terrible battle begin?

**8.31.8**  And where was Bībhatsu when Karṇa advanced upon Yudhiṣṭhira? For who, with Arjuna near at hand, could dare to close with Yudhiṣṭhira?

**8.31.9**  He who once, single-handed, vanquished all beings at the Khāṇḍava — who, save Rādheya, would fight against him and still wish to live?

**8.31.10**  Saṃjaya said: Hear the ordering of the array, and how Arjuna went forth, having committed the king to others' care, and how the battle then came to pass.

**8.31.11**  Kṛpa, son of Śaradvat, O king, and the swift Māgadha, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvata line held the right wing.

**8.31.12**  On their flank Śakuni and the great chariot-warrior Ulūka, with horsemen bearing bright lances, guarded your host.

**8.31.13**  with unshaken men of Gāndhāra and unconquerable hillmen, who came on in swarms like locusts and were as dreadful to look upon as Piśācas.

**8.31.14**  Thirty-four thousand chariots that never turned back — the battle-mad Saṃśaptakas — guarded the left flank.

**8.31.15**  Mustered by your sons and eager to slay Kṛṣṇa and Arjuna, they had as their wing the Kāmbojas, the Śakas, and the Yavanas.

**8.31.16**  At the command of the Sūta's son, with their chariots, horse, and foot, they took their stand, challenging Arjuna and the mighty Keśava.

**8.31.17**  In the center, at the army's head, Karṇa took his post, mailed, with gleaming armor and armlets and garlands, guarding the front of the host.

**8.31.18**  Guarded by your fiercely wrathful sons, that best of weapon-bearers, the hero, shone as he led on the vanguard of the army.

**8.31.19**  Iron-fisted, mighty-armed, with the radiance of the sun and of fire, tawny-eyed and fair to behold, mounted on the shoulders of a great elephant, Duḥśāsana, surrounded by troops, stood at the rear of the array.

**8.31.20**  Behind him, O great king, came King Duryodhana himself, guarded by his own brothers with their gaily caparisoned horses and bright armor.

**8.31.21**  Guarded by the mighty Madrakas and Kekayas joined together, he shone, O great king, like Indra of the hundred sacrifices amid the gods.

**8.31.22**  Aśvatthāman and the foremost great chariot-warriors of the Kurus, and ever-rutting elephants ridden by valiant Mlecchas, followed that array of chariots like rain-shedding clouds.

**8.31.23**  With their banners and pennons, with blazing peerless weapons, and manned by their riders, they shone like mountains clothed in forest.

**8.31.24**  Footguards by the thousand — valiant men bearing pikes and swords, who never turned back — went at the feet of those elephants and foot-soldiers.

**8.31.25**  With its horsemen, chariots, and elephants richly adorned, that king of arrays shone forth like the host of gods and Asuras.

**8.31.26**  Disposed after the fashion of Bṛhaspati, well ordered by its wise commander, the great array seemed to dance, and struck terror into the foe.

**8.31.27**  From its wings and flanks the eager warriors poured forth — foot, horse, chariots, and elephants — like storm-clouds in the rainy season.

**8.31.28**  Then, beholding Karṇa at the army's head, King Yudhiṣṭhira spoke to Dhanaṃjaya, slayer of foes, the matchless hero:

**8.31.29**  "Behold, Arjuna, the great array set in order by Karṇa for the battle; furnished with wings and flanks, the host stands resplendent."

**8.31.30**  "Looking, then, upon this mighty hostile force, let such a plan be devised that it may not overwhelm us."

**8.31.31**  Thus addressed by the king, Arjuna, with hands joined in reverence, said to him: "It is wholly as you say, and not otherwise."

**8.31.32**  "The destruction appointed for it I shall accomplish, O Bhārata; its ruin lies in the slaying of its chief, and that I will achieve."

**8.31.33**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: Therefore you yourself take Rādheya, Bhīmasena Suyodhana, Nakula Vṛṣasena, and Sahadeva the son of Subala;

**8.31.34**  Śatānīka take Duḥśāsana, the bull of the Śini line take Hārdikya, Dhṛṣṭadyumna take Droṇa's son, and I myself will go against Kṛpa;

**8.31.35**  let the sons of Draupadī with Śikhaṇḍin take the remaining sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra; and let each of my men slay each of those several enemies of ours.

**8.31.36**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed by the King of Righteousness, Dhanaṃjaya answered, "So be it," and gave his orders to his own troops, and himself advanced to the army's front.

**8.31.37**  Then, seeing that chariot of wondrous aspect coming on, Śalya spoke once more to Karṇa, the son of Adhiratha, that man maddened for battle:

**8.31.38**  "Here comes that very chariot, drawn by white horses and driven by Kṛṣṇa — the son of Kuntī, slaying his foes, after whom you have been asking again and again."

**8.31.39**  "A tumultuous din is heard, the loud rumble of chariot-wheels; this dust has risen up and stands veiling the very sky."

**8.31.40**  "Pounded by chariot-wheels the earth trembles, Karṇa; this great wind blows on every side of your host; and these flesh-eating beasts cry out, making a fearful clamor."

**8.31.41**  "Behold, Karṇa, most dreadful, terrifying, and hair-raising, a headless trunk dark as a cloud, standing there veiling the sun."

**8.31.42**  "Behold how the sun is stared at from every quarter by manifold herds of beasts and by mighty, arrogant tigers."

**8.31.43**  "Behold the herons and the vultures gathered by the thousand, standing fronting us, dreadful, calling out one to another."

**8.31.44**  "White are the horses yoked to your great chariot, Karṇa; these meteors blaze, and your banner too is trembling."

**8.31.45**  "Behold his horses springing forward, huge of frame and swift, fair to see, as though flying through the sky like Garuḍas."

**8.31.46**  "Surely, at these omens, the kings, slain in hundreds and in thousands, Karṇa, shall lie sleeping, covering the earth."

**8.31.47**  "A tumultuous, hair-raising sound is heard, Rādheya — of conch-shells and of war-drums and of tabors, on every side."

**8.31.48**  "Hear, Karṇa, the manifold twang of arrows, the din of men and horses and chariots, and the noise of bowstrings, hand-guards, and shafts of those great-souled warriors."

**8.31.49**  Wrought by craftsmen from cloths burnished with gold and silver, hangings of many hues gleam upon the chariot, trembling as the wind sets them astir.

**8.31.50**  Behold these banners, hung with little bells and bearing emblems of the golden moon, the stars, and the sun—they flash, O Karṇa, on Arjuna's car like streaks of lightning in a thundercloud.

**8.31.51**  The standards jangle and chime, driven by the wind, and the chariots too, with their pennons, belong to the high-souled Pāñcālas.

**8.31.52**  As he smites the flood of your elephants, horses, chariots, and foot-soldiers, the tip of his banner can be seen, and the twang of his bowstring is heard.

**8.31.53**  Today, Karṇa, you shall behold that hero of the white horses, whose charioteer is Kṛṣṇa, striking down his foes in battle—the very warrior of whom you keep asking.

**8.31.54**  Today, Karṇa, you shall see those two tigers among men, red-eyed scorchers of the foe, Vāsudeva and Arjuna, standing together upon a single car.

**8.31.55**  He whose charioteer is the Vārṣṇeya and whose bow is the Gāṇḍīva—if you can slay him, O Rādheya, you shall become our king.

**8.31.56**  "Summoned by the Saṃśaptakas, he has gone straight to meet them, and that mighty one wreaks havoc upon those foes in the fray"—so spoke the lord of the Madras, and Karṇa, full of furious wrath, answered him.

**8.31.57**  Look—beset on every side by the enraged Saṃśaptakas, the son of Pṛthā is no more to be seen, veiled as the sun is veiled by clouds. This is his end, O Śalya: Arjuna has sunk in an ocean of sorrow.

**8.31.58**  Śalya said: Who could slay Varuṇa with water, or the fire with fuel? Who could fetter the wind, or who could drink up the great ocean?

**8.31.59**  Such, I deem, would be the subduing of Pṛthā's son in battle. For Arjuna cannot be conquered by all the gods and demons together, with Indra at their head.

**8.31.60**  Yet if it pleases you to be so deluded, speak on and be of good cheer; but he cannot be conquered in war—set your heart upon some other wish.

**8.31.61**  He who could vanquish Arjuna in battle could lift up the earth with his two arms, could in his wrath burn up these creatures, could cast down the gods from the third heaven.

**8.31.62**  Behold the hero, Kuntī's son Bhīma, who toils untiringly, the mighty-armed one blazing in his splendor, standing immovable as Mount Meru.

**8.31.63**  Impatient of insult, ever seething, nursing his long grudge, here stands the valiant Bhīma in the fight, athirst for victory.

**8.31.64**  Here stands the best of the upholders of righteousness, the Dharma-king Yudhiṣṭhira, hard for foes to withstand in battle, that conqueror of the cities of his enemies.

**8.31.65**  And here stand those two tigers among men, brothers like the twin Aśvins—Nakula and Sahadeva—unconquerable in the fight.

**8.31.66**  Yonder are seen these five sons of Kṛṣṇā, arrayed for war like five mountains, each one Arjuna's equal in the field.

**8.31.67**  And here are the sons of Drupada, with Dhṛṣṭadyumna at their head—heroes of surpassing might, though bereft now of Satyajit—holding their ground.

**8.31.68**  Even as those two lions among men were thus conversing, the two armies clashed violently together, like the Gaṅgā meeting the Yamunā.

**8.32.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When the hosts were thus arrayed and locked in conflict, O Saṃjaya, how did the son of Pṛthā go against the Saṃśaptakas, and Karṇa against the Pāṇḍavas?

**8.32.2**  Tell me this battle in full, for you are skilled in the telling; I never tire of hearing the valorous deeds of heroes in the fray.

**8.32.3**  Saṃjaya said: Having stationed his mighty army in its place to face the foe, Arjuna marshalled a battle-array against your son's ill counsel.

**8.32.4**  Thick with horsemen and elephants, crowded with foot and chariots, that great host, arrayed by Dhṛṣṭadyumna and the other leaders, shone resplendent.

**8.32.5**  With horses dove-grey in hue, and splendor like the moon's and the sun's, the son of Pṛṣata, bow in hand, blazed forth like Death himself made flesh.

**8.32.6**  About the son of Pṛṣata stood the sons of Draupadī, eager for battle, dread of aspect, with their followers, as the host of stars about the moon.

**8.32.7**  Then, when the hosts were marshalled, Arjuna, beholding the Saṃśaptakas on the field, rushed upon them in wrath, brandishing the Gāṇḍīva bow.

**8.32.8**  Then the Saṃśaptakas charged at Pṛthā's son, athirst to slay, resolved upon victory, having made death their only turning-back.

**8.32.9**  That force, teeming with troops of horse, thronged with rutting elephants and chariots, dense with footmen and floods of brave warriors, rushed swiftly upon Arjuna.

**8.32.10**  The tumultuous clash between them and the Diademed One was such as we had heard of in his fight against the Nivātakavacas.

**8.32.11**  Chariots, horses, banners, elephants, foot-soldiers, and lords of chariots, arrows, bows, swords, discuses, and battle-axes—

**8.32.12**  —and arms upraised with their weapons, and weapons held aloft, and heads of his foes by the thousand, the son of Pṛthā cut asunder.

**8.32.13**  Thinking his chariot sunk in that great whirlpool of an army, like the whirlpool of the nether deep, the Saṃśaptakas roared aloud for joy.

**8.32.14**  But Bībhatsu in his wrath slew his foes before him, and with the rear and the north and the south of his car as well, like Rudra slaughtering the beasts.

**8.32.15**  Then, O venerable one, between the Pāñcālas, the Cedis, and the Sṛñjayas and your warriors there raged a most terrible battle.

**8.32.16**  Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman, and Śakuni the son of Subala, their troops exultant and furious, smiting hard with their hosts of chariots—

**8.32.17**  —battle-maddened, they fought against the Kosalas, the Kāśis, the Matsyas, the Karūṣas, the Kekayas, and the brave Śūrasenas, valiant warriors all.

**8.32.18**  That fight, which made an end of them, destroyed the sins of their bodies; for those warriors—Śūdra, Vaiśya, and Kṣatriya—it was righteous, heaven-winning, and bestowing fame.

**8.32.19**  Duryodhana too, O bull of the Bhāratas, together with his brothers, guarded by the foremost Kuru heroes and the great car-warriors of the Madras,

**8.32.20**  —that Kuru hero shielded Karṇa as he fought in the field against the Pāṇḍavas with the Pāñcālas, the Cedis, and Sātyaki.

**8.32.21**  And Karṇa too, smiting down the great host with his sharpened arrows and crushing the foremost car-warriors, sorely beset Yudhiṣṭhira.

**8.32.22**  Stripping his foes by the thousand of their armor, their weapons, their bodies, and their breath, and joining them to heaven and to fame, he brought joy to his own.

**8.32.23**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How Karṇa, having pierced the army of the sons of Pṛthā and wrought slaughter among men, fell upon the king—tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**8.32.24**  Which heroes of the Pārthas held Karṇa back in the fight? And whom did the son of Adhiratha crush before he beset Yudhiṣṭhira?

**8.32.25**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing the Pārthas arrayed with Dhṛṣṭadyumna at their head, Karṇa, the harrower of foes, charged swiftly upon the Pāñcālas.

**8.32.26**  As he rushed swiftly on, the Pāñcālas, flushed with the look of victory, went forth to meet him, O great king, like wild geese flying toward the mighty ocean.

**8.32.27**  Then on both sides arose the heart-stirring blast of a thousand conches and the terrible thunder of war-drums.

**8.32.28**  And the sound of many instruments, the clamor of elephants, horses, and chariots, and the lions' roar of the heroes grew terrible in that hour.

**8.32.29**  The earth with its mountains, trees, and seas, the sky with its winds and clouds, and the heaven with its sun, moon, planets, and stars—all visibly reeled.

**8.32.30**  Living things were overwhelmed by that uproar and were sore afflicted; and of the creatures that could not swim, the most part perished.

**8.32.31**  Then Karṇa, exceedingly wrathful, loosing his weapons swiftly, smote the Pāṇḍava army as Maghavat smote the host of demons.

**8.32.32**  Swiftly piercing among the Pāṇḍava chariots and loosing his shafts, he slew seventy and seven of the foremost of the Prabhadrakas.

**8.32.33**  Then that best of car-warriors, with twenty-five fair-feathered, whetted chariot-arrows, slew five and twenty Pāñcālas.

**8.32.34**  With gold-feathered iron shafts that cleave through the bodies of foes, the hero slew the Cedis by hundreds and by thousands.

**8.32.35**  As he wrought thus in battle his more-than-human deeds, O great king, the chariot-throngs of the Pāñcālas hemmed him round.

**8.32.36**  Then fitting five unbearable shafts to his bow, O Bhārata, Karṇa Vaikartana, the bull among men, slew five Pāñcālas.

**8.32.37**  Bhānudeva, Citrasena, and Senābindu, O Bhārata, and Tapana and Śūrasena—these Pāñcālas he slew in the fray.

**8.32.38**  As the brave Pāñcālas were being struck down by his arrows, a great wail of woe arose among the Pāñcālas in that mighty battle.

**8.32.39**  As they were scattered in rout, the quarters were filled with their cries of woe; and again Karṇa swiftly slew them with his feathered shafts.

**8.32.40**  Now the guards of Karṇa's chariot-wheels were his two sons, O venerable one, hard to conquer—Suṣeṇa and Satyasena—who fought, having staked their very lives.

**8.32.41**  And the guard of Karṇa's rear was his eldest son, the great car-warrior Vṛṣasena, who himself protected Karṇa from behind.

**8.32.42**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Sātyaki, the sons of Draupadī, Vṛkodara, Janamejaya and Śikhaṇḍin, and the heroic Prabhadrakas,

**8.32.43**  the Cedis, Kekayas, and Pāñcālas, the twins and the Matsyas, all mailed and smiting hard, charged upon Rādheya, eager to slay him.

**8.32.44**  With weapons of every kind and with showers of arrows they rained upon him, crushing him, as in the rainy season the clouds beat upon a mountain.

**8.32.45**  But Karṇa's sons, smiting fiercely, sought to save their father, and your other heroes too, O king, held those heroes back.

**8.32.46**  Suṣeṇa, having cut Bhīmasena's bow with a broad-headed shaft and pierced Bhīma in the breast with seven iron arrows, let out a roar.

**8.32.47**  Then Vṛkodara of dread prowess, taking up another stout bow and stringing it, cut asunder Suṣeṇa's bow.

**8.32.48**  And in his wrath, as though dancing, he pierced him with nine arrows, and swiftly pierced Karṇa too with three and seventy whetted shafts.

**8.32.49**  And Satyasena, Karṇa's son, with his horses, his charioteer, his banner, and his weapons, he struck down with ten shafts in the very midst of his beholding friends.

**8.32.50**  His head, struck off by the razor-arrow, its face fair as the moon, was lovely still to look upon, like a lotus fallen from its stalk.

**8.32.51**  Having slain Karṇa's son, Bhīma fell once more upon your men; and severing the bows of Kṛpa and the son of Hṛdika, he afflicted those two as well.

**8.32.52**  Piercing Duḥśāsana with three and Śakuni with six iron shafts, he made both Ulūka and Patatrin chariotless.

**8.32.53**  "Ho, Suṣeṇa, you are slain!"—so crying, he took up an arrow; but Karṇa cut it down and struck him with three shafts.

**8.32.54**  Then Bhīma seized yet another, well-jointed and keenly sharpened, and loosed it at Suṣeṇa; but that one too the bull-like Karṇa cut down.

**8.32.55**  Again Karṇa, seeking to save his son, pierced the cruel Bhīmasena with three and seventy chariot-arrows, fierce shafts loosed in his desire to kill.

**8.32.56**  And Suṣeṇa, seizing an excellent bow that could bear a heavy strain, smote Nakula with five arrows in the arms and the breast.

**8.32.57**  But Nakula, piercing him in turn with twenty stout, strain-bearing shafts, let out a mighty roar that struck fear into Karṇa.

**8.32.58**  Then Suṣeṇa, O great king, that great car-warrior, having pierced him with ten swift arrows, quickly cut his bow with a razor-shaft.

**8.32.59**  Then Nakula, beside himself with wrath, took up another bow and held Suṣeṇa back in the encounter with many arrows.

**8.32.60**  And that slayer of hostile heroes, O king, covering the quarters with his arrows, smote his charioteer, and then struck Suṣeṇa with three shafts, and cut his stout bow in three with three broad-headed arrows.

**8.32.61**  Then Suṣeṇa, beside himself with rage, took up another bow and pierced Nakula with sixty shafts and Sahadeva with seven.

**8.32.62**  That battle grew vast and terrible, like the war of gods and demons, as they swiftly struck one another with arrows, each bent on the other's death.

**8.32.63**  Sātyaki, having slain Vṛṣasena's charioteer with three shafts, cut his bow with a broad arrow, killed his horses with seven, struck down his banner with a single dart, and pierced him in the heart with three.

**8.32.64**  Then, having sunk down upon his own chariot, he rose again in a moment, and bearing sword and shield rushed at the scion of Śini, eager to slay him.

**8.32.65**  As Vṛṣasena came bounding swiftly forward, Sātyaki struck his sword and shield with ten boar-eared arrows.

**8.32.66**  But Duḥśāsana, seeing him stripped of chariot and weapon, took him up at once into his own car and bore him off to another chariot.

**8.32.67**  Then, mounting another chariot, Vṛṣasena the great warrior, unassailable in battle, once more guarded Karṇa's rear.

**8.32.68**  And the scion of Śini, with nine and nine swift shafts, stripped Duḥśāsana of charioteer, horses, and car, and struck him with three upon the brow.

**8.32.69**  But he, mounting another chariot duly equipped, fought once more against the Pāṇḍavas, swelling the strength of Karṇa's host.

**8.32.70**  Then Dhṛṣṭadyumna pierced Karṇa with ten shafts, the sons of Draupadī with seventy-three, and Yuyudhāna with seven.

**8.32.71**  Bhīmasena struck him with sixty-four, Sahadeva with five, Nakula with thirty arrows, Śatānīka with seven, the hero Śikhaṇḍin with ten, and the King of Righteousness with a hundred.

**8.32.72**  These and other foremost heroes, O lord of kings, greedy for victory, fell upon that mighty archer, the charioteer's son, in the great affray.

**8.32.73**  Them the charioteer's son, that valiant subduer of foes, ranging gracefully upon his car, pierced in return with ten and ten sharpened arrows each.

**8.32.74**  There, O great king, we beheld the might of Karṇa's weapons and the deftness of that high-souled one, and it seemed a thing of wonder.

**8.32.75**  For they could not see that great warrior taking up his arrows, nor fitting them to the string, nor loosing them in his fury.

**8.32.76**  Heaven, sky, earth, and the quarters were swiftly overrun with his keen shafts; and in that place the firmament shone as if veiled with crimson cloud.

**8.32.77**  As if dancing, bow in hand, the glorious son of Rādhā pierced in turn each of those by whom he had been struck, with thrice as many arrows.

**8.32.78**  And piercing them again with ten and ten apiece, he roared aloud; whereupon they, with their horses, charioteers, banners, and parasols, gave way and left him an opening.

**8.32.79**  Crushing those mighty archers with his showers of shafts, the son of Rādhā, tormentor of foes, entered the king's host where its ranks were thinnest.

**8.32.80**  Having slain three hundred chariots of the unretreating Cedis, the son of Rādhā with his keen arrows then assailed Yudhiṣṭhira.

**8.32.81**  Then those Pāṇḍavas, O king, with Śikhaṇḍin and Sātyaki, encircled the king to shield him from the son of Rādhā.

**8.32.82**  In like manner all your own host, the great archers of the army, intent in battle, guarded Karṇa, irresistible in the fray, on every side.

**8.32.83**  The blare of many instruments arose, O lord of peoples, and the lion-roar of the unretreating warriors broke forth.

**8.32.84**  Then once again the Kurus and Pāṇḍavas, fearless, came together — the sons of Pṛthā led by Yudhiṣṭhira, and we led by the charioteer's son.

**8.33.1**  Saṃjaya said: Karṇa, having rent that host asunder, rushed upon the King of Righteousness, surrounded by thousands of chariots, elephants, horsemen, and foot-soldiers.

**8.33.2**  The thousands of varied weapons hurled by his foes that bull among men cut apart with hundreds of fierce arrows, and, unperturbed, pierced his enemies in turn.

**8.33.3**  He severed their heads, their arms, and their thighs on every side; slain, they fell to the earth, while others, broken, fled away.

**8.33.4**  But the Draviḍas, Andhras, and Niṣādas, urged on once more by Sātyaki, those foot-soldiers fell upon Karṇa in battle, seeking to slay him.

**8.33.5**  Stripped of arms, heads, and helmets, struck down by Karṇa's shafts, they fell upon the earth all at once, like a felled grove of sāla trees.

**8.33.6**  Thus hundreds, thousands, and tens of thousands of warriors, slain in battle, sank to the earth in their bodies, filling the quarters with their fame.

**8.33.7**  Then the Pāṇḍavas and Pāñcālas hemmed in Karṇa Vaikartana, wrathful in battle like Death himself, as men hem in a disease with spells and herbs.

**8.33.8**  But crushing them, he fell once more upon Yudhiṣṭhira, like some most virulent disease that defies the remedy of spell and herb.

**8.33.9**  Hemmed in by the Pāṇḍavas, Pāñcālas, and Kekayas, all eager to seize the king, he could not break past them, as Death cannot pass a knower of Brahman.

**8.33.10**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, slayer of hostile heroes, his eyes red with wrath, spoke to Karṇa, who stood at no great distance, held in check.

**8.33.11**  Karṇa, Karṇa of vain insight, son of a charioteer, hear my word! Ever in battle you vie with the glorious Phalguna, and ever you harry us, standing fast in the will of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son.

**8.33.12**  Whatever strength, whatever valor, and whatever hatred of the Pāṇḍavas is yours — show it all today, calling up your utmost manhood; for this day, in this great battle, I shall purge you of your appetite for war.

**8.33.13**  Having spoken thus, O great king, the son of Pāṇḍu then pierced Karṇa with ten keen iron arrows fledged with gold.

**8.33.14**  The charioteer's son, that subduer of foes and mighty archer, pierced him in return with nine calf-toothed shafts, as though laughing, O Bhārata.

**8.33.15**  Then the hero, with two razor-tipped shafts and with smooth-jointed arrows, slew in battle the two Pāñcāla guardians of the high-souled king's chariot-wheels.

**8.33.16**  Those two foremost heroes, on either flank of the King of Righteousness, had shone beside his chariot like the twin Punarvasu stars beside the moon.

**8.33.17**  Yudhiṣṭhira again pierced Karṇa with thirty shafts, and struck Suṣeṇa and Satyasena with three apiece.

**8.33.18**  He pierced Śalya with ninety arrows and the charioteer's son with seventy-three, and pierced those who guarded him with three straight-flying shafts each.

**8.33.19**  Then the son of Adhiratha, laughing and shaking his bow, cleft the king's bow with a broad arrow, pierced him with sixty shafts, and roared aloud in joy.

**8.33.20**  Then the foremost heroes of the Pāṇḍavas ran toward Yudhiṣṭhira, seeking to save him from the charioteer's son, and assailed Karṇa with their arrows.

**8.33.21**  Sātyaki and Cekitāna, Yuyutsu and the Pāṇḍya, Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, the sons of Draupadī, and the Prabhadrakas;

**8.33.22**  the twins and Bhīmasena, and the son of Śiśupāla, the Karūṣas, the surviving Matsyas, the Kekayas, the Kāśis and Kosalas — these heroes, in haste, held Vasuṣeṇa in check.

**8.33.23**  And Janamejaya the Pāñcāla pierced Karṇa with his shafts — with boar-eared darts, iron arrows, tubular bolts, and keen arrows, with calf-toothed shafts, broad-headed darts, razor-edged and sparrow-mouthed arrows.

**8.33.24**  With diverse and dreadful weapons the riders of chariots, elephants, and horses rushed at Karṇa from every side, hemming him in with intent to kill.

**8.33.25**  Beset on all sides by the foremost of the Pāṇḍavas, he invoked the Brāhma weapon, filling the quarters with his arrows.

**8.33.26**  Then the fire that was Karṇa, with arrows for its mighty flames and his valor for its heat, ranged gracefully through the battle, consuming the forest of the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.33.27**  That mighty archer, having turned aside the great weapons of those high-souled warriors, laughed and cut the bow of the lord of men with his shafts.

**8.33.28**  Then, fitting ninety smooth-jointed arrows in the twinkling of an eye, Karṇa with his keen shafts pierced through the king's armor in the battle.

**8.33.29**  That mail, wrought with gold, gleamed as it fell, like the remnant of a lightning-laden cloud, scattered by the wind from before the sun.

**8.33.30**  That body of the lord of men, its armor fallen away, shone adorned with celestial jewels, like the cloudless sky by night.

**8.33.31**  Then the son of Pṛthā, stripped of mail by the arrows and drenched in blood, in his fury hurled an all-iron lance at the son of Adhiratha.

**8.33.32**  That lance, blazing as it were in the sky, he cut with seven arrows; severed by the mighty archer's shafts, it fell to the ground.

**8.33.33**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, striking Karṇa upon both arms, upon the brow, and upon the heart with four javelins, roared aloud in joy.

**8.33.34**  Karṇa, blood streaming forth, hissing in his wrath like a serpent, cut the banner with a broad arrow, pierced the Pāṇḍava with three, severed his two quivers, and shattered his chariot to bits.

**8.33.35**  Thus the son of Pṛthā withdrew, his flank-guards and charioteer slain, unable, in his despair, to stand before Karṇa's face.

**8.33.36**  The son of Rādhā, rushing upon him, touched his shoulder with his hand and spoke, O king, laughing, as if to revile the Pāṇḍava:

**8.33.37**  How should one born in a noble line, established in the warrior's duty, abandon his foes in combat to save his own life in the great battle?

**8.33.38**  It is my belief that you are not skilled in the duties of a warrior; you are suited rather to the strength of the brahmin — to study and the rites of sacrifice.

**8.33.39**  Do not fight, son of Kuntī; do not close with heroes, do not speak harsh words to them, and do not venture into the great battle.

**8.33.40**  Having so spoken, the mighty one let the son of Pṛthā go and fell to smiting the Pāṇḍava host as the thunderbolt-bearer smites the demon hordes; then the lord of men, O king, departed swiftly, as if ashamed.

**8.33.41**  Then the Cedis, Pāṇḍavas, and Pāñcālas, and Sātyaki the great warrior, the valiant sons of Draupadī, and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī, followed the king as he withdrew, unshaken.

**8.33.42**  Then Karṇa, seeing Yudhiṣṭhira's host turned away in flight, pursued its rear together with the Kuru heroes who rode behind him.

**8.33.43**  Then, with the blare of conches and kettledrums and the twang of bows, the lion-roar of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons arose.

**8.33.44**  But Yudhiṣṭhira, O scion of Kuru, mounting in haste the chariot of Śrutakīrti, O great king, looked upon Karṇa's prowess.

**8.33.45**  Seeing his host driven before the foe, the King of Righteousness, Yudhiṣṭhira, wrathful, said to those warriors: Slay him! Slay him by the thousand!

**8.33.46**  Then, given leave by the king, the great warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, all led by Bhīmasena, charged against your sons.

**8.33.47**  There arose a tumultuous din of warriors, O Bhārata — of elephants, horses, chariots, and foot-soldiers, and of weapons, on every side.

**8.33.48**  Rise! Strike! Advance! Fall upon them! — so crying out to one another, the warriors slew each other on the field of battle.

**8.33.49**  There, from the showers of arrows in the air, came a darkness as of cloud-shadow, as the foremost of men, met together, struck one another down.

**8.33.50**  Bereft of pennons, banners, and parasols, of horses, charioteers, and weapons, their limbs mangled, their lords slain, the spent warriors fell upon the ground.

**8.33.51**  The noblest of elephants, like the towering peaks of mountains, fell slain with their riders, like crags split by the thunderbolt.

**8.33.52**  Horses by the thousand, their armor and trappings cut, broken, and overturned, fell with their riders, their heroes slain.

**8.33.53**  And bands of foot-soldiers by the thousand, their weapons and limbs scattered about, were slain in the press by elephants, horses, chariots, and by hostile warriors.

**8.33.54**  On every side the earth was strewn with the heads of battle-drunken warriors — heads with broad, long, coppery eyes and faces like the lotus and the moon.

**8.33.55**  And in the wide expanse of the heavens men heard a sound — the celestial cars of the throngs of Apsarases and the strains of their song and music.

**8.33.56**  The heroes slain and cut down while facing the foe — by the thousand the hosts of Apsarases bore them up, one by one, into their celestial cars, and departed.

**8.33.57**  Beholding that great marvel with their own eyes, the heroes, their hearts thrilled with longing for heaven, rushed swiftly against one another.

**8.33.58**  Splendidly they fought in battle—chariot-warriors against chariot-warriors, foot-soldiers against foot-soldiers, elephants against elephants, and horses against horses.

**8.33.59**  As the battle raged thus, with its slaughter of elephants, horses, and men, and the army shrouded in dust, one's own struck down one's own, and the foe struck the foe.

**8.33.60**  The fight blazed hair against hair, tooth against tooth, nail against nail, with fists and grappling—a struggle that destroyed both body and sin.

**8.33.61**  As the battle ran on with its destruction of elephants, horses, and men, a river of blood streamed forth from the bodies of men, horses, and elephants, and it bore along the many fallen bodies of men, horses, and elephants.

**8.33.62**  In that press of men, horses, and elephants, a most terrible river whose waters were blood and whose mire was gore, fed by the slaughtered riders of men, horses, and elephants, flowed on bearing their bodies—a thing of dread to the faint-hearted.

**8.33.63**  Those who sought victory made for its farther shore, some crossing by the fords, some swimming, and yet others sinking and rising up again.

**8.33.64**  Their limbs smeared with blood, their armor, weapons, and garments dyed red, they bathed in that river, drank of its gore, and grew faint, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**8.33.65**  Chariots, horses, men, and elephants, weapons and ornaments, garments and armor, the dying and the dead, the earth, the sky, the heavens, and all the quarters—nearly everything we saw was crimson.

**8.33.66**  From the smell of the blood, from its touch, its taste, its excessive sight, and the very sound of its trickling, a great despondency fell upon nearly the whole army, O Bhārata.

**8.33.67**  That sorely battered army of yours the heroes once more assailed—the chariot-warriors led by Bhīmasena and headed by Sātyaki.

**8.33.68**  Unable to withstand the onset of those great-souled warriors as they charged, the mighty host of your sons turned its back in flight, O king.

**8.33.69**  That host, its chariots, horses, and elephants scattered, choked with men and steeds, its shields and armor shattered, its weapons and bows flung away—

**8.33.70**  —your army fled, churned up on every side, like a herd of elephants harried by a lion in the great forest.

**8.34.1**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing the Pāṇḍavas charging upon them, your army, O king, did not turn back, though your son cried out to them.
