**8.43.72**  Having slain him, he comes on again; behold Vṛkodara smiting other striking elephants, dark as rain-clouds and mounted by their drivers, with bolts like spears and lances.

**8.43.73**  Slaying seven and seven of those elephants together with their pennons and banners with sharp arrows, the elder of the Pārtha cut them down—each single elephant slain by ten iron shafts.

**8.43.74**  Nor is that clamor of the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra now heard, since that bull of the Bhāratas, equal to Puraṃdara in his wrath, has returned to the fray.

**8.43.75**  Three full divisions of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, banded together, were thus checked by the wrathful lion among men, Bhīmasena.

**8.43.76**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing that most difficult feat accomplished by Bhīmasena, Arjuna scattered the remaining foes with his keen arrows.

**8.43.77**  Those bands of the saṃśaptakas, being slain in the battle, O lord, attained the guesthood of Śakra and became free of sorrow, filled with joy.

**8.43.78**  And Pārtha, that tiger among men, with his well-jointed arrows smote the fourfold host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son.

**8.44.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When Bhīmasena had turned back, and the Pāṇḍava Yudhiṣṭhira too, and when my host was being slain by the Pāṇḍavas and Sṛñjayas—

**8.44.2**  and when the flood of my army was streaming away with cries again and again of despair—what then did the Kurus do? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**8.44.3**  Saṃjaya said: Beholding the mighty-armed Bhīma, the glorious son of the Sūta, his eyes red with wrath, O king, rushed upon Bhīmasena.

**8.44.4**  And seeing your army turning its face away from Bhīmasena, the strong one, O king, with great effort steadied it again.

**8.44.5**  Having rallied your son's host, the mighty-armed Karṇa then went forth to meet the Pāṇḍavas, maddened with battle.

**8.44.6**  And the great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas went forth to meet Rādheya, shaking their bows in the fray and loosing their arrows.

**8.44.7**  Bhīmasena, the grandson of Śini, Śikhaṇḍin, Janamejaya, and the strong Dhṛṣṭadyumna, and all the Prabhadrakas as well—

**8.44.8**  and the Pāñcālas, those tigers among men, enraged and flushed with the look of conquest in battle, charged your host on every side.

**8.44.9**  And likewise your great chariot-warriors, O king, charged in haste upon the Pāṇḍavas' host, eager to slay.

**8.44.10**  Dense with chariots, elephants, and horses, thronged with footmen and banners, the army became, O tiger among men, a wondrous sight to behold.

**8.44.11**  And Śikhaṇḍin went against Karṇa, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna against your son Duḥśāsana, O great king, who was girt with a mighty host.

**8.44.12**  Nakula went against Vṛṣasena, and Yudhiṣṭhira against Citrasena, and Sahadeva, O king, advanced upon Ulūka in the battle.

**8.44.13**  Sātyaki went against Śakuni, and Bhīmasena against the Kauravas, while the great chariot-warrior, the son of Droṇa, set himself against the watchful Arjuna in the battle.

**8.44.14**  Gautama fell upon the great archer Yudhāmanyu in the battle, and the strong Kṛtavarman charged against Uttamaujas.

**8.44.15**  Bhīmasena of the mighty arms, O honored one, single-handedly held back all the Kurus and your sons together with their troops.

**8.44.16**  Then Śikhaṇḍin, slayer of Bhīṣma, O great king, checked with his arrows Karṇa, who was ranging fearlessly over the field.

**8.44.17**  Thus opposed, Karṇa, his lips quivering with rage, struck Śikhaṇḍin with three arrows between the brows.

**8.44.18**  Bearing those arrows, Śikhaṇḍin shone in great splendor, like a silver mountain crowned with three peaks.

**8.44.19**  That mighty archer, sorely wounded by the sūta's son in the encounter, pierced Karṇa in battle with ninety sharpened arrows.

**8.44.20**  Karṇa, that great chariot-warrior, slew his horses and his charioteer with three arrows, and tore down his banner with a razor-edged shaft.

**8.44.21**  Then that great chariot-warrior, scorcher of foes, leaping down from his car of slain horses, in fury hurled a spear at Karṇa.

**8.44.22**  Cleaving that spear in battle with three arrows, O Bhārata, Karṇa then pierced Śikhaṇḍin with nine sharpened shafts.

**8.44.23**  Warding off the arrows loosed from Karṇa's bow, that best of men, Śikhaṇḍin, foremost of the victorious, then swiftly withdrew.

**8.44.24**  Then Karṇa, O great king, scattered the Pāṇḍava forces, as a swift-rushing wind falls upon a heap of cotton.

**8.44.25**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna, O great king, hard pressed by your son, pierced Duḥśāsana with three arrows in the breast.

**8.44.26**  Duḥśāsana in turn pierced his left arm, O honored one, with a sharp, gold-feathered, broad-headed arrow of smooth joints.

**8.44.27**  Pierced through, the unforgiving Dhṛṣṭadyumna, enraged, sped a dreadful arrow at Duḥśāsana, O Bhārata.

**8.44.28**  As that arrow loosed by Dhṛṣṭadyumna sped toward him with great force, your son cut it apart with three shafts, O lord of peoples.

**8.44.29**  Then, closing in, he tormented Dhṛṣṭadyumna in the arms and chest with seventeen more broad-headed, gold-adorned arrows.

**8.44.30**  Then that son of Pṛṣata in fury cut his bow apart, O honored one, with a keen-edged razor-shaft, whereupon the people raised a great shout.

**8.44.31**  Then, taking up another bow, your son, O bull of the Bhāratas, hemmed in Dhṛṣṭadyumna on every side with showers of arrows.

**8.44.32**  Beholding the prowess of your high-souled son, the warriors in the field, the Siddhas, and the hosts of Apsarases broke into joyous acclaim.

**8.44.33**  Then there raged between your men and the foe a battle dreadful and terrible of aspect, as though it were the appointed hour of doom for all living things, O scorcher of foes.

**8.44.34**  Vṛṣasena, having pierced Nakula with five iron arrows, struck him again with three others as he stood near his father.

**8.44.35**  Then Nakula, enraged, as if with a smile, pierced Vṛṣasena deep in the heart with a keen iron shaft.

**8.44.36**  Sorely wounded by his powerful foe, that harasser of enemies pierced his foe with twenty arrows, and the other struck him with five.

**8.44.37**  Then those two bulls among men covered each other with a thousand arrows, and the host was broken asunder.

**8.44.38**  Seeing Dhṛtarāṣṭra's army in flight, the sūta's son pursued and forcibly rallied it, O lord of peoples; and when Karṇa had thus turned away, Nakula advanced against the Kauravas.

**8.44.39**  And Karṇa's son, leaving Nakula in the battle, swiftly hastened to guard the wheel of Rādheya's chariot, O honored one.

**8.44.40**  Ulūka, enraged in battle, was checked by Sahadeva; and the valiant Sahadeva, having slain his four horses, dispatched his charioteer to the abode of Yama.

**8.44.41**  Then Ulūka, that delight of his father, leaping down from his car, O lord of peoples, made his way to the full strength of the Trigartas.

**8.44.42**  Sātyaki, having pierced Śakuni with twenty sharpened arrows, cut down the banner of Subala's son with a broad-headed shaft, as if in sport.

**8.44.43**  Subala's son, valiant and enraged in that battle, O king, rent his armor and in turn cut down his golden banner.

**8.44.44**  Then Sātyaki struck him back with sharpened arrows and tormented his charioteer, O great king, with three shafts; and swiftly with his arrows he sent his horses to the abode of Yama.

**8.44.45**  Then Śakuni, that great chariot-warrior, leaping down at once, O bull of the Bhāratas, swiftly mounted Ulūka's car and was quickly borne away from the battle-loving grandson of Śini.

**8.44.46**  Then Sātyaki in the battle, O king, rushed with speed upon the host of your men, and thereupon the array was broken.

**8.44.47**  Driven by the arrows of Śini's grandson, the army, O lord of peoples, swiftly fled in all ten directions and fell as if its life had departed.

**8.44.48**  Your son withstood Bhīmasena in the encounter; but Bhīma in a moment stripped that lord of the realm of horses, charioteer, car, and banner, whereat the Cāraṇas were well pleased.

**8.44.49**  Then the king withdrew there from the range of Bhīmasena; and thereupon the whole Kuru army rushed upon Bhīmasena, and a great uproar arose among those eager to slay Bhīma alone.

**8.44.50**  Yudhāmanyu, having pierced Kṛpa, swiftly cut his bow apart; then Kṛpa, foremost of weapon-bearers, took up another bow.

**8.44.51**  He brought down to the earth Yudhāmanyu's banner, his charioteer, and his parasol; then Yudhāmanyu, that great chariot-warrior, withdrew upon his car.

**8.44.52**  And Uttamaujas at once covered with arrows the son of Hṛdika, of terrible prowess, as a cloud covers a mountain with its rain.

**8.44.53**  That battle was vast and terrible of aspect, O scorcher of foes, such a battle as I had never before beheld, O lord of peoples.

**8.44.54**  Then Kṛtavarman, O king, pierced Uttamaujas in the heart in that combat, and he thereupon sank down on the floor of his car.

**8.44.55**  His charioteer bore that best of car-warriors away upon his chariot; and then in haste, O king, Kṛtavarman fell upon the Pāṇḍava host.

**8.45.1**  Saṃjaya said: Droṇa's son, surrounded by a great array of chariots, charged suddenly, O king, to where the king was stationed.

**8.45.2**  As he charged in suddenly, the hero Pārtha, with Śauri at his side, at once held him back, as the shore holds back the abode of sea-monsters.

**8.45.3**  Then, enraged, O great king, the valiant son of Droṇa covered Arjuna and Vāsudeva with his arrows.

**8.45.4**  Seeing the two Kṛṣṇas thus shrouded, the great chariot-warriors of the Kurus, struck with the utmost wonder, gazed on then.

**8.45.5**  Then Arjuna, as if with a smile, brought forth a celestial weapon; but the brāhmaṇa warded off that weapon in the fight, O Bhārata.

**8.45.6**  Whatever weapon the Pāṇḍava launched in battle, eager to slay, that very weapon the mighty archer, Droṇa's son, beat down.

**8.45.7**  As the dreadful combat of weapons went on, O king, we beheld Droṇa's son in the battle like Death with gaping jaws.

**8.45.8**  Having veiled all the quarters and the intermediate quarters with his crooked-flying shafts, he pierced Vāsudeva with three arrows in the right arm.

**8.45.9**  Then Arjuna, having slain all the horses of that high-souled one, made the ground of the battle a river coursing with streams of blood.

**8.45.10**  Slain by the arrows loosed from Pārtha's bow, the car-warriors fell, and the horses, their harness loosed, ran hither and thither.

**8.45.11**  Seeing that deed of Pārtha, the splendid in battle, Droṇa's son covered Kṛṣṇa on every side with sharpened arrows in the fight.

**8.45.12**  Then, O great king, Droṇa's son, drawing back a feathered shaft, reached Arjuna and struck him in the region of the breast in that encounter.

**8.45.13**  Sorely wounded in the battle by Droṇa's son, O Bhārata, he took up a dreadful iron club and hurled it at Droṇa's son.

**8.45.14**  That iron club, adorned with gold, as it hurtled toward him, Droṇa's son at once cut apart, whereupon the people raised a shout.

**8.45.15**  Shattered in many pieces by the arrows of Bharadvāja's grandson, it fell to the earth, O king, like a mountain riven by the wind.

**8.45.16**  Then Arjuna in the battle pierced Droṇa's son with ten arrows and with a broad-headed shaft swept his charioteer from the chariot-seat.

**8.45.17**  Taking the reins of his horses himself, he covered the two Kṛṣṇas with his arrows; therein we beheld the wondrous swiftness of Droṇa's son's prowess.

**8.45.18**  That he held the horses in check and yet did battle with Phalguna—for that, O king, all the warriors in the field honored him.

**8.45.19**  But when Phalguna was being overborne in battle by Droṇa's son, then Jaya cut apart the reins of his chariot-horses with razor-edged shafts.

**8.45.20**  Those horses, harried by the force of the arrows, bolted away, and thereupon a great clamor again arose in your army, O Bhārata.

**8.45.21**  And the Pāṇḍavas, having won the victory, fell upon your army, loosing sharpened arrows on every side, eager for triumph.

**8.45.22**  By the Pāṇḍavas, O great king, those heroes flushed with victory, the great host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons was broken again and again.

**8.45.23**  Even before the eyes, O great king, of your sons skilled in varied modes of warfare, of Śakuni the son of Subala, and of the high-souled Karṇa,

**8.45.24**  the great army, though rallied by your sons, O lord of men, would not stand its ground in the battle, being smitten on every side.

**8.45.25**  Then, O great king, with the warriors fleeing this way and that in terror, the great host of your sons fell into utter confusion.

**8.45.26**  Though the sūta's son cried again and again, "Stand! Stand!", that army, slaughtered by the high-souled ones, would not stand fast.

**8.45.27**  Then, O great king, a great shout was raised by the Pāṇḍavas, flushed with conquest, as they saw the army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son fleeing on every side.

**8.45.28**  Then Duryodhana said to Karṇa, as if in affection: "See, Karṇa, how sorely our army is afflicted by the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.45.29**  Even while you stand here, it flees on every side in terror. Knowing this, O mighty-armed subduer of foes, do what the hour demands.

**8.45.30**  Thousands of warriors, O bull among men, O hero, being routed by the Pāṇḍavas in battle, cry out to you alone."

**8.45.31**  Hearing this weighty speech of Duryodhana, Rādheya, that joy of the sūta, spoke these words to the king of the Madras:

**8.45.32**  "Behold the might of my arms and of my weapons, O lord of men. This day I shall slay in battle all the Pāñcālas together with the sons of Pāṇḍu. Drive on the horses with care, O tiger among men, O lord of men."

**8.45.33**  Having spoken thus, O great king, the valiant son of the sūta, that hero, took up Vijaya, his ancient and surpassing bow, strung it, O great king, and stroked it again and again.

**8.45.34**  And having stayed his own warriors by a true oath, that mighty one of immeasurable soul brought to bear the Bhārgava weapon.

**8.45.35**  Then, O king, sharp arrows by the thousands, the hundred-thousands, the hundred-millions, and the tens of millions poured forth in that great battle.

**8.45.36**  Veiled by those blazing, most dreadful shafts winged with the feathers of heron and peacock, the Pāṇḍava army could no longer be discerned at all.

**8.45.37**  A great cry of anguish arose among the Pāñcālas, O lord of peoples, tormented in the encounter by the mighty Bhārgava weapon.

**8.45.38**  As elephants fell, O king, and men too by the thousands, and chariots and horses as well, O tiger among men, on every side,

**8.45.39**  the earth trembled, O king, beneath those slain on every side, and the whole great host of the Pāṇḍavas fell into utter confusion.

**8.45.40**  But Karṇa alone, foremost of warriors, blazed forth like a smokeless fire; burning up his foes, O tiger among men, that scorcher of enemies shone resplendent.

**8.45.41**  Slaughtered by Karṇa, the Pāñcālas together with the Cedis reeled this way and that, bewildered like elephants caught in a forest fire; and they cried out, O tiger among men, those best of men, as they had done before.

**8.45.42**  Hearing them cry out, terrified at the head of the battle, fleeing in all directions, O king, panic-stricken on every side, there arose a great wail of anguish, like that of the dead at the dissolution of the world.

**8.45.43**  Seeing them being slain by the Sūta's son, O venerable one, all creatures trembled, even those born in the brute creation.

**8.45.44**  Cut down in battle by the Sūta's son, the Sṛñjayas called out again and again upon Arjuna and Vāsudeva, as the senseless dead cry out upon the King of the Dead in his own city.

**8.45.45**  Then Dhanaṃjaya, son of Kuntī, spoke to Vāsudeva, seeing the most terrible Bhārgava weapon loosed there with fury:

**8.45.46**  "Behold, mighty-armed Kṛṣṇa, the prowess of the Bhārgava weapon! This weapon can in no way be countered in battle.

**8.45.47**  And behold the Sūta's son in his fury, O Kṛṣṇa, in this great battle—a hero like Death himself, working a fearful slaughter.

**8.45.48**  Sharply urging on his horses, he looks toward me again and again; nor do I see any sign that Karṇa would flee from the field.

**8.45.49**  A man who lives wins in battle either victory or defeat; but for one already conquered, O Hṛṣīkeśa, there is only death—how then can there be victory?"

**8.45.50**  Then Janārdana set off, wishing to see Yudhiṣṭhira, and resolved to wear Karṇa down by exertion in the fight, O venerable one.

**8.45.51**  And Kṛṣṇa said to Arjuna: "The king is sorely wounded. Comfort him first, O best of the Kurus, and then you shall slay Karṇa."

**8.45.52**  Then Dhanaṃjaya, to see the king tormented by arrows, drove swiftly upon his chariot through the battle at Keśava's command.

**8.45.53**  As the son of Kuntī went on, wishing to behold the King of Righteousness, he scanned the host but did not see his elder brother there.

**8.45.54**  Having waged an unbearable combat with Droṇa's son, O Bhārata, the wielder of the thunderbolt-like bow, the son of Kuntī overcame in battle the descendant of Bhṛgu.

**8.45.55**  Having vanquished Droṇa's son, that fierce bowman, having accomplished a great and arduous deed of nobility, Dhanaṃjaya then surveyed his own army—he whom no enemies could assail.

**8.45.56**  As he fought, that hero Savyasācin cheered the brave men stationed at the front of the host; praising them with renowned deeds of old, he made firm in the line the warriors of his own chariot-division.

**8.45.57**  But the diademed, garlanded one, not seeing in the fight his eldest brother, scion of Ajamīḍha, approached Bhīma swiftly and said, "What news is there here, O king, of the king?"

**8.45.58**  Bhīma said: "The king, Yudhiṣṭhira, son of Dharma, has withdrawn from here, his limbs broken by Karṇa's arrows—if indeed he yet lives at all."

**8.45.59**  Arjuna said: "Then go you swiftly from here for news of the king, best of the Kurus; for surely, pierced most grievously by Karṇa's shafts, the king has gone to the camp."

**8.45.60**  "He who, in the night battle when it was joined, though pierced most grievously by Droṇa, that impetuous one stood his ground even there, awaiting victory, so long as he was not slain by Droṇa—

**8.45.61**  that foremost of the Pāṇḍavas, that great-souled one, has today been brought into peril by Karṇa in battle. Go quickly now to learn of him, O Bhīma; I shall stand here, holding back the enemy hosts."

**8.45.62**  Bhīma said: "Go you yourself, great-souled one, to learn the news of the king, that bull of the Bhāratas; for if I go there, Arjuna, the warriors will say of me, 'He is afraid.'"

**8.45.63**  Then Arjuna said to Bhīmasena: "The sworn warriors stand arrayed against me; without slaying these, I cannot withdraw from here, from this throng of the enemy host."

**8.45.64**  Then Bhīmasena said to Arjuna: "Relying on my own valor, O foremost of the Kurus, I shall fight back against all the sworn warriors in battle—go, Dhanaṃjaya!"

**8.45.65**  Hearing these words of Bhīmasena, his brother, words most hard to utter in the midst of foes, Arjuna, resolving to go and behold the best of the Kurus, then spoke to the foremost of the Vṛṣṇis:

**8.45.66**  "Urge on the horses, Hṛṣīkeśa, and plunge through this ocean of chariots; I wish to behold King Ajātaśatru, O Keśava."

**8.45.67**  Then the chief of all the Dāśārhas urged on the horses and said this to Bhīma: "This deed of yours today is no wonder, O hero; we shall go—slay the enemy hosts, Bhīma."

**8.45.68**  Then Hṛṣīkeśa drove on to where King Yudhiṣṭhira was, faster and faster, O king, with steeds that rivaled Garuḍa.

**8.45.69**  Having stationed Bhīmasena, that subduer of foes, against the opposing line, and having given Vṛkodara his charge concerning the battle, O lord of kings—

**8.45.70**  then those two foremost of men, having gone and come upon the king lying alone, both descended from the chariot and bowed at the feet of the King of Righteousness.

**8.45.71**  Beholding those two tigers among men come to him unharmed and in joy, O bull among men—the two Kṛṣṇas, like the Aśvins approaching Vāsava—

**8.45.72**  the king welcomed them, as Vivasvat welcomes the Aśvins, or as the preceptor welcomed Śakra and Viṣṇu when the great Asura Jambha had been slain.

**8.45.73**  Believing Karṇa slain, Yudhiṣṭhira, the King of Righteousness, well pleased, addressed those two scorchers of foes in a voice faltering with joy.

**8.46.1**  Saṃjaya said: But seeing those two of great spirit, Keśava and Arjuna, come together, he supposed the son of Adhiratha had been slain in battle by the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva.

**8.46.2**  The son of Kuntī welcomed them with words most gracious and kind, that slayer of foes honoring them with a smile, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**8.46.3**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: "Welcome, son of Devakī! Welcome to you, Dhanaṃjaya! Dear to me indeed is the sight of you both, O Acyuta and Arjuna.

**8.46.4**  Unhurt and unscathed, how did you fight that great chariot-warrior, like a venomous serpent in battle, skilled in every weapon—

**8.46.5**  the foremost of all the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, their refuge and their armor, guarded by Vṛṣasena and by the archer Suṣeṇa—

**8.46.6**  of mighty valor, sanctioned by Rāma, invincible in arms, the protector of the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, who advanced at the head of the host—

**8.46.7**  the destroyer of enemy armies, the crusher of hostile throngs, devoted to Duryodhana's welfare and ever ready for battle with us—

**8.46.8**  unassailable in great battle even by the gods with Vāsava at their head, equal to fire and wind in splendor and in might—

**8.46.9**  deep as the netherworld, the increaser of his friends' joy, like Death to his foes—having slain Karṇa in the great battle, by good fortune you two have come back, like the immortals after conquering an Asura!

**8.46.10**  For today, O Acyuta and Arjuna, I joined battle with that undaunted one, who, like enraged Death bent on destroying all creatures—

**8.46.11**  by him my banner was cut down, and my flank-charioteers were slain, and my horses too were killed, while Yuyudhāna looked on—

**8.46.12**  while Dhṛṣṭadyumna, the twins, the heroic Śikhaṇḍin, the sons of Draupadī, and all the Pāñcālas looked on.

**8.46.13**  Having vanquished these mighty warriors and many enemy throngs, Karṇa overcame me too, O mighty-armed one, as I strove in the great battle.

**8.46.14**  And pursuing me in the fight, that foremost of warriors spoke many a harsh word, again and again, scorning me—of this there is no doubt.

**8.46.15**  That I live at all, Dhanaṃjaya, is by the might of Bhīmasena. But what need of many words here?—I have no will to endure that any longer.

**8.46.16**  For thirteen years, Dhanaṃjaya, in fear of him I got no sleep by night, nor any ease by day at any time.

**8.46.17**  Seized with hatred of him, I am consumed within, Dhanaṃjaya, knowing my own death at hand, like an elephant who has scented the lion.

**8.46.18**  All this time of mine has passed, O lord, in brooding upon this: how might Karṇa be destroyed by me in battle?

**8.46.19**  Waking and sleeping, son of Kuntī, ever I saw Karṇa and Karṇa alone; this whole world seemed to me to have become Karṇa, wherever I turned.

**8.46.20**  Wherever I went, Dhanaṃjaya, in dread of Karṇa, there too I would see Karṇa himself standing before me.

**8.46.21**  And I, O Pārtha, conquered by that hero who never fled from battle, with my horses and my chariot, was spared alive and let go.

**8.46.22**  What use have I now of life, or what use again of a kingdom, thus disgraced as I am here by Karṇa, the ornament of battle?

**8.46.23**  What I never met before from Bhīṣma, from Kṛpa, or from Droṇa in combat, that I have met today in battle from the Sūta's son, the great chariot-warrior.

**8.46.24**  Therefore I ask you, son of Kuntī—for I am in distress—tell me in full how Karṇa was slain by you.

**8.46.25**  He whose valor in battle equaled Śakra's, whose might matched Yama's, and who was Rāma's equal in arms—how indeed was he struck down?

**8.46.26**  Renowned as a great chariot-warrior, skilled in every kind of fighting, the foremost of bowmen, the one man above all others—

**8.46.27**  honored by Dhṛtarāṣṭra and his sons, O lord of peoples, ever for your destruction—that son of Rādhā, how was he slain by you?

**8.46.28**  For of all warriors, Arjuna, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, that bull among men, ever held Karṇa to be your death in battle.

**8.46.29**  That one, O tiger among men, how was he struck down by you in battle? Tell me, Bībhatsu, how Karṇa was slain by you.

**8.46.30**  And his lofty head—was it carried off before the eyes of his friends by you, O tiger among men, as a tiger carries off the head of a deer?

**8.46.31**  He, the Sūta's son, who beset every quarter and region, seeking to seize you in battle—Karṇa, who would give away whole droves of elephants in war—is he now, struck by your keen heron-feathered shafts,

**8.46.32**  slain by you in battle—does that evil-souled Sūta's son lie upon the earth? Have you done me this day my dearest joy, by slaying the Sūta's son in the fight?

**8.46.33**  He who ranged on every side for your undoing, drunk with pride and arrogance, that Sūta's son—he who deemed himself a hero—having met him in battle, have you slain him today in combat?

**8.46.34**  He who, for your undoing, would give away to others a golden chariot yoked with the finest elephants, that wicked one who ever vied in battle—has he been slain by you, my son, in the fight?

**8.46.35**  He who, ever drunk with the pride of his prowess, would boast in the assembly of the Kauravas, the exceedingly dear friend of Suyodhana—has that wicked one been slain by you today?

**8.46.36**  Pierced by the blood-seeking birds loosed from your bow and sped by you, does that wicked one lie today with his body cleft? Is the arm of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son broken at last?

**8.46.37**  He who, full of arrogance, ever boasted in the midst of kings, gladdening Duryodhana, saying in his folly, 'I am the slayer of Phalguna'—has his chariot now been brought to nothing thereby?

**8.46.38**  "Never will I wash my feet so long as Pārtha lives"—such was that dull-witted one's vow at all times, O son of Śakra. Has that Karṇa been slain by you this day?

**8.46.39**  He of evil mind who said to Kṛṣṇā, that Karṇa, in the assembly amid the Kuru heroes, 'Why do you not abandon the Pāṇḍavas, Kṛṣṇā—those weaklings, fallen and bereft of strength?'—

**8.46.40**  That vow which Karṇa made for your undoing—'I shall not come back here without having slain Pārtha together with Kṛṣṇa'—does that evil-minded one now lie with his body riven by arrows?

**8.46.41**  Was this clash of the Sṛñjayas and Kauravas in battle—wherein I was brought to such a plight—made known to him? Has he been slain by you today, joined in combat?

**8.46.42**  Has the head of that exceedingly dull-witted one, with its earrings, radiant as the sun, been severed from its body by you in battle, O Savyasācin, with blazing arrows loosed from the Gāṇḍīva?

**8.46.43**  That for which I, struck down by his arrows, fixed my mind upon you for the slaying of Karṇa, O hero—has that thought of mine been made fruitful by you today through the felling of Karṇa?

**8.46.44**  That with which Suyodhana, full of arrogance, looked down upon us, relying upon Karṇa—has that prop of Suyodhana been broken by you today by your valor?

**8.46.45**  He who once called us 'barren sesame seeds' in the midst of the assembly, before the eyes of the kings—has that evil-minded, exceedingly wrathful Sūta's son, encountered in battle, been slain by you?

**8.46.46**  That evil-souled Sūta's son who, laughing, once said, 'She is won by the son of Subala—drag the daughter of Yajñasena here yourself by force!'—has he too been slain by you here this day?

**8.46.47**  That dull-witted wretch who, though reckoned but half a chariot-warrior, dared to revile the foremost weapon-bearer on earth, our grandsire — that vile son of Adhiratha — has he been slain by you this day?

**8.46.48**  This unrelenting fire, fanned ever by his treachery, lodged within my heart — quench it for me today, O Phalguna, by saying: "That evil-minded man I met and slew this day."

**8.47.1**  Saṃjaya said: Having heard those words of the righteous king, enraged at the son of Adhiratha, the great-souled and victorious Arjuna, of boundless might, spoke thus to the indomitable, undaunted Yudhiṣṭhira:

**8.47.2**  As I was fighting today with the Saṃśaptakas, O king, the son of Droṇa, the vanguard of the Kuru host, suddenly took his stand before me, loosing arrows that flew like venomous serpents.

**8.47.3**  Seeing this cloud-like chariot of mine, the Ambaṣṭha host stood firm, ready to die. Having slain five hundred of them, I then came upon the son of Droṇa, O foremost of kings.

**8.47.4**  Then he discharged volley upon volley of arrows, drawn to the ear and loosed at full stretch, by the force of his training and the strength of his weapons — even as the rain-cloud pours down in the season of storms.

**8.47.5**  We could not tell when he took an arrow nor when he fitted it, nor by which hand — the left or the right — he would let it fly: so swiftly did that son of Droṇa wheel about in the battle.

**8.47.6**  The son of Droṇa pierced me with five sharp arrows and Vāsudeva with five more; but I, in the space of an eyeblink, crushed him with thirty shafts like thunderbolts.

**8.47.7**  Streaming blood from all his limbs, overwhelmed by me, he withdrew into the chariot-ranks of the Sūta's son, not heeding the foremost of his own warriors, all of them drenched in gore.

**8.47.8**  Then, seeing his army crushed in the fight, its warriors scattered, its horses and elephants in flight, the destroyer Karṇa, joined by fifty chief chariot-warriors, came swiftly against me.

**8.47.9**  Having destroyed them and left Karṇa aside, I hastened to see you; for all the Pāñcālas shrink from Karṇa as cattle shrink from the scent of a lion.

**8.47.10**  Like men plunging into the mouth of a great fish, the Prabhadrakas rush upon Karṇa; encountering Karṇa, O king, the Prabhadrakas enter as into the gaping jaws of Death.

**8.47.11**  Come and behold today both me and the Sūta's son, each resolved on victory and eager for the fray. Six thousand royal princes, O Bhārata, those chariot-warriors, have sunk down — bound for heaven and its world.

**8.47.12**  Meeting the Sūta's son in battle, O foremost of kings, as the Thunderer met Vṛtra, I shall fight him hard, O Bhārata — that son of the Sūta — if only he shows himself in this engagement today.

**8.47.13**  If today I do not by main force slay Karṇa as he fights, together with his kinsmen, O king, then may I go to that grievous fate that awaits those who promise and do not perform, O lion among kings.

**8.47.14**  I take my leave of you — wish me victory in battle, before the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra overwhelm Bhīma. I shall slay the Sūta's son, O lion among men, and his army, and all the hosts of our foes.

**8.48.1**  Saṃjaya said: Hearing that Karṇa was hale and of lofty valor, the king Yudhiṣṭhira, tormented by Karṇa's arrows, grew wrathful and spoke these words to Phalguna, to Dhanaṃjaya of measureless might:

**8.48.2**  Had you said this back in the Dvaita forest — "O king, I cannot bring myself to fight Karṇa" — then, O Pārtha, we would all have taken such measures as the hour demanded, then and there.

**8.48.3**  Having promised me his death, O hero, and the trusty army with him, why then, having led us into the very midst of the foe, did you lift us up only to dash us down upon the bare ground?

**8.48.4**  We placed our hopes in you, O Arjuna, longing to attain much that is good and desired; yet all that has come to nothing for us, O prince — like the over-flowering nicula tree to those who craved its fruit.

**8.48.5**  Like a hook hidden by its bait, like a wild ox lured by a false call, you have shown me ruin in the guise of a kingdom — to me who sought a kingdom — and to no purpose at all.

**8.48.6**  When you, dull-witted one, were but seven days old, a voice from the sky said to Pṛthā: "This son who is born has the prowess of Vāsava; he shall vanquish all the heroes who are your foes."

**8.48.7**  "This one, of surpassing might, shall conquer at Khāṇḍava the hosts of the gods and all beings besides; this one shall vanquish the Madrakas, the Kaliṅgas, and the Kekayas; this one shall slay the Kurus in the midst of kings."

**8.48.8**  "No bowman shall ever surpass this one, nor has any ever been who could conquer him. This noble one, master of himself and perfect in every art, could, if he wished, bring all beings under his sway."

**8.48.9**  "In beauty he is the equal of the moon, in swiftness of the wind, in steadfastness of Meru, in forbearance of the earth, in radiance of Sūrya, in splendor of the Lord of Wealth, in heroism of Śakra, in strength of Viṣṇu —

**8.48.10**  so is this great-souled son of yours, O Kuntī — a slayer of foes born as Viṣṇu was born of Aditi: renowned, of measureless might, the preserver of his line, for the victory of his own and the destruction of his enemies."

**8.48.11**  Thus spoke the voice from the sky upon the summit of Śataśṛṅga, in the hearing of the ascetics. Yet you have proved to be no such man this day — surely, then, even the gods speak falsehood.

**8.48.12**  Hearing the like from those other most excellent sages whom we ever revered, I had never thought to bow to Suyodhana — nor did I know you to be one stricken with fear of the son of Adhiratha.

**8.48.13**  Mounted upon that fair, ape-bannered car wrought by Tvaṣṭṛ, its axles never creaking, grasping your sword bright with gold and this Gāṇḍīva bow as tall as a palm-tree, and borne onward by Keśava himself — how, O Pārtha, could you have turned and fled in fear of Karṇa?

**8.48.14**  Had you, base-souled one, handed this bow to Keśava and yourself become the driver in the battle, then Keśava would have slain the fierce Karṇa, as the Lord of the Maruts, thunderbolt in hand, slew Vṛtra.

**8.48.15**  Better had you miscarried in the fifth, the perilous, month, or had Pṛthā's womb never borne you at all! That, O prince, would have spared you this toil — better that than to flee from battle, O base-souled one!

**8.49.1**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed by Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Kuntī, he of the white steeds, in a great rage seized his sword, bent on slaying the bull of the Bhāratas.

**8.49.2**  Seeing his fury, Keśava, the knower of hearts, then said: "What is this, O Pārtha — why have you taken up your sword?"

**8.49.3**  "I see no one here for you to fight, O Dhanaṃjaya; for the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra have all been shattered by wise Bhīma."

**8.49.4**  "You came away from the field, O son of Kuntī, saying the king must be seen; and now you have seen that king, and Yudhiṣṭhira is well."

**8.49.5**  "Now that you behold this tiger among kings, of a tiger's own prowess, and the hour for rejoicing has come — why has wrath seized hold of you?"

**8.49.6**  "I see no one here, O son of Kuntī, who ought to be slain by you. Why then do you so hastily grasp your great sword?"

**8.49.7**  "This I ask of you, O son of Kuntī, O man of wondrous valor: what is it you mean to do, that in your wrath you lay hold of the sword?"

**8.49.8**  Thus addressed by Kṛṣṇa, Arjuna, his eyes fixed upon Yudhiṣṭhira, spoke to Govinda, hissing in his anger like a serpent:

**8.49.9**  "Whoever should urge me, saying 'Give the Gāṇḍīva to another,' him I would behead — such is my secret vow."

**8.49.10**  "That very thing has been said to me by the king, O undaunted one of measureless prowess, here before your face, Govinda; and this I cannot bring myself to forgive."

**8.49.11**  "Therefore I shall slay this king, scrupulous though he be in dharma; by killing this best of men I shall keep my vow. For this end have I taken up the sword, O delight of the Yadus."

**8.49.12**  "So, having slain Yudhiṣṭhira, I shall be discharged of my debt even to truth, and I shall become free of grief and free of fever, O Janārdana."

**8.49.13**  "Or what do you deem fitting in this hour that has now arisen? You, dear one, know all the comings and goings of this world; so shall I act in whatever way you shall direct me."

**8.49.14**  Kṛṣṇa said: Now, O Pārtha, I know that you have never attended upon the elders, since at the wrong hour, O tiger among men, you act with such reckless fury. For one who knows the distinctions of dharma would never act thus, O Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.49.15**  He who treats what ought not to be done as a thing to be done, and what ought to be done as a thing to be left undone — he, O Pārtha, is the basest of men.

**8.49.16**  You do not know the settled conviction of those seers who, following dharma, have attained it — those who understand it both in brief and at length.

**8.49.17**  For a man who lacks sure knowledge in deciding what is to be done and what is not, is helplessly bewildered, O Pārtha — even as you, in your folly, are now bewildered.

**8.49.18**  It is by no means easy to know what should be done and what should not; all this is known through sacred learning, and that learning you do not comprehend.

**8.49.19**  Out of ignorance you, who deem yourself a knower of dharma, would uphold dharma; but a righteous man, O Pārtha, does not look upon the slaying of living beings as dharma.

**8.49.20**  The non-slaying of living creatures, dear one, I hold to be the highest of all; rather should a falsehood be spoken than that one should ever do harm.

**8.49.21**  How then, O best of men, would you slay your eldest brother, the king, a man learned in dharma — as if you were some other, common man?

**8.49.22**  The slaying of one who does not fight, who bears no weapon, who turns his back and flees, or who seeks refuge, who joins his palms in supplication or surrenders — such killing, O Bhārata, is not approved by the wise.

**8.49.23**  And this vow of yours, O Pārtha, was made of old when you were but a boy; therefore, out of folly, you now resolve upon a deed bound up with unrighteousness.

**8.49.24**  How, O Pārtha, would you slay your elder, claiming to be mindful of dharma, without having weighed the course of duties — a course subtle and hard to trace?

**8.49.25**  And I shall declare to you this secret of dharma, O bull of the Bhāratas — what Bhīṣma, or the dharma-knowing Yudhiṣṭhira, might tell you,

**8.49.26**  or what Vidura the steward, or even the renowned Kuntī, might say — that I shall tell you in its truth; mark it well, O Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.49.27**  To speak the truth is good; there is nothing higher than truth. Yet in its essence this is very hard to know — what truth is, for him who would practice it.

**8.49.28**  There may be truth that ought not to be told, and falsehood that ought to be told; when one's whole substance is being plundered, a falsehood ought to be spoken.

**8.49.29**  When life is at stake, and in matters of marriage, a falsehood ought to be spoken; for there are cases where falsehood becomes truth, and truth itself becomes falsehood.

**8.49.30**  The simple-minded man sees as truth only that truth which he himself has practiced; but only after he has discerned aright between truth and falsehood does one become a knower of dharma.

**8.49.31**  What wonder is it that even a man of cultivated wisdom, though most cruel, should gain great merit — as Balāka did by slaying the blind one?

**8.49.32**  And what wonder, again, that a fool, devoted to dharma yet unlearned, should incur grievous sin — as Kauśika did concerning the rivers?

**8.49.33**  Arjuna said: Tell me this, O blessed one, that I may understand it — the tale bound up with Balāka and the blind one, and the tale of the rivers and of Kauśika.

**8.49.34**  Kṛṣṇa said: There was once a certain hunter of deer named Balāka, O Bhārata, who slew beasts to sustain his wife and children, not out of wanton desire.

**8.49.35**  He supported his blind mother and father, and others who depended on him; ever devoted to his own duty, truthful in speech, and free from malice.

**8.49.36**  Once, eager for game and striving hard, he found no quarry; then he caught sight of a beast of prey that had drunk water and saw by scent alone.

**8.49.37**  That creature, the like of which he had never seen before, he then slew; and at once a rain of flowers fell down upon him from the sky.

**8.49.38**  And a delightful celestial chariot, resounding with the song and music of Apsarases, descended from heaven to bear that hunter of deer away.

**8.49.39**  For that creature, O Arjuna, had by its austerities won a boon for the destruction of all beings, and had been made blind by the Self-Existent.

**8.49.40**  By slaying that beast, which was bent upon the annihilation of all beings, Balāka thus went to heaven. So hard to know is dharma.

**8.49.41**  There was also a brāhmaṇa named Kauśika, an ascetic but of little learning, who is said to have dwelt at the meeting of rivers, not far from a village.

**8.49.42**  "I must always speak the truth" — this was the vow he had taken; and so he became renowned as a teller of truth, O Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.49.43**  Now once, in fear of robbers, certain people entered that forest; and the cruel robbers too came on, searching diligently for them.

**8.49.44**  Then, coming up to Kauśika the truth-teller, they said: "By which path, O reverend one, did those many folk go? Adjured by your truth, speak; if you know where they are, tell us."

**8.49.45**  Thus questioned, Kauśika spoke to them a truthful word: "They have taken shelter in this forest, thick with trees and creepers and thickets." And so, the tradition tells, those cruel men found them and slew them.

**8.49.46**  By that great unrighteousness, that ill-spoken word, Kauśika went to a most grievous hell — unskilled in the subtleties of dharma, of scanty learning, a fool who knew not the distinctions of duty.

**8.49.47**  He who, without asking the elders to resolve his doubt, deserves thereby to fall into a great pit. In this there will be some mark and indication for guidance.

**8.49.48**  The highest knowledge is hard to attain; herein one determines it by reasoning. "Sacred revelation is dharma" — so say many people.

**8.49.49**  Yet this I do not gainsay; for not all is ordained therein. The teaching of dharma was made for the welfare and thriving of living beings.

**8.49.50**  From its upholding, they say, dharma takes its name, for dharma upholds all creatures. Whatever is bound up with upholding — that, it is determined, is dharma.

**8.49.51**  When men, wishing to seize what is not theirs by right, demand something, then if release can be had by saying nothing, one should by no means speak out at all.

**8.49.52**  But where one must needs speak, or where silence itself would breed suspicion — there, it is reasoned, to utter a falsehood is better than the truth.

**8.49.53**  In peril of life, or at a marriage, or to save all one's kinsmen and wealth, or in the course of jesting, a falsehood may be spoken — and in this those who see into the true meaning of dharma perceive no wrong.

**8.49.54**  He who is freed of dealings with thieves even by means of oaths — for him to speak falsely there is the better course, and that, beyond all reasoning, is the truth.

**8.49.55**  Nor should wealth be given to such men, by any means, while one has the power to refuse; for wealth given to the wicked torments even the giver. Therefore one who utters falsehood for the sake of dharma does not thereby become a liar.

**8.49.56**  This summary of dharma's marks I have set forth for you according to rule. Having heard it, tell me, Pārtha: is Yudhiṣṭhira to be slain?

**8.49.57**  Arjuna said: As a man of vast wisdom would speak, as one of great understanding would speak, and as is for our good — even so is this speech of yours.

**8.49.58**  You are to us as a mother, and likewise as a father, and our supreme refuge, Kṛṣṇa; therefore your word is wondrous.

**8.49.59**  For in the three worlds there is nothing anywhere unknown to you; therefore you know the highest dharma, all of it, exactly as it is.

**8.49.60**  I hold the Pāṇḍava, Yudhiṣṭhira the Dharma-king, not to be slain. Within the bounds of that compact, speak some favor for him. And hear yet this other thing I would say, that lies within my heart.

**8.49.61**  You know my vow, O Dāśārha: should any man among mortals say to me, 'Give over your Gāṇḍīva, Pārtha, to another,' or, 'There is one who surpasses you in arms' —

**8.49.62**  — him would I slay outright, Keśava; and one who calls me a beardless weakling, Bhīma would slay. Now this very thing the king said to me in your presence, lion of the Vṛṣṇis: 'Give up your bow,' again and again.

**8.49.63**  If I slay him, Keśava, I will not remain even a moment longer in the world of the living. Yet that vow of mine, known throughout the world, must be made true, O foremost of the upholders of dharma. So devise it, Kṛṣṇa, that the Pāṇḍava may live and I as well — such counsel you must give me now.

**8.49.64**  Vāsudeva said: The king is worn out and wounded, gashed by Karṇa in the fray with showers of whetted arrows. That is why, Pārtha, he spoke harshly to you; for upon Karṇa today the whole game of battle is staked.

**8.49.65**  'With him slain, the Kurus will be vanquished' — such is the mind of the king, the son of Dharma. When a man suffers a great humiliation, then, though he lives, he is called as good as dead.

**8.49.66**  This king has ever been honored — by you, by Bhīma, and by the twins, and by elders and the foremost of men throughout the world. Therefore apply to him only a fraction of disrespect.

**8.49.67**  Address this venerable one, Pārtha, with the familiar 'thou' — say 'thou' to Yudhiṣṭhira; for a guru addressed as 'thou' is thereby, as it were, slain, O Bhārata.

**8.49.68**  So conduct yourself, son of Kuntī, toward the Dharma-king Yudhiṣṭhira; effect in this way a token of dishonor, O upholder of the Kurus.

**8.49.69**  For this is a teaching of the Atharvan and the Aṅgirasas, the highest of revealed teachings; and it is to be carried out without question, always, by men who desire their welfare.

**8.49.70**  For this, Pāṇḍava, will be reckoned a slaying of the Dharma-king at your hands; thus shall it be understood. And thereafter, having bowed to his feet and soothed him, you must speak peace, O Pārtha.

**8.49.71**  Your wise brother, the king, the son of Pāṇḍu, will never harbor any anger against you. Freed of falsehood and of a brother's slaying, Pārtha, joyfully strike down Karṇa, the sūta's son.

**8.49.72**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed by Janārdana, Pārtha approved that 'slaying' of his dear one, and then Arjuna spoke to the Dharma-king, harshly and by force, words such as he had never uttered before.

**8.49.73**  Speak not, O king, while others speak; you do not even hold your ground within a krośa's distance of the battle's midst. It is Bhīma who has the right to reproach me — he who fights as the foremost champion of all warriors,

**8.49.74**  — who, in season pressing back the foe in battle and slaying those heroic lords of earth, having killed more than a thousand elephants, roared out his tumultuous lion's roar;

**8.49.75**  That hero does deeds most hard to do, such as you could never do at any time: leaping down from his chariot and seizing his mace, with it he strikes down horses, men, and elephants in the fray.

**8.49.76**  With his fine sword he slays steeds, chariots, horses and elephants, and with chariot-wheels and bow he slays his foes; trampling his enemies underfoot he destroys them, and again with his two arms — he whose valor is that of Indra of the hundred sacrifices.

**8.49.77**  Of mighty strength, the equal of Kubera and of Yama, slayer by main force of his foes as they deserve — it is that Bhīmasena who has the right to reproach me, not you, who are forever guarded by your friends.

**8.49.78**  Crushing great car-warriors, the noblest elephants, horses, and chief among the foot-soldiers, Bhīma alone has plunged into the host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons; it is that subduer of foes who has the right to rebuke me.

**8.49.79**  He who slays in their multitudes the hosts of the Kaliṅgas, Vaṅgas, Aṅgas, Niṣādas, and Māgadhas — ever rut-maddened, like dark thunderclouds — he is able to upbraid me who am blameless.

**8.49.80**  Mounting in season his well-yoked car, drawing his bow, his fist full of arrows, that hero pours forth showers of shafts in the great battle, as a cloud pours forth its streams of water.

**8.49.81**  The wise say that the strength of the best of brahmins lies in speech, but the strength of the warrior in the might of his arms. You, Bhārata, are strong only in words, and harsh withal; you yourself know what manner of man I am.

**8.49.82**  I strive ever to do what is dear to you, with wife and sons and life and my own self; and yet you strike me down with the arrows of your speech. From you we have never known any happiness at all.

**8.49.83**  You have despised me, you who keep to Draupadī's couch, while for your sake I slay great car-warriors; thus, over-suspicious, Bhārata, you are harsh. From you I have known no happiness whatever.

**8.49.84**  By Bhīṣma, true to his word, the manner of his own death was himself declared; and for your pleasure, O lord of men, in battle the heroic Śikhaṇḍin, Drupada's great son, guarded by me, slew him.

**8.49.85**  Nor do I rejoice in your sovereignty, since you clung to the dice to our ruin; having yourself committed a sin practiced only by the ignoble, you now wish to cross over your foes in battle by means of these your brothers.

**8.49.86**  Many are the faults of the dice, contrary to all law — those that Sahadeva named, you heard them. Yet you would not cross beyond those vices, fit only for the wicked, through which we have all gone down to hell.

**8.49.87**  You were the gambler; through you came the loss of the kingdom; from you arose our calamity, O lord of men. Goad us no more with the cruel whips of your words, O king, kindling the wrath of us ill-fated ones.

**8.49.88**  Having made the steadfast-minded king hear these harsh words, Savyasācin cut him to the quick; then the son of the king of gods repented, and sighing deeply drew forth his sword.

**8.49.89**  Kṛṣṇa said to him: Why now do you draw from its sheath this sword, sky-bright? Tell me truly your answer again; then will I speak the word of counsel for the accomplishing of the end.

**8.49.90**  Thus questioned by that best of men, deeply grieved, Arjuna spoke to Keśava: I shall slay my own body by force — this body wherewith I have done so unfit a thing.

**8.49.91**  Hearing that word of Pārtha, the foremost of the upholders of dharma said this to Dhanaṃjaya: Proclaim here your own virtues, Pārtha; thus at once will your heart's grief be lifted from you.

**8.49.92**  'So be it, Kṛṣṇa,' said Dhanaṃjaya, hailing the word, and lowering his bow he spoke to Yudhiṣṭhira, the foremost of the upholders of dharma — 'Hear me, O king,' said the son of Śakra:

**8.49.93**  There is no other bowman like me, O lord of men, save the god who bears the Pināka; for, sanctioned by that great one, in a moment I could destroy the world with all that moves and moves not.

**8.49.94**  For by me, O king, all the quarters, with the lords that rule them, were conquered and brought under your sway; that Rājasūya, with its gifts complete, and your celestial hall — these were yours through my might.

**8.49.95**  Upon my palm these shaft-marks are inscribed, and in battle my bow is drawn taut with its arrow; my feet too are marked with shafts and banner. One like me, gone to battle, none can conquer.

**8.49.96**  The northern peoples are slain, the western struck down, the eastern routed, the southern destroyed; of the Saṃśaptakas but a remnant is left. Half of the entire host has been slain by me.

**8.49.97**  The army of the Bhāratas, bright as the host of the gods, lies slain by me, O king. Those who know not the celestial weapons I slay with ordinary arms; therefore I do not reduce the world to ashes.

**8.49.98**  Having spoken thus, Pārtha said again to Yudhiṣṭhira, the foremost of the upholders of dharma: By him shall Rādhā be made childless, or Kuntī by me — know this for the truth, O king. Be gracious, O king; forgive what I have spoken; in time you will understand it. To you my homage.

**8.49.99**  Having appeased the king, the conqueror of foes, he stood and, drawing near, said to him: I go now to deliver Bhīma from the fray, and with my whole soul to slay the sūta's son.

**8.49.100**  For your pleasure is my very life — I speak the truth; know it so, O king. And so, having clasped his feet, the Diademed One of blazing splendor rose and set out: 'Soon, not long hence, this shall come to pass; yonder he turns — I go against him.'

**8.49.101**  Hearing this, the Pāṇḍava, the Dharma-king, his brother Phalguna's harsh speech, rose from that couch and, his mind overwhelmed with grief, spoke then to Pārtha:

**8.49.102**  I have acted, Pārtha, in a way that was not good, whereby this most dreadful calamity has come upon you all. Therefore strike off this head of mine today — the head of one who is the ruin of his line, the basest of men.

**8.49.103**  Of a sinner, beset with the vice of sin, of dull wit, slothful, and a coward, a scorner of his elders and a harsh man too — what use is it to you to follow me, a hard man, any longer?

**8.49.104**  I, a sinner, shall go this very day to the forest; may you live happily, rid of me. The great-souled Bhīmasena is fit to be king; what business has a eunuch like me with ruling?

**8.49.105**  Nor am I able to bear these harsh words of yours again, spoken in anger. Let Bhīma be king; what use is there in my life, O hero, now that I am despised?

**8.49.106**  Having spoken thus, the king sprang up at once, leaving that couch behind, and sought to go forth then to the forest; but Vāsudeva, bowing, said to him:

**8.49.107**  O king, this is known to you — how the vow of the Gāṇḍīva-bearer, faithful to his word, concerning the Gāṇḍīva is renowned far and wide.

**8.49.108**  Whoever should say to him, 'Give over your Gāṇḍīva to another' — that man in the world is to be slain by him; and you spoke to him in just such terms.

**8.49.109**  Therefore, to keep that vow of his true, Pārtha, at my urging, has shown you this disrespect, O lord of earth; for disrespect toward elders is called a slaying.

**8.49.110**  Therefore, O mighty-armed one, this transgression of us both — of me and of Pārtha — forgive it, O king, with regard to Arjuna.

**8.49.111**  To you, great king, we have both come for refuge; you should forgive me, O king, as I bow and entreat you.

**8.49.112**  This day the earth shall drink the blood of the wicked son of Rādhā; truly I promise you — know the sūta's son as slain today. He whose death you desire — his life is already gone this day.

**8.49.113**  Hearing these words of Kṛṣṇa, the Dharma-king Yudhiṣṭhira hastily raised up Hṛṣīkeśa, who bowed before him, and then, with palms joined, spoke this word in answer:

**8.49.114**  It is even as you say; this transgression is indeed mine. I am won over, Govinda, and delivered this day, Mādhava; rescued from dread calamity are we this day by you, O Acyuta.

**8.49.115**  Having gained you as our protector, we two, bewildered by ignorance, have this day crossed over from the dreadful ocean of calamity.

**8.49.116**  Boarding the raft of your wisdom, we, together with our counselors, have crossed over from the ocean of grief and sorrow; protected by you we are, O Acyuta.

**8.50.1**  Saṃjaya said: Having thus, at Kṛṣṇa's word, retorted to Yudhiṣṭhira, Pārtha grew downcast, as though he had committed some sin.

**8.50.2**  Then Vāsudeva, as if smiling, said to the Pāṇḍava: How indeed could this have come to pass, Pārtha — that you should slay with a keen-edged sword the son of Dharma, who stands firm in dharma?

**8.50.3**  Merely by saying 'thou' to the king you fell into such despondency; but had you slain the king, Pārtha, what answer could you have made? So very hard is dharma to discern, especially by those of feeble understanding.

**8.50.4**  You, from your dread of unrighteousness, would surely have gone to the most dreadful and terrible hell, for the slaying of your eldest brother.

**8.50.5**  Therefore do you appease the king, the best of the upholders of dharma, joined to dharma, the foremost of the Kurus; this is my counsel herein.

**8.50.6**  Having appeased the king with devotion, and Yudhiṣṭhira being made glad, let us go in haste to fight against the chariot of the sūta's son.

**8.50.7**  Slaying the hard-to-conquer Karṇa today with your whetted shafts, bring abundant joy to the son of Dharma, O giver of honor.

**8.50.8**  This, O mighty-armed one, is my counsel, fitting to the hour; and when this is done, your task too will be accomplished.

**8.50.9**  Then Arjuna, O great king, filled with shame, fell with his head at the feet of the Dharma-king, O sinless one.

**8.50.10**  He said, O best of the Bhāratas: 'Be gracious,' again and again; 'forgive, O king, what was spoken by one who loves dharma and is fearful of it.'

**8.50.11**  Seeing Dhanaṃjaya, the slayer of foes, fallen at his feet and weeping, O bull of the Bhāratas, the Dharma-king Yudhiṣṭhira —

**8.50.12**  — the king, the Dharma-king, raised up his brother Dhanaṃjaya and, embracing him with affection, the lord of earth burst into tears.

**8.50.13**  Having wept for a long while, the two brothers of great splendor, those tigers among men, their hearts unburdened, became filled with mutual love.

**8.50.14**  Then, embracing the son of Pāṇḍu lovingly and kissing him upon the head, filled with the highest affection and smiling, he spoke to the Conqueror:

**8.50.15**  "O mighty-armed one, before the eyes of the whole army Karṇa cut down with his arrows my armor, my banner, my bow, my spear, my horses, and my mace, O great archer, even as I strove in the battle.

**8.50.16**  Having known and seen his prowess in battle, O Phalguna, I sink down in grief, and life itself is no longer dear to me.

**8.50.17**  O hero, if today you do not slay that son of a charioteer, I shall abandon my very breath of life — for what use have I of living?"

**8.50.18**  Thus addressed, the Ever-Victorious replied, O bull of the Bhāratas: "By truth I swear to you, O king, and by your own favor, and by Bhīma, O best of men, and by the twins, O lord of the earth —

**8.50.19**  that this very day in battle I shall slay Karṇa, or else, slain, I shall fall upon the earth. By truth I lay my hand upon my weapon."

**8.50.20**  Having spoken thus to the king, he addressed these words to Mādhava: "This day in battle I shall slay Karṇa, O Kṛṣṇa — there is no doubt. Set your mind, may you prosper, upon the death of that evil-souled one."

**8.50.21**  Thus addressed, Keśava said to Pārtha, O best of kings: "I am able, O best of the Bhāratas, to make every effort according to my strength.

**8.50.22**  And this too has ever been my desire, O great chariot-warrior; my thought is always how you might slay Karṇa in battle."

**8.50.23**  And the wise Mādhava spoke further to the son of Dharma: "Yudhiṣṭhira, you should soothe this Bībhatsu, and grant him leave today for the slaying of the evil-souled Karṇa.

**8.50.24**  For having heard that you were tormented by Karṇa's arrows, both he and I came here to learn how things stood, O joy of the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.50.25**  By good fortune, O king, you are free of hurt; by good fortune you were not taken captive. Console Bībhatsu, and bid the Conqueror to victory, O sinless one."

**8.50.26**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: "Come, come, O Pārtha, Bībhatsu, embrace me, son of Pāṇḍu. I was told by you what ought to have been said, though it was harsh — and that I have forgiven.

**8.50.27**  I grant you leave — slay Karṇa, O Dhanaṃjaya. And bear no anger, O Pārtha, for the cruel words I spoke to you."

**8.50.28**  Saṃjaya said: Then Dhanaṃjaya, O king, bowing with his head, clasped with both hands the feet of his eldest brother, O venerable one.

**8.50.29**  Then the king, raising him up and embracing him close, and kissing him upon the head, spoke to him once more these words:

**8.50.30**  "O mighty-armed Dhanaṃjaya, you have honored me well. May you attain greatness and victory once more, and that everlasting."

**8.50.31**  Arjuna said: "This day, closing with that evil-doing Rādheya, proud of his strength, together with his kinsmen, I shall bring him to his end in battle with my arrows.

**8.50.32**  He by whom you were tormented with arrows, drawing his bow taut — for that deed Karṇa shall this day reap a terrible fruit.

**8.50.33**  This day, having slain Karṇa, O lord of the earth, I shall come to you to do you honor amid the cries of war — this I tell you truly.

**8.50.34**  I shall not turn back from the field of battle this day without having slain Karṇa — and on this truth I touch your feet, O lord of the world."

**8.50.35**  Saṃjaya said: Having won over the King of Righteousness, Pārtha, with gladdened inmost heart and bent upon the slaying of the charioteer's son, spoke to Govinda:

**8.50.36**  "Let the chariot be made ready once more, and let the finest horses be yoked, and let all the weapons be set in order upon the great car.

**8.50.37**  Let the well-trained horses and the skilled horsemen, with all the gear of the chariot, come forth in haste."

**8.50.38**  When this was spoken, O great king, by the high-souled Phalguna, Kṛṣṇa said to Dāruka: "Do all as Arjuna, best of the Bhāratas, foremost of all bowmen, has said."

**8.50.39**  Then, commanded by Kṛṣṇa, Dāruka, O best of kings, yoked that tiger-skinned chariot, the scorcher of foes.

**8.50.40**  Mounting the chariot now yoked by the high-souled Dāruka, taking leave of the King of Righteousness and bidding the brāhmaṇas pronounce their blessings, amid auspicious benedictions he ascended that finest of chariots.

**8.50.41**  Upon him the king, the deeply wise Yudhiṣṭhira, the Lord of Righteousness, bestowed the highest blessings, fit for the slaying of Karṇa.

**8.50.42**  Seeing that great archer setting forth, O Bhārata, the creatures reckoned Karṇa already slain by the high-souled son of Pāṇḍu.

**8.50.43**  All the quarters grew clear on every side, O king; and blue jays and woodpeckers and herons, O lord of men, then circled rightward about the joy of the Pāṇḍus.

**8.50.44**  Many birds, O king, bearing masculine names, auspicious and benign, urging Arjuna on to battle, cried out in joyful guise.

**8.50.45**  Herons, vultures, cranes, and crows, O lord of peoples, went on before him, fearsome, in their craving for flesh.

**8.50.46**  And the auspicious omens proclaimed for Pārtha the destruction of the enemy's host and the slaying of Karṇa as well.

**8.50.47**  Then, as Pārtha set forth, a great sweat broke upon him, and a deep anxiety arose: "How indeed will this come to pass?"

**8.50.48**  Then the Slayer of Madhu, seeing Pārtha strained and gripped by anxiety, spoke to the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva:

**8.50.49**  "O wielder of the Gāṇḍīva, of those whom you have vanquished in battle with your bow, there is no human conqueror to be found here other than you.

**8.50.50**  For many heroes have been seen, in valor equal to Śakra, who, meeting you in battle, O hero, have gone to the highest goal.

**8.50.51**  For who, O venerable one, could stand against Droṇa and Bhīṣma and Bhagadatta, and Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti, and Sudakṣiṇa of the Kāmbojas,

**8.50.52**  and the mighty Śrutāyus, and Acyutāyus too, and emerge unharmed — who is there so capable as you?

**8.50.53**  For yours are the celestial weapons, and lightness of hand, and strength; yours, O Arjuna, the piercing, the casting, the aim, and the union of all; and freedom from bewilderment in battle, and the bending of all knowledge to your will.

**8.50.54**  You could slay all the gods and demons together with all that moves and moves not; for upon the earth, O Pārtha, there is no warrior, no man, equal to you in battle.

**8.50.55**  For of all the bow-wielding warriors, mad with the lust of battle, from the gods downward, I neither see nor hear of one equal to you.

**8.50.56**  By Brahmā were the creatures created, and so too the marvelous Gāṇḍīva with which you fight, O Pārtha; therefore there is none equal to you.

**8.50.57**  Yet I must surely tell you what is wholesome for you, O son of Pāṇḍu: do not, O mighty-armed one, hold lightly Karṇa, who shines in battle.

**8.50.58**  For Karṇa is strong and bold, accomplished in arms and a great chariot-warrior, skilled and a wondrous fighter, expert in place and time.

**8.50.59**  In splendor he is like fire, in swiftness equal to the wind's rush, in wrath the very likeness of Death, with the frame of a lion, and mighty.

**8.50.60**  With arms long as iron bars, mighty-armed, broad-chested, hard to conquer, exceedingly proud, valiant, a foremost hero, and fair to look upon.

**8.50.61**  Endowed with every warrior's virtue, a giver of safety to his friends, ever a hater of the Pāṇḍavas, and devoted to the welfare of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son.

**8.50.62**  Rādheya is, to my mind, one who cannot be slain by all the gods together with Vāsava — save by you. This day, then, slay the charioteer's son.

**8.50.63**  Even by the gods arrayed for war, bearing flesh and blood, all of them eager to fight, he cannot be conquered in battle.

**8.50.64**  Slaying that evil-souled one, wicked-minded and cruel, of corrupt understanding, ever set against the sons of Pāṇḍu, and forsaking his own good in his enmity with the Pāṇḍavas — by slaying Karṇa this day, become one whose purpose is fulfilled.

**8.50.65**  He through whom the wicked Suyodhana deems himself a hero — that son of a charioteer, the root of all these evils, conquer this day, O Dhanaṃjaya."

**8.51.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then again the immeasurable-souled Keśava spoke to Arjuna, who was resolved and wholly intent upon the slaying of Karṇa:

**8.51.2**  "This day, O Bhārata, the seventeenth day is passing of that most dreadful destruction of men and elephants and horses.

**8.51.3**  Vast though the army of your kinsmen was, closing with the foe in mutual battle, but little of it now remains, O lord of peoples.

**8.51.4**  For the Kauravas, O Pārtha, abounding in elephants and horses, having met you as their foe, were destroyed at the head of battle.

**8.51.5**  And all these Pāñcālas and Sṛñjayas with their kindred, and the Pāṇḍavas, having gained you, the unassailable, stand firm.

**8.51.6**  By the Pāñcālas, the Pāṇḍavas, the Matsyas, the Karūṣas, the Cedis and the Kekayas, guarded by you, O slayer of foes, was the host of enemies brought to ruin.

**8.51.7**  For who, dear one, is able to conquer in battle the Kauravas gathered together, save the Pāṇḍavas, those great chariot-warriors, when guarded by you in war?

**8.51.8**  For you are able in battle to conquer the three worlds with their gods, demons, and men, all together arrayed — how much more, then, the host of the Kauravas?

**8.51.9**  For King Bhagadatta, O tiger among men, who else but you was able to conquer — even were he the equal of Vāsava?

**8.51.10**  And this vast army guarded by you, O sinless Pārtha — all the kings of the earth could not even look upon it with their eyes.

**8.51.11**  And likewise, O Pārtha, by Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, ever guarded by you in battle, were Bhīṣma and Droṇa brought down.

**8.51.12**  For who among the Pāñcālas, O Pārtha, was able to vanquish in battle by force of arms those two great chariot-warriors, Bhīṣma and Droṇa, equal in valor to Śakra?

**8.51.13**  For who, in the clash of battle, could face the son of Śaṃtanu, Droṇa, Vaikartana, Kṛpa, the son of Droṇa, the son of Somadatta, and Kṛtavarman too, the lord of the Sindhus, the king of the Madras, and King Suyodhana —

**8.51.14**  all those heroes, accomplished in arms in battle, each with his followers, fierce commanders of armies, furious and mad with the lust of war?

**8.51.15**  And many companies have been brought low, their horses, chariots, and elephants torn asunder — fierce hosts from divers lands, of warriors who would brook no offense.

**8.51.16**  Of the Govāsas, the Dāsamīyas, and the Vasātis, O Bhārata, of the Vrātyas, the Vāṭadhānas, and the proud Bhojas as well —

**8.51.17**  and the swelling great hosts of the brāhmaṇa-warriors, O Bhārata, having met you, went to their destruction, together with their horses, chariots, and elephants.

**8.51.18**  And the fierce, cruel-doing Tukhāras, Yavanas, and Khaśas, the Dārvābhisāras, Daradas, Śakas, Ramaṭhas, and Taṅgaṇas,

**8.51.19**  and the Andhrakas and Pulindas, the Kirātas of fierce prowess, and the Mlecchas and the mountain-dwellers, and those who dwell by the sea and its marshlands — wrathful, drunk with battle, mighty, with steadfast hands —

**8.51.20**  these, raging for Suyodhana's sake together with the Kurus, could be conquered in battle by none other than you, O scorcher of foes.

**8.51.21**  For seeing the host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son towering and arrayed in mighty strength, what man, whose protector you were not, would dare advance against it?

**8.51.22**  That army, surging like the ocean and veiled in dust, was rent asunder and slain by the wrathful Pāṇḍavas guarded by you, O lord.

**8.51.23**  The lord of the Māgadhas, the mighty Jayatsena, was slain in battle by Abhimanyu just seven days ago this day.

**8.51.24**  Then ten thousand elephants of terrible deeds, that king's retinue, Bhīma slew with his mace; and other elephants too were slain, and chariots by the hundred, by his might.

**8.51.25**  Thus, dear one, as that great and dreadful battle raged on, the Kauravas, encountering Bhīmasena and you, O Pāṇḍava, went from here to the realm of Death, together with their horses, chariots, and elephants.

**8.51.26**  And when the van of the army there had been struck down by the Pāṇḍavas, O Pārtha, Bhīṣma loosed fierce showers of arrows, O venerable one.

**8.51.27**  Covering with his arrows the Cedis, the Kāśis, the Pāñcālas, the Karūṣas, the Matsyas, and the Kekayas, that knower of cruel weapons led them to their destruction.

**8.51.28**  And with the arrows loosed from his bow, the rippers of the bodies of foes, gold-feathered and flying straight, the whole sky was filled.

**8.51.29**  Moving by the tenth course, abandoning the nine evil courses, he advanced and slew horses, chariots, and elephants; that mighty one, drawing his bow at full stretch, loosed his shafts.

**8.51.30**  For ten days Bhīṣma, smiting your host, emptied the seats of the chariots and laid low elephants and horses.

**8.51.31**  Revealing in battle a form like Rudra's or Upendra's, he plunged into the ranks of the Pāṇḍavas and shattered them.

**8.51.32**  Slaying the guardians of the earth—the Cedis, the Pāñcālas, the Kekayas—he consumed the Pāṇḍava army thronged with men, horses, and elephants.

**8.51.33**  Eager to rescue the foolish Suyodhana sinking in a sea without a raft, he ranged through the battle blazing like the sun, so that neither the Sṛñjayas nor the other kings of the earth could bear to look upon him.

**8.51.34**  As he thus ranged through the fight with the radiance of victory, the Pāṇḍavas with all their might rushed suddenly upon him.

**8.51.35**  But routing in battle the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas alike, Bhīṣma, alone in the fray, attained to the rank of a single peerless hero.

**8.51.36**  That mighty car-warrior, that tiger among men, Śikhaṇḍin—guarded by you—closed with and struck down with his straight-jointed arrows.

**8.51.37**  There the grandsire lies fallen upon his bed of arrows, having met you, O tiger among men, as a vulture meets a crow.

**8.51.38**  For five days the dread Droṇa scattered the armies of his foes; arraying his battle-formation in the great war, he brought down mighty car-warriors.

**8.51.39**  That mighty car-warrior, guarding Jayadratha in the fight, fierce as Death itself, fought through the dreadful night and consumed living men.

**8.51.40**  For two days, counting today, the valiant hero, the son of Bharadvāja, fought; then, encountering Dhṛṣṭadyumna, he attained the supreme goal.

**8.51.41**  And had you not held off in the battle the enemy's car-warriors led by the sūta's son, Droṇa would never have perished in the fray.

**8.51.42**  But by you the whole strength of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son was checked, and so Droṇa was slain in battle by Pṛṣata's grandson, O Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.51.43**  What other warrior, what other kṣatriya in battle, save you, could have done what you did, O Pārtha, in the slaying of Jayadratha?

**8.51.44**  Having held back a mighty host and slain heroic kings, you laid low the king of the Sindhus by the might and splendor of your weapons.

**8.51.45**  The kings deem the slaying of the lord of the Sindhus a marvel; yet from you it is no marvel, for you, O Pārtha, are a mighty car-warrior.

**8.51.46**  Once it has met you in battle, O Bhārata, the warrior class could not, even in a single day, remain unbroken and at ease—such is my conviction.

**8.51.47**  This terrible army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, O Pārtha, with all its heroes, was as good as slain in the fray the moment Bhīṣma and Droṇa fell.

**8.51.48**  With its foremost warriors broken, its horses, men, and elephants slain, the Bhārata host today shines like the sky bereft of sun, moon, and stars.

**8.51.49**  This army has been shattered in battle, O Pārtha, by Bhīma's prowess, as of old the host of the Asuras was shattered by the might of Śakra.

**8.51.50**  Of them, when the slain are counted, there remain but five mighty car-warriors: Aśvatthāman, Kṛtavarman, Karṇa, the lord of the Madras, and Kṛpa.

**8.51.51**  Slaying today these five mighty car-warriors, O tiger among men, and so destroying your foes, bestow upon the king the earth with its islands and cities.

**8.51.52**  May Pārtha of measureless valor and glory today win the earth, with its sky and waters and nether realms, with its mountains and its great forests.

**8.51.53**  As of old Viṣṇu, having slain the Daityas and Dānavas, gave the earth to Śakra, so do you, O Hari-like one, bestow this earth upon the king.

**8.51.54**  Today let the Pāñcālas rejoice when their foes are slain by you, even as the gods rejoiced when the sons of Danu were slain by Viṣṇu.

**8.51.55**  But if, O best of men, out of reverence for Droṇa your teacher, you feel pity for Aśvatthāman, or for Kṛpa from regard for a preceptor's dignity—

**8.51.56**  or if, honoring those bound to you by long and close kinship, you will not, on meeting Kṛtavarman, send him to the abode of Yama—

**8.51.57**  and if, meeting your mother's brother Śalya, the lord of the Madra people, you, O lotus-eyed one, in your compassion will not seek to slay him—

**8.51.58**  yet this wicked-minded, base Karṇa, ever utterly hostile to the Pāṇḍavas—him, O best of men, slay this day swiftly with your whetted shafts.

**8.51.59**  This will be a righteous deed of yours; nothing here is unfitting. We too know this well: in this there is no fault whatever.

**8.51.60**  In the burning of your mother and her sons by night, O sinless one, and in the gambling-match wherein Suyodhana moved against you all—in all of this, O Arjuna, the wicked-souled Karṇa was the root.

**8.51.61**  For Suyodhana ever looks to Karṇa for his protection; and so, emboldened, he set out to lay hold even of me.

**8.51.62**  Firm, O bestower of honor, is the conviction of the king, Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, that Karṇa will conquer all the Pārthas in battle, beyond all doubt.

**8.51.63**  Relying upon Karṇa, O son of Kuntī, Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son chose strife with you, though he knew full well your strength.

**8.51.64**  For Karṇa ever boasts: 'I shall conquer the assembled Pārthas, and Vāsudeva, and the king with them, in the great battle.'

**8.51.65**  Inciting the evil-souled son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, that most malign Karṇa roars in the assembly—slay him this day, O Bhārata.

**8.51.66**  And in every wrong that Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son has wrought against you, the wicked-souled, evil-minded Karṇa has been the foremost agent.

**8.51.67**  And when I beheld the hero, the son of Subhadrā, with the eyes of a bull, slain by six cruel mighty car-warriors of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's side—

**8.51.68**  he who made the heroes Droṇa, Droṇa's son, and Kṛpa, mighty car-warriors, tremble; who emptied elephants of their riders and stripped great car-warriors of their cars—

**8.51.69**  who left horses without their riders and footmen bereft of weapons and life—that bull-shouldered youth, the glory of the Kurus and Vṛṣṇis—

**8.51.70**  who scattered the ranks and confounded the mighty car-warriors, dispatching men, horses, and elephants to the abode of Yama—

**8.51.71**  the son of Subhadrā, straining at the work, consuming the host as it were with his arrows—the memory of this burns my very limbs, my friend; I swear it to you in truth.

**8.51.72**  And there too the wicked-souled Karṇa wrought him harm, O lord—Karṇa, who could not stand before Abhimanyu in the forefront of battle.

**8.51.73**  Pierced by the arrows of Subhadrā's son, swooning, drenched in blood, breathing hard, inflamed with rage, turned away and tormented by the shafts—

**8.51.74**  bent on flight, and despairing even of his life, he stood there utterly distraught in the fray, worn out by the blows he had taken.

**8.51.75**  Then in the battle Karṇa, hearing the cruel counsel of Droṇa, fitting to that hour of doom, thereupon cut through the youth's bow.

**8.51.76**  Then, his weapon severed by that one of crafty cunning, the five mighty car-warriors—and he himself—struck him down in the fray with showers of arrows.

**8.51.77**  And the harsh words that Karṇa spoke to Kṛṣṇā in the assembly, before the very faces of the Pāṇḍavas and the Kurus, like a man without mercy—

**8.51.78**  'The Pāṇḍavas are ruined, O Kṛṣṇā; they have gone to everlasting hell. Choose another lord, O broad-hipped, soft-spoken one.'

**8.51.79**  'O delicately-browed, O you with curving lashes and lovely eyes, become a slave and enter the household of Dhṛtarāṣṭra; your husbands are no more.'

**8.51.80**  Thus that knower of no righteousness, that most malign and sinful Karṇa, spoke his sinful words within your very hearing, O Bhārata.

**8.51.81**  Let your gold-wrought arrows, whetted upon stone and life-severing, shot by you, silence forever the speech of that sinner.

**8.51.82**  And whatever other evils the wicked-souled one has wrought against you—let your shafts this day quiet them all, together with his life.

**8.51.83**  As his limbs feel today the dreadful shafts sped from the Gāṇḍīva, let the wicked-souled Karṇa remember the words of Droṇa and Bhīṣma.

**8.51.84**  Your gold-feathered iron shafts, slayers of foes, bright as lightning, loosed by you, shall pierce his vitals and drink his blood.

**8.51.85**  Let the fierce, keen shafts loosed from your arm, swift and rending the vitals, send Karṇa this day to the abode of Yama.

**8.51.86**  Today let the lords of the earth, crying out in dismay, wretched and stricken by your shafts, behold Karṇa toppling from his chariot.
