**8.51.87**  Today let his own kinsmen and friends behold Karṇa sunk in his own blood, lying fallen upon the earth, his weapon flung aside.

**8.51.88**  Struck down by your broad-headed arrow, let the great elephant-girth standard of Adhiratha's son tremble and fall to the ground.

**8.51.89**  Let Śalya in his terror flee, abandoning the gold-adorned chariot, its warrior slain, its frame riddled by your hundreds of shafts.

**8.51.90**  Then, beholding Adhiratha's son slain by you, let Suyodhana this day despair of his life and of his kingdom alike, O Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.51.91**  See, O best of the Bhāratas, the Pāñcālas, eager to rescue the Pāṇḍavas, take flight as they are cut down by Karṇa's keen arrows.

**8.51.92**  The Pāñcālas, the sons of Draupadī, Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, the sons of Dhṛṣṭadyumna, and Śatānīka the son of Nakula—

**8.51.93**  Nakula and Sahadeva, Durmukha and Janamejaya, Suvarman and Sātyaki—know them all to have fallen under Karṇa's power.

**8.51.94**  The dreadful outcry of the Pāñcālas, your kinsmen, smitten by Karṇa in the great battle, is heard, O scorcher of foes.

**8.51.95**  Yet never would the Pāñcālas, for all their fear, turn their backs; for those great archers, those mighty car-warriors, reckon death as nothing.

**8.51.96**  He who alone encompassed the Pāṇḍava army with floods of arrows—even Bhīṣma—the Pāñcālas, when they met him, did not turn their backs upon.

**8.51.97**  So too they climbed in their might against the unassailable Droṇa, blazing like the fire of weapons, the preceptor of all bowmen, consuming all before him.

**8.51.98**  Ever ready to conquer their foes in battle, those tamers of enemies, the Pāñcālas, would never out of fear of Adhiratha's son turn their backs.

**8.51.99**  As they rush upon him, the valiant Karṇa takes the lives of those swift Pāñcālas with his arrows, as fire devours moths.

**8.51.100**  Those heroes who thus front him, who lay down their lives for their friends' sake—the son of Rādhā brings them to destruction, the Pāñcālas by the hundred in battle.

**8.51.101**  The terrible weapon that Karṇa long ago received from Rāma of Bhṛgu's line, the most excellent of sages—its dreadful form now displays itself.

**8.51.102**  Scorching all the armies, terrible of form and most dire, it enwraps the great host and blazes with its own splendor.

**8.51.103**  See how these arrows loosed from Karṇa's bow range through the battle like swarms of bees, scorching your warriors.

**8.51.104**  See how the Pāñcālas scatter in all directions, O Bhārata, meeting in battle Karṇa's weapon, irresistible by those who lack his command of self.

**8.51.105**  There is Bhīma of unyielding wrath, O Pārtha, hemmed in on every side; fighting Karṇa with the Sṛñjayas about him, he is hard pressed by the keen shafts.

**8.51.106**  If left unchecked, O Bhārata, Karṇa would destroy the Pāṇḍavas, the Sṛñjayas, and the Pāñcālas alike, as a spreading disease destroys the body.

**8.51.107**  I see no warrior in Yudhiṣṭhira's host save you who, having encountered the son of Rādhā, could return home unscathed.

**8.51.108**  Slay him this day with your whetted arrows, O bull of the Bhāratas, and fulfilling your vow, O Pārtha, win renown.

**8.51.109**  For you alone are able in battle to conquer the Kauravas, Karṇa and all; no other warrior can, O best of fighters. This truth I tell you.

**8.51.110**  Having accomplished this great deed, having slain Karṇa the mighty chariot-warrior, your purpose fulfilled and your labor rewarded, O Pārtha, be happy, O best of men.

**8.52.1**  Saṃjaya said: Having heard the words of Keśava, O Bhārata, Bībhatsu in a moment became free of sorrow and filled with delight.

**8.52.2**  Then, stroking the bowstring, he swiftly drew the Gāṇḍīva bow, resolved upon Karṇa's destruction, and spoke to Keśava.

**8.52.3**  With you as my protector, Govinda, this victory of mine is assured—you who are gracious to me this day, lord of what has been, what shall be, and what is.

**8.52.4**  With you as my ally, Kṛṣṇa, even the three worlds gathered together I could dispatch to the world beyond—how much more then Karṇa in this great battle?

**8.52.5**  I see the army of the Pāñcālas fleeing, Janārdana; I see Karṇa ranging fearlessly through the fight.

**8.52.6**  And I see the Bhārgava weapon ranging on every side, loosed by Karṇa, O Vārṣṇeya, as Śakra looses the mighty thunderbolt.

**8.52.7**  This indeed is that battle, Kṛṣṇa, in which my deeds living beings will recount for as long as the earth shall endure.

**8.52.8**  This day, Kṛṣṇa, my barbless shafts, loosed from the Gāṇḍīva and driven by my hand, shall bring Karṇa to his death, wearing him down.

**8.52.9**  This day King Dhṛtarāṣṭra shall despise his own judgment, by which he consecrated Duryodhana—unfit for kingship—to the throne.

**8.52.10**  This day, O mighty-armed one, Dhṛtarāṣṭra shall be parted from his kingdom and his ease, from fortune, realm, and city, and from his sons.

**8.52.11**  This day, when Karṇa is slain, King Duryodhana shall despair even of his life, Kṛṣṇa—this truth I tell you.

**8.52.12**  This day, beholding Karṇa torn to shreds by my arrows, the lord of men shall remember your words spoken in favor of peace.

**8.52.13**  This day, Kṛṣṇa, let that son of Subala learn that my arrows are the dice, the Gāṇḍīva the gaming-board, and my chariot the gaming-circle.

**8.52.14**  That son of the charioteer who in battle deems no other man on earth his equal—this day the earth shall drink his blood, and the shafts loosed from the Gāṇḍīva shall send Karṇa to his final end.

**8.52.15**  This day Rādheya shall repent the cruel words he spoke against the daughter of Pāñcāla in the midst of the assembly, reviling the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.52.16**  Those who were there called barren sesame seeds shall this day become true seeds, once Karṇa Vaikartana, the vile son of the charioteer, is slain.

**8.52.17**  When he declared, 'I shall save you from the sons of Pāṇḍu,' my sharpened arrows shall prove that boast a lie.

**8.52.18**  He who said, 'I shall slay all the Pāṇḍavas together with their sons'—that very Karṇa I shall this day slay before the eyes of all the bowmen.

**8.52.19**  He in whose valor the arrogant son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra trusted, that foolish and evil-souled one who ever held us in contempt—that Karṇa Rādheya I shall this day slay, O slayer of Madhu.

**8.52.20**  This day, when Karṇa is slain, Kṛṣṇa, let the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra with their kings flee in terror to every quarter, like deer scattered by a lion.

**8.52.21**  This day let King Duryodhana gaze upon the earth, when Karṇa is slain by me in the fray together with his sons and his kinsmen and friends.

**8.52.22**  This day, beholding Karṇa slain, let the wrathful son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra know me, Kṛṣṇa, to be the foremost of all bowmen in battle.

**8.52.23**  This day, Kṛṣṇa, I shall become free of my debt to the bearers of bows, to my own wrath, to the Kurus, to my arrows, and to the Gāṇḍīva.

**8.52.24**  This day I shall be freed of the grief amassed through thirteen years, by slaying Karṇa in battle, Kṛṣṇa, as Maghavat slew Śambara.

**8.52.25**  This day, when Karṇa is slain in battle, let the great chariot-warriors of the Somakas, eager to serve their friends in the fight, deem their task accomplished.

**8.52.26**  And I do not know, Mādhava, how great this day shall be the joy of the grandson of Śini, when Karṇa is slain and I emerge crowned with victory.

**8.52.27**  By slaying Karṇa and his son, the mighty chariot-warrior, in battle, I shall give delight to Bhīma, to the twins, and to Sātyaki as well.

**8.52.28**  To Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin and to the Pāñcālas, Mādhava, I shall discharge my debt by slaying Karṇa in this great battle.

**8.52.29**  This day let them behold in the fight Dhanaṃjaya, unyielding in his wrath, battling the Kauravas in the fray and bringing down the charioteer's son. And in your presence I shall once more speak my own praise.

**8.52.30**  In the science of archery there is none in the world my equal, nor in valor who is a match for me. Who else is my peer in forbearance? And in wrath too there is no other like me.

**8.52.31**  With bow in hand, asuras and gods and all creatures gathered together, by the might of my own arms I would drive to ruin. Know my prowess to be supreme above the supreme.

**8.52.32**  With the Gāṇḍīva flaming with arrows, I alone, falling upon all the Kurus and the Bāhlīkas, would burn them and their hosts by force, as fire reaching dry brushwood at winter's end.

**8.52.33**  These shafts are as if drawn upon my hand; the bow with its arrow rests fixed in my left hand; my feet stand firm with chariot and banner. None can conquer one like me once I have entered the battle.

**8.53.1**  Saṃjaya said: Their battalions, with towering banners, mighty and arrayed for war, came together roaring with the thunder of kettle-drums, as cloud-masses thunder against cloud at the close of the hot season.

**8.53.2**  Thick with great elephants like storm-clouds, its rain the weapons, resounding with the noise of instruments, chariot-rims, and palms; lightning-bright with weapons adorned in gold, and clamorous with the press of mighty chariot-warriors.

**8.53.3**  Of terrible swiftness, bearing torrents of blood, thronged with swords, carrying off the lives of warriors—untimely, cruel, raining down what none desired—it became the destruction of living beings.

**8.53.4**  Chariots with their drivers and horses, elephants, and all his foes, Pārtha brought under the sway of death with floods of arrows—horses with their riders, and the throngs of foot-soldiers likewise.

**8.53.5**  Kṛpa and Śikhaṇḍin met together in battle; Sātyaki advanced upon Duryodhana; Śrutaśravā closed with the son of Droṇa, and Yudhāmanyu with Citrasena.

**8.53.6**  The son of Karṇa, the chariot-warrior, came against Suṣeṇa; Uttamaujas against the Sṛñjayas; and Sahadeva, ravenous, rushed upon the king of Gāndhāra as a lion upon a great bull.

**8.53.7**  Śatānīka, son of Nakula, a youth, assailed the youthful Vṛṣasena, son of Karṇa, with floods of arrows; and the heroic son of Karṇa, in turn, struck the prince of Pāñcāla with many showers of shafts.

**8.53.8**  The bull among chariot-warriors, Nakula son of Mādrī, skilled in varied modes of fighting, came against Kṛtavarman; and the lord of the Pāñcālas, son of Yajñasena, came against the commander Karṇa with his troops.

**8.53.9**  Duḥśāsana, O Bhārata, and the mighty host of the Saṃśaptakas of the Bhāratas then closed in battle with Bhīma, foremost of weapon-bearers, whose onset none could withstand.

**8.53.10**  There the hero Uttamaujas struck down the son of Karṇa and forcibly severed his head, which fell to the ground, making the earth and the sky resound with its noise.

**8.53.11**  Beholding Suṣeṇa's head fallen to the earth, Karṇa, then stricken with grief, in his wrath cut down with keen, well-honed arrows the horses, the chariot, and the banner of his foe.

**8.53.12**  But Uttamaujas pierced him with sharp shafts, and with a gleaming sword slew the flank-guards and the horses of Kṛpa, and then mounted the chariot of Śikhaṇḍin.

**8.53.13**  But Śikhaṇḍin, mounted on his chariot, seeing Kṛpa deprived of his car, would not strike him with arrows; and the son of Droṇa, covering him, lifted Kṛpa onto his chariot as one would raise a cow sunk in the mire.

**8.53.14**  Golden-armored Bhīma, son of the Wind, with sharp shafts scorched the army of your sons most terribly, as the sun set in mid-sky scorches in the bright hot season.

**8.54.1**  Saṃjaya said: Now, in the tumultuous fray, surrounded—one against many foes—in that great peril, Bhīma, holding back the army of the Dhārtarāṣṭras, spoke thus to his charioteer: 'Drive on, charioteer, swiftly with the horses; I shall send these sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra to Yama.'

**8.54.2**  Thus urged by Bhīmasena, the charioteer of fierce speed drove on toward the host of your sons, to wherever Bhīma wished that army to be reached.

**8.54.3**  Then others among the Kurus advanced against him on every side with elephants, chariots, horse, and foot, and on all sides struck Bhīma's swift, high-mettled horses with clouds of arrows.

**8.54.4**  Then the great-souled one cut down the oncoming arrows with shafts feathered in gold; and those gold-feathered shafts fell, cleft in two and in three by Bhīma's arrows.

**8.54.5**  Then, O king, amid your hosts, from the elephants, chariots, and horses struck down by Bhīma there arose a fearful roar, O lord of men, like that of mountains riven by the thunderbolt.

**8.54.6**  And those foremost of kings, slaughtered and pierced through by Bhīmasena's choicest shafts, swarmed upon Bhīma in the battle from every side, as birds settle upon a tree for the sake of its blossoms.

**8.54.7**  Then, swift as one possessed of utmost speed, he made his onslaught in the midst of your army, eager to consume and destroy, like Time itself with rod in hand bringing creatures to their end at the hour of doom.

**8.54.8**  His exceeding onrush in battle your men could not withstand, even as none can withstand the gaping, swift-falling onset of Time bearing off creatures at the appointed hour.

**8.54.9**  Then the host of the Bhāratas, O great-souled Bhārata, burning in the battle and stricken with fear, scattered to every quarter, driven by Bhīma, as a mass of clouds is scattered by a mighty wind.

**8.54.10**  Then the wise and mighty Bhīmasena, joyful once more, spoke to his charioteer: 'Charioteer, mark whether these chariots and banners closing upon us are foes or our own; for fighting on I can discern nothing—let me not cover my own troops with my shafts.'

**8.54.11**  'Viśoka, look out upon the foes on every side; yet anxiety sorely afflicts my mind. The king is in distress, and the Diademed One has not come; many griefs, O charioteer, are mine.'

**8.54.12**  'This is my grief, charioteer—that the Dharma-king, leaving me, has gone into the midst of the foe. I know not whether he lives or lives no longer, nor whether Bībhatsu lives; that is my deepest sorrow this day.'

**8.54.13**  'I therefore shall destroy this towering host of the enemy, supremely confident; and having slain these gathered together in the midst of battle, I shall this day be glad together with you.'

**8.54.14**  'Look over all the quivers and the shafts: what store of arrows remains in my chariot, of what kinds, and how many? Knowing this clearly, tell it to me, charioteer.'

**8.54.15**  Viśoka said: Sixty thousand arrows there are, O hero; razor-shafts and broad-headed shafts numbering ten thousand each; two thousand iron arrows, O hero, and three thousand barbed darts, O Pārtha.

**8.54.16**  There remains weaponry yet, O scion of Pāṇḍu, such as a cart drawn by six oxen could not bear. Knowing this, loose them by the thousands; and you have besides your mace, your sword, and the wealth of your own arms.

**8.54.17**  Bhīma said: Charioteer, behold this day a battle of terrible aspect, where kings are pierced through by swift, fierce arrows loosed by Bhīma—the sun blotted out, a scene like the world of death itself.

**8.54.18**  This very day, charioteer, it shall be known to all the kings, down to the very children, whether Bhīmasena has sunk down in the battle, or whether alone he is the conqueror of the Kurus in the fray.

**8.54.19**  Let all the Kurus fall upon me in battle, or let the worlds, down to the children, sing my praise: alone I shall bring them all down, or let them all together strike at Bhīmasena.

**8.54.20**  May the gods, my well-wishers, alone bring to fulfillment this highest deed of mine. Let Arjuna, slayer of foes, come hither this day, swiftly as Śakra summoned to a sacrifice.

**8.54.21**  Behold this Bhārata host breaking apart: why do these kings flee? Surely the wise Savyasācin, foremost of men, is swiftly covering this army with his arrows.

**8.54.22**  Look upon the banners fleeing, Viśoka—the elephants, the horses, and the throngs of foot-soldiers in the fray; behold the chariots shattered, struck down by arrow and lance, and behold these chariot-warriors too, charioteer.

**8.54.23**  That Kaurava army, ever filling its ranks anew yet most grievously slaughtered, is devoured by the arrows of Dhanaṃjaya—swift as the thunderbolt, fledged with peacock-feathers and gold.

**8.54.24**  These chariots, horses, and elephants flee, trampling down the bands of foot-soldiers; bewildered, all the Kauravas flee like elephants in terror of a forest fire; and crying woe in the battle, Viśoka, the lordly elephants send forth vast bellowings.

**8.54.25**  Viśoka said: All your desires, O Pāṇḍava, are fulfilled: the ape-bannered one is seen amid the elephant-host. As lightning leaping forth from a dark cloud, so I behold that bow flashing.

**8.54.26**  That ape is seen on every side, mounted upon the banner-tip of Dhanaṃjaya; and this divine jewel, bright as the sun, blazes set within his diadem.

**8.54.27**  At his side, terrible and shining like a white cloud, behold the conch Devadatta of fair sound, in the hand of Janārdana who, reins in hand, plunges into the army of the foe.

**8.54.28**  Bright as the sun, with adamant nave and razor-edged rim, behold the discus standing at the side of Janārdana, ever increasing the glory of Keśava and forever revered by the Yadus—behold it, hero.

**8.54.29**  Bhīma said: For this welcome tidings, charioteer, well pleased I give you fourteen choice villages, a hundred handmaids, and twenty chariots besides—since you announce Arjuna to me, Viśoka.

**8.55.1**  Saṃjaya said: Hearing the rumble of the chariot and the lion-roar in the battle, Arjuna said to Govinda: 'Drive the horses swiftly.'

**8.55.2**  Hearing Arjuna's words, Govinda said to Arjuna: 'I go this instant, with all speed, to where Bhīma stands his ground.'

**8.55.3**  He came on, drawn by horses white as snow and conch-shell, harnessed with nets of gold, pearl, and gem—like Indra, lord of the gods, in fierce wrath, gripping the thunderbolt and bent on victory over Jambha.

**8.55.4**  Hosts of chariots, horses, elephants, and foot-soldiers—those lions among men, enraged—rose up against him for victory, making earth and the quarters resound with the twang of arrows and the din of wheel-rims and hooves.

**8.55.5**  Then between them and Pārtha there raged a mighty, fair-fought battle—destroyer of bodies, of lives, and of sin—even as once it was for the god Viṣṇu, best of the victorious, against the Asuras for the sake of the three worlds.

**8.55.6**  The diadem-crowned hero, alone, cut to pieces the flood of weapons high and low that they hurled, and with razor-edged and crescent-headed shafts, with whetted arrows, lopped off in numbers their heads and arms.

**8.55.7**  Parasols, yak-tail fans, banners, horses, chariots, throngs of footmen, and elephants—mangled in countless ways, they fell to the earth like forests shattered by the wind.

**8.55.8**  Great elephants, draped in nets of gold and arrayed with triumphal flags, standards, and warriors, studded with gold-feathered arrows, blazed like mountains afire.

**8.55.9**  Rending elephants and chariots and horses with peerless shafts like Indra's thunderbolt, he sped swiftly on, eager to slay Karṇa, as of old the lord of the Maruts went forth to break Bala.

**8.55.10**  Then that tiger among men, O tamer of foes, the mighty-armed one, plunged into the army of the Sūta's son as a sea-monster plunges into the ocean.

**8.55.11**  Seeing him, O king, your men, with chariots and footmen, thronged with elephants, horses, and riders, rushed upon the Pāṇḍava.

**8.55.12**  As they charged upon Pārtha there arose a tremendous roar, like the surging clamor of waters in a frenzied ocean.

**8.55.13**  Those mighty car-warriors, like tigers, fell upon that tiger among men in battle, casting off all fear for their lives.

**8.55.14**  As they bore down on him, loosing showers of arrows, Arjuna scattered their host as a great wind scatters the clouds.

**8.55.15**  Those mighty bowmen, banding together with their lines of chariots, charged Arjuna and pierced him with whetted shafts.

**8.55.16**  Then with his arrows Arjuna dispatched thousands of chariots, elephants, and horses to the abode of Yama.

**8.55.17**  Slaughtered in the fray by the shafts loosed from Pārtha's bow, those great car-warriors, as terror seized them, slunk away in this direction and that.

**8.55.18**  Four hundred of their heroes, great car-warriors striving hard, Arjuna sent with his whetted arrows to the dwelling of Yama.

**8.55.19**  Cut down in battle by his whetted arrows of many forms, they abandoned Arjuna and fled in terror to every quarter.

**8.55.20**  Loud was the din of those fleeing at the army's front, like that of a great flood—good fortune be yours—bursting apart as it strikes a mountain.

**8.55.21**  Having sorely pierced that army and routed it with his arrows, Pārtha, O excellent one, advanced straight against the divisions of the Sūta's son.

**8.55.22**  Great was the sound of him as he bore down upon the foe, like that of Garuḍa swooping of old for the sake of the serpents.

**8.55.23**  Hearing that sound, the mighty Bhīmasena was filled with the highest joy, longing to behold Pārtha.

**8.55.24**  The valiant Bhīmasena, the moment he heard that Pārtha was coming, set his life at naught, O great king, and crushed your host.

**8.55.25**  Matching the wind in fury, equal to the wind's rush in speed, the valiant Bhīma, son of the Wind, ranged the field like the wind itself.

**8.55.26**  Tormented by him, O lord of kings, O lord of peoples, your army reeled, O great king, like a foundering ship upon the sea.

**8.55.27**  Displaying the deftness of his hand, Bhīma then hewed at that army with cruel shafts, minded to send it to the realm of Yama.

**8.55.28**  Beholding there Bhīma's more-than-human might, O Bhārata, the warriors trembled in battle as before Time at the ending of an age.

**8.55.29**  Seeing his fearsome forces thus tormented by Bhīmasena, O Bhārata, King Duryodhana spoke these words.

**8.55.30**  That mighty bowman, O bull of the Bhāratas, commanded all his soldiers and warriors in the battle: "Slay Bhīma! Once he is slain, I deem the whole army slain to the last man."

**8.55.31**  Accepting that command of your son, the kings veiled Bhīma on every side with showers of arrows.

**8.55.32**  Elephants in multitudes, O king, and men greedy for victory, chariots and horses, O lord of kings, encircled Vṛkodara.

**8.55.33**  Hemmed in on all sides by those heroes, the hero, O king, O best of the Bhāratas, shone like the moon amid the stars.

**8.55.34**  That comely best of men so blazed in the battle, O great king, no whit different from Vijaya himself.

**8.55.35**  There all those kings, their eyes red with rage, cruel and bent on killing Vṛkodara, let loose their showers of arrows.

**8.55.36**  Cleaving that great host with his smooth-jointed shafts, Bhīma broke free of the fray as a fish breaks from the net in the water.

**8.55.37**  Having slain ten thousand elephants that never turn back, and two hundred thousand men and two hundred more, O Bhārata,

**8.55.38**  and having slain five thousand horses and a full hundred chariots, Bhīma set flowing a river of mire and blood.

**8.55.39**  Its waters were blood, its eddies chariots, thronged with elephants for crocodiles; men were its fish, horses its alligators, and hair its moss and grasses.

**8.55.40**  Severed arms were its serpents, great elephants its waves; it bore off jewels in plenty, thighs were its seizing crocodiles, marrow its mud, and it teemed with heads for stones.

**8.55.41**  Bows were its reeds, arrows its strewn rushes, maces and iron bars its banners; bearing throngs of warriors in the battle, it flowed toward the abode of Yama.

**8.55.42**  In a moment that tiger among men set that river flowing, like the dread Vaitaraṇī, impassable to the unrighteous of soul.

**8.55.43**  Wherever that best of car-warriors, the son of Pāṇḍu, turned his course, there he struck down warriors by the hundreds of thousands.

**8.55.44**  Seeing this deed wrought by Bhīmasena in the battle, Duryodhana, O great king, spoke thus to Śakuni.

**8.55.45**  "Conquer the mighty Bhīmasena in battle, O uncle. Once he is conquered, I deem the mighty sons of Pāṇḍu conquered."

**8.55.46**  Then, O great king, the valiant son of Subala set forth for the great combat, made ready and surrounded by his brothers.

**8.55.47**  Coming upon Bhīma of fearsome valor in the battle, that hero held him back as the shore holds back the abode of sea-monsters; yet Bhīma, though checked, turned upon him with his whetted shafts.

**8.55.48**  Śakuni, O lord of kings, sped into his left side, between the breasts, iron shafts gold-feathered and whetted on stone.

**8.55.49**  Piercing the golden mail of that great soul, the arrows, fledged with heron and peacock feathers, sank deep, O great king.

**8.55.50**  Sorely pierced in the fray, Bhīma at once sped a gold-adorned shaft against the son of Subala, O Bhārata.

**8.55.51**  That terrible oncoming shaft Śakuni, scorcher of foes, cut into a hundred pieces, O king—mighty and deft of hand.

**8.55.52**  When it had fallen to the earth, Bhīma in wrath, O lord of peoples, severed with a broad-headed arrow the bow of Subala's son, as though laughing.

**8.55.53**  Casting aside the sundered bow, the valiant son of Subala swiftly took up another bow and sixteen broad-headed shafts.

**8.55.54**  With those smooth-jointed broad-headed shafts, O great king, he struck the charioteer with four and Bhīma with five.

**8.55.55**  With one he cut the banner, with two the parasol, O lord of peoples, and with four the son of Subala pierced the four horses.

**8.55.56**  Then in wrath, O great king, the valiant Bhīmasena hurled in the battle an iron spear with a haft of gold.

**8.55.57**  Loosed from Bhīma's arm, flickering like a serpent's tongue, it fell swiftly upon the chariot of the great-souled son of Subala.

**8.55.58**  Then, seizing that very gold-decked spear, he, fierce of aspect, hurled it back at Bhīmasena, O lord of peoples.

**8.55.59**  Piercing the left arm of the great-souled Pāṇḍava, it then fell to the earth like a flash of lightning loosed from the sky.

**8.55.60**  Then a great shout went up, O great king, from Dhṛtarāṣṭra's men on every side; but Bhīma could not brook that lion's roar of the swift ones.

**8.55.61**  Grasping his strung bow in haste, that great car-warrior, O lord of kings, in but a moment veiled with his shafts the host of Subala's son in battle, the mighty one heedless of his own life.

**8.55.62**  Having slain his four horses and his charioteer, O lord of peoples, the valorous one in haste cut down his banner with a broad-headed shaft.

**8.55.63**  Abandoning in haste his car with its slain horses, that best of men stood twanging his bow, his eyes red with rage and panting; and with many arrows, O king, he assailed Bhīma on every side.

**8.55.64**  But the valiant Bhīmasena swiftly warded him off, and in fury severed his bow and pierced him with whetted shafts.

**8.55.65**  Sorely pierced by his mighty foe, that harrier of foes fell then to the earth, barely alive, O ruler of men.

**8.55.66**  Then, knowing him stricken, your son, O lord of peoples, bore him away from the battle on his chariot before Bhīmasena's very eyes.

**8.55.67**  When that tiger among men was on the chariot, the men of Dhṛtarāṣṭra turned their backs and fled in fear to every quarter, as great dread of Bhīma arose.

**8.55.68**  When the son of Subala had been vanquished, O king, by the bowman Bhīmasena, your son Duryodhana, broken by great fear, withdrew on his swift horses, anxious for his uncle.

**8.55.69**  Seeing the king turn his back, O Bhārata, the troops broke and scattered, abandoning their single combats on every side.

**8.55.70**  Seeing all those great car-warriors of Dhṛtarāṣṭra turn away, Bhīma swiftly fell upon them, scattering many hundreds of shafts.

**8.55.71**  Slaughtered by Bhīma, the men of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, turned in flight, reached Karṇa and made their stand around him on every side, O king; for that one of great prowess and mighty strength had become their island of refuge.

**8.55.72**  As shipwrecked sailors, O king, O tiger among men, find peace upon reaching an island when their hour of doom is past,

**8.55.73**  even so, having reached Karṇa, your men, O bull of the Bhāratas, took heart and stood firm, O king, rejoicing one with another, and came together again for battle, making their stand against death.

**8.56.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When the armies had been broken by Bhīmasena in the battle, what did Duryodhana say, and what the son of Subala, O Saṃjaya?

**8.56.2**  Or Karṇa, best of the victorious, or my warriors in the fight, or Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman, or the son of Droṇa, or even Duḥśāsana?

**8.56.3**  Most wondrous, I deem, was this prowess of the son of Pāṇḍu; yet Rādheya too made good his vow concerning the warriors.

**8.56.4**  For all the Kurus, Karṇa the slayer of foes was their shelter, their armor, their mainstay, and their hope of life, O Saṃjaya.

**8.56.5**  Seeing that host shattered by the son of Kuntī of measureless might, what did Karṇa the great car-warrior, son of Rādhā, do in the fight?

**8.56.6**  And my unassailable sons, and the kings, those great car-warriors—tell me all of this, for you are skilled, O Saṃjaya.

**8.56.7**  Saṃjaya said: In the afternoon, O great king, the valiant son of the Sūta slew all the Somakas before Bhīmasena's very eyes; and Bhīma too, of surpassing strength, crushed the host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**8.56.8**  Seeing his force routed by the wise Bhīmasena, Karṇa said to his driver: "Bear me straight against the Pāñcālas."

**8.56.9**  Then Śalya, king of the Madras, the mighty one, urged on his white horses of great speed against the Cedis, the Pāñcālas, and the Karūṣas.

**8.56.10**  Plunging into that host, Śalya, crusher of hostile armies, joyfully reined the horses wherever their foremost driver wished to go.

**8.56.11**  At the sight of that chariot, dark as a thundercloud and draped with tiger-skins, the Pāṇḍavas and Pāñcālas were stricken with terror, O lord of peoples.

**8.56.12**  Then in that great battle the rumble of the chariot arose, loud as a rain-cloud, like the crash of a mountain splitting apart.

**8.56.13**  Then Karṇa too, with hundreds of keen shafts drawn back to the ear and let fly, slew the army of the Pāṇḍavas by hundreds and by thousands.

**8.56.14**  As he wrought that more-than-human work in the fray, the great bowmen and mighty car-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas encircled him.

**8.56.15**  Śikhaṇḍin and Bhīma, Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata, Nakula and Sahadeva, the sons of Draupadī together with Sātyaki — all surrounded Rādheya, seeking to slay him with downpours of arrows.

**8.56.16**  Then Sātyaki, that hero and best of men, struck Karṇa in the battle with twenty sharpened shafts upon the region of the collarbone.

**8.56.17**  Śikhaṇḍin pierced Karṇa with twenty-five and Dhṛṣṭadyumna with five, the sons of Draupadī with sixty-four and Sahadeva with seven, and Nakula in the encounter pierced Karṇa with a hundred shafts.

**8.56.18**  And the mighty Bhīmasena, enraged in the battle, pierced Rādheya in the region of the collarbone with ninety straight-jointed arrows.

**8.56.19**  Then the son of Adhiratha, laughing, drew his excellent bow and, exceedingly strong, loosed keen arrows that pressed them hard; and Rādheya pierced each of them in turn with five arrows apiece.

**8.56.20**  And that bull among men, having cut down Sātyaki's bow and his banner, then smote him with nine arrows between the breasts.

**8.56.21**  But Bhīmasena, enraged, pierced him with thirty shafts, and that scorcher of foes struck his charioteer with three arrows.

**8.56.22**  And that bull among men robbed the sons of Draupadī of their chariots in the mere twinkling of an eye, and it seemed a marvel.

**8.56.23**  Having turned all of them aside with his straight-jointed shafts, the hero slew the Pāñcālas and the great car-warriors of the Cedis.

**8.56.24**  As they were being slaughtered in the battle, O lord of peoples, the Cedis and Matsyas rushed upon Karṇa alone and tormented him with masses of arrows; but the son of the charioteer, that great car-warrior, struck them down with sharpened shafts.

**8.56.25**  This most marvellous thing I beheld in Karṇa, O descendant of Bharata — that the valiant son of the charioteer, alone in battle, faced those heroes.

**8.56.26**  He gave battle to those bowmen who strove with their utmost might, and held back the Pāṇḍavas in the fray with his arrows, O great king.

**8.56.27**  There at the deftness of the great-souled Karṇa, O descendant of Bharata, all the gods were pleased, and the Siddhas and the supreme seers.

**8.56.28**  The great bowmen, the followers of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, honoured that best of men, Karṇa, foremost among the finest of car-warriors, the foremost of all the wielders of the bow.

**8.56.29**  Then Karṇa, O great king, consumed the host of his foes as a great fire, kindled in the summer heat, burns up the dry brushwood.

**8.56.30**  Slaughtered by Karṇa, the Pāṇḍavas, terrified, fled this way and that across the battlefield at the sight of the mighty Karṇa.

**8.56.31**  There arose a great wailing of the Pāñcālas in that mighty battle, as they were cut down by the keen shafts sped from Karṇa's excellent bow.

**8.56.32**  Affrighted by that uproar, the great army of the Pāṇḍavas — those foes there in the battle — reckoned Karṇa to be the one fighter that was.

**8.56.33**  There Rādheya, harrier of foes, wrought a supreme marvel, in that all the Pāṇḍavas together were unable so much as to look upon him alone.

**8.56.34**  As a flood, striking against the mightiest of mountains, is shattered and scattered, so that Pāṇḍava army, coming upon Karṇa, was broken asunder.

**8.56.35**  And Karṇa too, O king, blazing in the battle like a smokeless fire, the mighty-armed one, stood there consuming the great army of the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.56.36**  And swiftly, O great king, the hero severed with his shafts the heads of heroes — together with their swaying earrings — their ears, and their arms.

**8.56.37**  Elephants' tusks, hilts and swords, banners, lances, horses, elephants, and chariots of many kinds, O king, ensigns and fans —

**8.56.38**  axles, arrows, yokes and traces, and wheels of every kind — Karṇa cut them into a hundred pieces, keeping fast to the warrior's vow.

**8.56.39**  There, O descendant of Bharata, with the elephants and horses slain by Karṇa, the earth became impassable, a mire of flesh and blood.

**8.56.40**  With slain horsemen and foot-soldiers, with chariots and elephants, neither rough ground nor level could be told apart — nothing whatever could be discerned.

**8.56.41**  Neither one's own warriors nor the enemy's could be told from one another, in that dreadful darkness of arrows when Karṇa's weapon-storm had spread wide.

**8.56.42**  By the gold-decked arrows loosed from Rādheya's bow, O great king, the striving great car-warriors were wholly overspread.

**8.56.43**  Those Pāṇḍavas, the great car-warriors, though they strove their hardest, were broken again and again by Karṇa in that battle, O great king.

**8.56.44**  As an enraged lion drives off herds of deer in the forest, so did the far-famed Karṇa scatter the warriors here and there in the battle, and harried that army as a wolf harries flocks of beasts.

**8.56.45**  And seeing the Pāṇḍava army turn its back, the great bowmen of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's host charged forward, raising terrible roars.

**8.56.46**  For Duryodhana, O lord of kings, filled with the highest joy and exulting, had all manner of instruments sounded on every side.

**8.56.47**  The Pāñcālas too, great bowmen and best of men, though broken again and again, turned back like heroes, making death itself their rallying-point.

**8.56.48**  As those heroes returned to the battle, Rādheya, tormentor of foes, that bull among men, broke them many times over, O great king.

**8.56.49**  There, O descendant of Bharata, twenty car-warriors of the Pāñcālas were slain by Karṇa in his wrath, together with their horsemen, and more than a hundred of the Cedis.

**8.56.50**  Having made empty the seats of the chariots and the backs of the horses, O descendant of Bharata, and the necks of the elephants bereft of men, and the foot-soldiers put to rout —

**8.56.51**  hard to gaze upon as the sun at noon, that scorcher of foes, the cruel son of the charioteer, ranged about wearing the form of Time the Ender.

**8.56.52**  Thus, O great king, having slain those men and horses, chariots and elephants, the great bowman Karṇa, destroyer of hosts of foes, stood firm.

**8.56.53**  As mighty Time, having destroyed the multitudes of creatures, stands alone, so did that great car-warrior, having slain the Somakas, stand there alone.

**8.56.54**  There we beheld a marvel — the prowess of the Pāñcālas, who, though being slaughtered by Karṇa, did not forsake the forefront of the battle.

**8.56.55**  The king Duryodhana and Duḥśāsana, and Kṛpa the son of Śaradvat, Aśvatthāman, Kṛtavarman, and Śakuni the son of Subala — they slew the Pāṇḍava army by hundreds and by thousands.

**8.56.56**  And the two sons of Karṇa, O lord of kings — brothers of true valour — destroyed the mighty Pāñcālas here and there; and the battle that raged there then was cruel, a great carnage.

**8.56.57**  In like manner the heroic Pāṇḍavas, Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, and the sons of Draupadī, all enraged, smote down your army.

**8.56.58**  Thus did this destruction befall the Pāṇḍavas here and there, and yours likewise in the battle, when they came upon the mighty Bhīma.

**8.57.1**  Saṃjaya said: But Arjuna, O great king, having marshalled the army in its several divisions, and beholding the son of the charioteer raging furiously in that great battle —

**8.57.2**  having made the earth a stream of blood, a river bearing flesh and marrow and bone — that bull among men spoke these words to Vāsudeva:

**8.57.3**  "Yonder, Kṛṣṇa, is seen in the battle the banner of the charioteer's son; and Bhīmasena and the rest there are giving battle to great car-warriors; and yonder the Pāñcālas flee, terrified of Karṇa, O Janārdana."

**8.57.4**  "Yonder is King Duryodhana, beneath his gleaming white parasol, shining bright as he drives off the Pāñcālas already broken by Karṇa."

**8.57.5**  "Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman, and the mighty son of Droṇa — these guard the king, themselves guarded by the charioteer's son; and if not slain by us, they will destroy the Somakas."

**8.57.6**  "Yonder Śalya, seated in the chariot, skilled in the handling of the reins, shines bright, O Kṛṣṇa, as he drives the chariot of the charioteer's son."

**8.57.7**  "There my resolve is fixed: drive this mighty chariot thither. Until I have slain Karṇa in the battle, I shall by no means turn back."

**8.57.8**  "Else Rādheya will make an utter end, in the battle, of the sons of Pṛthā and the great car-warriors of the Sṛñjayas, while we two look on, O Janārdana."

**8.57.9**  Then Keśava swiftly drove his chariot through your host toward the great bowman Karṇa, for a duel of chariots with Savyasācin.

**8.57.10**  And mighty-armed Hari set forth, with the Pāṇḍava's assent, heartening the armies of the Pāṇḍavas everywhere with that very chariot.

**8.57.11**  The clamour of the Pāṇḍava's chariot resounded in the fray, O honoured one, like the thunderbolt of Vāsava, like a vast flood.

**8.57.12**  With the great din of his chariot, the Pāṇḍava of true valour, that immeasurable spirit Vijaya, advanced upon your host.

**8.57.13**  Beholding him come on, the warrior of white steeds with Kṛṣṇa for his charioteer, the king of the Madras, seeing the banner of that great-souled one, spoke to Karṇa:

**8.57.14**  "Yonder comes that chariot of white steeds, with Kṛṣṇa for charioteer, slaying foes in the battle — the one of whom, O Karṇa, you keep asking."

**8.57.15**  "Here stands the son of Kuntī, his hand upon the Gāṇḍīva bow; if you can slay him this day, that will be our good fortune."

**8.57.16**  "Yonder the army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son is being shattered on every side, in swift terror of Arjuna as he slays a multitude of foes."

**8.57.17**  "Passing by all the other troops, Dhanaṃjaya presses on in haste — for your sake, I think, judging by how his frame is roused."

**8.57.18**  "For the son of Pṛthā, blazing with wrath because Vṛkodara is hard pressed, will be stayed in his eagerness to fight with none but you."

**8.57.19**  "Seeing the King of Righteousness deprived of his chariot and sorely wounded, and Śikhaṇḍin and Sātyaki, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna son of Pṛṣata —

**8.57.20**  the sons of Draupadī, Yudhāmanyu and Uttamaujas as well, and beholding his two brothers Nakula and Sahadeva —

**8.57.21**  the son of Pṛthā comes at you all at once with a single chariot, O scorcher of foes, his eyes red with rage, wrathful, eager to slay the foremost of all bowmen."

**8.57.22**  "In haste he falls upon us, leaving the troops aside, beyond doubt. Go forth against him, Karṇa, for there is no other bowman to match him."

**8.57.23**  "In this world I see no bowman other than you who, like a shore against the sea, could hold back Arjuna enraged in battle."

**8.57.24**  "And I see no guard of his, neither at his rear nor at his flank; alone he comes against you — behold the fulfilment of your purpose."

**8.57.25**  "For you are able to overcome the two Kṛṣṇas in the fray. This is your charge, Rādheya: go forth to meet Dhanaṃjaya."

**8.57.26**  "For by Bhīṣma, and by Droṇa, the son of Droṇa, and Kṛpa, you have indeed been made the match of Savyasācin — turn back that Pāṇḍava."

**8.57.27**  "Like a serpent darting its tongue, like a bellowing bull, like a tiger crouched to spring — strike down Dhanaṃjaya, O Karṇa."

**8.57.28**  "Yonder the great car-warriors of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's host flee in the battle, those lords of men, in swift terror of Arjuna, heedless of all else."

**8.57.29**  "And of them, as they flee in the fight, there is no other man, O hero, who could dispel their fear save you, O joy of the charioteer's race."

**8.57.30**  "All these Kurus, having found in you their island in the battle, stand fast, O tiger among men, seeking refuge from you."

**8.57.31**  "The Videhas, the Ambaṣṭhas, and the Kāmbojas, and likewise the Nagnajits, and the Gāndhāras, so hard to conquer, were vanquished by you in battle by that same firmness —"

**8.57.32**  Summon that same resolve, O Rādheya, and then go forth to meet the Pāṇḍava and Vāsudeva of the Vṛṣṇis, who rejoices in the Diademed One.

**8.57.33**  Karṇa said: Now, O Śalya, you appear to me restored to your true self and well-disposed, O mighty-armed one, and unafraid of Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.57.34**  Behold this day the strength of my arms, and behold my mastery of arms: alone today I shall destroy the great host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**8.57.35**  And the two Kṛṣṇas, those tigers among men—this truth I tell you: never shall I withdraw without having slain those two heroes in battle.

**8.57.36**  Either I shall lie slain by them in sleep—for victory in battle is never assured—or I shall achieve my purpose by killing them; otherwise I shall fall myself.

**8.57.37**  Never in this world, as far as we have heard, has there been so peerless a chariot-warrior. Against such a Pārtha I shall fight in the great battle—behold then my manhood.

**8.57.38**  This foremost of chariot-heroes ranges upon his car with swift horses, this prince of the Kuru kings. Either he shall bring me to grief this day, or else—mark this—the end of all of you lies in the end of Karṇa.

**8.57.39**  The prince's hands neither sweat nor tremble; they are broad and calloused with use. Firm in his weapon, skilled, swift of hand—there is no warrior equal to the son of Pāṇḍu.

**8.57.40**  He grasps many heron-feathered shafts as though they were but one, and crushing the lords of earth those unerring arrows fall a full krośa away. What warrior on earth is his equal?

**8.57.41**  The son of Pāṇḍu, that swift over-chariot-warrior with Kṛṣṇa for his second, gratified the Fire-eater—there the great-souled Kṛṣṇa won his discus, and the Pāṇḍava Savyasācin won the Gāṇḍīva bow.

**8.57.42**  From the Bearer of Oblations the mighty-armed one of undaunted spirit obtained that foremost chariot of fair thunder yoked with white horses, two great inexhaustible quivers of divine form, and divine weapons.

**8.57.43**  So too in Indra's realm he slew countless Daityas and all the Kālakeyas, and there he won the conch named Devadatta. Who upon earth, then, is greater than he?

**8.57.44**  That man of mighty power gratified Mahādeva himself in fair combat face to face, and thereby won the dread Pāśupata, the great weapon that works the destruction of the three worlds.

**8.57.45**  The guardians of the worlds, gathered together and each apart, gave him immeasurable weapons—with which that lion among men swiftly slew in battle the assembled Kālakhañja Asuras.

**8.57.46**  And in the city of Virāṭa, with a single chariot, he conquered us all assembled there, carried off that wealth of cattle in the midst of battle, and stripped the garments from the great chariot-warriors.

**8.57.47**  Such a one, endowed with the virtues of valor, with Kṛṣṇa for his second, do I choose to fight—him guarded by Keśava of boundless might, by the matchless Nārāyaṇa.

**8.57.48**  The virtues of that great soul—conch, discus, and sword in hand, Viṣṇu the conqueror, son of Vāsudeva—could not be told in ten thousand years even by all the worlds together. Fear indeed arises in me, and a shudder, to behold the two Kṛṣṇas joined upon a single car.

**8.57.49**  Both are brave, accomplished, firm in their weapons, great chariot-warriors of well-knit frame. Against such as Phalguna and Vāsudeva, who other than I, O Śalya, would come forth?

**8.57.50**  Either I shall lay these two low in battle, or the two Kṛṣṇas shall slay me this day. So speaking to Śalya, Karṇa the slayer of foes roared in the fight like a thundercloud.

**8.57.51**  Greeted with applause by your son when he drew near, he came together with the Kuru heroes and addressed them—Kṛpa and the Bhoja, both mighty-armed, the Gāndhāra king with his younger brothers, the preceptor's son, and his own younger brother, and others, the foot-soldiers and the elephant-riders:

**8.57.52**  "Hem them in, charge upon Acyuta and Arjuna, wear them down at once on every side with toil, that when both have been sorely wounded by you, O lords of earth, I may slay them with ease this day."

**8.57.53**  Saying "So be it," those bravest of the brave swiftly bore down upon Arjuna, eager to slay him; but as the great brimming ocean swallows the rivers and streams, so did Arjuna swallow them up in battle.

**8.57.54**  Neither in the nocking nor in the loosing of his finest shafts could Dhanaṃjaya be seen by his foes; and torn and slain by his arrows there fell men, horses, and elephants.

**8.57.55**  Flaming with arrows, his Gāṇḍīva drawn to a fair full circle, blazing with splendor like the sun at the dissolution of the age—the Kauravas could no more bear to gaze on the victor than men with diseased eyes can look upon the sun.

**8.57.56**  Kṛpa rushed upon him loosing arrows, and so too the Bhoja and your son himself; but in that great battle the Pāṇḍava, hastening, cut to pieces those finest shafts which they had sped with deadly care and effort, and pierced each of them through the breast with three arrows apiece.

**8.57.57**  With his Gāṇḍīva drawn to its full and ample circle, scorching his foes, Arjuna the sun shone forth—his rays the cruel arrows, ranging through the bright clear sky as the sun set within its halo.

**8.57.58**  Then Droṇa's son pierced Dhanaṃjaya with ten foremost arrows, Acyuta with three, the four horses with four, and the ape upon the banner he overwhelmed with choice iron shafts.

**8.57.59**  In return Dhanaṃjaya, with his quivering bow taken up, brought down from Droṇa's son's chariot his bow with three arrows, the driver's head with a razor-shaft, his four horses with four, and his banner with three.

**8.57.60**  Filled with fury, he took up another bow, adorned with gold like lightning's bolt, gleaming like the coils of Takṣaka, well-strung—as one might lift a great serpent-king from a mountain.

**8.57.61**  Strewing his own broken weapon upon the ground and stringing his bow afresh, the son of Droṇa, foremost in skill, from close at hand struck with his finest shafts those two unconquered best of men who rode together on one car.

**8.57.62**  And Kṛpa, the Bhoja, and your son fell upon him like rain-clouds upon the sun that dispels the dark; but with his shafts Pārtha shattered Kṛpa's bow together with its arrow, his horses, his banner, and his driver.

**8.57.63**  With his arrows he cut down the banner and bow of your roaring son, and the splendid hero slew the fair horses of Kṛtavarman and clove his banner asunder.

**8.57.64**  With their horses and drivers, their bows and their standards, he hastened and slew elephants, horses, and chariots; then your mighty host was scattered and torn through like a dam broken by the flood; and swiftly with Arjuna's chariot Keśava left the afflicted foes upon his left.

**8.57.65**  Then, as Dhanaṃjaya pressed swiftly onward like the Lord of a Hundred Sacrifices who had slain Vṛtra, other warriors eager for battle pursued him once more on well-yoked chariots with banners raised high.

**8.57.66**  Then the great chariot-warriors—Śikhaṇḍin, the grandson of Śini, and the twins—came up to ward off those foes from Dhanaṃjaya's car, and rending them with sharp arrows they shouted most terribly.

**8.57.67**  Then in their wrath they smote one another with straight-flying, keen-edged arrows—the Kuru heroes together with the Sṛñjayas—even as of old the Asuras fought against the foremost of the gods.

**8.57.68**  Longing for victory and eager to go to heaven, O scorcher of foes, elephants, horses, and chariots fell; they roared aloud, and mightily, each apart, they wounded one another with well-loosed arrows.

**8.57.69**  When the great-souled warriors had wrought a darkness of arrows upon one another in that mighty fray, the ten quarters and the heavens themselves grew dim, O king, and the radiance of the sun was wrapped in gloom.

**8.58.1**  Saṃjaya said: O king, Dhanaṃjaya, longing to rescue the son of Kuntī—Bhīma, beset by the foremost forces of the Kurus and as if sinking down—came to his aid.

**8.58.2**  Crushing with his arrows the army of the charioteer's son, O Bhārata, Dhanaṃjaya dispatched the enemy heroes to the realm of Death.

**8.58.3**  Then his nets of arrows, veiling the sky in successive flights, became visible, and others besides, smiting your host.

**8.58.4**  Filling with his arrows the sky thronged with flocks of birds, Dhanaṃjaya, O great king, became as the End-Maker to the Kurus.

**8.58.5**  Then with broad-headed shafts, razor-arrows, and bright iron darts Pārtha wasted their limbs and severed their heads.

**8.58.6**  On every side the field was strewn with warriors—limbs hewn off, armor stripped away, heads gone—both with the fallen and with those still falling.

**8.58.7**  With chariots, horses, men, and elephants struck down by Dhanaṃjaya's arrows, the field of battle, O king, became like the great Vaitaraṇī river.

**8.58.8**  The earth was strewn with the chariots of the combatants—their poles, wheels, and axles broken, some without horses, some still with their horses, some with their drivers and some with drivers slain.

**8.58.9**  Mounted by warriors in golden mail and corselets and decked with gold, noble elephants of perpetual rut, armored and enraged, urged on by enraged drivers, advanced against Arjuna.

**8.58.10**  Four hundred of them, slain by the showers of arrows of the Diademed One, fell down like the living-creatured peaks of a great mountain hurled to the ground.

**8.58.11**  The earth strewn with the finest elephants laid low by Dhanaṃjaya's arrows, Arjuna's chariot pressed on like the sun cleaving through the massed clouds.

**8.58.12**  With slain elephants, men, and horses, with chariots shattered in many ways, with valiant fighters whose weapons, plumes, and armor were gone and whose breath had fled, with weapons cast away, the path was strewn over by Phalguna.

**8.58.13**  And the Gāṇḍīva thundered with a great and terrible sound, dread as the crash of the thunderbolt of a storm in the sky.

**8.58.14**  Then the host, struck by Dhanaṃjaya's arrows, was torn asunder, like a great ship lashed by a mighty gale upon the sea.

**8.58.15**  Arrows of many forms, striking weapons sped from the Gāṇḍīva, like firebrands, meteors, and thunderbolts, consumed your army utterly.

**8.58.16**  As a bamboo grove ablaze by night upon a great mountain, so your mighty army flared and quivered, tormented by his arrows.

**8.58.17**  Crushed, burned, and shattered by the Diademed One, your army, smitten and stricken by his arrows, fled on every side to all the quarters.

**8.58.18**  As herds of deer seized by a forest fire in the great wood, so the Kurus, scorched by Savyasācin, turned and wheeled about.

**8.58.19**  For when they had let go the mighty-armed Bhīmasena in that battle, the whole army of the Kurus, panic-stricken, turned its back in flight.

**8.58.20**  Then, when the Kurus were broken, the unvanquished Bībhatsu came up to Bhīmasena and lingered with him for a while.

**8.58.21**  Having joined Bhīma and taken counsel with him, Phalguna told him that Yudhiṣṭhira was free of his arrow and healed of his pain.

**8.58.22**  Then, with Bhīmasena's leave, Dhanaṃjaya went forth, making earth and heaven resound with the thunder of his chariot, O Bhārata.

**8.58.23**  Then Dhanaṃjaya was surrounded by ten terrible bull-like foes, your sons, the younger brothers of Duḥśāsana.

**8.58.24**  With drawn bows, cruel, as if dancing, O Bhārata, they assailed him with arrows as men torment an elephant with firebrands.

**8.58.25**  But the slayer of Madhu kept them upon the left with his chariot's wheeling; then those heroes fled, even while Arjuna's car was turned away from them.

**8.58.26**  As they rushed on, Pārtha swiftly brought down their banners, chariots, bows, and arrows with iron shafts and crescent-headed darts.

**8.58.27**  Then with ten more broad-headed shafts he struck off their heads to the ground—their eyes red with wrath, their lips bitten through; and those faces shone in the sky like clusters of stars.

**8.58.28**  Having pierced those ten Kauravas with golden armlets with ten swift broad-headed shafts feathered with gold, the slayer of foes passed on.

**8.59.1**  Saṃjaya said: As he who bore the banner of the chief of apes rode on with swift-coursing horses, ninety chariot-heroes of the Kurus charged forth to battle; those tigers among men encircled the tiger among men, Arjuna, in the fray.

**8.59.2**  Kṛṣṇa urged the white, swift horses, decked with gold and covered with nets of pearls, toward Karṇa's chariot.

**8.59.3**  Then, as Dhanaṃjaya rode toward Karṇa's chariot, slaying his foes, the chariots of the Saṃśaptakas advanced, smiting him with showers of arrows.

**8.59.4**  But all those ninety heroes, hastening on with their drivers, bows, and banners, Arjuna slew with his whetted arrows.

**8.59.5**  Slain by the arrows of many forms loosed by the Diademed One, they fell as Siddhas with their celestial cars fall from heaven when their merit is spent.

**8.59.6**  Then the Kurus, with their chariots, elephants, and horses, fearlessly bore down, O best of the Kurus, upon Phalguna, the foremost of the Bhāratas.

**8.59.7**  That great army of your sons—strained to the utmost, its weapons not yet spent, its mighty elephants reared up—Dhanaṃjaya held in check.

**8.59.8**  With spears, lances, javelins, and darts, with maces, swords, and arrows, the great-bowed Kurus overspread the delight of the Kurus.

**8.59.9**  That outspread and upraised rain of weapons of the Kurus the Pāṇḍava scattered with his arrows, as the sun dispels the darkness with its rays.

**8.59.10**  Then the Mlecchas, with thirteen hundred elephants in rut held ready, smote Pārtha upon his flank at the command of your son.

**8.59.11**  With barbed shafts, reed-arrows, and iron darts, with lances, javelins, and spears, with kampana-missiles and bhiṇḍipāla-slings, they harried Pārtha as he stood upon his chariot.

**8.59.12**  That rain of weapons hurled by the Yavanas mounted on elephants, Phalguna, smiling, cut to pieces with his whetted broad-headed and crescent-headed shafts.

**8.59.13**  Then all those elephants, with their banners and their riders, he pierced with great arrows of many kinds, as the thunderbolt cleaves the mountains.

**8.59.14**  Studded with gold-feathered arrows, garlanded as if with gold, those great elephants fell slain like mountains crowned with flames of fire.

**8.59.15**  Then a great din of the Gāṇḍīva arose, O lord of peoples, mingled with the bellowing and the moaning of men and elephants and horses.

**8.59.16**  The elephants, stricken, O king, fled away on every side, and the horses, their riders slain, ran scattering in all ten directions.

**8.59.17**  Chariots bereft of their warriors and their steeds, O great king, were seen by the thousands, looking like the phantom cities of the gandharvas.

**8.59.18**  The horsemen, O great king, fleeing this way and that, were seen falling here and there, struck down by Pārtha's arrows.

**8.59.19**  In that moment the strength of the Pāṇḍava's arms was made manifest, for alone in battle he conquered cavalry and elephants and chariots.

**8.59.20**  Then, O bull of the Bhāratas, seeing the Diademed One surrounded by a great threefold host, O king, Bhīmasena—

**8.59.21**  leaving behind the few chariots of your side that survived the slaughter, charged with speed, O king, toward Dhanaṃjaya's chariot.

**8.59.22**  Then that army, the greater part of it slain and stricken, took to flight, when it saw Bhīma go toward his brother Arjuna.

**8.59.23**  The swift horses that had survived Arjuna's killing, Bhīma, mace in hand and unwavering, scattered in that great battle.

**8.59.24**  He wielded a mace fierce as the Night of Doom, a devourer of men and elephants and horses, a shatterer of ramparts and watchtowers and city gates, exceedingly dreadful.

**8.59.25**  Then Bhīma swiftly hurled the mace among men and elephants and horses, and it slew many steeds and their riders, O honored one.

**8.59.26**  Those men and horses clad in armor of bronze and iron the Pāṇḍava crushed with his mace, and with a great noise they fell down slain.

**8.59.27**  Having destroyed that elephant division, mighty Bhīmasena remounted his own chariot and followed in Arjuna's rear.

**8.59.28**  The enemy's host, slain, all but turned in flight, drained of spirit, lingered there, O great king, for the most part encircled by weapons.

**8.59.29**  Seeing that army faltering, dispirited, and standing irresolute, Arjuna overwhelmed it with arrows that tormented the life-breath.

**8.59.30**  Then there arose among the Kurus a wail of anguish in that great battle, as they were slaughtered by Arjuna's shafts that stole the lives of chariot-warriors, horses, and elephants.

**8.59.31**  Crying out in dismay, clinging close one to another, your army then whirled about like a circle traced by a firebrand.

**8.59.32**  That army of yours, set ablaze and stripped of its armor by the arrows, was drenched in its own blood, like a grove of aśoka trees in full crimson bloom.

**8.59.33**  Seeing there the prowess of Savyasācin, the Kurus all grew hopeless of Karṇa's life.

**8.59.34**  Deeming Pārtha's torrent of arrows unbearable in the fray, the Kurus turned back, vanquished by the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva.

**8.59.35**  Abandoning Pārtha in the battle and mown down by his shafts, they fled in terror in all directions and cried out to the Sūta's son.

**8.59.36**  Pārtha pursued them, scattering many hundreds of arrows, gladdening the Pāṇḍava warriors led by Bhīmasena.

**8.59.37**  But your sons, O great king, made for Karṇa's chariot; as they sank in fathomless waters, Karṇa then became their island.

**8.59.38**  For the Kurus, O great king, like serpents drained of their venom, clung close to Karṇa alone, out of dread of the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva.

**8.59.39**  As all creatures that are bound to action, in their fear of death, O Bhārata, take refuge in dharma alone,—

**8.59.40**  so did your sons, O ruler of men, in their terror of the great-souled Pāṇḍava, cling to Karṇa, that mighty archer.

**8.59.41**  To them, drenched in blood, set in dire straits, tormented by arrows, Karṇa said: "Do not fear—come hither, to me, on every side."

**8.59.42**  Seeing your host broken by force at Pārtha's hands, Karṇa, twanging his bow, stood firm with intent to slay the foe, and charged once more upon the Pāñcālas before the very eyes of Savyasācin.

**8.59.43**  Then in an instant the lords of earth, their eyes blood-red like wounds, rained floods of arrows upon Karṇa, as the clouds upon a mountain.

**8.59.44**  Then thousands of arrows loosed by Karṇa, O honored one, parted the Pāñcālas from their lives, O best of living beings.

**8.59.45**  Then there was a great battle, O lord of peoples, as the Pāñcālas, slayers of foes, were cut down by the Sūta's son for the sake of his friend.

**8.60.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, when the Kurus had fled, Karṇa, the Sūta's son, O king, with his white-horsed, armored car, scattered the sons of the Pāñcālas with his mighty shafts, as the wind scatters the masses of clouds.

**8.60.2**  Felling Janamejaya's charioteer from the car with an añjalika shaft, he slew his horses; and with broad-headed arrows he overwhelmed Śatānīka and Sutasoma, and cut their bows asunder.

**8.60.3**  Then he pierced Dhṛṣṭadyumna with six arrows and slew his right-hand horse in the fight; and having killed the steeds of Sātyaki, the Sūta's son slew Viśoka, the son of the Kaikeya king.

**8.60.4**  When the prince was slain, the Kaikeya commander, fierce of bow, charged upon him; pierced sorely by his swift and violent shafts, he in turn struck Suṣeṇa, Karṇa's own son.

**8.60.5**  With three crescent-headed arrows Karṇa forcibly severed both his arms and his head; and lifeless he fell from his chariot to the earth, like a śāla tree hewn down by axes.

**8.60.6**  Suṣeṇa, the charioteer's grandson, his own horses slain, covered the hero of Śini's line with swift, keen shafts and seemed almost to dance; yet, struck by an arrow of the Śaineya, he fell.

**8.60.7**  His son slain, his mind seized by wrath, Karṇa, eager to kill the bull of Śini's line, crying "You are slain, O Śaineya!" loosed an arrow that overcomes all foes.

**8.60.8**  But Śikhaṇḍin cut that arrow apart and struck back at Karṇa with three shafts after three; and Karṇa, cutting through Śikhaṇḍin's bow and banner, slew with two arrows the warrior Sujāta.

**8.60.9**  The fierce and self-controlled one pierced Śikhaṇḍin with six arrows and struck off the head of Dhṛṣṭadyumna's son; then the great-souled son of Adhiratha pierced Sutasoma with a whetted shaft.

**8.60.10**  Then, amid the tumultuous wailing, when the son of Dhṛṣṭadyumna had been slain there, Kṛṣṇa said, O lion among kings: "The Pāñcālas are being unmade—go, O Pārtha, and slay Karṇa."

**8.60.11**  Then, laughing, the foremost of men swiftly drove his chariot against the chariot of Adhiratha's son, the strong-armed one desiring to shield those who were being struck down by that leader of chariot-hosts.

**8.60.12**  Twanging the dread-sounding Gāṇḍīva and striking the bowstring hard against the leather guard, making at once a darkness of arrows, he slew elephants and horses and chariots and men.

**8.60.13**  Bhīmasena followed him with his chariot, guarding from behind that lone hero among the Pāṇḍavas; and the two princes, hastening on their two cars, set forth against Karṇa, breaking free of the foe.

**8.60.14**  Meanwhile the Sūta's son waged a mighty battle, crushing the Somakas; he slew their hosts of chariots, horses, and elephants, and shrouded all the directions with arrows.

**8.60.15**  Uttamaujas and Janamejaya, enraged, and Yudhāmanyu and Śikhaṇḍin, together with the son of Pṛṣata, assailed Karṇa with their shafts, crushing him in the press.

**8.60.16**  Those five Pāñcāla chariot-warriors, rushing upon Vaikartana Karṇa with their fair arrows, could not dislodge him from his chariot—no more than the senses can move a self-mastered man from his steadfastness.

**8.60.17**  Cutting away their bows, their banners, horses, charioteers, quivers, and pennons with his arrows, Karṇa struck those five with five shafts and then roared like a lion.

**8.60.18**  As he shot at and smote them down, bowstring and arrow in hand, at the twang of his bow the people sank in dread, thinking, "Surely the earth, with its mountains and trees, is being shattered."

**8.60.19**  With his bow, drawn taut and likened to Indra's rainbow, the son of Adhiratha, loosing arrows, shone in battle with a halo of blazing rays, like the sun garlanded in light.

**8.60.20**  He pierced Śikhaṇḍin with twelve whetted arrows, and Uttamaujas with six; with three swift shafts he struck Yudhāmanyu, and with three each the sons of Somaka and of Pṛṣata.

**8.60.21**  Those five great chariot-warriors, crushers of foes, vanquished by the Sūta's son in that great battle, O honored one, stood there bereft of effort, like the objects of sense overcome by a self-disciplined man.

**8.60.22**  As they were sinking in the sea that was Karṇa, like merchants with foundered ships upon the deep, the sons of Draupadī drew up their maternal uncles from the flood with their well-furnished chariots, as with boats from the ocean.

**8.60.23**  Then the bull of Śini's line, cutting apart with keen shafts the many arrows sped by Karṇa, and rending Karṇa with whetted iron-tipped darts, struck your eldest son with eight.

**8.60.24**  Then Kṛpa and the Bhoja and your son, and Karṇa himself, smote him with sharp arrows; and that best of the Yādavas fought against those four, as the lord of the Daityas against the guardians of the quarters.

**8.60.25**  With his bow evenly drawn, humming, bent to its full and raining countless arrows, that Sātyaki grew ever harder to assail, like the sun risen to mid-heaven in autumn.

**8.60.26**  Regaining their chariots, the Pāñcāla warriors, well-armored scorchers of foes, rallying in that great battle, guarded the hero of Śini's line, as the host of Maruts guards Indra in the crushing of his enemies.

**8.60.27**  Then there arose an exceedingly dreadful battle between your foes and your soldiers, destructive of chariots and horses and elephants, even as of old there was between the gods and the asuras.

**8.60.28**  Chariots and elephants, horsemen and footmen alike, wrapped about with weapons of every kind, wheeled to and fro; struck by one another they stumbled, cried out in anguish, and fell down lifeless.

**8.60.29**  In such a pass, your fearless son, the king's younger brother, advanced against Bhīma, scattering arrows; and Vṛkodara rushed swiftly upon him, as a lion springs upon a great ruru deer.

**8.60.30**  Then between those two, gambling with life as their stake, there was a battle beyond the human, each fixed in fury upon the other, those two exalted ones, like Śambara and Indra of old.

**8.60.31**  With body-ending, keenly sharpened arrows the two struck each other hard, like two great elephants in rut, their minds set on a single she-elephant between them, smiting again and again.

**8.60.32**  Then Vṛkodara, hastening, cut your son's bow and his banner with two razor-edged arrows, pierced his brow as well with a feathered shaft, and struck the head of his charioteer from the body.

**8.60.33**  That prince, taking up another bow, pierced Vṛkodara with twelve arrows; and himself reining in his steeds, he once more rained straight-flying shafts upon Bhīma.

**8.61.1**  Saṃjaya said: There the prince Duḥśāsana, fighting in the tumult, performed a hard feat: with a razor-edged arrow he cut Bhīma's bow, and pierced his charioteer with six shafts.

**8.61.2**  Then the great-souled one swiftly pierced Bhīmasena with many choice arrows; but he, pouring forth blood like an elephant in rut, hurled his mace at him in the tumult.

**8.61.3**  With it Bhīmasena, by main force, swept Duḥśāsana away the space of ten bow-lengths; struck by that swift mace, Duḥśāsana fell down trembling.

**8.61.4**  His horses with their charioteer were slain, O king, and his chariot ground to powder by the falling mace; his armor, ornaments, garments, and garlands shattered, he writhed, racked with grievous pain.

**8.61.5**  Then mighty Bhīmasena, recalling all the enmity that your sons had wrought, leapt down from his chariot and came to the ground, fixing his gaze with care upon him.

**8.61.6**  Drawing his keen, well-honed sword and treading upon the throat of the trembling man, he tore open the breast of the fallen one upon the ground, and drank his warm blood; and tasting it again and again and gazing about, wrathful beyond measure, he spoke these words:

**8.61.7**  "Than a mother's milk, than honey or clarified butter, than well-prepared mead, than the taste of celestial water, than the choicest draught churned from milk and curds—sweeter than all of these, I deem, is this taste of the lifeblood of my enemy that I drink today."

**8.61.8**  As he spoke thus and again rushed forward, tasting the blood and leaping in fierce exultation, those who beheld Bhīmasena then, shaken with terror, fell to the ground.

**8.61.9**  And those men there who did not fall—their weapons dropped from their hands, and they cried aloud in fear, and with closed eyes they would not look upon it.

**8.61.10**  All who there beheld Bhīma on every side drinking the blood of Duḥśāsana fled, overtaken by terror, crying out, "This is no man!"

**8.61.11**  In the hearing of the heroes of the world he spoke these words: "This blood of yours I drink from your very throat, O basest of men. Now, in your high fury, say once again, 'A cow! A cow!'"

**8.61.12**  "The sleep that you contrived at Pramāṇakoṭi, the feeding of the kālakūṭa poison, the cruel biting by serpents, the burning in the house of lac;—"

**8.61.13**  "the seizing of our kingdom by the game of dice, and the dwelling in the forest that followed, the arrows and weapons in the battles, and the miseries in our own house;—"

**8.61.14**  "these sorrows we know, and never once any joy—we, ever, through the wickedness of Dhṛtarāṣṭra together with his sons."

**8.61.15**  Having spoken these words, O king, and won his victory, Vṛkodara, O great king, smiling, spoke again to Keśava and Arjuna both:

**8.61.16**  "What I vowed against Duḥśāsana in battle, O heroes, all of that I have this day accomplished here. This very day I shall offer up the second sacrifice as well: slaughtering Duryodhana like a sacrificial beast, and crushing the head of that vile one with my foot, I shall win peace before the very eyes of the Kauravas."

**8.61.17**  Having spoken these words, exultant and his limbs drenched with blood, he roared aloud; and that mighty-souled hero of surpassing strength danced, even as the Thousand-eyed One had danced after slaying Vṛtra.

**8.62.1**  Saṃjaya said: When Duḥśāsana had been slain, ten of your sons, great chariot-warriors, heroes venomous with mighty wrath and never fleeing in battle, men of vast valor, O king, covered Bhīma over with their arrows.

**8.62.2**  They were Kavacin, Niṣaṅgin, Pāśin, Daṇḍadhāra, Dhanurdhara, Alolupa, Śala, Saṃdha, Vātavega, and Suvarcas.

**8.62.3**  These, gathered together and grieved by the calamity that had befallen their brother, hemmed in with their arrows the mighty-armed Bhīmasena.

**8.62.4**  Hemmed in on every side by the arrows of those great chariot-warriors, Bhīma, his eyes reddened with anger and full of wrath, shone like Death himself.

**8.62.5**  But the son of Pṛthā, his armlets of gold, with ten swift, keen broad-headed shafts feathered with gold, sent them, every one, to the abode of Yama.

**8.62.6**  When those heroes had been slain, your host, tormented with fear, fled before the Pāṇḍava, even as the Sūta's son looked on.

**8.62.7**  Then, O great king, Karṇa entered into the mighty fray, having beheld the prowess of Bhīma, which was like that of the Destroyer among living creatures.

**8.62.8**  But Śalya, splendid in assembly and knowing his bearing and his mood, spoke to Karṇa words fitting to the hour, O tamer of foes: "Yield not to dismay, O son of Rādhā; this becomes you not."

**8.62.9**  "These kings are fleeing, tormented by fear of Bhīmasena, and Duryodhana too is stupefied, stricken with grief at the destruction of his brothers."

**8.62.10**  "While Duḥśāsana's blood was being drunk by that great-souled one, their senses failed them and their spirits were broken by grief."

**8.62.11**  "Surrounding him on every side, O Karṇa, Kṛpa and the rest, together with the brothers who survived the slaughter, wait upon Duryodhana."

**8.62.12**  "And the Pāṇḍavas, sure of their aim and led by Dhanaṃjaya, those heroes, have come forward facing you alone, ready for battle."

**8.62.13**  "Therefore, O tiger among men, standing firm in your great manhood and setting the warrior's duty foremost, go forth to meet Dhanaṃjaya."

**8.62.14**  "For the whole burden has been laid upon you by Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son; bear it up, O mighty-armed one, according to your power and your strength. In victory there will be abundant glory, and in defeat heaven is assured."

**8.62.15**  "And while you are sunk in bewilderment, O son of Rādhā, your enraged son Vṛṣasena is rushing upon the Pāṇḍavas."

**8.62.16**  Having heard this speech of Śalya of measureless splendor, he steadied his human spirit firmly in his heart for the battle.

**8.62.17**  Then the enraged Vṛṣasena rushed against Vṛkodara—who had slain his foe and mounted his own chariot, mace in hand like Death wielding his rod—as he was crushing your warriors.

**8.62.18**  Against him the foremost hero Nakula rushed, in his fury piercing the foe with his shafts—against Karṇa's son, exultant in the fray—the victorious one eager to slay him, as Maghavan once sought to slay Jambha.

**8.62.19**  Then the hero Nakula with a razor-edged shaft cut down the banner, bright with crystal and conch-shell, and with a broad-headed arrow shattered Karṇa's son's gleaming bow, bound with bands of pure gold.

**8.62.20**  Then Karṇa's son, taking up another bow with great speed, pierced the Pāṇḍava; that master of mighty weapons struck Nakula with celestial great weapons, seeking to avenge Duḥśāsana.

**8.62.21**  Then the enraged, great-souled Nakula pierced him with arrows like great meteors; and Karṇa's son too, skilled in arms, pierced Nakula in return with celestial weapons.

**8.62.22**  Karṇa's son, O king, with his choicest weapons destroyed all the horses of the delicate Nakula—horses of the Vanāyu breed, white and swift, adorned with gold.

**8.62.23**  Then, alighting from his car whose horses were slain, he took up a beautiful shield marked with eight moons and, grasping a sword the color of the sky, he ranged about, leaping like a bird.

**8.62.24**  Then, coursing aloft through the air in wondrous paths, he cut down foremost men and horses and elephants; slain by his sword, they fell to the earth, like beasts slaughtered by the immolators at a horse-sacrifice.

**8.62.25**  Two thousand renowned warriors, valiant in battle, from many lands, well-nourished and true to their pledges, were swiftly cut down by Nakula alone, as fine sandalwood trees are felled by one who craves their essence.

**8.62.26**  As Nakula came on, he too fell upon him and pierced him on every side with his shafts; and Nakula, thus stung with arrows, pierced the hero in return, who, wounded, was filled with wrath.

**8.62.27**  As he, single-handed, scattered the foremost of men, horses, elephants, and chariots, sporting as he went, Karṇa's son pierced that hero with eighteen shafts; and he, wounded, was filled with wrath.

**8.62.28**  Then the heroic son of Pāṇḍu rushed in the fray, eager to slay Karṇa's son; but Karṇa's son with his arrows shattered, in that great battle, his shield studded with a thousand stars.

**8.62.29**  As he brandished his iron sword, whetted and keen-edged, unsheathed, able to bear a heavy stroke, the bearer-away of foemen's bodies, most terrible, dreadful in form like a serpent—

**8.62.30**  —that subduer of foes swiftly, with six keen, well-edged arrows, cut the sword in two, and again with sharp, whetted shafts, oil-treated, pierced him deep between the breasts.

**8.62.31**  Then Mādrī's son, his horses slain and sore beset by Karṇa's son, leapt up onto Bhīmasena's chariot, like a lion onto a mountain-peak, while Dhanaṃjaya looked on.

**8.62.32**  Then, learning that Nakula's bow and sword were severed, his car destroyed, himself tormented by the foe's arrows and broken by Karṇa's son's weapons, those chariots—banners shaken by the wind, rumbling, horses prancing, driven by the best of men—swiftly came on.

**8.62.33**  The five foremost sons of Drupada, with Śini's grandson as the sixth, and the five sons of Drupada's daughter, subduers of foes, slaying your elephants, chariots, men, and horses with arrows like serpent-lords, came on with weapons in hand.

**8.62.34**  Then your foremost chariot-warriors went forth against them in haste—Hṛdika's son and Kṛpa, Droṇa's son and Duryodhana, Śakuni, Śuka, and Vṛka, and Krātha and Devāvṛdha—with chariots that thundered like elephants and rain-clouds, and with their bows.

**8.62.35**  Your foremost of warriors, smiting those eleven heroes with their choicest excellent shafts, hemmed them in; but the Kuṇindas rode up against them on elephants the hue of fresh rain-clouds, like mountain-peaks and of terrible speed.

**8.62.36**  Well-caparisoned, born of the Himālaya, furious with rut, mounted by skilled men eager for battle, those elephants overspread with nets of gold shone forth like rain-clouds charged with lightning.

**8.62.37**  The son of the Kuṇinda king with ten great iron shafts sorely afflicted Kṛpa together with his charioteer and horses; but slain by the arrows of Śaradvat's son, he fell to the ground along with his elephant.

**8.62.38**  But the younger brother of the Kuṇinda's son, with iron lances like the rays of the sun, shook a chariot and roared aloud; thereupon the lord of the Gāndhāras struck off his head as he roared.

**8.62.39**  Then, when those Kuṇindas had been slain, your great chariot-warriors, exultant in look, loudly blew their conches born of the salt sea, and, bows in hand, advanced against the foe.

**8.62.40**  Then there arose once more a most terrible battle between the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas and Sṛñjayas—with arrows, swords, spears, lances, maces, and battle-axes—a fearfully turbulent fray that bore off the life-breath of men and horses and elephants.

**8.62.41**  Then chariots, horses, elephants, and foot-soldiers, striking one another, fell to the earth, like clouds charged with lightning and thunder driven together from the quarters by fierce winds.
