**8.62.42**  Then Śatānīka slew your great elephants, your chariots, and your troops of foot-soldiers; and the Bhoja chief slew the horses; and the elephants, cut down and disarmed by Kṛtavarman, fell.

**8.62.43**  Then three other elephants, struck by the arrows of Droṇa's son, with all their weapons, warriors, and standards, fell lifeless to the earth, hurled down like great mountains smitten by the thunderbolt.

**8.62.44**  He who was next after the younger brother of the Kuṇinda king smote your son between the breasts with excellent feathered shafts; but your son with his keen arrows pierced his body and his elephant.

**8.62.45**  That lord of elephants, together with the prince, fell, pouring forth much blood on every side, as a mountain of red ochre, struck by the thunderbolt of Śacī's lord, pours down its waters at the coming of the clouds.

**8.62.46**  Another elephant, urged on by the Kuṇinda's son, crushed Śuka together with his charioteer, horses, and car; then, torn by Krātha's arrows, it fell along with its rider, like a mountain smitten by the thunderbolt.

**8.62.47**  The chariot-warrior Krātha, the unconquerable lord, was struck by the arrows of the mountain-born one mounted on his elephant and fell, together with his horses, charioteer, and bow, like a great tree felled by a mighty wind.

**8.62.48**  Vṛka sorely pierced with twelve arrows the dweller of the great mountains who rode his elephant; thereupon that one in haste with four shafts crushed swift-moving Vṛka, together with his horses and car, at the feet.

**8.62.49**  That lord of elephants, together with its driver, fell, sorely smitten by the arrows of Babhru's son; and Devāvṛdha's son too, afflicted and struck down by Sahadeva's son, fell.

**8.62.50**  On an elephant whose tusks, trunk, and other limbs were slayers of foes, the Kuṇinda's son went with speed to slay Śakuni, sorely afflicting him; thereupon the lord of the Gāndhāras struck off his head.

**8.62.51**  Then your great elephants, horses, chariots, and bands of foot-soldiers, slain by Śatānīka, like trees struck by the wind of Garuḍa's wings, went helplessly to the earth, crushed to powder.

**8.62.52**  Then the Kuṇinda's son, smiling, pierced Nakula's son with many keen arrows; thereupon Nakula's son with a razor-edged shaft severed from his body his head, whose face was like a lotus.

**8.62.53**  Then Karṇa's son pierced Śatānīka with three swift keen shafts, Arjuna with three, Bhīma with three, Nakula with seven, and Janārdana with twelve arrows.

**8.62.54**  Beholding that deed of his, surpassing the deeds of men, the Kurus, rejoicing, applauded him; yet those who knew Dhanaṃjaya's prowess deemed him already as an oblation cast into the fire.

**8.62.55**  Then the Diademed One, slayer of hostile heroes, beholding that foremost of men, Vṛṣasena, whose horses had been slain, rushed against him in battle, as he stood then before the Sūta's son.

**8.62.56**  As that fierce hero among men came on in the great battle, bearing a thousand arrows, Karṇa's son, the great chariot-warrior, fell upon him, even as Namuci fell upon Indra in the days of old.

**8.62.57**  Then, having pierced the son of Pṛthā with a wondrous hundred arrows, the Sūta's son's son, of great might, roared aloud, as Namuci of old roared when he had pierced Śakra.

**8.62.58**  Again Vṛṣasena pierced the son of Pṛthā with fierce arrows in the joints of the shoulders, and likewise afflicted Kṛṣṇa with nine, and again the son of Pṛthā with ten keen-pointed shafts.

**8.62.59**  Then the Diademed One, at the head of the battle, knitting in his wrath a three-forked frown upon his brow, the great-souled one loosed his keen arrows, O king, to slay the Sūta's son in the fray.

**8.62.60**  And the Diademed One pierced him forcibly with ten shafts in his vital parts where he was unguarded, and with four razor-edged arrows, fierce ones, cut off his bow, his arms, and his very head.

**8.62.61**  Struck by the arrows of Pṛthā's son, he fell from his chariot to the earth, armless and headless, his huge frame like a fair-blossomed, leaf-laden sāla tree shaken by the wind from a mountain-crest.

**8.62.62**  Beholding his son, struck by the arrows, falling from the chariot, the swift-acting son of the Sūta, sorely pained by his son's slaying, swiftly drove his car with speed against the chariot of the Diademed One.

**8.63.1**  Saṃjaya said: Beholding Vṛṣasena slain, the Bull among men, filled with grief and indignation, suddenly let fall from his eyes the waters born of sorrow.

**8.63.2**  Karṇa the splendid drove his chariot straight against the foe, his eyes coppery-red with wrath, challenging Dhanaṃjaya to battle.

**8.63.3**  Those two chariots, bright as the sun and draped with tiger-skins, men saw come together there like two suns met in one place.

**8.63.4**  Borne by white horses, those two suns among men, those crushers of foes, those great-souled ones, shone as they stood, like the moon and the sun in the sky.

**8.63.5**  Beholding the two, all beings fell into wonder, O sir, as though they were Indra and the son of Virocana striving for the conquest of the three worlds.

**8.63.6**  With the din of chariots, bowstrings, and leather guards, and with the clamor of arrows and conches, those two chariots rushed onward, while the lords of earth looked on.

**8.63.7**  And seeing the two standards joined in close conflict—Karṇa's elephant-girth emblem and the ape of the Diademed One—wonder arose in them.

**8.63.8**  And seeing those two chariots locked together, O Bhārata, the kings raised lion-roars and abundant cries of applause.

**8.63.9**  And hearing of the single combat between the two, the warriors on every side raised a great clamor of beating arms and waving garments.

**8.63.10**  The Kurus came thither then, accompanied by music, and, gladdening Karṇa, blew their conches in abundance.

**8.63.11**  Likewise all the Pāṇḍavas, gladdening Dhanaṃjaya, made all the quarters resound with the blare of trumpets and conches.

**8.63.12**  With roars, the slapping of arms, and shouts, there was tumult on every side, and the din of warriors' arms at the meeting of Karṇa and Arjuna.

**8.63.13**  Beholding those two tigers among men, foremost of chariot-warriors, standing upon their cars, their great bows grasped, armed with arrows, spears, and maces—

**8.63.14**  —clad in mail, swords girded on, borne by white horses, splendid with conches, furnished with excellent quivers, the two of them were fair to behold.

**8.63.15**  Their limbs smeared with red sandal-paste, like two bulls in rut, resembling venomous serpents, like Yama, Time, and the Destroyer—

**8.63.16**  —wrathful like Indra and Vṛtra, radiant as the sun and moon, fierce as two great planets risen at the ending of an age—

**8.63.17**  —divinely born, godlike, equal to the gods in form—beholding those two tigers among men, Karṇa and Dhanaṃjaya, come together.

**8.63.18**  Both bore the finest weapons, both were seasoned in the toil of battle, and both made the vault of heaven resound with the clamor of their arms.

**8.63.19**  Both were renowned for their deeds, in manhood and in might, and both were equals in war to Śambara and to the king of the gods.

**8.63.20**  In battle they were the peers of Kārtavīrya, and the equals of the son of Daśaratha; in valor they matched the might of Viṣṇu, and in combat they were the equals of Bhava.

**8.63.21**  Both had white horses, O king, and both rode in the finest of chariots; and the charioteers of each were peerless and of mighty strength.

**8.63.22**  Beholding those two great warriors in their splendor, O mighty king, wonder took hold of the hosts of the Siddhas and the Cāraṇas.

**8.63.23**  Then the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra with their forces, O bull of the Bhāratas, swiftly closed in around the great-souled Karṇa, resplendent in the fray.

**8.63.24**  In the same way the Pāṇḍavas, exultant and led by Dhṛṣṭadyumna, closed in around the great-souled Pārtha, who had no equal in battle.

**8.63.25**  In the battle, O lord of peoples, Karṇa was the stake of your men, just as Pārtha was the stake of the Pāṇḍavas in the fight.

**8.63.26**  They themselves were there as the assembled members of the gaming-court and became its spectators; and for those two who staked their lives, victory and defeat hung in the balance.

**8.63.27**  Upon those two the dicing turned, for victory or for the other lot, as we and the Pāṇḍavas stood ranged at the head of the battle.

**8.63.28**  Those two stood firm in the battle, O great king, skilled in war, each enraged against the other, each seeking the other's defeat.

**8.63.29**  Both longed to strike each other down on every side, like Indra and Vṛtra; terrible of form, they stood like two great smoke-wreathed planets of ill omen.

**8.63.30**  Then in the sky, O bull of the Bhāratas, there arose taunting wrangles and mutual divisions among the beings over Karṇa and Arjuna; the quarters were split, my lord, and all the worlds took different sides.

**8.63.31**  Gods, Dānavas, and Gandharvas, Piśācas, serpents, and Rākṣasas took up opposing partisanships at the encounter of Karṇa and Arjuna.

**8.63.32**  The heaven with its constellations was intent on Karṇa's side, O lord of peoples; while the broad earth was for Pārtha, O Bhārata, as a mother for her son.

**8.63.33**  The rivers and the oceans, and the mountains too, O best of men, the trees and the herbs—all there took the side of the Diademed One.

**8.63.34**  The Asuras and the Yātudhānas and the Guhyakas, O scorcher of foes, together with the birds that range the sky, took their stand on Karṇa's side.

**8.63.35**  All the jewels and the treasures, and the Vedas with their tales for a fifth, together with the Upavedas and Upaniṣads, the secret doctrines and the digests—

**8.63.36**  Vāsuki and Citrasena, Takṣaka and Upatakṣaka, and all the mountains too, and the sons of Kadrū with their lineages—the venom-bearing serpents of great wrath—took their place on Arjuna's side.

**8.63.37**  The serpents of Airāvata's race, the sons of Surabhī, and the Vaiśāleyas—these stood on Arjuna's side, while the lesser snakes were for Karṇa.

**8.63.38**  The wolves and beasts of prey, and the auspicious creatures, beasts and birds—all of these, O king, looked to Pārtha's victory and cleaved to it.

**8.63.39**  The Vasus, the Maruts, the Sādhyas, the Rudras, the Viśvedevas and the two Aśvins, Agni and Indra and Soma and the Wind, and the ten quarters—these came to Dhanaṃjaya, while the Ādityas stood on Karṇa's side.

**8.63.40**  The gods, together with the ancestors and their hosts, sided with Arjuna; and Yama and Vaiśravaṇa and Varuṇa stood where Arjuna stood.

**8.63.41**  The hosts of the divine, brahmin, royal, and human seers were on the Pāṇḍava's side; and the Gandharvas led by Tumburu, O king, were where Arjuna was.

**8.63.42**  The Prāveyas together with the Mauneyas, the hosts of Gandharvas and Apsarases, along with the wolves and beasts of prey, the elephants, and the chariot-riders and foot-soldiers—

**8.63.43**  Borne aloft thus by the clouds and the wind, the wise ones came together, eager to behold the encounter of Karṇa and Arjuna.

**8.63.44**  Gods, Dānavas, Gandharvas, serpents, Yakṣas, and winged beings, the great seers learned in the Vedas, and the ancestors who feed on the funeral offering—

**8.63.45**  Austerities, sciences, and the healing herbs, robed and gleaming in their varied forms, took their stand in the sky, O great king, crying aloud.

**8.63.46**  Brahmā, together with the brahmin-seers and the Prajāpatis, attended by Bhava, came in a celestial car to that place.

**8.63.47**  Seeing the Lord of creatures, the Self-existent, the gods drew near and said: "O god, let the victory of these two lions among men be even."

**8.63.48**  Hearing this, Maghavat bowed to the Grandsire and said: "Let not the whole world be destroyed through the ruin of Karṇa and Arjuna.

**8.63.49**  O Self-existent One, speak the word: let the victory of these two be equal. So let it be—homage to you; be gracious to me, O Lord."

**8.63.50**  Then Brahmā and Īśāna spoke this word to the lord of the thirty gods: "Let the victory belong assuredly to the great-souled Vijaya.

**8.63.51**  He is high-minded, strong, and brave, accomplished in arms, rich in austerity; and the mighty-splendored one wields the whole science of archery without exception.

**8.63.52**  By his greatness of soul he might overstep what fate ordains for him, through some turn of events; and were that overstepped, the destruction of the worlds would surely follow.

**8.63.53**  There is nowhere any withstanding the two Kṛṣṇas when they are wroth; for both of these bulls among men are the creators of what is not and of what is.

**8.63.54**  These two are Nara and Nārāyaṇa, the ancient and most excellent of seers; uncontrolled themselves, they are the controllers of all, fearless and scorchers of the foe.

**8.63.55**  Let this Karṇa, the bull among men, attain the foremost worlds—the hero Vaikartana, the brave; but let the victory be the two Kṛṣṇas'.

**8.63.56**  And let him win a place in the world of the Vasus, or of the Maruts, and, together with Droṇa and Bhīṣma, be honored in the world of heaven."

**8.63.57**  Thus addressed by the two gods of gods, the Thousand-eyed spoke his word, calling upon all beings by the command of Brahmā and Īśāna:

**8.63.58**  "You have heard what the two Lords have spoken for the good of the world. So shall it be and not otherwise—so let your anger pass, and abide in peace."

**8.63.59**  Hearing Indra's words thus, all beings, my lord, were filled with wonder, O king, and did honor to that decree.

**8.63.60**  And from the sky the gods let fall showers of fragrant flowers of varied kinds, and sounded the celestial drums.

**8.63.61**  Eager to behold the matchless single combat of those two lions among men, the gods, Dānavas, and Gandharvas all took their stations; and those two chariots, white-horsed, with banners set and loud of sound, drew on.

**8.63.62**  The heroes of the world, come together, blew their conches each apart—the valiant Vāsudeva and Arjuna, and Karṇa and Śalya, O Bhārata.

**8.63.63**  Then there arose that battle, terrifying to the timid, between the two who vied in valor, like that of Śakra and Śambara.

**8.63.64**  The two banners of those warriors, garlandless, shone forth as they stood upon the chariots, each of a different form, and they seemed to rouse each other's wrath in the fight.

**8.63.65**  Karṇa's banner, like a venomous serpent, set with the essence of jewels, firm, resembling Purandara's bow, an elephant-girth, shone resplendent.

**8.63.66**  But Pārtha's emblem, the foremost of apes, with gaping jaws and terrible, struck fear with its fangs, hard to look upon as the sun.

**8.63.67**  Eager for battle, the banner of the wielder of Gāṇḍīva drew near to Karṇa's banner, and it spoke, roaring out loud.

**8.63.68**  Springing up with great force, the ape struck at the elephant-girth with its claws and teeth, as Garuḍa falls upon a serpent.

**8.63.69**  Adorned with fair little bells, of iron like the noose of Death, the serpent-girth, sorely enraged, rushed upon the great ape.

**8.63.70**  As the supreme single combat of the two was joined like a game of dice, the two banners waged their own war, and the horses neighed defiance at the horses.

**8.63.71**  The Lotus-eyed one pierced Śalya with the arrows of his glance, and he in turn looked back upon the Lotus-eyed one just so.

**8.63.72**  There Vāsudeva overcame Śalya with the arrows of his eyes, and Kuntī's son Dhanaṃjaya likewise overcame Karṇa with his gaze.

**8.63.73**  Then the charioteer's son, speaking to Śalya with a smile, said: "If Pārtha should slay me here in battle today by any chance, what would then be your course thereafter? Tell me truly, my friend."

**8.63.74**  Śalya said: "If today, O Karṇa, the White-horsed one should slay you in battle, then I myself, with this one chariot, would slay both Mādhava and the Pāṇḍava."

**8.63.75**  Saṃjaya said: In just this way did Arjuna speak in turn to Govinda; and Kṛṣṇa, laughing, said this further word to Pārtha:

**8.63.76**  "The maker of day might fall from his place, the earth might shatter into many pieces, fire might turn cold—but Karṇa will not slay you, O Dhanaṃjaya.

**8.63.77**  But if such a thing should somehow come to pass, like the overturning of the worlds, then I myself would slay Karṇa, and Śalya too, with my bare arms in the battle."

**8.63.78**  Hearing Kṛṣṇa's words, the ape-bannered Arjuna, laughing, replied to Kṛṣṇa, the tireless doer of deeds: "Even both of them, Karṇa and Śalya, are no match for me, O Janārdana.

**8.63.79**  Karṇa with his pennon and banner, with Śalya and his chariot and steeds, with his parasol and armor, with his spear, arrows, and bow—

**8.63.80**  Today you shall see Karṇa cut to pieces by my arrows in the battle—him, with his chariot, his steeds, his spear, his armor, and his weapons; for my enmity finds no rest, since once he laughed aloud at Kṛṣṇā.

**8.63.81**  Today, O Govinda, you shall see Karṇa torn down by me, as a flowering forest tree is torn down by a maddened elephant.

**8.63.82**  Today, O slayer of Madhu, you shall hear those sweet words; today, freed of my debt, you shall comfort Abhimanyu's mother and Kuntī, your father's sister, with great gladness, O Janārdana.

**8.63.83**  Today, O Mādhava, you shall comfort tear-faced Kṛṣṇā, and with words like nectar shall comfort the king of righteousness, Yudhiṣṭhira."

**8.64.1**  Saṃjaya said: Thronged with the hosts of gods and serpents, Asuras and Siddhas, and with the hosts of Gandharvas, Yakṣas, and Apsarases, frequented by brahmin-seers and royal-seers and the bird-king's kin, the heaven shone with a form to fill one with wonder.

**8.64.2**  Resounding with sweet cries, with the music of instruments and song and hymns of praise and dances, all the men gazed up into the sky and beheld those sky-dwelling forms wondrous to look upon.

**8.64.3**  Then the warriors of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas, exultant, with the din of instruments, the rustle of feathered shafts, and lion-roars, made the earth and the quarters resound, and with that uproar all of them fell upon their foes.

**8.64.4**  Crowded with the swarm of countless horses, elephants, and chariots, unbearable with the rain of fine swords, spears, and lances, frequented by the fearless and heaped with slain bodies, the field of battle shone red with blood.

**8.64.5**  As the rout of the weapon-bearers thus set in, Dhanaṃjaya and the son of Adhiratha, both armored, veiled the quarters and the host, and each other, with their sharp, straight-flying shafts.

**8.64.6**  Then, when darkness was wrought by the arrows of your men and of the foe, they could discern nothing; but out of fear all took refuge in those two chariots alone, the dispellers of gloom, like the rays of light spread out in the sky.

**8.64.7**  Then those two scattered each other's missile with their own, like the winds of east and west; and amid the thick-spread darkness, dispellers of gloom, they shone exceedingly bright, like two risen suns.

**8.64.8**  "These two are not to be assailed"—so urged, the foe and your men alike then stood still, ringing those two great warriors about on every side, as gods and Asuras ring Vāsava and Śambara.

**8.64.9**  While the air rang with the sound of tabors, kettledrums, cymbals, and drums, O Bhārata, and with the blare of conches, those two best of men, roaring like lions, shone forth like the moon and the sun amid a gathering of clouds.

**8.64.10**  Both standing within the circle of their great bows, both full of splendor, with a thousand arrow-rays for beams, as if longing to burn up the world of moving and unmoving things, they were unbearable in battle, like two suns set at the age's end.

**8.64.11**  Both unconquerable, both destroyers of the enemy, those two accomplished warriors sought to slay each other; in the great battle the two best of heroes, Karṇa and the Pāṇḍava, met like Indra and Jambha.

**8.64.12**  Then those two wielders of great bows, loosing mighty missiles with their fearful shafts, the matchless pair slew men, horses, and elephants, and struck at each other with their finest arrows.

**8.64.13**  Then, tormented again by the arrows of those two best of men, the followers of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas scattered, with their elephants, foot-soldiers, horses, and chariots, and fled to the quarters, even as forest creatures flee in terror of the lion.

**8.64.14**  Then Duryodhana, the Bhoja, and the son of Subala, and Kṛpa together with the son of Śaradvat—those five great warriors—smote Dhanaṃjaya and Acyuta with arrows that make an end of the body.

**8.64.15**  Their bows, their quivers, their horses, their banners, their chariots and their drivers Dhanaṃjaya at once cut to pieces with his arrows and struck them down; and with twelve choice shafts he pierced the son of the charioteer.

**8.64.16**  Then a hundred chariots and a hundred elephants rushed swiftly upon Arjuna, weapons drawn to kill, and the Śakas, the Tukhāras, the Yavanas and their horsemen, together with the foremost Kāmbojas, eager to slay him.

**8.64.17**  Their fine weapons grasped in their hands, hands and all, and their heads he swiftly sheared off with his razor-edged shafts; and the horses, the elephants and the chariots of those who fought—Dhanaṃjaya wiped out that whole host of foes.

**8.64.18**  Then in the sky the divine drums sounded, set ringing by the delighted ones with cries of 'Well done!', and showers of the choicest flowers fell, fair of form and fragrance, auspicious, borne on the wind.

**8.64.19**  Beholding that marvel, witnessed by gods and men alike, the creatures were filled with wonder, O king; yet your son and the charioteer's son felt neither dismay nor astonishment, both fixed in a single resolve.

**8.64.20**  Then the son of Droṇa spoke to your son, pressing his hand with his own to soothe him: 'Relent, Duryodhana, make peace with the Pāṇḍavas; enough of this enmity—a curse upon this war!'

**8.64.21**  'Slain is the preceptor, the equal of Brahmā, knower of the great weapons, and likewise those bulls among men led by Bhīṣma; but I cannot be slain, nor can my uncle—so rule the kingdom long together with the Pāṇḍavas.'

**8.64.22**  'Dhanaṃjaya will stand down when restrained by me; Janārdana himself desires no quarrel; Yudhiṣṭhira is ever bent on the welfare of beings, Vṛkodara obeys his will, and so too the twins.'

**8.64.23**  'Should you and the sons of Pṛthā be reconciled with one another, the people would find peace, for it lies with you; let the surviving kings depart to their own cities, and let the soldiers lay aside their feuds.'

**8.64.24**  'But if you will not heed my words, O lord of men, surely you shall come to grief, struck down by your enemies in battle; and this you have seen, together with the whole world—what the diadem-crowned one has wrought single-handed, such as neither Indra the smasher of Bala, nor Death, nor the lord Varuṇa, nor the king of the Yakṣas could do.'

**8.64.25**  'And Dhanaṃjaya is greater still in virtues than this, and he will accept my counsel entire and will fall in line behind you; relent, O king, for the peace of the world.'

**8.64.26**  'Highest is my regard for you always; therefore do I speak to you, and out of the deepest affection; for I will hold back even Karṇa, once you have become friendly.'

**8.64.27**  'The wise declare a friend to be of four kinds—the inborn, the one won by conciliation, the one won by wealth, and the one brought near by valor; and all four exist between you and the Pāṇḍavas.'

**8.64.28**  'By nature they are your kinsmen, O hero; win them firmly again by conciliation. If, when you are gracious, they would come to friendship, then surely act so, O king of kings.'

**8.64.29**  Thus addressed by his friend with these salutary words, he pondered, sighed, and, downcast, replied: 'It is just as you say, my friend; yet hear too what I, who would make my own mind known, have to say.'

**8.64.30**  'Having slain Duḥśāsana, that evil-minded Vṛkodara spoke much by force, like a tiger; that stands fixed in my heart—it is not hidden from you—so how can there be peace?'

**8.64.31**  'And out of regard, O son of the preceptor, you ought not, O steadfast one, tell Karṇa to desist; Phalguna is worn out today with great toil, and this Karṇa will slay him by main force.'

**8.64.32**  Having spoken thus to him, and pressed him again and again, your son commanded his own soldiers: 'Strike, charge these foes, with the twang of bowstrings—why should we stand idle?'

**8.65.1**  Saṃjaya said: As the blare of conch and kettledrum swelled, those two foremost of men, whose horses were white, met together—Vaikartana the charioteer's son and Arjuna—through the ill counsel of your son, O king.

**8.65.2**  As two Himālayan elephants in rut, tusks lowered, close for the sake of a she-elephant, so those two heroes of dreadful speed came together—Dhanaṃjaya and the son of the charioteer.

**8.65.3**  As cloud closes with cloud, or as by chance mountain with mountain, so those two, ringing with the slap of bowstring against guard and the rim of the wheels, met, raining down showers of arrows.

**8.65.4**  Like two mountains broken loose, mighty, their swelling peaks dense with trees, creepers and herbs, and teeming with all manner of mountain-dwelling beasts, so did they smite one another with their great weapons.

**8.65.5**  And that clash of theirs was vast, like that of the lord of the gods and the son of Virocana of old—its waters a sharp flood of blood, with limbs, drivers and mounts shattered by arrows, unbearable to any other.

**8.65.6**  As two great lakes, rich in lotuses and lilies, fish and turtles, loud with flocks of birds, drawn near and lashed up by the wind, so those two banner-bearing chariots met.

**8.65.7**  Both equal in valor to great Indra, both the likeness of great Indra, mighty car-warriors, with shafts like the thunderbolt of great Indra they struck at each other, as Indra and Vṛtra of old.

**8.65.8**  Both armies, with their elephants, foot, horse and chariots, their garlands, garments and ornaments of varied hue, trembled and reared up in wonder, as did those who watched from the sky, at the clash of Arjuna and Karṇa.

**8.65.9**  Arms with thunderbolt-fingers were raised aloft, with lions' roars, by the delighted onlookers, when the son of the charioteer bore down upon Arjuna, eager to slay him, as one rutting elephant upon another.

**8.65.10**  There the Somakas cried out to the son of Pṛthā: 'Make haste, go forth, Arjuna, pierce Karṇa! Sever his head—no more delay!—and cut off the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's hope of the kingdom!'

**8.65.11**  And many warriors of ours called out then to Karṇa: 'On! On!'—'Slay Arjuna, Karṇa, and then let the sons of Pṛthā go back to the forest in their bark garments for a long age!'

**8.65.12**  Then Karṇa first pierced the son of Pṛthā there with ten great arrows; and Arjuna, exceedingly wroth, pierced him back with ten sharp-pointed shafts in the side, between the armpits.

**8.65.13**  With keenly sharpened shafts the two—the charioteer's son and Arjuna—gashed each other, and, seeking each an opening in the other, advanced upon one another in dreadful conflict, exulting.

**8.65.14**  Unable to bear it in that great fray, the great-souled Bhīmasena grew enraged there, and, striking palm against palm, biting his lip, dancing as it were and clapping out a beat, he cried: 'How, O diadem-crowned one, did the charioteer's son pierce you first with ten great arrows?'

**8.65.15**  'With that resolve by which you vanquished all creatures, giving them as food to the fire at Khāṇḍava, with that resolve do you slay the charioteer's son—or else I will crush him with my mace.'

**8.65.16**  Then Vāsudeva too spoke to the son of Pṛthā, seeing his arrows beaten back on the car: 'Karṇa today has utterly worn down your shafts, weapon against weapon—what is this, O diadem-crowned one?'

**8.65.17**  'O hero, why are you bewildered, why so unmindful? These Kauravas roar in their delight, putting Karṇa to the fore; for all of them have seen your weapon struck down by his weapons.'

**8.65.18**  'With that resolve by which the weapon of darkness was overcome, and the dread Rākṣasas age after age, and Dambhodbhava and the Asuras in their battles, with that resolve do you slay the charioteer's son.'

**8.65.19**  'Or with this razor-rimmed discus Sudarśana, hurled by me, sever today by force the head of this foe, as Śakra severed that of his enemy Namuci with the thunderbolt.'

**8.65.20**  'And that great resolve by which the Lord in the form of a Kirāta was won over by you—taking up that resolve once more, O hero, slay the charioteer's son with all his train.'

**8.65.21**  'Then bestow upon the king this earth girdled by the sea, with her cities, rich in villages and prosperous, her hosts of foes destroyed, and win for yourself, O son of Pṛthā, glory beyond compare.'

**8.65.22**  Spurred on by Bhīma and Janārdana, recollecting himself then and surveying his own strength, and knowing the purpose behind the great-souled one's coming, Arjuna spoke thus to Keśava:

**8.65.23**  'I now bring forth this great and terrible weapon, for the welfare of the world and for the slaying of the charioteer's son; for this let me have leave from you, and from the gods, from Brahmā, from Bhava, and from all who know the Brahman.'

**8.65.24**  Having spoken thus, he brought forth, governed by his mind, the unbearable Brāhma weapon; then that one of abundant splendor veiled all the quarters and intermediate quarters with his shafts, and the bull of the Bhāratas loosed his arrows by the hundredfold, as if they were one, of headlong speed.

**8.65.25**  By Vaikartana likewise, there in the midst of battle, were loosed masses of arrows by the thousand; roaring, they bore down upon the Pāṇḍava, like streams of water released by the rain-cloud.

**8.65.26**  He of more-than-human deeds and terrible strength struck Bhīmasena, and Janārdana, and the diadem-crowned one as well, each with three shafts, and roared dreadfully in a mighty voice.

**8.65.27**  Struck by Karṇa's arrows, the diadem-crowned one, gazing upon Bhīma and Janārdana so wounded, could not bear it; and the son of Pṛthā once more drew forth eighteen shafts.

**8.65.28**  Piercing Suṣeṇa with one arrow, Śalya with four, and Karṇa with three, he then slew with ten well-loosed shafts the lord of the assembly, clad in golden mail.

**8.65.29**  That prince, headless, armless, bereft of horses and driver, of bow and standard, fell broken from the front of his chariot, like a śāla tree hewn down with axes.

**8.65.30**  And again, piercing Karṇa with three, with eight, with two, with four and with ten, and slaying four hundred war-elephants with their fighters, he struck down eight hundred chariots, a thousand horses and their riders too, and eight thousand foot-warriors.

**8.65.31**  Beholding then the two foremost of the fight, those best of heroes, slayers of foes, locked in battle, men eager to watch—those in the sky and those on earth—reined in their mounts and stood still over Karṇa and the son of Pṛthā.

**8.65.32**  Then the bowstring of the Pāṇḍava, drawn back with a sudden mighty pull, snapped with a loud report; and in that instant the charioteer's son covered the son of Pṛthā with a hundred small arrows.

**8.65.33**  And with sixty iron shafts, sharp as serpents newly sloughed, washed in oil and winged with the feathers of birds, he pierced Vāsudeva; and in that interval the Somakas rushed forward.

**8.65.34**  Then, swiftly restringing his bow and scattering the arrows the son of the charioteer had loosed, the son of Pṛthā, in great fury, his body torn by Karṇa's shafts, took up the Somakas in battle; not even the birds could fly through the sky, so was the darkness made by his swifter weapon.

**8.65.35**  And the son of Pṛthā, laughing, pierced Śalya hard through the armor with ten shafts; then, piercing Karṇa with twelve well-loosed arrows, he struck him again with seven.

**8.65.36**  Struck hard by the feathered shafts of fearsome speed, driven by the force of the son of Pṛthā's bow, his body riven, his limbs bathed in blood, Karṇa shone like Rudra with his bow drawn.

**8.65.37**  Then with three arrows the son of the charioteer pierced Dhanaṃjaya, the equal of the lord of the thirty gods, and, bent on slaying him, launched five shafts like blazing serpents at the steadfast one.

**8.65.38**  Well loosed, they broke through the gold-wrought armor of that best of men and fell; with great speed they entered the earth, and, bathed there, turned back again to face Karṇa.

**8.65.39**  Each one of them Dhanaṃjaya cut threefold and threefold again with five swift, well-loosed broad-headed arrows, and they fell to the earth—great serpents winged like the sons of Takṣaka.

**8.65.40**  Then the diadem-and-garland-crowned one blazed up with wrath, like a fire consuming dry brushwood; with blazing, life-ending arrows drawn and loosed to the ear, he pierced Karṇa in the vital parts—and he reeled with pain, yet, by his boundless steadiness, stood firm.

**8.65.41**  Then, O king, with floods of arrows the quarters and the intermediate quarters, the radiance of the sun, and Karṇa's chariot became invisible, when Dhanaṃjaya was enraged—like the sky veiled in frost and mist.

**8.65.42**  Those guardians of the wheels, those guardians of the feet, the vanguard and all the rearguard, the slayers of foes appointed by Duryodhana, chosen out, fine charioteers, the very flower of the host—

**8.65.43**  —two thousand foremost warriors of the Kurus, them in battle Savyasācin, the bull of the Kurus, in a moment brought, every one, with their chariots, horses and drivers, to destruction, O king—he, a single hero.

**8.65.44**  Then your sons and the surviving Kurus fled, abandoning Karṇa, scattering as they went the slain and the arrow-wounded—their sons and their fathers wailing aloud.

**8.65.45**  Looking about him on every side at the emptied quarters, deserted by the Kurus rent with fear, Karṇa, O Bhārata, was not dismayed there, but charged straight on against Arjuna.

**8.66.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then the Kurus, their ranks broken, having drawn off the space of an arrow's flight, halted, and saw on every side, gleaming like lightning, the weapon of Dhanaṃjaya being raised up.

**8.66.2**  That weapon of Arjuna devoured the heroes, the firmament and the boundless-roaring sky, loosed swiftly by the wrathful son of Pṛthā in the great fray, for the slaying of Karṇa.

**8.66.3**  With the Atharvaṇa weapon of great majesty, the destroyer of foes, obtained from Rāma, Karṇa scattered that burning weapon of Arjuna and struck the son of Pṛthā with sharpened arrows.

**8.66.4**  Then a most mighty struggle arose, O king, between that Arjuna and the son of the charioteer, as they fell upon each other with their shafts, like two elephants with the dreadful blows of their tusks.

**8.66.5**  Then Karṇa set to his bow a foe-slaying arrow, keenly whetted, serpent-mouthed, blazing, terrible, well-burnished for the fray—meant for the son of Pṛthā and guarded over an exceedingly long time.

**8.66.6**  Ever honored, resting in powdered sandalwood, lying in a golden case, of deadly venom, ablaze, sprung from the lineage of Airāvata—with it he sought to carry off Phalguna's head in the battle.

**8.66.7**  To him the great-souled king of the Madras spoke, seeing Vaikartana with arrow already fixed: 'This shaft, Karṇa, will not reach the neck; take careful aim and fit a shaft that will strike the head.'

**8.66.8**  Then, his eyes red with wrath, Karṇa, his shaft already fitted, replied to Śalya by main force: 'Karṇa does not fit an arrow twice, Śalya; men like me are not given to guile.'

**8.66.9**  So saying, he loosed that shaft Balāhaka, honored by the dense rain-clouds, and cried, 'Phalguna, you are slain!'—then, hastening, he sent it forth with full force.

**8.66.10**  Seeing the serpent being fitted to the bow by Karṇa, Mādhava, best of the strong, pressed down the chariot with his feet by his strength.

**8.66.11**  As the chariot sank into the earth, the horses went down upon their knees; and then that arrow struck the diadem of that wise one.

**8.66.12**  Then that supreme adornment of Arjuna's head, renowned through earth, sky, heaven and the waters, the son of the charioteer carried off from his brow with that shaft, through the utmost effort, force, weaponry and wrath of his loosing.

**8.66.13**  Its splendor like that of the sun, the moon, fire and the planets, adorned with a net of gold, pearls and gems—it had been fashioned with effort and austerity, for Indra's sake, by the Maker of the world himself.

**8.66.14**  Of most precious form, terrifying to foes, exceedingly pleasant and fragrant, it shone—that which the lord of the gods, well pleased, gave with his own hand to the diadem-crowned one when he had slain the foes of the gods.

**8.66.15**  That which not even Hara, Varuṇa, Indra and the lord of wealth, those best of gods, with the Pināka, the noose, the thunderbolt and their choicest shafts, could batter to harm—Karṇa carried it off by force with his serpent-arrow.

**8.66.16**  Crushed by that best of shafts, kindled by the venom's fire, ablaze and flaming toward the earth, the beloved supreme diadem of the son of Pṛthā fell, like the blazing sun from the mountain of setting.

**8.66.17**  Then the serpent bore the diadem, adorned with many jewels, by force from Arjuna's head, as the thunderbolt of great Indra strikes the topmost peak of a mountain, with its trees in fair bud and flower.

**8.66.18**  As earth, mid-air, the heavens, and the waters appear riven asunder by the wind, O Bhārata, even so did that sound arise then throughout the worlds; men reeled in dismay and lost their footing.

**8.66.19**  Then, binding up his hair with a strip of white cloth, Arjuna stood unshaken, and shone like the mountain of sunrise crowned with the full-rayed, radiant sun upon its summit.

**8.66.20**  Then the great cloud-like shaft, sped by Karṇa's arm, blazing like fire and sun—the mighty serpent who bore an old feud with Arjuna—reaching the diademed one's crown, sprang forth.

**8.66.21**  Kṛṣṇa said, "Know him, this one who has wronged me, whose enmity was born of his mother's slaying." Then Kṛṣṇa said to Pārtha in the battle, "Slay you this great serpent who bears the ancient feud."

**8.66.22**  Thus addressed by the slayer of Madhu, the wielder of Gāṇḍīva, fierce-bowed among foes, said, "Who indeed is this serpent of mine today, who has come of his own accord into the mouth of Garuḍa?"

**8.66.23**  Kṛṣṇa said, "He whom you, the bowman who sated the fire-god in Khāṇḍava, drove into the sky—his body severed by your arrows, taking many shapes—his mother it was you slew."

**8.66.24**  Then the victorious one, turning aside the rest, cut with six keen and well-edged shafts the serpent that flew slantwise across the sky; and with severed body it fell to the earth.

**8.66.25**  In that very instant, with ten arrows whetted on stone and fledged with peacock feathers, Karṇa pierced Dhanaṃjaya, foremost of men, as he gazed askance.

**8.66.26**  Then Arjuna, having let fly twelve keen shafts drawn back to the ear, loosed an iron dart swift as a venomous snake, drawn full to the ear.

**8.66.27**  That choicest of shafts, well loosed, rending his bright armor as though tearing out his life, drank Karṇa's blood and entered the earth, its feathers smeared with gore.

**8.66.28**  Then the Bull-like one, enraged by the fall of that arrow, like a great serpent struck with a staff, swift in act, loosed his choicest shafts as a deadly snake spews its uttermost venom.

**8.66.29**  He smote Janārdana with twelve fresh shafts, and Arjuna with ninety; and piercing the Pāṇḍava once more with a dreadful arrow, Karṇa roared aloud and laughed.

**8.66.30**  The Pāṇḍava brooked not this exultation of his; knowing the vital points, he pierced his foe's vitals with feathered shafts, valiant as Indra, as Indra with his might struck down Bala.

**8.66.31**  Then Arjuna loosed at Karṇa ninety-nine arrows like the rod of Death; and Karṇa, his body sorely racked by those shafts, trembled like a mountain riven by the thunderbolt.

**8.66.32**  His fair ornament, adorned with choice gems, the finest diamonds and gold, struck off by the feathered shafts of Dhanaṃjaya, fell to the earth, and his splendid earrings as well.

**8.66.33**  That priceless, gleaming armor of his, wrought with toil over long ages by the best of craftsmen, the Pāṇḍava shattered in a moment, breaking it in many places with his arrows.

**8.66.34**  Then, in wrath, he smote that armorless one with four of his finest shafts; and stricken by his foe, he writhed exceedingly, like a sick man afflicted by ailments of bile, phlegm, and wind.

**8.66.35**  With keen shafts sped from the circle of his great bow, hurled with skill, effort, and strength, Arjuna in haste hewed Karṇa with many a choice arrow and pierced him even in his vital parts.

**8.66.36**  Struck hard by Pārtha with various keen-pointed, swift-flying shafts, Karṇa shone like a mountain red with ochre ore, streaming red water down its cascades.

**8.66.37**  The diademed one covered Karṇa, with his horses and his chariot, with calf-tooth shafts, O Bhārata, and overspread the very quarters with arrows golden-feathered, sped with all his might.

**8.66.38**  That son of Adhiratha, broad and full of chest, studded with calf-tooth shafts, shone like a mountain decked with aśoka and palāśa and śālmali trees in full bloom, and with spandana and sandalwood.

**8.66.39**  With arrows lodged in his body in many places, Karṇa shone in the battle, O lord of peoples, like the great mountain Mahendra, its slopes and caverns thick with trees and lovely with karṇikāra blossoms.

**8.66.40**  Loosing volleys of arrows from his bow, Karṇa shone, rayed with his net of shafts, blood-reddened, his orb of red beams—like the day-maker turned toward his setting.

**8.66.41**  The arrows loosed from between the arms of Adhiratha's son, blazing like great serpents, the keen-pointed shafts sped from Arjuna's arms met and scattered to the quarters.

**8.66.42**  Then his chariot-wheel sank into the earth, and the charioteer's son was bewildered in the battle; his car lurched by the brāhmaṇa's curse, and the weapon learned from Rāma would not come to his mind.

**8.66.43**  Unable to bear these calamities, wringing his hands, he reviled it: "Knowers of dharma have ever said that dharma guards those devoted to dharma; yet today, though I am bowed before it, it guards not its votaries—I think dharma does not always protect."

**8.66.44**  Thus speaking, his horses and driver staggering, shaken by the fall of Arjuna's weapons, faltering in his deeds from the blows upon his vitals, again and again he reviled dharma in the fight.

**8.66.45**  Then Karṇa pierced Pārtha's hand in the battle with three most dreadful shafts, and struck him besides with seven.

**8.66.46**  Then Arjuna loosed seventeen fierce-flaming arrows, straight-flying, dreadful, like Indra's bolt and like fire itself.

**8.66.47**  Those shafts of terrible speed pierced him through and fell upon the ground; and Karṇa, shaken to his soul, yet by sheer strength kept up his exertions.

**8.66.48**  Then, steadying himself by force, he sent forth the Brahmā weapon; and Arjuna, seeing it, invoked the weapon of Indra in answer.

**8.66.49**  Charging Gāṇḍīva, the string, and the arrows with sacred spells, Dhanaṃjaya loosed showers of shafts, as Purandara pours down his rains.

**8.66.50**  Then arrows full of splendor, issuing from Pārtha's car, appeared, full of great might, close beside Karṇa's chariot.

**8.66.51**  But Karṇa, the great car-warrior, made those shafts that sped before him fall in vain; and when that weapon was undone, the hero of the Vṛṣṇis spoke.

**8.66.52**  "Loose your supreme weapon, Pārtha—Rādheya devours your shafts." And Arjuna, having charged it with spells, then sent forth the Brahmā weapon.

**8.66.53**  Then, covering Karṇa with arrows and wheeling about, Arjuna pressed him; but Karṇa, in wrath, cut his bowstring with keen, well-tempered shafts.

**8.66.54**  Then, fastening another string and drawing his hand along it, the Pāṇḍava overwhelmed Karṇa with blazing arrows by the thousand.

**8.66.55**  So swift was he that Karṇa, in the battle, did not even perceive the cutting of the string nor the fixing of the new—and this was as though a marvel.

**8.66.56**  Rādheya struck down weapon with weapon against the ambidextrous archer, and displayed his own prowess as surpassing even Pārtha's.

**8.66.57**  Then Kṛṣṇa, seeing Arjuna hard pressed by Karṇa's weapon, said to Pārtha, "Strike! Bring to bear your peerless weapon."

**8.66.58**  Then Dhanaṃjaya charged with spells another arrow, like fire, like serpent's venom, made of tempered steel, and divine.

**8.66.59**  And as the diademed one took up the dread weapon, eager to hurl it, in that great battle the earth swallowed Rādheya's chariot-wheel.

**8.66.60**  With his wheel devoured, Rādheya in his anguish let fall tears, and he said to Arjuna, "Forbear a moment, O Pāṇḍava."

**8.66.61**  "Seeing my wheel here swallowed by fate, Pārtha, forsake the resolve that befits only a coward.

**8.66.62**  Upon one whose hair is loosened, who turns away, upon a brāhmaṇa with joined palms, upon one who has come for refuge, who has laid down his arms, or who is fallen into distress, O Arjuna—

**8.66.63**  upon one without an arrow, his armor fallen, his weapon slipped or broken—heroes strike not in battle, nor do princes so deal with a king. You too are a hero, son of Kuntī; therefore forbear a moment.

**8.66.64**  Until I lift this wheel from the earth, Dhanaṃjaya, you, standing in your car, ought not to slay me who am on the ground and unready. Not of Vāsudeva, nor of you, son of Pāṇḍu, am I afraid.

**8.66.65**  You are an heir of the kṣatriya line, the increaser of a great house. Remembering the teaching of dharma, forbear a moment, O Pāṇḍava."

**8.67.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then Vāsudeva, standing in the car, spoke: "Rādheya, how fortunate that you remember dharma now! Commonly the base, when sunk in calamity, blame fate—but not their own ill deeds, never those."

**8.67.2**  "When Draupadī, clad in a single garment, was haled into the hall—you, and Suyodhana, and Duḥśāsana, and Śakuni son of Subala—then, Karṇa, dharma did not shine forth for you there.

**8.67.3**  When in the hall Śakuni, skilled at the dice, defeated the son of Kuntī, Yudhiṣṭhira who knew not the dice—where then had your dharma gone?

**8.67.4**  When Kṛṣṇā, in her courses, stood in Duḥśāsana's power, and you laughed aloud in the hall, Karṇa—where then had your dharma gone?

**8.67.5**  Greedy for the kingdom, Karṇa, leaning on the king of Gāndhāra, you summoned the Pāṇḍava again—where then had your dharma gone?"

**8.67.6**  When Rādheya had thus been addressed by Vāsudeva, a fierce wrath entered the Pāṇḍava, as Dhanaṃjaya called to mind each of those deeds.

**8.67.7**  From his wrath, O great king, flames of splendor sprang forth from every pore of his body; and that was as though a marvel.

**8.67.8**  Seeing this, Karṇa then rained upon Dhanaṃjaya with the Brahmā weapon and strove anew to free his chariot. Warding off that weapon with a weapon, the Pāṇḍava struck at him.

**8.67.9**  Then the son of Kuntī loosed another weapon, the cherished one of the fire-god, aimed at Karṇa; and it blazed up fiercely.

**8.67.10**  With the weapon of Varuṇa Karṇa then quenched that fire, and with masses of cloud made all the quarters dark and overcast.

**8.67.11**  But the valiant son of Pāṇḍu, unperturbed, with the weapon of Vāyu drove away those clouds while Rādheya looked on.

**8.67.12**  That foremost standard—bright with the elephant-girth emblem, burnished with gold and pearls and gems and diamonds, fashioned fair and lofty and unclouded by the labor over long time of the most skillful of craftsmen—

**8.67.13**  ever giving strength to your host, terrifying to foes, of praiseworthy form, renowned throughout the world as belonging to one equal to the sun, in radiance like fire, sun, and moon—

**8.67.14**  with a keen, gold-feathered razor-shaft, intent, the great-souled diademed one tore down the banner of Adhiratha's son, that great car-warrior—the standard ablaze with splendor.

**8.67.15**  With that banner, O sir, glory and dharma and victory and all things dear fell away, and with it the hearts of the Kurus sank; and a great cry of "Alas, alas!" arose.

**8.67.16**  Then, hastening to slay Karṇa, Pārtha the Pāṇḍava took from his quiver the añjalika shaft, like the thunderbolt of great Indra, like the rod of fire, like the supreme ray of the thousand-rayed sun—

**8.67.17**  piercer of vitals, smeared with blood and flesh, like fire and sun, of priceless worth, slayer of the lives of men and horses and elephants, three cubits long, six-feathered, straight in flight, of terrible speed—

**8.67.18**  equal in splendor to the bolt of the thousand-eyed god, hard to bear as a flesh-devouring fiend, like Pināka and Nārāyaṇa's discus, terrible, the destroyer of all that breathes.

**8.67.19**  Joining it to a supreme great weapon, the knower of spells drew Gāṇḍīva and said aloud: "This great, matchless arrow is fixed, the breaker of bodies and the taker of life from the malign foe.

**8.67.20**  If austerity I have performed, and my elders satisfied, and the welfare of my friends desired by me, and sacred lore heard—by that truth let this well-aimed and unfailing shaft slay Karṇa, my enemy."

**8.67.21**  Having so spoken, Dhanaṃjaya loosed that dreadful arrow for the slaying of Karṇa—fierce as a spell of the Atharva-Aṅgiras, blazing, unbearable in battle even to Death himself.

**8.67.22**  The diademed one, exceedingly glad, said as he loosed it: "May this arrow of mine bring me victory; let it, of power equal to sun and moon, bear Karṇa to his end and to Yama."

**8.67.23**  With that choicest of shafts, victory-bringing, of splendor equal to sun and moon, the diademed and garlanded one, glad of aspect, the drawn-bow warrior bent on slaying, transfixed his foe.

**8.67.24**  That fair head of the army's lord, of splendor like the risen sun, like the sun at the midpoint of the autumn sky, fell to the earth, like the day-maker with his red orb sinking from the firmament.

**8.67.25**  With utmost reluctance the indweller forsook the body of that ever-blessed, exceedingly fair-formed doer of noble deeds—as a lord, attached to it, leaves a house of great abundance.

**8.67.26**  Broken by arrows, lifeless and stripped of armor, the towering body of Karṇa fell, its wounds streaming like a flow of ochre-tinted water, as the thunder-smitten summit of a mountain falls.

**8.67.27**  From the body of the fallen Karṇa a blazing splendor swiftly rose and plunged into the sky; and all the warriors among men beheld that marvel, O king, when Karṇa was slain.

**8.67.28**  The Somakas, beholding him slain and lying low, raised in joy a shout together with their hosts, and overjoyed they beat their drums, and waved their garments and their arms; and others, full of strength, danced, embracing one another, and cried aloud as they spoke.

**8.67.29**  They saw Karṇa moaning upon the ground, slain, struck down from his car by the arrow, like a fire flung down by a great wind—like the laying down of the sacred fire at the close of the rite, at the ending of the night—

**8.67.30**  his every limb thick-set with arrows, deluged in floods of blood, the body of Karṇa shone like the sun amid its own rays.

**8.67.31**  Having scorched the hostile host with the blazing beams of his arrows, the sun that was Karṇa was brought to its setting by the mighty Arjuna acting as Time.

**8.67.32**  As the sun, going to its setting, departs taking the light with it, so the arrow departed, bearing away the life of Karṇa.

**8.67.33**  In the late afternoon following midday, O honored one, the head of the Sūtaputra, severed in battle by the añjalika arrow, fell to the earth high-crested.

**8.67.34**  That arrow swiftly bore away, high over the heads of the armies, the lofty head of his foe Karṇa, carrying it straight on.

**8.67.35**  Saṃjaya said: But when the king of the Madras saw the hero Karṇa fallen upon the earth, studded with arrows, his limbs smeared with blood, lying upon the ground, he withdrew with his chariot, its banner cut away.

**8.67.36**  When Karṇa was slain, the Kurus fled, tormented by fear and deeply wounded in the fray, glancing again and again at the great banner of Arjuna, blazing with its form.

**8.67.37**  As the thousand-rayed sun sinks at the close of day, so fell to the earth the fair head of him whose deeds rivaled the thousand-eyed Indra, whose face was like the thousand-petaled lotus.

**8.68.1**  Saṃjaya said: But Śalya, seeing the armies ground down by the arrows in the clash of Karṇa and Arjuna, and watching Duryodhana as he came on, showed him the field of battle, O Bhārata.

**8.68.2**  Beholding that host with its chariots, horses, and elephants struck down, and the Sūtaputra slain, Duryodhana, his eyes brimming with tears, sighed again and again, his form stricken with grief.

**8.68.3**  And around the hero Karṇa, fallen upon the earth, studded with arrows, his limbs smeared with blood, like the sun by some chance brought down to the ground, they stood encircling him, eager to gaze upon him.

**8.68.4**  Some were elated, some terrified, some despondent, some bewildered, and others as if sunk in grief; both your foes and your own men became, each toward the other, just as their nature was.

**8.68.5**  Seeing Karṇa, his armor, ornaments, garments, and weapons scattered, struck down by Dhanaṃjaya and his vigor destroyed, the Kurus fled in utter confusion, like cattle whose bull has been slain.

**8.68.6**  Having waged a fierce struggle with Arjuna, when the king of the Madras saw Karṇa slain and lying upon the ground—like an elephant felled by a lion—he, afraid, withdrew with his chariot in all haste.

**8.68.7**  And the lord of the Madras, his mind bewildered, came swiftly upon his chariot whose banner had been carried off to the side of Duryodhana, and addressing him, stricken with sorrow, spoke these words.

**8.68.8**  Your host, its elephants, horses, chariots, and champions shattered, has become like the realm of Yama, slain as they met one another in combat by mighty men, horses, and elephants vast as mountain peaks.

**8.68.9**  Never, O Bhārata, has there been such a battle as today was waged between Karṇa and Arjuna; for Karṇa, closing in, had seized both the Kṛṣṇas and all the rest of those who were your foes.

**8.68.10**  But that Fate which moves of its own power protects the Pāṇḍavas and destroys us; for all those heroes who labored to achieve your purpose have been overpowered and slain by the enemy.

**8.68.11**  Heroes equal in might to Kubera, to Yama the son of Vivasvat, to Vāsava, and to Varuṇa the lord of waters—endowed with valor, with prowess, with strength, and with whole floods of manifold virtues—

**8.68.12**  those kings, well-nigh unslayable, who sought your welfare, have been slain in battle by the sons of Pāṇḍu. Grieve not for this, O Bhārata; this is destiny. Success comes by turns; it is not forever assured.

**8.68.13**  Hearing these words of the lord of the Madras, and surveying in his mind his own misconduct, Duryodhana, his spirit broken and his senses gone, sighed again and again, his form stricken with grief.

**8.68.14**  To him, mute with brooding, pitiable, sorely stricken and wretched, the son of Ārtāyana spoke these words: "Behold this dreadful battlefield, strewn with men, horses, and elephants, and with slain heroes.

**8.68.15**  With great elephants like mountains, fallen, flung down at a single stroke, their vital parts pierced by arrows—some of them writhing, others lifeless—their drivers, weapons, armor, and warriors all shattered;

**8.68.16**  like lordly mountains shattered by the thunderbolt, their rocks, beasts, trees, and herbs broken apart—their bells, goads, lances, and banners flung away, garlanded with gold and drenched in floods of blood;

**8.68.17**  with horses pierced by arrows and fallen, some still breathing, others vomiting blood, wretched and groaning, their eyes rolling, biting the earth and crying out piteously;

**8.68.18**  with elephant-warriors and horsemen flung down, some with breath ebbing, others lifeless—with men, horses, elephants, and chariots all crushed, the earth is grim to look upon, like the great Vaitaraṇī river of death.

**8.68.19**  With elephants their trunks and limbs lopped off, shuddering as they fell to the earth; with glorious elephant-, chariot-, and horse-warriors, and with foot-soldiers, the enemies who fell facing the foe—their armor, ornaments, garments, and weapons scattered—the earth lies covered, as though strewn with fires burnt out at dawn.

**8.68.20**  With mighty men struck down by arrow-strokes, gazed upon as they lay fallen by the thousand—their senses lost, then breathing once more—the earth seemed as if pervaded by fires; the ground shone as if strewn with planets fallen from heaven and blazing bright, like a clear night-sky aflame with stars.

**8.68.21**  The arrows loosed from the arms of Karṇa and Arjuna, tearing through the bodies of elephants, horses, and men and swiftly casting out their life-breaths, entered the earth, like great serpents seeking their lairs on every side.

**8.68.22**  With men, horses, and elephants slain in the fray, and chariots shattered by arrows, the ground along the path of Dhanaṃjaya and the son of Adhiratha became impassable even for elephants, exceedingly hard to traverse.

**8.68.23**  With chariots, and warriors mangled by choice shafts, their drivers, horses, fine weapons, and banners pierced through, their arms scattered, their seats hewn apart, their wheels, axles, yokes, and triple-poles cut to pieces;

**8.68.24**  with their gear torn loose, their iron fittings broken, their appendages destroyed, their quivers and frames split, their bodies shattered though adorned with gems and gold—the earth lay strewn with them, like the sky with autumn clouds.

**8.68.25**  By heaps of men, elephants, chariots, and horses, war-chariots well-fashioned, swift and adorned, their drivers slain—as they rushed swiftly on, dragged along—were ground to powder in manifold ways.

**8.68.26**  Iron bludgeons banded with gold, battle-axes, spiked iron clubs, pestles, and pikes; and spotless swords drawn from their sheaths; and maces bound with bands of refined gold—all these had fallen.

**8.68.27**  Bows adorned with golden armlets and ornaments; arrows with shafts bright with gold; gleaming, spotless rapiers drawn from their scabbards; and javelins with swords, all shining like gold;

**8.68.28**  Parasols, yak-tail fans, conches, and garlands bright with the choicest flowers and gold; elephant-housings wrapped in banner-cloths; diadem-wreaths and gleaming crowns;

**8.68.29**  Whisks scattered about, and housings, and necklaces with quivering pendants of choicest pearls; head-ornaments, armlets, and fine bracelets; neck-chains and golden ornaments strung on threads of gold;

**8.68.30**  finest gems, diamonds, gold, and pearls, and auspicious jewels both great and small; bodies long accustomed to the utmost ease, and heads whose faces were like the moon;

**8.68.31**  Forsaking their bodies, their enjoyments, their possessions, and all that was delightful and pleasant, and attaining a great steadfastness in their own duty, they passed to the worlds that they had filled with their fame.

**8.68.32**  Having spoken thus, Śalya fell silent; and Duryodhana, his mind beset with grief, crying "Alas, Karṇa! Alas, Karṇa!", was stricken, bereft of his senses, his eyes streaming with tears.

**8.68.33**  All the kings, with the son of Droṇa at their head, comforted him and went on together, glancing again and again at the great banner of Arjuna, blazing with glory.

**8.68.34**  The earth, drenched red with the blood streaming from the bodies of men, horses, and elephants, shone like a woman robed and garlanded in red, gleaming with gold—open to the approach of all.

**8.68.35**  Their forms hidden by blood, O king, shining brightly in that dreadful hour, the Kurus could not stand their ground when they looked upon it; all were driven forth, as if banished from the worlds of the gods.

**8.68.36**  Sorely grieved by the slaying of Karṇa, crying "Alas, Karṇa! Alas, Karṇa!", they hastened swiftly to their camps, O king, gazing upon the reddened sun.

**8.68.37**  His body studded with the keen, gold-shafted arrows loosed from the Gāṇḍīva, their feathers smeared with blood, Karṇa shone upon the earth, slain though he was, like the sun wreathed in its rays.

**8.68.38**  The blessed Vivasvat, compassionate to his devotee, touching with his rays the blood-drenched body of Karṇa and taking on a deep red hue, drew near to the far ocean as if longing to bathe.

**8.68.39**  Reflecting thus, the hosts of gods and sages set out and went, each to his own abode; and the people too, having pondered, dispersed at their ease across the sky and the surface of the earth.

**8.68.40**  Having witnessed that wondrous battle of the foremost Kuru heroes, terrible to all living things—the battle of Dhanaṃjaya and the son of Adhiratha—the people, marveling and full of praise, then went their way.

**8.68.41**  Though the hero Rādheya, his armor cut through by arrows, slain in the slaughter, had given up his life, yet his beauty did not forsake him even then.

**8.68.42**  Adorned with manifold ornaments, O king, his armlets of burnished gold, the slain Vaikartana lay like a tree in blossom.

**8.68.43**  Bright as the finest gold, like a blazing fire, that tiger among men, with his son, was quenched by the splendor of Pārtha, O king—he who had scorched the Pāṇḍavas and the Pāñcālas with the fire of his weapons.

**8.68.44**  He who, ever a good man among the good, when entreated by suppliants would say "I shall give," and never "There is none"—that bull among men was slain in single chariot-combat.

**8.68.45**  For that great-souled one, all was bestowed upon the brāhmaṇas, nothing kept for himself; there was nothing he would not give to the brāhmaṇas—not even his own life.

**8.68.46**  Ever dear to men, a giver whose gifts were dear, he has gone to heaven, bearing away the hope of victory of your sons, their refuge and their shield.

**8.68.47**  When Karṇa was slain, the rivers ceased to flow; the sun went down darkened; and the planet that is the son of Yama, glowing aslant with the hue of the burning sun, rose up, O king.

**8.68.48**  The sky split asunder, and the earth roared; the winds blew harsh beyond all measure; the quarters, wreathed in smoke, blazed fiercely; and the great oceans heaved with a thunderous din.

**8.68.49**  The mountains with their forests and their ranges trembled, and the hosts of creatures were sore afflicted, O honored one; and Bṛhaspati, oppressing Rohiṇī, took on a hue like that of the moon and the sun.

**8.68.50**  When Karṇa was slain, the quarters could not be discerned, veiled in darkness; the heaven shook, the earth quaked; a meteor fell, bright as fire; and the prowlers of the night grew exceedingly glad.

**8.68.51**  When Arjuna struck down with his razor-arrow the head of Karṇa, whose face shone like the moon, then in the firmament, in heaven, and here below, again and again arose the cry "Alas!" from the people.

**8.68.52**  Having slain in battle his foe Karṇa, honored by gods, gandharvas, and men, Arjuna the son of Pṛthā shone with surpassing splendor, like the thousand-eyed Indra when he had slain Vṛtra.

**8.68.53**  Then upon his chariot—roaring like a mass of thunderclouds, gleaming like the sun at the zenith of the autumn sky, decked with pennons, its standard sounding a terrible note, shining like snow, the moon, conch, and crystal, adorned with gold, pearls, gems, diamonds, and coral, and of matchless speed—

**8.68.54**  those two best of men, crushers of the foes of the Pāṇḍavas, exalted and like unto fire and the sun, free from fear, shone upon the field of battle, as though Viṣṇu and Vāsava rode together in one car.

**8.68.55**  Then with the twang of bowstring, the slap of the palm-guard, and the rumble of chariot-wheels, having forcibly stripped their foes of their luster and overwhelmed the Kurus with floods of arrows, the ape-bannered one and he whose banner bore the king of birds blew their resounding conches with might, casting down the spirits of their enemies.

**8.68.56**  Grasping in their hands those finest of conches, overspread with nets of gold, deep-voiced, and white as snow, those best of men set them to their lips—pressed them with their noble mouths—and blew them together as one.

**8.68.57**  The blast of both the Pāñcajanya and the Devadatta filled the earth, the firmament, the heavens, and the waters.

**8.68.58**  Those two heroes, making the forests, mountains, rivers, and quarters ring with the sound of their conches, terrifying the army of your sons, gladdened Yudhiṣṭhira.

**8.68.59**  Then the Kurus, the moment they heard the rising sound of the conches, fled with speed, abandoning the lord of the Madras and their own lord, Duryodhana, O Bhārata, the lord of the Bhāratas.

**8.68.60**  In that great battle the assembled hosts of beings then applauded Dhanaṃjaya, resplendent in his glory, and Janārdana too, as men hail the two luminaries newly risen.

**8.68.61**  Both those scorchers of foes, Acyuta and Arjuna, studded with Karṇa's arrows, shone upon the field, like the spotless moon and sun newly risen, wreathed in their rays, having scattered the darkness.

**8.68.62**  Then, casting off those swarms of arrows, the two lords of matchless valor, surrounded by their friends, returned and entered their own camp at ease, like Vāsava and Acyuta summoned to a sacrificial assembly.

**8.68.63**  By gods, gandharvas, men, and cāraṇas, by great sages, yakṣas, and mighty serpents, they were honored with the highest exaltation of victory, having slain Karṇa then in that supreme battle.

**8.69.1**  Saṃjaya said: When Karṇa had thus been struck down and your army had fled, the lord of the Dāśārhas embraced Pārtha and out of joy spoke these words.

**8.69.2**  "Vṛtra was slain by the cleaver of strongholds; Karṇa has been slain by you, O Dhanaṃjaya. Men will tell of these slayings, of Karṇa and of Vṛtra together.

**8.69.3**  Vṛtra was slain in battle by the wielder of the thunderbolt of vast splendor; but Karṇa was slain by you with your bow and sharpened arrows.

**8.69.4**  This valor of yours, renowned in the world and bearing your fame, let us proclaim, O son of Kuntī, to the wise King of Righteousness.

**8.69.5**  By announcing to the King of Righteousness the slaying of Karṇa in battle—long desired through all this time—you shall be freed of your debt."

**8.69.6**  When Pārtha had said "So be it," Keśava, that bull of the Yadus, calmly turned about the chariot of that foremost of charioteers.

**8.69.7**  To Dhṛṣṭadyumna, to Yudhāmanyu, to the two sons of Mādrī, to Vṛkodara, and to Yuyudhāna, Govinda spoke these words.

**8.69.8**  "Stand watchful, facing the foe—may it be well with you—until it is announced to the king that Karṇa has indeed been slain by Arjuna."

**8.69.9**  Granted leave by those heroes, Govinda, taking Pārtha with him, went to the king's quarters and beheld Yudhiṣṭhira.

**8.69.10**  That tiger among kings lay upon a splendid golden couch; and the two of them, rejoicing, clasped the feet of the monarch.

**8.69.11**  Marking their great delight and the marks of their superhuman blows, and judging that Rādheya had been slain, Yudhiṣṭhira rose up.

**8.69.12**  Then Vāsudeva, the delight of the Yadus, ever pleasant of speech, related to him the death of Karṇa just as it had come to pass.

**8.69.13**  Then Kṛṣṇa, the Acyuta, smiling faintly, his hands folded in reverence, spoke to King Yudhiṣṭhira, whose enemy was now slain.

**8.69.14**  "By good fortune the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva and the Pāṇḍava Vṛkodara are safe, and you yourself, O king, are well, and Mādrī's two sons, the Pāṇḍavas."

**8.69.15**  "You have been delivered from this destruction of heroes, from this hair-raising battle. Swiftly now, O lord of the earth, attend to the tasks of the time that follows."

**8.69.16**  "The cruel Vaikartana, the mighty son of the charioteer, is slain. By good fortune you are victorious, O lord of kings; by good fortune you prosper, O Pāṇḍava."

**8.69.17**  "That basest of men who once laughed at Kṛṣṇā when she was won at the gambling match — today the earth drinks the blood of that son of the charioteer."

**8.69.18**  "That foe of yours lies there, his limbs torn by arrows, O bull of the Kurus. Behold him, O tiger among men, pierced in many places by shafts."

**8.69.19**  Yudhiṣṭhira, greatly delighted, honored in turn the scion of the Dāśārhas, crying "By good fortune! By good fortune!" O lord of kings, and then, in his joy, he spoke these words.

**8.69.20**  "This is no wonder in you, O mighty-armed son of Devakī — that with you as his charioteer Pārtha should this day perform such a feat of manhood."

**8.69.21**  And the best of the Kurus, the upholder of righteousness, taking hold of the right arm adorned with its armlet, addressed both Keśava and Arjuna, calling on Pārtha.

**8.69.22**  "You two were declared by Nārada to be the gods Nara and Nārāyaṇa, the ancient and supreme among men, appointed for the establishment of righteousness."

**8.69.23**  "And more than once the wise lord Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana told me this divine tale, O mighty-armed one."

**8.69.24**  "By your power, O Kṛṣṇa, Dhanaṃjaya with the Gāṇḍīva conquers the foes who stand before him, and nowhere has he ever turned his back."

**8.69.25**  "Victory is assured for us, and never defeat, since you have taken up the office of Pārtha's charioteer in battle."

**8.69.26**  Having spoken thus, O great king, that mighty warrior mounted the chariot adorned with gold, yoked with horses ivory-white in hue and black-tailed,

**8.69.27**  that tiger among men, surrounded by his own forces; and then, taking leave with kind regard of the two heroes Kṛṣṇa and Arjuna,

**8.69.28**  he came to behold the battlefield with its many turns of fortune, conversing all the while with those two heroes, Mādhava and Phalguna.

**8.69.29**  He saw Karṇa, that bull among men, lying on the field of battle, hewn to pieces on every side by the shafts loosed from the Gāṇḍīva.

**8.69.30**  Seeing Karṇa slain together with his son, King Yudhiṣṭhira praised both those tigers among men, Mādhava and the Pāṇḍava.

**8.69.31**  "This day I am a king upon the earth, O Govinda, together with my brothers, protected by you, my lord, a hero and a sage."

**8.69.32**  "Seeing the proud Rādheya, that tiger among men, struck down, this evil-souled son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will today despair of his very life and of his kingdom both, now that the great warrior Karṇa is slain."

**8.69.33**  "By your grace we too have attained our purpose, O bull among men; you and the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva are the victors, O joy of the Yadus. By good fortune you triumph, O Govinda; by good fortune Karṇa has been brought down."

**8.69.34**  Thus, again and again, in his great gladness, did Yudhiṣṭhira the Dharmarāja praise Janārdana and Arjuna too, O lord of kings.

**8.69.35**  Then, surrounded by all his brothers headed by Bhīma, the great warriors, filled with joy, hailed the king with their praises.

**8.69.36**  Nakula and Sahadeva, and the Pāṇḍava Vṛkodara, and Sātyaki, O great king, the foremost chariot-warrior of the Vṛṣṇis,

**8.69.37**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, and the Pāṇḍavas, the Pāñcālas, and the Sṛñjayas — all honored the son of Kuntī, now that the son of the charioteer was slain.

**8.69.38**  Having hailed the king, Pāṇḍu's son Yudhiṣṭhira — they who bore the look of conquerors, who had attained their aim, those battle-drunk smiters,

**8.69.39**  praising the two Kṛṣṇas, those scorchers of foes, with words full of laudation, the great warriors, joined with gladness, went each to his own camp.

**8.69.40**  "Thus did this vast and hair-raising destruction come to pass, through your evil counsel, O king. Why now do you grieve for what is past?"

**8.69.41**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having heard those unwelcome tidings, O king, Dhṛtarāṣṭra the lord of the earth, that Kaurava, overcome with utmost anguish, fell senseless to the ground, and so too did the lady Gāndhārī, faithful to her vows and seer of righteousness.

**8.69.42**  Vidura caught the king as he fell, and Saṃjaya likewise, and both of them sought to comfort that lord of the earth.

**8.69.43**  In the same way the women of the royal household raised up Gāndhārī. Comforted by those two, the king sat silent, his senses gone.
